It was the middle of spring in the year 1492, more than three hundred and fifty years ago, when the angel of dawn, spreading his broad, slow wings, journeyed from the Levant to the Pillars of Hercules, crossed the snow-capped Alps, and descended upon the bare western islands. In that timeless flight, he beheld the same vast outlines of firm earth and restless sea, the identical mountain shadows stretching over the same valleys, and the olive groves and pine forests standing where they stand today. He saw the great domes and spires of cities rising beside the rivers, intertwined with the curved masts along the coast, in those exact spots they occupy now. As the pale light of his passage pierced the homes of men, it illuminated the same scenes it illuminates today: the rosy warmth of sleeping infants, the weary awakening of the sick and sorrowful, the hurried rise of the laborer, and the final slumber of the student who had spent the night questioning the stars, the sages, or his own soul in search of a hidden truth to break the barriers of mortal life and reveal a path through the darkness, an arc within an immeasurable circle of glory. The great rivers that shape human destiny have scarcely altered their courses, nor have the life-streams flowing within the human heart ceased to pulse with the same profound needs, loves, and terrors. As our thought trails slowly in the wake of that dawn, we are struck by the broad uniformity of the human condition, unchanged in its essential history: hunger and toil, planting and harvest, love and death.

If, instead of following the dim twilight, our imagination halts at a specific point in history to await the full morning, we may behold a world-renowned city that has retained its outline almost untouched since the days of Columbus. It stands as an almost unblemished symbol amidst the torrent of changing things, reminding us that we resemble the men of the past far more than we differ from them, for the great mechanical principles upon which those domes and towers were erected ensure a similarity in human construction that transcends all possible alteration. Doubtless, if the spirit of a Florentine citizen, whose eyes were closed forever while Columbus waited and argued over his three humble vessels at the port of Palos, could return from the shades and pause where our thoughts now pause, he would find fellowship and understanding among the inheritors of his birthplace.

Let us imagine that such a shade has been granted permission to revisit glimpses of that golden morning and stands once more upon the famous hill of San Miniato, overlooking Florence from the south.

The spirit wears the habit of his living days: the folds of his well-lined black silk garment, or *lucco*, hang in solemn, unbroken lines from neck to ankle. Upon his head rests a plain cloth cap, adorned with a *becchetto*, a long hanging strip of drapery serving as a scarf in times of need, covering a penetrating face. It is a countenance, perhaps not strikingly handsome, yet distinguished by a firm, well-cut mouth, preserved in distinct humanity by a closely shaved lip and chin. It is a face heavy with the memories of a keen and varied life passed upon the banks of the gleaming river below. As he gazes upon the scene before him, the sense of familiarity overwhelms his perception of change, leading him to think it possible to descend once more into the streets and resume that bustling life where he left it. For it is not merely the mountains and the westward-curving river that he recognizes, nor the dark flanks of Mount Morello opposite him, nor the long valley of the Arno stretching its grey, low-tufted luxuriance toward the distant ridges of Carrara, nor the steep height of Fiesole crowned with monastic walls and cypresses. He sees all the green and grey slopes sprinkled with villas, naming them as his gaze sweeps over them. He perceives other familiar objects intimately tied to his daily walks. Though he notes the absence of the seventy or more towers that once surmounted the city walls like a regal diadem, his eyes refuse to dwell on that void; they are irresistibly drawn to the single tower springing, like a tall flower-stem reaching for the sun, from the square, turreted mass of the Old Palace in the very heart of the city—the tower that looks no worse after four centuries have passed since he used to stroll beneath it. The great dome, too, the greatest in the world, which in his early boyhood had been nothing more than a daring dream in the mind of a small, quick-eyed man—there it rises, casting its large curves over the hills. And the well-known bell-towers—Giotto's, with its distant hint of rich color, the gracefully spired Badia, and the others—he viewed them all from the shoulder of his nurse.

"Surely," he muses, "Florence can still ring her bells with the solemn hammer-sound that once beat upon the hearts of her citizens and kindled the fires within them. And here, on the right, stands the long, dark mass of Santa Croce, where we buried our famous dead, laying laurels upon their cold brows and fanning them with the breath of praise and banners. Yet Santa Croce had no spire then; we Florentines were so consumed with great building projects that we could not execute them all in stone and marble. We had our frescoes and shrines to fund, not to mention rapacious condottieri, bribed royals, and purchased territories, so our façades and spires were forced to wait. But what architect could the Frati Minori, the Franciscans, have employed to construct that spire for them? If it had been built in my day, Filippo Brunelleschi or Michelozzo would have devised something of another fashion—something worthy to crown the church of Arnolfo."

At these words, the spirit lets out a sigh, and his gaze travels onward to the city walls, where now he dwells with wonder at these modern times. Why have five of the eleven convenient gates been closed? And why, above all, should the towers that were once a glory and defense have been leveled? Has the world become so peaceful, and do Florentines dwell in such harmony, that there are no longer conspiracies to bring ambitious exiles home with armed bands at their backs? These are difficult questions; it is far easier and more pleasant to recognize the old than to account for the new. And there flows the Arno, with its bridges precisely where they stood—the Ponte Vecchio, least like any other bridge in the world, laden with the same quaint shops where our spirit remembers lingering, perhaps to observe the progress of that great palace which Messer Luca Pitti had begun constructing with huge stones gathered from the Hill of Bogoli (now Boboli) close behind, or perhaps to conduct some business with the cloth-dressers in Oltrarno. The exorbitant line of the Pitti roof remains hidden from San Miniato, but the yearning of the old Florentine is not to see Messer Luca's overly ambitious palace, built for his own aggrandizement; it is to be down among those narrow streets and busy, humming piazzas where he inherited the eager life of his fathers. Is not the anxious voting with black and white beans still taking place down there? Who are the Priori in these months, dining with sober regulation in the Palazzo Vecchio, feasting on tripe and boiled partridges seasoned by practical jokes against the unfortunate butt among those potent lords? Are not the significant banners still hung from the windows, still distributed with decent pomp under Orcagna's Loggia every two months?

Life possessed a zest for the old Florentine when he, too, trod the marble steps and shared in those dignities. His politics spanned an area as wide as his trade, stretching from Syria to Britain, yet they retained the passionate intensity and detailed practical interest belonging only to a narrow stage of corporate action; only to members of a community shut in close by hills and walls of six miles' circuit, where men knew one another as they passed in the street, set their eyes daily upon the memorials of their commonwealth, and were conscious not merely of the right to vote, but of the chance of being voted for. He loved his honors and his gains, the business of his counting-house, his guild, and the public council chamber; he loved his enmities, too, and fingered the white bean destined to keep a hated name out of the *borsa* with more complacency than if it had been a golden florin. He loved to strengthen his family through a good alliance, returning home with a triumphant light in his eyes after concluding a satisfactory marriage for his son or daughter under his favorite loggia in the evening cool. He loved his games of chess played under that same loggia, his biting jests, and even his coarse jokes, seeing nothing beneath the dignity of a man eligible for the highest magistracy. Through these experiences, he had gained insight into all manner of affairs at home and abroad: he had served in the "Ten" managing the war department, in the "Eight" attending to home discipline, as a Prior or Signore heading the executive government, and even risen to the supreme office of Gonfaloniere. He had participated in embassies to the Pope and the Venetians, and served as commissary to the Republic's hired army, directing inglorious, bloodless battles in which no man died of brave breast wounds—*virtuosi colpi*—but only of casual falls and trampling. Thus, he learned to distrust men without bitterness, viewing life primarily as a game of skill,

The Shipwrecked Stranger.

The Loggia de' Cerchi lay nestled in the labyrinthine heart of ancient Florence, hidden behind the Badia in a network of narrow lanes that few strangers ventured to thread. They sought it only when driven by a desperate need to find a specific, austere door marked with an inscription: *Qui Nacque Il Divino Poeta*. To Dante's ear, these same alleys had once echoed with the clamor of bloody feuds between warring factions; but by the fifteenth century, they resounded merely with the raucous, unbecoming squabbles and broad jests of wool-carders working in the districts of San Martino and Garbo.

Beneath this loggia, upon the early morning of the ninth of April, 1492, two men gazed at one another. One stood slightly stooped, peering down with the scrutinizing curiosity of a tradesman assessing his wares; the other lay sprawled upon the cobblestones, gazing upward with the wide-eyed surprise of a dreamer abruptly awakened.

The standing figure spoke first. He was a gray-haired man of broad shoulders, physically "moulded with the fist and polished with the pickaxe" in the rough Tuscan idiom. Yet, a gravity etched deeply into the lines of his brow and mouth served to counterbalance any contempt one might derive from the rugged, unrefined nature of his appearance. At his feet rested a large sack of leather, filled to capacity, and before him hung a peddler's basket adorned with trinkets for women—thread, pins, and shards of glass, likely acquired in trade for these smaller goods.

"Young man," he said, gesturing to a ring on the finger of the reclining figure, "once your beard grows thick enough, you'll learn better than to nap in street corners with a gem like that on your forefinger. By the holy Evangelists! If anyone else had stood over you two minutes past, you'd be in trouble—but Bratti Ferravecchi is no thief. The cat cannot devour her mouse if she does not catch it alive, and Bratti cannot enjoy profit without tasting the sweetness of a bargain. Indeed, on St. John's Day, three years hence, the Saint sent a dead body my way—a blind beggar, his cap lined with coins. My stomach revolted at the unearned money until I realized St. John was owed his tithe for what I spent annually at the festival. So I buried the body, paid for a mass, and found it a fair trade. But how does a youth with the countenance of Messer San Michele sleep on a stone bed with the wind for a curtain?"

The deep, guttural tones of the speaker were barely intelligible to the newly awakened listener, yet he grasped the gesture regarding the ring. With a half-automatic obedience to the warning, he removed it and shoved it inside his doublet, rising simultaneously to stretch his limbs.

"Your tunic and hose clash badly with that jewel, young man," said Bratti, speaking deliberately. "One might say the Saints sent *you* a corpse; but if you took the jewels, I trust you interred him—and you can certainly afford a couple of masses for him, along with the rest."

A chill, akin to pain, seemed to shoot through the listener's frame, halting his careless stretching. For a fleeting moment, he turned upon Bratti with a sharp frown, but quickly masked it with an air of indifference. He plucked a red Levantine cap, hanging like a great pouch over his left ear, pushed back his long, dark-brown curls, glanced at his attire, and smiled.

"You speak truth, friend: my garments are as weather-stained as an old sail, and they are not old sails, only like an old sail, they have been sprinkled with the sea as well as the rain. The fact is, I am a stranger in Florence. When I arrived footsore last night, I preferred casting myself into a corner of this hospitable porch to hunting further for a hostel, which might prove to be a nest of bloodsuckers of every variety."

"A stranger, truly," said Bratti, "for your words melt from your throat so smoothly that neither a Christian nor a Florentine can distinguish a hook from a hanger. Are you not from Genoa? More likely Venice, judging by the cut of your clothes?"

"At this present juncture," said the stranger, smiling, "where I come from matters less than where I may go next for a morsel of breakfast. Your city casts a grim aspect upon me right here: can you direct me to a livelier quarter where I might procure a meal and lodging?"

"That I can," said Bratti, "and it is your good fortune, young man, that I have walked in from Rovezzano this morning and detoured to Mercato Vecchio to offer an Ave at the Badia. That, I say, is your luck. But it remains to be seen what is my profit. Nothing for nothing, young man. If I guide you to Mercato Vecchio, you shall swear by your patron saint to let me bid for that stained suit of yours when you acquire better gear—as doubtless you will."

"Agreed, by San Niccolò," said the other, laughing. "But now let us depart to this said Mercato, for I feel the urgent lack of a better lining for this doublet which you covet."

"Coveting? Nay," said Bratti, hoisting his bag onto his back and stepping forward. However, he paused mid-step, bursting into loud, harsh tones that grated like a cartwheel on stones: "*Chi abbaratta*—*baratta*—*b'ratta*—*chi abbaratta cenci e vetri*—*b'ratta ferri vecchi*?" ("Who wants to exchange rags, broken glass, or old iron?")

"It is worth but little," he remarked presently, returning to his conversational register. "Hose and all, your clothes fetch a low sum. Nevertheless, if you wish to equip yourself with a lute surpassing any new instrument, or a sword worn by a Ridolfi, or a paternoster of the latest fashion, I can offer you a grand bargain by allowing credit for your clothes. For, simple as I stand here, I possess the best-furnished shop in the Ferravecchi district, situated close to the Mercato. Blessed be the Virgin! It is not a pumpkin I carry on my shoulders. Yet I do not remain caged in my shop all day; I have a wife and a raven to tend the stock at home. *Chi abbaratta*... And now, young man, whence do you come, and what is your business in Florence?"

"I thought you delighted in nothing without a bargain," observed the stranger, somewhat scornfully. "You have offered me nothing yet in exchange for that information."

"Well, well; a Florentine does not mind paying a fair price for news, even if it fills the stomach less than winning a pair of hose. If I take you to the fairest maiden in the Mercato for a cup of milk—that shall constitute a fair bargain."

"Nay; I can locate her myself if she truly resides in the Mercato, for beautiful heads often peer from doors and windows. No, no. Moreover, a sharp trader like yourself ought to know that he who bids for nuts and news may discover them hollow."

"Ah! young man," said Bratti, casting a sideways glance of admiring surprise, "you were not born on a Sunday—the salt shops were open when you entered the world. Are you not a Hebrew? Hark from Spain or Naples? Let me inform you that the Friars Minor are attempting to heat Florence as hot as Spain against those dogs of hell who seek to monopolize the profits of usury, leaving none for Christians. And when you stroll the Calimala with a scrap of yellow cloth in your cap, it will mar your beauty far more than a sword-cut across that smooth olive cheek of yours.—*Abbaratta, baratta*, *chi abbaratta*? I tell you, young man, gray cloth stands against yellow cloth; and there is as much gray cloth in Florence as would clothe and hood the entire Duomo, whereas there is not so much yellow cloth as would make hose for Saint Christopher—blessed be his name, and send me a glimpse of him today!—*Abbaratta, baratta, b'ratta*—"

"All that is delightful information you are dispensing gratis," said the stranger, "but it unfortunately concerns me not. I am no Hebrew."

"See, now!" exclaimed Bratti, triumphant. "I have secured a good bargain with mere words. I have compelled you to reveal something, young man, though you are as slippery as a lamprey. St. John be praised! A blind Florentine matches two one-eyed men. But here we arrive at the Mercato."

They had now emerged from the narrow thoroughfares into a vast square, known to the older chroniclers of Florence as Mercato Vecchio, or the Old Market. Although this piazza had served as a provision market from time immemorial and, according to fond imagination, might have been the precise spot where the Fiesolan ancestors of the Florentines descended from their lofty fastnesses to trade with the valley's rustic populace, it had never been shunned by Florentine wealth. In the early decades of the fifteenth century, which now drew to a close, the Medici and other powerful families of the *popolani grassi*, or commercial nobility, maintained residences there. Their ears

Once Bratti had merged into the throng of gossipers, the young stranger, having grown impatient and hopeless of uncovering the cause of the city's agitated humors, decided that lingering for an escort served no purpose. He began to wander the piazza, seeking a vendor of sustenance who possessed less than the average citizen's fervent curiosity regarding public affairs. Suddenly, as if struck by a sudden resolve, he plunged his hand into the pouch hanging at his waist, probing it repeatedly with a furrowed brow.

"Not a single coin!" he muttered, in a tongue that was neither Tuscan nor purely Italian. "I believed I kept one poor fragment. Well, I shall seek my breakfast through charity alone!"

He had not walked far before locating a quarter of the market where such currency would likely go unchallenged. In a secluded corner, distant from the clusters of chattering folk, stood two mules, decked out in vibrant red tassels and collars. One bore wooden vessels for milk; the other, panniers overflowing with fresh herbs and salads. Leaning her elbow upon the neck of the milk-bearing beast, sat a young girl, seemingly no older than sixteen. A red hood enveloped her countenance, rendering her features entirely hidden save for a glimpse of her cheek, which appeared even more infantile in its delicacy due to the total seclusion of her hair. Perhaps the poor child, exhausted by her labor in the pre-dawn gloom preparing for the journey from a castle several miles away, had succumbed to slumber in that semi-reclining posture. Yet, the stranger felt no hesitation in waking her. His approach, however, was so gentle that it seemed to the sleeping damsel merely a twig of thyme brushing against her lips as she bent to gather greens.

The dream shattered instantly. She opened wide, azure eyes and sprang upright, startled and confused by the sudden presence of the youth standing before her. She heard him speak in a voice that seemed strangely soft, so that even if she had been composed, she would have assumed he spoke words utterly unintelligible to her. Her immediate reaction was to turn her head slightly aside and lift a corner of her green serge mantle to use as a screen. He repeated his words gently—"Forgive me, fair one, for disturbing your rest. I am starving, and the aroma of milk renders breakfast more desirable than ever."

He had chosen the phrase "_muoio di fame_" (I die of hunger) knowing it would resonate with her ears, and he delivered it with a playful cadence, mimicking the intonation of a mendicant. This time, understanding dawned. The corner of the mantle fell, and within moments, a large cup of fragrant milk was offered to him. He accepted it without further compliment, lifting it to his lips. As he drank, the little maiden gathered the courage to gaze upward at the long, dark curls of this singular-spoken stranger. Though his garments were tattered, he bore no resemblance to the beggars she had encountered before.

As this observation unfolded, another current of feeling moved her hand into a bag suspended beside the mule. When the stranger finally set down his cup, he saw a large slice of bread extended toward him, accompanied by a fleeting glance from those blue eyes, clearly encouraging him to accept this additional gift.

"But perhaps that is your own meal," he remarked. "No, I have had sufficient without payment. A thousand thanks, my gentle soul."

There was no verbal reply, yet the bread was nudged closer, as if impatient at his refusal. As the stranger's long, dark eyes rested upon the childish face, it seemed the girl was gathering strength to look up and meet his gaze.

"Ah, then, if I must take the bread," he said, placing his hand upon it, "I shall grow bolder still, and beg for another kiss to sweeten the loaf."

Despite the strangeness of his voice, which had initially seemed jarring, his speech grew wonderfully clear. She flushed deeply and lifted a corner of her mantle to cover her mouth. Just as the somewhat presumptuous stranger leaned forward, his fingers touching the arm that held up the shielding cloth, a harsh voice rang sharply in his ear.

"Who are *you*—curse upon you! No honest buyer, I warrant, but a parasite of the dice-house—or something fouler. Go! Dance away and find keener company, or I shall give you a tune to a faster beat than you care for!"

The young stranger recoiled slightly, then regarded the speaker with a gaze provocatively devoid of alarm or contrition. A slight, saucy amusement on his face blossomed into a broad, beaming smile as he took in the figure of his threatener. She was a stout, burly woman, wearing a man's jerkin thrown loosely over her green serge gown, with the peaked hood of some discarded mantle fastened tightly around her sunburnt face. Beneath the coarseness and premature lines of age, there lingered a half-tragic, half-comic maternal likeness to the tender, baby-like visage of her daughter—a resemblance that often serves as a more chilling, shudder-inducing prophecy than the grim visage of Death itself.

There was something undeniably appeasing in that bright, youthful smile, yet Monna Ghita was not a woman to display any weakness. She continued to speak, her tone seemingly sharpened by exasperation.

"Yes, yes, you may grin as readily as any monkey in a cap and jerkin. You're a minstrel or a mountebank, I'll swear; you look as foolish as a tumblers when he has fallen upside-down and managed to land on his heels. And what devilish tricks hast thou played, Tessa?" she added, turning to her daughter, whose frightened countenance was now an open invitation for scolding. "Do you give away milk and provisions? Ay, ay, thou wouldst carry water in thy ears for any idle vagabond who did not wish to stoop for it, thou silly, staring rabbit! Turn thy back and lift the herbs from the panniers, or I shall make thee recite a few Hail Marys without counting."

"Nay, Madonna," the stranger pleaded with a smiling plea, "do not be angry with your lovely Tessa for showing pity to a hungry traveler who found himself unexpectedly without a single coin. Your handsome face looks so striking when it frowns that I long to see it illuminated by a smile."

"_Go via_! I know what paste you are made of. You may tickle me with that straw straw for a long while before I laugh, mark my word. Be gone, with a wretched Easter! Else I shall put a beauty-spot or two on that face of yours that shall ruin your kissing before Advent."

As Monna Ghita raised her formidable hands, ready to enforce the first and final requirement of her eloquence, Bratti, who had arrived a minute prior, addressed his companion. "What dost thou think of this pretty parrot, Nello? Does not his tongue smack of Venice?"

"Nay, Bratti," whispered the barber, lowering his voice, "thy wisdom contains much of the ass, as I told thee but moments ago; especially regarding the ears. This stranger is a Greek, or else I am not the barber who has held the exclusive shave of the excellent Demetrio, and extracted more than one sorrowful tooth from his learned jaw. And this youth appears to have come straight from Olympus—at least, once I have applied my razor."

"_Orsù_! Monna Ghita!" continued Nello, delighted to witness some sport. "What tempest hath caused such a thunderclap? Hath this young stranger behaved ill?"

"By Saint John!" said the cautious Bratti, who had not yet shed his original suspicions regarding the shabbily-clad possessor of jewels, "he acted rightly in fleeing from *me*, if he intended to stir up mischief. I can swear that I found him beneath the Loggia de' Cerchi, wearing a ring upon his finger such as I have beheld upon Bernardo Rucellai himself. Not another rusty nail's worth do I know about him."

"The truth is," said Nello, eyeing the stranger with good humor, "this _bello giovane_ has been somewhat too presumptuous in admiring the charms of Monna Ghita, and attempted to kiss her while her daughter's back was turned; for I observe that the pretty Tessa is currently too busy to look this way. Was it not so, Messer?" Nello concluded in a tone of courtesy.

"You have divined the offense like a prophet," the stranger laughed. "Only that I lacked the fortune to find Monna Ghita here at first. I begged a cup of milk from her daughter and accepted this gift of bread, for which I was offering a humble token of gratitude, before I enjoyed the higher pleasure of facing these riper charms, which I may have been too bold to admire."

"_Go, go_! Depart, every one of you, and remain in purgatory until I pay to extract you, will ye?" cried Monna Ghita fiercely, elbowing Nello aside and guiding her mule forward to compel the stranger to step aside. "Tessa, thou simpleton, draw the mule forward a trifle; the cart approaches."

As Tessa turned to take the mule's bridle, she cast one timid glance at the stranger, who was now moving with Nello out of the path of an approaching market-cart. The glance lasted just long enough to catch the beckoning motion of his hand, indicating that he had been awaiting this moment for

The Barber's Shop.

"To tell you the truth," the young stranger remarked to Nello as they pushed their way past the tangle of carts and mules, "I am relieved to be delivered from that patron of mine to someone with a less barbarous accent and a less puzzling trade. Is it customary among you Florentines for a traveling peddler of shattered glass and rags to boast of selling lutes and swords?"

"Customary? By no means. Our Bratti is no ordinary man. He operates on a theory, and adheres to it with a rigor I cannot claim for any philosopher I have ever had the honor of shaving," replied Nello, whose verbosity flowed like an overfilled vessel, refusing to dispense merely a drop. "Bratti intends to extract the maximum possible pleasure—that is to say, the greatest profit—from this life, concluding his existence with a bargain for the easiest passage through purgatory by surrendering his winnings to the Holy Church. He has drafted his last will on the cheapest terms a notary could secure. Yet I have often told him, 'Bratti, your bargain is a limp one, and you stand on the lame side of it. Does it not sadden you to gaze upon the paintings of Paradise? You shall never there haggle over rags and rusty nails; the saints and angels require no pins or tinder. Save perhaps with San Bartolomeo, who carries his skin about in a cumbersome fashion, I see no opportunity for trading in secondhand garments.' But God forgive me," Nello added, shifting his tone and crossing himself, "such levity is unfit for a morning when Lorenzo lies dead and the Muses rend their hair—an image painfully familiar to a barber's mind. And you yourself, Messere, are likely under a cloud; when a gentleman of your speech and bearing accepts such humble lodgings, it betokens some calamity."

"What Lorenzo do you speak of whose passing causes such commotion?" the stranger inquired, appearing so preoccupied with this point that he missed the indirect query trailing behind it.

"What Lorenzo? There is but one Lorenzo," Nello asserted, "whose demise could stir the Mercato into turmoil, make the lantern of the Duomo leap in despair, and compel the lions of the Republic to devour one another. I mean Lorenzo de' Medici, the Pericles of our Athens—if I may draw such a parallel in the hearing of a Greek."

"Why not?" the other laughed. "For I suspect Athens, even in the days of Pericles, could not have produced a learned barber so profound."

"Yes, yes; I felt sure I was not mistaken," said the rapid Nello. "Otherwise, I have shaved the venerable Demetrio Calcondila to little purpose. But forgive me, I am lost in wonder: your Italian surpasses his, though he has resided in Italy for forty years—even better than that of the accomplished Marullo, who may be said to have wed the Italic Muse in senses beyond number, having married both our learned and lovely Alessandra Scala."

"It will assuage your surprise to learn that I descend from Greek stock planted in Italian soil far older than the mulberry trees, which have flourished so readily here. I was born at Bari, and raised by an Italian. In fact, I am a Greek, very much as your peaches are Persian. The Greek hue has been subdued in me, I presume, by prolonged residence and extensive travel in the land of gods and heroes. And, to confess a private matter, this same Greek dye, along with a few ancient gems I possess, constitutes the sole fortune shipwreck has left me. But—when towers fall, you know it is ruin for the small nest-builder—the death of your Pericles makes me regret that I did not turn my steps toward Rome, as I should have, but for a deceptive Minerva in the guise of an Augustinian monk. 'At Rome,' he said, 'you will vanish into a throng of starving scholars; but at Florence, every corner bathes in the sunshine of Lorenzo's patronage: Florence is the fairest market in Italy for commodities like yours.'"

"_Gnaffè_," said Nello, "and so it shall remain, I trust. Lorenzo was not the sole patron and arbiter of learning in our city—heaven forbid! Because he was a large melon, every other Florentine is not a pumpkin, I assure you. Have we not Bernardo Rucellai, Alamanno Rinuccini, and countless others? And if you seek information on such matters, I, Nello, am your man. It feels like a millennium before I can serve a _bel erudito_ like yourself. First, however, the matter of your hair. That beard, my fine young man, must go, were it as dear to you as the nymph of your dreams. Here in Florence, we dislike a man whose nose projects over a waterfall of hair. Remember, you have crossed the Rubicon once you are shaven: if you repent and allow your beard to regrow after it has hardened in battle with the razor, your mouth will eventually reveal not the divine prerogative of lips as Messer Angelo termed it, but appear like a dark cavern fringed with brambles."

"That is a dreadfully ominous prophecy," the Greek observed, "particularly if your Florentine maidens are as lovely as the little Tessa I kissed this morning."

"Tessa? She is a rough-handed peasant girl. You will ascend to the favor of ladies who bear no scent of the mule-stables. To achieve that end, you must not carry the air of a _sgherro_ or a man of ill repute; you must resemble a courtier, a scholar of the most refined sort, such as our Pietro Crinito—one who sins among the well-bred and well-fed, rather than sucking down vile _vino di sotto_ in a tavern."

"With all my heart," the stranger agreed. "If the Florentine Graces demand it, I am willing to sacrifice this trifling matter of my beard, yet—"

"Yes, yes," Nello interjected. "I foresee what you would say. It is the _bella zazzera_—the hyacinthine locks you refuse to part with. There is no need. A little trimming—ecco!—and you will look not unlike the illustrious Prince Pico di Mirandola in his prime. And behold, we are timely arrived in the Piazza San Giovanni, before the door of my establishment. Yet you pause; naturally, you wish to view our wonder of the world, our Duomo, our Santa Maria del Fiore. Very well, a fleeting glance suffices; I beseech you reserve a closer scrutiny for after you have been shaved. I am trembling with the inspiration of my art, quite to the edge of my razor. Ah, then, follow this way."

The mercurial barber grasped the stranger's arm and guided him to a spot on the south side of the square, offering an immediate view of the massive dark shell of the dome, the slender, soaring grace of Giotto's bell tower, and the quaint octagon of San Giovanni facing them, displaying its unique gates of storied bronze, which still retained a somewhat dimmed splendor of their original gold. The inlaid marbles appeared fresher then, in hues of pink, white, and purple, than they do today when four centuries of winter have bleached their white to the rich ochre of well-aged pipestone; the façade of the cathedral did not stand in ignominy of faded stucco, but bore the magnificent promise of the half-finished marble inlays and statue niches Giotto had conceived a century and a half prior. As the campanile, in all its harmonious variety of color and form, lifted the eyes upward into the clear air of this April morning, it seemed a prophetic symbol, declaring that human life must, somehow and sometime, align itself with that pure, aspiring beauty.

Yet this was not the impression it evoked in the Greek. His eyes were drawn irresistibly upward, but standing with folded arms and curls falling backward, a faint trace of scorn lingered on his lip. When his gaze returned, it swept the scene with a scanning coolness that rather pricked Nello's Florentine spirit.

"Well, my fine young man," the Greek said, with some impatience, "you seem to regard our Cathedral as lightly as if you were the Angel Gabriel descended straight from Paradise. I should like to know if you have ever beheld finer craftsmanship than Giotto's tower, or any dome that would not look a mere mushroom beside Brunelleschi's there, or any marbles more exquisite or cunningly worked than those our Signoria procured from distant quarries at a price sufficient to purchase a dukedom. Come, have you ever witnessed anything to equal them?"

"If you asked me that question with a scimitar at my throat, in Turkish fashion, or even with your own razor," the young Greek smiled cheerfully, stepping toward the gates of the Baptistery, "I dare say you might extort a confession of the true faith. But with my throat free from peril, I venture to tell you that your buildings smell too much of Christian barbarism for my palate. I harbor a shuddering intuition of what lies within—hideous, smoke-blackened Madonnas; fleshless saints in mosaic, staring down with idiotic astonishment and rebuke from the apse; skin-clad skeletons hanging on crosses, speckled with arrows, or stretched on gridirons; women and monks with heads twisted aside in eternal lamentation. I have seen enough of those wry-necked favorites of heaven at Constantinople

First Impressions.

"Good day, Messer Domenico," Nello greeted the first visitor stepping into the shop, offering a silent nod to the second. "You arrive with the timing of cheese upon macaroni. Ah, you are in haste, seeking a shave without delay—eccolo! And today is a morning where every soul carries heavy burdens upon their mind. Florence bereaved—the very hinge of Italy torn away—and heaven itself bewildered as to its next move. *Oimè*!" Nello sighed, though the sun was already climbing steadily toward dinner hour. "As I remarked, you arrive like freshly grated cheese. This young stranger seeks an honorable merchant willing to advance funds against a specific ring of value. If I were to count every goldsmith and money-lender in Florence upon my fingers, I could not find a finer name than Menico Cennini. Moreover, he possesses other wares wherein you trade—Greek learning and youthful eyes, a dual implement that printers like yourselves are perpetually in want of."

The grave elderly gentleman, son of Bernardo Cennini, who two decades prior had heard tales of the German printing press and subsequently cast his own typefaces in Florence, sat in a lathered, passive silence while Nello poured forth this torrent of conversation. Yet, the old man slowly turned a sideways glance toward the stranger.

"This handsome youth offers you unlimited Greek, Latin, or Italian," Nello continued, delighting in expansive paraphrase. "He is a marvel of juvenile learning, akin to Francesco Filelfo or our own unparalleled Poliziano. A second Guarino, too, though fate has dealt him a cruel blow; shipwrecked, he has likely lost a treasury of precious manuscripts that might have lent even greater correctness to your editions, Domenico. Fortunately, he has salvaged a few gems of rare worth. His name is—what did you call yourself, Messer?"

"Tito Melema," the stranger replied, sliding the ring from his finger and presenting it to Cennini. Nello, equally swift with his razor as with his tongue, had just freed the goldsmith from the shaving cloth.

Meanwhile, the man who had entered with the goldsmith—a tall figure of about fifty, sporting a short, trimmed beard, an old felt hat, and a threadbare mantle—had kept his gaze fixed upon the Greek. Now, he spoke abruptly.

"Young man, I am painting a depiction of Sinon deceiving old Priam, and I should be grateful for your likeness to serve as my Sinon, provided you grant me a sitting."

Tito Melema started, his face flushing with pale astonishment, as if struck by a sudden accusation. But Nello gave him no moment to flounder for a response. "Piero," the barber interjected, "you are the most extraordinary mixture of humors and fancies ever encased in human skin. What sorcery do you intend to work upon the visage of this learned youth to fashion it into a traitor? Pray ask him to lift his eyes upward; then, behold, you could mold a Saint Sebastian that would draw throngs of devout women to weep. Or, if you prefer a classical tone, drape myrtle around his curls and transform him into a young Bacchus—or, more accurately, a Phoebus Apollo—for his countenance burns with the warmth and brightness of a summer morn. It won me as a friend within the span of a single 'credo.'"

"Aye, Nello," the painter assented, his speech broken by abrupt pauses. "And if your tongue could cease its endless chirping long enough for your understanding to ponder the matter, you would see precisely why the face of Messere suits my traitor. A perfect traitor must possess a visage upon which vice leaves no mark—lips capable of lying with a dimpled smile, eyes of agate-like brilliance and depth that no infamy can tarnish, cheeks that remain un-haggard after a murder. I speak not that this young man is a traitor; I merely assert that he bears a face which would render him a more consummate villain if coupled with a treacherous heart. To state otherwise is to say nothing more nor less than that he possesses a beautiful face, animated by rich young blood, sufficient to maintain its color through simple nourishment without much aid from virtue. He may harbor the heart of a hero alongside it; I aver nothing to the contrary. Ask Domenico there if the lapidaries can always discern a gem solely by sight. And now, I shall stuff tow into my ears, for your chatter combined with the street bells is more than I can endure. Speak no further, but trim my beard."

With these final words, Piero—known as "di Cosimo" after his master, Cosimo Rosselli—took two pieces of tow, stuffed them firmly into his ears, and settled into the chair before Nello. The barber shrugged, casting a grimace of feigned intelligence at the Greek, as if to declare, "A whimsical fellow, do you see? Everyone takes his ramblings for mere jests."

Tito, standing transfixed with his long, dark eyes resting on the unknown man who had addressed him so ambiguously, seemed to regain his composure only when Piero shifted his position. Satisfied with the explanation, he returned his attention to Cennini, who now observed closely.

"This is a curious and valuable ring, young man. This intaglio, depicting a fish with a crested serpent above it, carved into the black stratum of the onyx—or rather, nicolo—is beautifully highlighted by the surrounding blue of the upper layer. Does this ring, doubtless, possess a history?" Cennini asked, looking up with keen scrutiny.

"Yes, indeed," Tito answered, meeting the gaze frankly. "The ring was discovered in Sicily. From those who devote themselves to gems and seals, I understand that both the stone and the carving possess virtues to bring fortune to the wearer, particularly at sea, and to restore whatever one may have lost." He smiled then. "However, although I have worn it constantly since departing Greece, it has not rendered me entirely fortunate at sea, as you perceive, unless escaping drowning counts as sufficient proof of its virtue. It remains to be seen whether my lost chests will surface. But to ensure no opportunity is missed, Messer, I implore you to hold the ring briefly as pledge for a small sum far below its true value. I shall redeem it the moment I can dispose of certain other gems secured within my doublet, or indeed as soon as I can earn something through any scholarly employment, if fortune deigns to allow me such luck."

"That shall be arranged, young man, if you will accompany me," Cennini said. "My brother Pietro, who judges scholarship far better than I, may perhaps provide you with a task testing your capabilities. In the meantime, return your ring until I can deliver the necessary florins, and if you wish, follow me."

"Yes, yes," Nello chimed in. "Go with Messer Domenico; you could not seek better company. He was born under the constellation that grants skill, riches, and integrity, whatever that celestial alignment may be—a detail of lesser consequence, for infants cannot select their own horoscopes, and indeed, if they could, there might be a disastrous rush of babes at particular epochs. Besides, our Phoenix, the incomparable Pico, has demonstrated that horoscopes are all a nonsensical dream—which is the least troublesome of opinions. *Addio, bel giovane!* Do not fail to return to me."

"No fear of that," Tito replied, bowing farewell as he turned his bright face toward the door. "You owe me a great service; that is the most positive guarantee of our future meetings."

"Say what you will, Piero," Nello muttered as the young stranger vanished. "I shall never view such an exterior without taking it as a sign of a lovable nature. Next, you will claim that Leonardo, whom you incessantly rave about, ought to have crafted his Judas as beautiful as Saint John! But you are as deaf as the summit of Mount Morello with that accursed tow in your ears. Well, well: I shall extract a bit more of this young man's history before escorting him to Bardo Bardi."

The Via de' Bardi, a thoroughfare immortalized in the chronicles of Florence, winds through Oltrarno, the district hugging the southern bank of the Arno. It stretches from the Ponte Vecchio to the Piazza de' Mozzi, where the Ponte alle Grazie rises. To the east, the street is bordered by a steep incline known in the fifteenth century as the Hill of Bogoli, a legendary quarry that supplied the city with its cobblestones—a foundation notoriously treacherous when the rain turned the stones to sludge. To the west, the architecture leans toward the river, presenting a jagged, irregular façade that offers the water a fractured mirror image as the sun dips toward the western horizon. Though picturesque, these structures, to the historian's eye, feel like a somewhat jarring replacement for the mighty palaces of the Bardi family, which the mob razed to the ground in a fit of popular fury in the mid-fourteenth century.

Those Bardi were a lineage of fierce vigor, prominent in the early internecine wars that tore Florence apart. When the noble towers darkened the narrow lanes and the gutters ran red with neighbor's blood, the old god Mars might have chuckled at the hypocrisy of those who worshipped Jupiter while slaughtering one another. Yet, the Bardi hands were not content merely to grip the sword-hilt; they possessed a refined taste for minted silver and gold. Their vision was equally acute, perceiving that empire could be built through trade as easily as through steel. By the middle of the fourteenth century, they had ascended from commoners to feudal counts of Vernio, acquiring vast estates and strongholds that rankled the republican sensibilities of their fellow citizens. They stood at the forefront of European commerce, the Christian Rothschilds of their age, furnishing specie for King Edward III's campaigns and receiving revenues "in kind"—chiefly wool, the lifeblood of Florentine galleys. When their great debtor defaulted and Sicilian creditors demanded immediate repayment, the resulting financial collapse sent shockwaves through the Mediterranean. Yet, unlike the humbled bankrupts of later ages, the Bardi did not bow their heads in shame. On the contrary, they doubled their arrogance, becoming among the most despised magnates who, according to Giovanni Villani, provoked the wrath of the armed populace in 1343. Fortified in their street between the two bridges, they held the narrow avenues like panthers cornered, until an assault from the hill forced them to retreat. Twenty-two of their great houses (*palagi e case grandi*) were sacked and burned, and many of their chiefs fled the city. Yet, like a plant with deep roots, the family survived. They re-emerged repeatedly in Florentine affairs, their history a tapestry woven with threads of dizzying altitude and crushing depth, shifting from opulence to destitution and back again, though they never reclaimed their old homes by the river, which by 1492 belonged to the Neri.

Within one of these Neri houses, however, dwelt a descendant of the Bardi, belonging to that same branch which had once been Counts of Vernio. In him, the old family pride and the hunger for preeminence survived, though transformed. This descendant was neither a warrior swift in the street nor a merchant bold in speculation. Nor did he delight in the grand strategy of global finance. Instead, he was a man whose hands were calloused by the endless labor of copying manuscripts, a frugal eater clad in threadbare garments, first by choice and eventually by necessity. He sat amidst his books and shattered fragments of antiquity, illuminated only by the lingering glow of distant, youthful eras. He was a penniless, blind old scholar: Bardo de' Bardi, the man to whom the barber Nello had pledged to introduce the young Greek, Tito Melema.

His residence stood on the hillside side of the street, a somber mass of stone punctuated by small, guarded windows and crowned by a roofed terrace. Heavy, grim doors, fitted with scrolled iron hinges and barred with iron grilles, opened onto a vaulted courtyard, empty save for a massive iron lamp hanging from the center. A smaller door led to a stone staircase and the ground-floor rooms, which served as warehouses. Both floors were crammed with precious stores destined for the markets of the Scheldt, the coasts of Africa, or the islands of the Aegean. Maso, the elderly servant, returned from the market laden with cheap vegetables, making his slow ascent to the second story where his master, Bardo, awaited entry.

Maso crossed the antechamber and pushed open the left-hand door. As he entered, a clear, youthful voice echoed from the far end of a long, spacious chamber lined with shelves holding books and antiquities in meticulous order. "Ah, you are come back, Maso," the voice said warmly. "It is well. We have wanted nothing."

Here and there, on separate pedestals, stood a beautiful feminine torso, a headless statue with an uplifted arm brandishing a sword, rounded infantile limbs severed from their trunks, inviting kisses upon the cold marble, alongside well-preserved Roman busts and vases from Magna Grecia. A large central table held antique bronze lamps and small vessels of dark pottery. The palette of the room was predominantly pale and sombre: the vellum bindings, with their deeply ridged spines, offered little contrast to the marble, which had grown livid from long burial. A once-splendid carpet at the far end had worn thin to a dull gray, and the dark bronzes lacked the sunlight necessary to reveal their verdigris, for the sun had not yet climbed high enough to penetrate the narrow windows overlooking the Via de' Bardi.

The sole splash of vibrant color in the gloom belonged to the hair of a tall maiden, perhaps seventeen or eighteen, standing before a carved *leggio*, or reading-desk, reminiscent of those found in Italian church choirs. Her hair was a reddish-gold, enriched by an unbroken, gentle ripple akin to the waves of sunset clouds on a grand autumn evening. It was confined by a black fillet above her small ears, rippling forward to veil her neck above a square-cut gown of black serge (*rascia*). Her gaze was fixed upon a large volume before her; one long, white hand rested on the desk, while the other clasped the back of her father's chair.

Her blind father sat with his head slightly turned, as if visually tracking her movement. His delicate pallor, framed by a black velvet cap atop his drooping white hair, accentuated the striking resemblance between his aged features and those of the young girl. Both possessed cheeks devoid of the rose, a refinement of brow and nostril, balanced by a full yet firm mouth and a powerful chin. This combination lent them an expression of proud tenacity and latent impetuosity, mirrored in the backward poise of the girl's head and the grand line of her neck and shoulders. It was a countenance that inspired not love, but a mixture of unwilling admiration and dread, a verdict ultimately to be decided by the eyes—which, in the father's case, had long been silent, and in the daughter's, were currently bent on the Latin pages of Politian's *Miscellanea*.

She was reading aloud from the eighty-first chapter: "There was a certain nymph of Thebes named Chariclo, especially dear to Pallas... Teiresias coming as a hunter to quench his thirst at the same fountain, inadvertently beheld Minerva unveiled, and immediately became blind. For it is declared in the Saturnian laws, that he who beholds the gods against their will, shall atone for it by a heavy penalty..."

As she reached the conclusion of the story, her hand slipped from the back of the chair to meet her father's, which he had just lifted. Yet, she did not look up, continuing to recite the Greek quotation from Nonnus, though her voice betrayed a slight alteration, hinting at suppressed emotion. Then, the old man spoke.

"Stay, Romola. Reach me my own copy of Nonnus. It is a more correct copy than any in Poliziano's hands, for I made emendations in it which have not yet been communicated to any man. I finished it in 1477, when my sight was fast failing me."

Romola walked to the far end of the room with a queenly step, the natural consequence of her tall, finely wrought frame, requiring no conscious adjustment of posture.

"Is it in the right place, Romola?" asked Bardo, perpetually seeking assurance that the external reality aligned with the minute details stored in his mind.

"Yes, father," Romola replied, her voice carrying a faint, weary undertone beneath habitual patience. "At the west end of the room, on the third shelf from the bottom, behind the bust of Hadrian, above Apollonius Rhodius and Callimachus, and below Lucan and Silius Italicus."

As she spoke, approaching her father, she saw his arms stretch out with nervous excitement to seize the volume. Her hazel eyes filled with pity. She hastened to lay the book on his lap and knelt beside him, looking up as if convinced that the love shining in her face must somehow pierce the darkness shutting out the rest of the world. At that moment, the ambiguous attractiveness of Romola's face, wherein pride and passion seemed to quiver against native refinement and intelligence, was transfigured into something lovably tender by a mixture of pity and affection. It was evident that the deepest founts of feeling within her had

When Maso returned to the threshold and admitted the visitors, Nello, performing a profound courtesy to Romola, gently propelled Tito forward, guiding him toward her father.

"Messer Bardo," Nello announced, his voice adopting a measured, deferential timbre uncommon to his usual boisterous self, "I bring you the Greek scholar who has long sought audience with you. Driven by both the renown of your learning and the value of your collections, and by an urgent necessity arising from a recent shipwreck, he desires your patronage. His name is Tito Melema, at your service."

Astonishment, akin to seeing a panther-clad stranger wielding a thyrsus, gripped Romola. The barber had concealed the youth's age and visage entirely. Among her father's academic guests, she rarely encountered anyone but middle-aged or silver-haired men. Yet, etched in her mind with indelible clarity, remained the singular masculine face of her lost brother: fair-complexioned, with sunny hair mirroring her own. But a defensive wall of proud self-reliance, forged by her father's complaints of worldly injustice, hemmed in the rushing tide of admiration. Tito's countenance, however, radiated a rich, untarnished beauty that required no competition from the color of his black *sajo* or knee-length tunic. To Romola's young, wintry existence—one inherited solely from the dark mines of books containing memories of a dead mother, a vanished brother, and a blind father's happier times—it appeared like a sudden wreath of spring dropped into a frozen landscape. Though she returned his initial bow with the same pale, reserved demeanor, as he approached the ice beneath her feet began to melt. When he glanced toward her again, a fleeting pink flush stained her cheeks, vanishing instantly as if summoned back by her imperious will. Yet, Tito's gaze held a gentle, beseeching adoration, a quality uniquely propitious to a shy, proud spirit, serving perhaps as the only atonement for being too handsome. The spell of his presence stemmed largely from an absence of demand or assumption, evoking the image of a fleet, dark-eyed beast that captivates not by fleeing indifference, but by resting its chin upon one's palm, seeking a stroke as if driven by love.

"Messere, I offer you welcome," said Bardo, his tone tinged with condescension. "Misfortune coupled with learning, particularly Greek learning, serves as a letter of credit earning the ear of every educated Florentine. As you surely know, since the era when Manuelo Crisolora spread his teachings a century ago, no one earns the title of scholar who possesses merely the transplanted, derivative literature of the Latins; such inert students are rightly stigmatized as *opici*, or barbarians, echoing the Roman phrase 'refilling their urns at the fountainhead.' I perceive, as Nello has forewarned, that I am wholly blind. A calamity to which Florentines are peculiarly susceptible, whether due to the biting spring winds from the Apennines, the jarring transition from indoor gloom to summer sunlight—which allegedly produced the multitude of the *lippi* among the ancients—or some occult cause defying our shallow guesses. Pray, be seated: Nello, my friend, sit."

After ensuring the visitors were settled, with Romola taking her customary chair at his right, Bardo inquired, "From which region of Greece do you hail, Messere? I presumed your unhappy homeland had exhausted its sons capable of cherishing any image of her primordial glory, though indeed the barbarous Sultans have recently shown willingness to graft upon their wild stock the precious vine they themselves have trampled. From whence do you come?"

"I sailed last from Nauplia," Tito replied. "However, I have resided in Constantinople and Thessalonica and traveled through regions seldom visited by Western Christians since the triumph of Turkish arms. Let me inform you, Messere, that I was not born in Greece, but at Bari. I spent my first sixteen years in Southern Italy and Sicily."

As Tito spoke, a subtle emotion, like a breeze rippling water, crossed Bardo's delicate features. Leaning forward, he extended his right hand toward Romola and turned his head as if to address her, but correcting himself, he withdrew and spoke in a subdued voice, "Excuse me; is it not true—that you are young?"

"I am twenty-three," Tito answered.

"Ah," Bardo murmured, his tone vibrating with restrained excitement, "and you undoubtedly possessed a father who cherished your early education—one who, perhaps, was himself a scholar?"

A brief pause ensued before Tito's response reached Bardo's ears; for Romola and Nello, it commenced with a sharp shock that caused a momentary tremor of the lip, doubtless reviving a supremely painful memory.

"Yes," Tito replied, "at least a father by adoption. He was a Neapolitan, a man of accomplished scholarship in both Latin and Greek. However," he added after another hesitation, "he is lost to me—lost on a voyage he rashly undertook to Delos."

Bardo sank back, too delicate to press further into a sorrow he sensed was fresh. Romola, acutely aware of the fibers Tito's voice had disturbed in her father, realized that this new acquaintance had, with startling swiftness, breached the barrier separating them from the alien world. Nello, perceiving that the conversation had momentarily stalled, seized the opportunity to relieve the silence.

"In truth, it is as transparent as Venetian glass that this fine youth has received the finest training. The two Cennini have already set him to work on their Greek texts. It seems to me they are not men who begin to cut until they have felt the edge of their tools; they tested him well beforehand, I am sure. And if there are two things that cannot be concealed—love and a cough—I posit a third: ignorance, once a man is compelled to act beyond mere nodding agreement. The *tonsor inequalis* (uneven barber) betrays himself inevitably when he picks up the shears; does not this hold true, Messer Bardo? I speak in the fashion of a barber, yet as Luigi Pulci declares—"

*"Perdonimi s'io fallo: chi m'ascolta / Intenda il mio volgar col suo latino."* ("Forgive me if I err: whoever listens / Must understand my vernacular through his Latin.")

"Nay, my dear Nello," Bardo interjected with an air of friendly severity, "you are not wholly illiterate and could likely achieve more respectable progress in learning if you had refrained somewhat from the *cicalata* and street-corner gossip to which our Florentines are so prone, and especially if you had not clogged your memory with those frivolous productions of which Luigi Pulci offers the most egregious example—a compendium of extravagances and incongruities infinitely distant from the models of a pure age, resembling rather the *grylli* or conceits of a period when mystical meaning served as a warrant for monstrous forms; with this distinction, however, that while the monstrosity remains, the mystic meaning is absent. In contemptible contrast to Virgil's great poem, which, as I long maintained with Filelfo before Landino assumed to expound similar views, embodied the deepest lessons of philosophy within a graceful, well-knit fable. And I cannot help regarding the proliferation of these babbling, lawless works, despite being countenanced by patronage and, to some degree, the example of Lorenzo himself—otherwise a friend to true learning—as a sign that the glorious hopes of this century are to be quenched in gloom; nay, that they serve as a delusive prologue to an age worse than the Age of Iron—an era of tinsel and gossamer, wherein no thought possesses the substance to be molded into consistent, enduring form."

"Again, pardon me," Nello exclaimed, spreading his palms outward and shrugging his shoulders, "I find myself knowing so many things in good Tuscan before I have time to consider the Latin equivalent; and Messer Luigi's rhymes invariably slip off the lips of my clients: that is what corrupts me. Indeed, speaking of clients, I have left my shop and my reputation too long in the custody of my sluggish Sandro, who does not deserve the title of *tonsor inequalis*, but rather to be pronounced simply a bungler in the vernacular tongue. So, with your permission, Messer Bardo, I shall take my leave—understood that I remain at your service whenever Maso calls upon me. It seems a millennium since I dressed and perfumed the damigella's hair, which deserves to shine in the heavens as a constellation, though indeed it would be a pity for it ever to stray so far from reach."

Three voices joined in a fugue of friendly farewells as Nello retreated, bowing to Romola and gesturing to Tito. The astute barber observed that the charming youth, who had somehow enchanted him through some potent magic, was firmly launched in Bardo's favor. Satisfied that his introduction had not failed thus far, he deemed it proper to withdraw.

The brief outburst of wrath provoked by Nello's unfortunate citation had diverted Bardo's mind from the feelings that had previously constrained further speech, and he now addressed Tito with his ordinary calmness.

"Ah! Young man, you are fortunate in uniting the advantages of travel with those of study, and you will be welcomed among us as a bearer of fresh

Bartolommeo Scala, the secretary of the Florentine Republic, resided in a stately mansion near the Porta Pinti, now known as the Casa Gherardesca. His coat of arms displayed an azure ladder crossing a golden field, topped with the motto *Gradatim*—"Step by Step"—emblazoned above the gate. This heraldic declaration served as proof to all passersby that the miller's son had climbed to his high station solely through his own exertions, a truth he proclaimed without shame. Though Scala possessed a vanity matched only by his pompous demeanor, he remained fundamentally honest; he genuinely believed in his own worth and saw no necessity for hypocrisy. By this time, he had ascended the highest rung of his ladder. Having held the secretaryship for twenty years, he had frequently delivered orations from the *ringhiera*, the balcony of the Old Palace, before visiting princes, with the Republican Lion wearing his golden crown and the crowd shouting "Viva Messer Bartolommeo!" He had served on an embassy to Rome, where he was appointed Titular Senator, Apostolic Secretary, and Knight of the Golden Spur. Eight years prior, he had become Gonfaloniere, the ultimate pinnacle of a Florentine citizen's ambitions. Meanwhile, his wealth had grown inexorable, and his gout had deepened, following the pattern common to successful mortals. The Knight of the Golden Spur was often forced to sit with his helpless, cushioned foot beneath the magnificent loggia he had constructed, overlooking the expansive gardens and lawns behind his palace.

On the day he granted an audience to Tito Melema, the sun of late May warmed the flowers and grass beyond the pleasant shade of his loggia. Scala had discarded his too-stately silk lucco, draping instead a light, loose mantle over his tunic. Seated beside him were his beautiful daughter, Alessandra, and her husband, the Greek soldier-poet Marullo. Opposite them sat two friends, neither sufficiently illustrious to dominate the conversation, thereby ensuring they became attentive listeners. Yet, despite his gout and his elevated status, Messer Bartolommeo's felicity was far from complete. It was embittered by a stack of papers lying before him, mostly comprising a correspondence between himself and the scholar Poliziano. It was a peculiar habit of the era—and one difficult to credit today—to recite personal quarrels and offer scholarly visitors the entirety of a lengthy exchange. This was not the first or second instance where Scala had sought the candid opinions of his friends regarding the balance of right and wrong in roughly thirty Latin letters exchanged between himself and Poliziano. These missives sprang from certain epigrams written in a tone of playful jest. It was the chronicle of a quintessential, albeit petty, dispute, one of interest here because it provided precisely that thorn of hatred necessary, according to Nello, to stimulate the sluggish pace of the cautious horse of Friendship.

Poliziano, having been a rejected suitor for the love and hand of Scala's daughter, kept a sharp and learned tooth ready against the prosperous and presumptuous secretary, who had refused the greatest scholar of the age in exchange for a son-in-law. Scala was a meritorious public servant and, furthermore, a fortunate man—traits naturally exasperating to an offended intellectual. Moreover, Scala suffered from an irresistible urge to authorship and was a poor writer—one of those exemplary individuals who, sitting in gout-ridden slippers, composed "poetical trifles" purely for their own amusement, with no intention of publication. Consequently, he dispatched them to friends, serving as the literary journals of the fifteenth century. Scala possessed an abundance of friends eager to praise his work: companions like Ficino and Landino, amiable browsers in the Medicean garden alongside him, who deemed his Latin prose style both elegant and masculine. Furthermore, the terrible Joseph Scaliger, who was destined later to pronounce Scala totally ignorant of Latinity, was still a century away. Yet, when does the fatal coquetry inherent in superfluous authorship ever find contentment with the ready praise of friends? That critical, haughty Poliziano—a companion far from amiable—must be made aware that the solid secretary demonstrated, in his leisure hours, a delightful fertility in verse, suggesting quite clearly what heights he might reach if he were not so burdened by worldly affairs.

An ineffable moment arrived when the man you secretly hate sends you a Latin epigram containing a false gender, hendecasyllables with a questionable elision, or perhaps a toe too many, attempting poetic figures that amount to manifest solecisms. This moment had come to Poliziano. The secretary had put forth his soft head from the official shell, and the terrible lurking crab descended upon him. Poliziano had exercised the liberty of a friend, correcting Scala's mistake in gendering the *culex* (a mosquito, ubiquitous on the banks of the Arno) as inferior or feminine, all within a polite Latin epigram. Scala retaliated with a bad joke, penned in suitable Latin verses, referencing Poliziano's unsuccessful romantic suit. Matters improved. Poliziano found the verses charming and highly facetious; the greater the pity that they were seriously incorrect. Since Scala had claimed to have written them in imitation of a Greek epigram, Poliziano, maintaining friendly terms, enclosed a Greek epigram of his own on the same intriguing insect. Presumably, this was not done to humble Scala, but to instruct him. This poem contained a lively conceit regarding Venus, Cupid, and the *culex*, a motif much enjoyed at the time, grounded partly on the zoological fact that the gnat, like Venus, was born from the waters.

In reply, Scala humbly requested to note that his verses were never intended for a scholar with such delicate olfactory senses as Poliziano, who stood nearest to the perfection of the ancients and possessed a taste so fastidious that sturgeon itself would seem insipid to him. He defended his own verses, though admittedly written hastily and uncorrected, meant merely as an agreeable distraction from the summer heat for himself and friends content with mediocrity. Unlike others who courted publicity through booksellers, Scala did not seek fame. As for the Greek epigram graciously sent him, he had barely enough Greek to comprehend its meaning, let alone taste its elegance. However, he added a postscript expressing fear that his incomparable friend's comparison of the gnat to Venus, based on their shared watery origins, was somewhat ticklish. On one hand, Venus might take offense; on the other, unless the poet alluded to the doctrine of Thales regarding cold and damp origins, it seemed doubtful to Scala that such a theory applied to a creature so fond of warmth. Perhaps a fish would be a better comparison, or, considering the power of flight, an eagle. Indeed, when darkness was considered, a bat or an owl offered a less obscure and more fitting parallel, and so forth.

Here lay a splendid opportunity for Poliziano. He was unaware, he wrote, that when he presented Scala's verses, there was any question of sturgeon, but rather of frogs and gudgeons. He made short work of Scala's defense of his Latin, mercilessly mangling him for the stupid criticisms he had ventured on the Greek epigram kindly forwarded as a model. "Wretched cavils," Poliziano exclaimed. Regarding the damp origin of the gnat, there was the authority of Virgil himself, who called it the "*alumnus*" of the waters. As for what his dear, dull friend had to say about fish, eagles, and the rest, it was "nihil ad rem"; for just because an eagle flies higher, it by no means follows that a gnat cannot fly at all, and so on. He was ashamed, yet reluctant, to dwell on such trivialities and swell a gnat into an elephant. Nevertheless, for his own part, he would only add that he possessed nothing deceitful or double, nor was he to be caught by the false blandishments of those who slandered him in his absence. He agreed rather with a Homeric sentiment on this matter, furnishing a Greek quotation to serve as powder for his bullet.

The quarrel could not conclude there. The logic deteriorated further, the secretary becoming increasingly pompously self-assertive, while the scholarly poet's temper grew more venomous. Poliziano had been generously willing to hold up a mirror, hoping that the too-inflated secretary, beholding his own likeness, might be induced to cease defending his ignorant Latin against ancient authorities whose consensus transcended centuries. Unless, indeed, he designed to sink in literature in proportion as he rose in honors, allowing men of letters to feel themselves his equals by compensation. In return, Poliziano was invited to examine Scala's writings, wherein nowhere would he find a more devout admiration of antiquity. The secretary was ashamed of the age in which he lived and blushed for it. Some wished to have their works praised and exalted to the level of divine monuments, but Scala could not oblige them. As for the honors offensive to the envious, they had been well earned; witness his entire life since arriving in Florence in penury. The elegant scholar, in reply, expressed no surprise that Scala found the Age distasteful, since he himself was so distasteful to the Age. Indeed, it was with perfect accuracy that he, the elegant scholar, had called Scala a "branny monster," formed from the off-scourings of monsters, born amidst the refuse of a mill, and eminently worthy of the long-eared office of turning paternal millstones (*in pistrinis sordibus natus et quidem pistrino dignissimus*).

It was not without

A Face in the Crowd.

To the northern folk, rising on Midsummer morning is a simple affair: one sees the dew upon the grassy verge of dusty paths, notes the fresh sprouts amidst the deeper greens of the oaks and firs in the coppice, and gazes over the gate at the newly shorn meadows, without needing to recall that it is the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist.

Not so for the Florentine—and least of all for the Florentine of the fifteenth century. To him, that specific morning held a brilliance in the eastern sun's touch upon the Arno that was singular and electric. The bells rang with articulation, proclaiming aloud the great summer festival of Florence, the Day of San Giovanni.

San Giovanni had served as Florence's patron saint for at least eight centuries, ever since the Lombard Queen Theodolinda commanded her subjects to render him peculiar honor. Indeed, the old chronicler Villani asserts that this devotion stretched back to the days of Constantine the Great and Pope Sylvester, when the Florentines deposed their idol, Mars, yet did so with a discretion that avoided contumely. While they consecrated their beautiful temples to God and "Beato Messere Santo Giovanni," they had once placed the old god of war respectfully atop a high tower near the River Arno. Ancient memorials suggested Mars had been elected their tutelary deity under specific astral influences; to break him or treat him with indignity would bring great damage and mutation to the city. However, by the fifteenth century, this discreet regard for the feelings of the Man-destroyer had long vanished. The god of spear and shield had ceased to frown beside the Arno, and the defenses of the Republic were believed to rest not in martial might, but in craft and coffers. For spears and shields could be purchased with gold florins, and upon those coins, the image of San Giovanni had always been struck.

Much good had accrued to Florence since the twilight struggles between the old patron and the new: undoubtedly, there had been quarrels and bloodshed between Guelf and Ghibelline, between Black and White, between orthodox sons of the Church and heretical Paterini. There had been floods, famines, and pestilences. Yet, much wealth and glory remained. Florence had achieved conquests over walled cities once mightier than herself, notably over Pisa, whose marble edifices were too lofty and beautiful, whose masts were too honored on the Greek and Italian coasts. The name of Florence grew prouder daily in the courts of Europe, nay, in Africa itself, bolstered by the purity of its gold coinage, the fineness of its dyes and textiles, pre-eminence in scholarship and poetry, and the wit of its citizens, most serviceable for statesmanship and banking. It was a name so omnipresent that a Pope with a turn for epigram dubbed the Florentines "the fifth element." And for this high destiny, though it might partly depend on the stars and the Madonna dell'Impruneta, and certainly on other higher powers less frequently named, the praise was largely due to San Giovanni, whose image crowned the fair gold florins.

Thus, it was fitting that the day of San Giovanni—an ancient Church festival already venerated in the days of Saint Augustine—should be a day of peculiar rejoicing. It was ushered in by a vigil kept in strict old Florentine fashion: with much dancing, much street jesting, perhaps with not a little stone-throwing and window-breaking, but emphatically with sights unique to a city employing a clever engineer and architect named Cecca, valuable alike in sieges and in pageantry. By Cecca's aid, the saints, surrounded by almond-shaped glory and floating on clouds with their joyful retinue of winged cherubs, as seen today in the works of Perugino, seemed to have brought a fragment of heaven down into the narrow streets, passing slowly through them. More wonderfully still, saints of gigantic stature, attended by angels, might be seen moving in a slow, mysterious manner along the thoroughfares, like a procession of colossal figures descended from the high domes and tribunes of the churches. The clouds were crafted from fine woven stuff; the saints and cherubs were mortal men supported by firm bars; and those mysterious giants were actually men of steady brains, balancing themselves on stilts, enlarged like Greek tragedians by huge masks and padded shoulders. Yet, a miserably unimaginative Florentine would think only of the mechanics, and thereby commit impiety, for in the images of sacred things lay some of the virtue of the sacred things themselves. Afterward, a company of merry black demons, well-armed with claws and thongs and other implements of sport, appeared ready to perform impromptu farces of bastinadoing and clothes-tearing. Such was the demons' way of keeping vigil, and they, too, might have descended from the domes and tribunes. The Tuscan mind slipped from the devout to the burlesque as readily as water rounds an angle. The saints had already done their turn, paused before the gates of San Giovanni to honor him on the eve of his *festa*, and departed.

On the morrow, the great day thus inaugurated, it was fitting that the tributary symbols paid to Florence by all its dependent cities, districts, and villages—whether conquered, protected, or of immemorial possession—should be offered at the shrine of San Giovanni within the old octagonal church, now the baptistery, where every Florentine had received the sign of the Cross anointed with chrism on his brow. All the city, from the white-haired elder to the stripling, from the matron to the lisping child, was clothed in its best to honor the great day and behold the great sight. Then, when the sun slanted low and the streets cooled, there would be the glorious race, or *Corso*. Unsaddled horses, clothed in rich trappings, would run straight across the city, from the Porta al Prato on the northwest, through the Mercato Vecchio, to the Porta Santa Croce on the southeast, where the richest *Palii*—velvet and brocade banners lined with silk and fringed with gold, befitting a city that half-clad the well-dressed world—awaited the winner upon a triumphal chariot.

Afterward followed more dancing. Nay, throughout the entire day, according to an old chronicler from the beginning of that century, there were weddings and the grandest gatherings, accompanied by so much piping, music, and song, balls, feasts, gladness, and ornament, that this earth might have been mistaken for Paradise.

In this year of 1492, however, it was perhaps a little less easy to make that mistake. Lorenzo the Magnificent, subtle and generous, was dead, and in his stead sat an arrogant, incautious Piero. An evil change for Florence, unless, indeed, the wise horse prefers the bad rider, as more easily thrown from the saddle. Already, regrets for Lorenzo were giving way to murmured desires for government on a broader basis, wherein corruption might be arrested, and wherein there might be that free play for everyone's jealousy and ambition which constituted the ideal liberty of the good, old, quarrelsome, struggling times. Those were the days when Florence raised her great buildings, reared her own soldiers, drove out would-be tyrants at the point of the sword, and was proud to keep faith at her own loss.
