Lorenzo was dead; Pope Innocent was dying; and a troublesome Neapolitan succession, coupled with an intriguing, ambitious Milan, might soon set Italy by the ears. The times were likely to be difficult. Still, there was all the more reason for the Republic to maintain its religious festivals.

Midsummer morning in this year 1492 was not less bright than usual. It was betimes in the morning that the symbolic offerings destined for the grand procession were assembled at their starting point in the Piazza della Signoria—that famous square where stood the massive, turreted Palace of the People, known as the Palazzo Vecchio, and the spacious Loggia built by Orcagna, the scene of all grand state ceremony. The sky spread out as the fairest blue tent, and beneath it, the bells swung so vigorously that every evil spirit possessing enough sense to be formidable must long since have taken flight. Windows and terraced roofs were alive with human faces; sombre stone houses glowed with hanging draperies. The boldly soaring palace tower, the yet older square tower of the Bargello, and the spire of the neighboring Badia seemed to keep watch above. Below, upon the broad polygonal flags of the piazza, lay the glorious display of banners, horses with rich trappings, and gigantic *ceri*, or tapers, fittingly called towers—strangely aggrandized descendants of those torches by whose faint light the Church worshipped in the Catacombs. Betimes in the morning, all processions needed to move under the Midsummer sky of Florence, where the shelter of narrow streets must every now and then be exchanged for the glare of wide spaces; and the sun would be high in the heavens before the long pomp had ended its pilgrimage in the Piazza di San Giovanni.

But here, where the procession was to pause, the magnificent city, aided by its ingenious Cecca, had provided another tent than the sky. The entire Piazza del Duomo, from the octagonal baptistery in the center to the façade of the cathedral and the walls of the houses on either side of the quadrangle, was covered, at a height of forty feet or more, with blue drapery adorned with well-stitched yellow lilies

The Piazza was thick with the throng, and Tito moved swiftly through them, his mind already drifting past the immediate bustle toward the Corso degli Adimari. As he walked, a fleeting thought lingered regarding the pair of blue eyes he had once glimpsed beneath the white linen hood of a country girl at festival time. He knew well enough that face belonged to Tessa, though he had never admitted it to anyone, least of all to Nello, whose curiosity he had brushed aside with a casual remark. There was something natural, indeed instinctive, in Tito's silence; it required no conscious effort, flowing from him as freely as any other impulse. Yet, unlike the sight of a flock of crows, which needed no hiding, this quiet acknowledgment of his attraction to Tessa remained a small, private secret, easily swallowed by the larger currents of his own life.

That brief longing for Tessa vanished almost instantly, however, giving way to a darker, more persistent memory: the face of the friar, pale and fanatical, who had stared at him with an unsettling intensity. Where else, in all his travels, could he have seen such a countenance? It was a vague, irritating ghost, clinging to his mind like dust motes in a sunbeam, demanding attention he did not wish to give. "Foolishness!" Tito chided himself mentally, brushing the thought aside. He had better matters for his thoughts. By the time he turned off the Corso into a narrower street, his focus had shifted entirely to the heat of the noonday sun and the delay it had caused him. He was late for the appointment in the Via de' Bardi, where a room awaited his arrival with anxious anticipation.

In his mind, he rehearsed the scene before he even reached the door. He saw Romola waiting, her blind eyes reflecting a joy that seemed to glow with an inner light, softening the shadows around her. He imagined the faint, rosy flush on her cheeks and neck, a sign of feminine sensitivity that somehow enhanced, rather than diminished, her majestic bearing. Their time together, circling the blind man's chair, would be filled with her constant appeals and his eager responses. Yet, despite this intimacy, Tito felt a profound, almost reverential subjugation to her. In her presence, he felt a strange, undefined awe, akin to the ancient worship paid to a goddess of nature—one who possessed a depth of life and power that transcended mere knowledge. He had never been alone with her, and his imagination struggled to construct scenes of love between them. All he could do was dream wildly, holding onto an impossible hope: that one day Romola would speak the words he secretly craved.

His thoughts drifted back to Greece, to a sunny day when he leaned against a wall and a peasant girl, dark-eyed and shy, had crept closer until she begged for a kiss, offering her innocent, olive-skinned cheek. Tito was accustomed to such spontaneous, unsought affections. But Romola's love was different. Would it ever come? Could it ever reach him at all? Nevertheless, it remained the highest apple on which his mind had settled. Though he was a man of fresh youth, not yet hardened by passion, he was deeply impressionable. It was as inevitable that he should feel drawn to Romola as it was for white irises to reflect in a sunlit stream. Yet, he possessed no vanity; he sensed clearly that she stood on a plane far above him. Many a man, standing before a large-eyed, simple child, has felt this same humbling distance.

Despite this sense of inferiority, Tito had experienced a rapid ascent in fortune that might have tempted lesser men to believe they deserved a wife like Romola, or even convinced her father, Bartolommeo Scala, to envision such a union. His first meeting with Scala had proven auspicious, leading to a growing favor that would have secured Tito's establishment in Florence even if the city offered no other allure. Politian, the renowned professor of Greek and Latin, found himself in a unique position. While the university seat had moved to Pisa, chairs for Greek remained in Florence. Once, the eminent Demetrio Calcondila had shared the honor with the dominant Italian scholar, but Calcondila had departed for Milan. Now, there was no rival to challenge Politian, nor any necessary check upon his ambition, save for those few friends who wished to teach him modesty. Scala and others, though not among the shallow admirers who drank in Tito's mediocre verses, were content to support the scholar morally. It was decided that Tito should be sustained in a Greek chair, following the precedent set by Lorenzo himself, who had similarly supported Calcondila. This move was framed not as jealousy, but as a zealous devotion to true learning and the education of Florentine youth.

Thus, Tito sailed under a fair wind. To the discerning eye, his talents harmonized perfectly with his sudden good fortune, and he wore this advantage with such ease and lack of pretense that no offense was taken. He was destined for the best circles of Florence, where silver plate abounded and the Signory permitted the wearing of the finest brocades. Who could refuse a handsome young scholar who could play the lute and sing a merry tune? His bright face, easy smile, and liquid voice seemed to bring a holiday spirit to the city, lifting the spirits of the labor-weary and the melancholy alike. He appeared to be the ideal professor to render the Greek classics beloved by the sons of great houses.

Yet, this external prosperity masked a deeper reality. Tito had sold all his jewels, keeping only one ring, and now held the master key to his freedom: five hundred gold florins. However, the moment he first laid hands on this sum marked the crisis of his first truly serious internal struggle. A thought, previously dismissed as a shadow, suddenly materialized with terrifying clarity, seizing him by the throat. He paused, forced to confront a terrible choice: surrender to the voice within and obey, or refuse it and accept consequences that might prove irrevocable.

It was in the room above Nello's shop, which Tito had recently rented, that the elderly Cennini handed him the final portion of the funds on behalf of Bernardo Rucellai, the buyer of the precious gems. "Here, my young friend!" the respectable printer and goldsmith exclaimed warmly. "You now possess a pretty little fortune. Take my advice, and let me invest these florins in a secure quarter, where they may grow and multiply, rather than slip through your fingers on banquets and the follies common to Florentine youth." Cennini shook his head knowingly. "It has become too fashionable among scholars, especially those like our Pietro Crinito, who believe their studies require scenting and embroidery, to squander wealth with one hand only to beg with the other. I have brought you the money, and you are free to make a wise or unwise choice; I await to see which way the scale tips. The Florentines do not consider a man a member of an Art until he has demonstrated his skill and been matriculated. Nor does one enter the Art of Life until one has been thoroughly tempted. If you decide to lend your florins to usurers, let me know tomorrow. A scholar may marry, and he ought to have something ready for the *morgen-cap*." [1]

[1] A gift given by the groom to the bride the day after the wedding. *Morgengabe*.

As Cennini closed the door, leaving the street noise outside, Tito turned. The smile that had graced his lips moments before died away, replaced by a fixed, hollow gaze directed at the table where the florins lay. He made no other movement, standing with his thumbs hooked into his belt, staring down at the coins in a trance-like state, as if concentrating all his consciousness on some invisible, tormenting image.

"A man's ransom!" The phrase echoed in his mind. Who had spoken those words? Five hundred florins, they said, was more than a man's ransom. But what if the man in question was a father? A man of advanced years, yet possessing lofty thoughts and a heart capable of immense passion. A man who, decades prior, had rescued a small boy from a life of beggary, filth, and cruelty, raising him with tender care, becoming a father figure. What if that man was now, under the burning summer sun of some distant coast, toiling as a slave, hacking wood and drawing water, perhaps beaten and mocked for lacking strength or speed? What if he were thinking to himself, "Tito will find me? He carried the manuscripts and the gems to Venice. He has raised money, and he will not rest until he finds me"? If that scenario were true—if it were certain—could Tito, seeing the price of the gems spread before him, allow himself to stay in Florence, bathed in the soft air of promised love and prosperity, refusing to risk everything for a father who might be suffering?

No, surely not, *if it were certain*. But certainty was elusive. The galley carrying the treasures had been intercepted by a Turkish vessel on its journey to Delos, according to reports from a companion ship that had escaped. Resistance had occurred; blood had likely been spilled. A man had been seen falling overboard. Who survived? What fate awaited them amidst the myriad possibilities? Had not Tito himself suffered a shipwreck, narrowly escaping drowning? He understood the omnipresence of accidents that could shatter any grand design. Rumors of pirate settlements on Delos were unreliable, perhaps irrelevant. What would happen if he left Florence for Venice, securing official letters and setting sail for the Archipelago? He might seize himself

Three weeks had passed since Tito handed over his florins to Cennini, and though he now stood upon the threshold of the Via de' Bardi, he bore no trace of anxiety. His spirit, buoyant and unburdened by the weight of future secrets, flowed effortlessly with the rhythm of his surroundings. There was no shadow of duplicity upon his brow, nor any stain of undue candour; it was simply the smooth, square forehead of a young man fully formed, reflecting a gaze as clear as polished glass. As he emerged from the blazing noonday sun into the cool, narrow alleys, brushing back his brown curls beneath his black *berretta*, his expression remained one of serene neutrality. To the casual observer, he appeared merely as a cipher capable of infinite interpretation, yet his presence commanded a silent, willing tribute of admiration from the throngs of holiday-makers.

The fervor of the Feast of St. John had spilled even into the labyrinthine side streets. Where the grand procession had once choked the avenues, crowds now swirled outward, driven by a restless, vaguely animal energy prone to sacrificing the solitary individual for the amusement of the many. As Tito ventured toward the neighborhood of San Martino, he encountered a denser knot of people gathered near the inn of the *Bertucce*. Amidst a chorus of raucous laughter, a high-pitched, distressed cry pierced the air: "Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!"

Curiosity drew Tito into the circle. At the center stood a young country girl, her white hood tangled and discarded, struggling frantically against the grip of a *cerretano*—a conjuror dressed in motley hues. The magician, a man with a face etched by upward-turning angles and a perpetual, impish grin, attempted to soothe her with jokes, while the surrounding spectators jeered, calling her a simpleton. For a fleeting moment, Tito saw only the back of her head, adorned with light-brown plaits secured by a silver pin. But as the struggle intensified, the girl's face turned toward him. It was the baby features of Tessa, her blue eyes swollen with tears, her lower lip trembling violently. Through the mist of her distress, a sudden spark of hope ignited in her gaze—the recognition of a familiar hand stretching out from the chaos.

Seizing the moment, Tito surged through the barrier of onlookers. "Loose this child!" he cried, his voice cutting through the laughter. "What right have you to hold her against her will?"

The conjuror released his grip instantly, shrinking back with a theatrical shrug and a half-apologetic bow. "I mean the *ragazza* no evil, Messere! Ask this respectable company. I was merely demonstrating a trick, aided by her kitten-like face, and promised her a handful of confetti. But surely, Messere, you possess finer sweets at hand?"

A ripple of laughter ensued, fueled by the look of profound relief in Tessa's eyes as she clung tightly to Tito's arm. To her, the mockery of the crowd sounded like the roar of wild beasts from which she had escaped; she attached no meaning to their jests. Tito, however, felt a pang of foreboding. Having rescued her, he now faced the awkward task of extricating himself from a situation where the deprived spectators might demand further entertainment.

"See, see, little one! Here is your hood," the conjuror chimed, flinging the white drapery over Tessa's head. "*Orsù*, bear me no malice; return when Messere can spare you."

"Aye, Maestro Vaiano, she'll come back presently, as the toad said to the harrow!" shouted a spectator.

Vexed by this delay, Tito propelled himself toward a side street, intending to shed his charge quickly. Yet, as they entered the alley, the crowd's attention shifted to the unusual pair, and he found himself compelled to address her gently. "Now, little one, where were you going? Did you come alone to the Festa?"

"No!" Tessa whispered, her fear resurfacing. "I lost my mother... and my father-in-law. They will be angry—he will beat me. Somebody pushed me in the crowd, and I couldn't stop. Oh, I don't know where they're gone! Please, don't leave me!" Her sobbing grew louder, the corners of her mouth drooping pitifully, her small chest heaving beneath the green serge gown.

Tito's heart softened instantly. Leaving her would have been brutality, and his nature abhorred cruelty. "My poor little Tessa, cease your weeping," he cooed, placing a hand on her apron to wipe away her tears and kissing her round cheek. "Let us see what can be done. Where is your home?"

Silence answered him, save for the diminishing sobs. "Come," he urged, "tell me where you wish to go, and I will escort you."

"I think I'll go home, if you'll take me," she murmured, her wide blue eyes fixed on him with a childlike, trusting calm. "Beyond Peretola, where the large pear-tree stands."

"And which gate, *pazzarella*?" Tito asked, feigning ignorance of the layout. "I am a stranger."

"The Por del Prato," she replied, gripping his arm with renewed vigor.

Undeterred by the complexity of the route, Tito steered them straight for Porta Rossa and onward to Ognissanti. The streets teemed with the festival's excess: frolicsome apprentices, bold-eyed women veiled in yellow, beggars, sharpers, and rough-hewn boys who delighted in mocking the solemn. Tessa's terror of being lost was not entirely misplaced in such a chaotic, morally ambiguous world.

At the Piazza d'Ognissanti, the heat of their walk demanded a respite. They sat upon a stone bench lining the ancient walls. "Holy Virgin!" Tessa breathed. "I am glad we have fled those women and boys. I was not frightened, because you protect me."

"You are safe with me, little Tessa," Tito smiled. "Do you feel secure?"

"Yes, because you are so beautiful—like those entering Paradise. They are all good."

Seeing the hollowness in her stomach, Tito noticed nearby stalls laden with fruit and confections. "Hungry?" he asked.

"Yes," she admitted. "If you will share."

Tito purchased apricots, cakes, and comfits, filling her apron. "Let us walk to the Prato," he suggested. "Perhaps then you need not fear the remainder of the journey."

"But you will have some of the treats," Tessa protested, gathering her apron like a bag.

"We shall see," Tito declared aloud, though inwardly he mused on the irony of his predicament. A peasant girl like Tessa inspired a pastoral idyl far superior to Lorenzo de' Medici's *Nencia da Barberino*, yet he lacked the leisure of a Theocritus. "Mischief is done," he sighed. "Another half-hour makes no difference."

As they strolled, Tessa revealed her domestic woes: a cross father-in-law, a mother who favored her younger sister, and a parish priest who was the only one to treat her kindly. "Nobody speaks nicely to me," she confessed, comparing her treatment to that of her "little black-faced kid." Unaware of the transformation in Tito's station, she viewed him not as a lord, but as a benevolent figure descended from Paradise, a voice of comfort in a harsh world.

They eventually reached the Prato, a vast, empty meadow where the Florentines practiced *Calcio*. Near the gates, where tents stood prepared for races, Tito paused. "Tessa, I must leave you to finish the path alone. *Addio!* Tomorrow, shall I purchase a cup of milk from you in the Mercato to ensure your safety?"

The suggestion caused her eyes to widen with sorrow, her mouth to drop. "I thought you would have some of the apricots," she complained, "and we could sit under a tree outside the gate and eat them together."

"Tessa, you little siren, you would ruin me!" Tito laughed, kissing both her cheeks. "I must return. You are in no danger. Take an apricot, *addio*!"

Before he could take two steps, he halted. Turning back, he saw her shoulders rising with a stifled sob, and the apricots rolling onto the grass. His conscience, quick to recoil from any scene of distress, compelled him to return. "Silly one," he said, retrieving the fruit. "We will sit under the tree. I cannot bear to see you weep, though I must depart."

Thus, they found refuge beneath a great plane-tree just beyond the gates. Tessa leaned against the trunk, her lap a mound of treats, while Tito stretched opposite, resting his elbows on the rough bark. Sunlight filtered through the leaves, dancing upon them like a winged spirit. Tessa's face regained its serenity, the flavors of the fruit seeming exquisite.

"What sins do you confess, Tessa?" Tito asked, gazing at her as she eyed the remaining sweets with hesitation.

"Oh, many. I am often naughty. I dislike work, and I am idle, knowing I shall be beaten. Sometimes I feed the mules the best fodder when unseen, and when Mother is angry, I deny it. I thought the conjuror was the devil, but

When Fra Luca fell silent, Tito lingered beside him, paralyzed by indecision. It was not until the crowd of pilgrims had thinned and the friar, moving with a slow, weary gait, stepped through the doors of the Church of Santa Felicita that Tito followed, turning down the Via de' Bardi.

"If this monk is truly Florentine," Tito murmured to himself, "and if he intends to stay, then every secret must see the light." He sensed a new crisis unfolding within the shifting landscape of his life, yet he remained composed enough to keep his appointment with Bardo. An apology for his recent absence would be necessary, a formal gesture to smooth over the awkward silence between them. However, Tito's natural aptitude for secrecy was rapidly evolving into something far more dangerous. While it was conceivable—even perhaps inevitable—that he might openly accept these changed circumstances and confess Baldassarre's existence, doing so would cast a harsh, retrospective glow upon his earlier silence. It would reveal his previous reticence not merely as caution, but as a calculated evasion designed to avoid fulfilling a claim he had privately acknowledged. Furthermore, it would highlight the irony of his own quiet settlement and the investment of his florins, undertaken before his benefactor's fate was ever confirmed. For the moment, prudence dictated a stance of feigned ignorance. There was ample time for meditation during the coming night, and no witness could pinpoint the exact instant he received the letter.

With a countenance only slightly brighter than usual—a brightness born of regret at arriving so late—he ascended to the second floor where Romola and her father sat surrounded by parchment and marble, isolated from the vibrant chaos of the streets whether on holiday or in ordinary times. This regret required no proof, for he had voluntarily surrendered the spectacle of the Corso to express it. Once inside, he strove to inject extra animation into the evening conversation, though beneath the surface, his consciousness operated like a complex machine, laying down tracks of thought entirely distinct from the thread of his speech. By the time he descended the stone stairs and emerged from the heavy door into the starlight, his mind had traversed a significant new terrain in the formation of his purpose.

The following day, once freed from his professorial duties, Tito turned up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco. His purpose was now fully crystallized. He intended to interrogate Fra Luca precisely regarding the extent of his suspicions about the truth and the basis upon which those suspicions rested, as well as the duration of the friar's stay at San Marco. Armed with this fuller knowledge, he hoped to craft a statement that would spare him the necessity of fleeing Florence. Tito had never before been compelled to construct an ingenious falsehood; however, the occasion had arrived, born inevitably of circumstantial silence, and his faculties of invention were already sharpened. He had convinced himself that he was not bound to seek out Baldassarre. He had once declared that, upon receiving fair assurance of his father's existence and whereabouts, he would immediately pursue him. Yet, why was he bound? Upon closer inspection, what was the ultimate end of life other than the extraction of the maximum sum of pleasure? Was not his own burgeoning life a promise of incomparably greater joy—not only for himself but for others—than the withered, wintry existence of a man past the prime of keen enjoyment, whose thoughts had hardened into barren rigidity? Those ideas had been sown in the fresh soil of Tito's mind, sprouting as lively germs; such was the natural order, treating maturity as nothing more than a temporary nest for youth. Baldassarre had completed his work, drawn his fill of life; Tito asserted that it was now his turn.

The prospect before him was vague yet seductive: "They intend to take me to Antioch." Here lay a vision! After a long voyage, to spend months, perhaps years, searching for a goal for which there was currently no guarantee of success, while leaving behind a life of distinction and love. If he found anything, he would return to the old, demanding companionship he knew by heart. Certainly, the gems—and consequently the florins—were, in a narrow legal sense, Baldassarre's. But viewed through a broader, more radically natural lens, wherein the world belongs to youth and strength, they belonged to whoever could extract the most pleasure from them. He was acutely aware that this sentiment did not align with the complicated play of human emotions engendered in society. The men around him expected him to immediately apply those florins to his benefactor's rescue. But what was the true sentiment of society? A mere tangle of anomalous traditions and opinions, offering no guidance to a wise man save where it pertained to personal comfort. Did he care for the florins save perhaps for Romola's sake? He would relinquish them readily enough. It was the joy that was due to him, hovering just within reach of his lips, which he felt he was not obligated to reject and thus travel onward, thirsting. Any maxim requiring a man to discard the good essential to making existence sweet was merely the lining of human selfishness turned outward, devised by men wishing others to sacrifice themselves for their own benefit. He would rather Baldassarre did not suffer; indeed, he disliked seeing anyone suffer. But could any philosophy prove to him that he was bound to care for another's suffering more than for his own? To do so, he must have loved Baldassarre devotedly, and he did not. Was that his fault? As for gratitude, scrutinized closely, it made no valid claim. His father's life would have been dreary without him; were we not convicted of a debt to men simply because they gave themselves pleasures?

Once Tito began to dismantle Baldassarre's claim, his thought process revealed itself as active as a virulent acid, eating its rapid way through the tissues of sentiment. His mind lacked that dread often erroneously dismissed as nothing higher than a man's animal concern for his own skin: the awe of Divine Nemesis felt by religious pagans, and, taking a more positive form under Christianity, still felt by the masses as a vague terror of anything termed wrongdoing. Such terror of the unseen stands far above mere sensual cowardice; it annihilates that cowardice. It is the initial recognition of a moral law restraining desire, checking the hard, bold scrutiny of imperfect thought into obligations that can never be proven to possess any sanctity absent feeling. "It is good," sang the old Eumenides in Aeschylus, "that fear should sit as the guardian of the soul, forcing it into wisdom—it is good that men should carry a threatening shadow in their hearts under the full sunshine; else, how should they learn to revere the right?" That guardianship may eventually become superfluous, but only when all outward law becomes needless—only when duty and love unite in a single stream to form a common force.

As Tito entered the outer cloister of San Marco and inquired for Fra Luca, there was no shadowy presentiment in his mind; he felt himself too cultured and skeptical for such foreboding. He had been nurtured in contempt for the tales of priests whose impudent lives were proverbs, and possessed an erudite familiarity with disputes concerning the Chief Good, which he ultimately regarded as a matter of taste. Yet, fear was a strong element in Tito's nature—the fear of what he believed or saw was likely to rob him of pleasure. He harbored a definite fear that Fra Luca might be the instrument driving him from Florence.

"Fra Luca? Ah, he has gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was carried off on a litter in the cool of the morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Might you leave a message for him?"

This reply came from a *fra converso*, or lay brother, whose accent plainly marked him as a raw countryman (*contadino*), and whose dull gaze implied no curiosity whatsoever.

"Thanks; my business can wait."

Tito turned away with a profound sense of relief. "This friar is unlikely to live," he told himself. "I saw he was worn down to a shadow. And at Fiesole, there will be nothing to recall me to his mind. Besides, if he returns, my explanation will serve as well then as now. Though..." He paused, his gaze lingering on the fading figure. "I wish I knew what it was about his face that recalled itself to me."

Tito moved with a light step, the paralyzing fear having evaporated like mist before the sun. His natural buoyancy returned, driving him toward Romola. Yet, as he approached, a shadow crossed his mind: her existence appeared an embodiment of selfless devotion and enduring patience, qualities he had previously shunned. However, he was not devoid of virtue, nor was he ready to surrender to vice entirely. In the bloom of his youth, his pulse quickened at the sight of beauty, and he still craved the simple pleasures of humanity. The poison he had swallowed worked slowly, insidiously. Though he had sold himself to evil, life currently felt unchanged to him; he remained unaware of the chains binding him. He intended for everything to proceed as before, internally and externally, winning favor through diligent effort and learned wit. He sought no discord with those he cherished above all, particularly Romola, whom he desired as his beloved wife. While alliances of greater wealth might eventually await a man of his rising stature, no woman in Florence compared to her. When she gazed upon him with those sincere hazel eyes, he felt overwhelmed by a magnetic influence, powerful and inevitable, akin to a melody that commands the soul until it is played anew.

Even before crossing the threshold, standing alone with Maso on the stone stairs, this influence began to stir, ignited merely by the anticipation of her presence.

"Welcome, Tito mio," the old man's voice called out before Tito could speak. There was a renewed vigor in it, a fresh cheerfulness in the blind face, since their meeting two months prior. "You have brought fresh manuscripts, I presume? But after our conversation last night, I've conceived new ideas: we must broaden our scope, return to our starting point."

Tito bowed respectfully to Romola as he advanced, adhering to his custom by moving straight to Bardo's chair. He placed his hand into the palm extended to receive it, settling onto the cross-legged leather seat with scrolled ends, nestled close to his master's elbow.

"Yes," Tito replied softly. "I have brought the new manuscript, but it may wait on your pleasure. I have youthful limbs, you know, and can climb the hill back without difficulty."

He did not meet Romola's gaze, though he was acutely aware that her eyes were fixed upon him with delighted attention.

"That is well said, my son." Bardo had taken to addressing Tito thus recently. "And I am glad to see you do not shrink from labor. Without toil, the poet wisely declares, life offers nothing to mortals. Too often, young ambition chases the *palma sine pulvere*—glory without the dust of the race. But what does the Greek say? 'Work in the morning of life; give counsel in the midday; pray in the evening.' Truthfully, I may seem to have reached that helpless evening, yet not so, while I possess counsel within me yet unspoken. For my mind, as I have often noted, was sealed behind a dam; the waters beneath lay dark and stagnant. But you, my Tito, have opened a channel for them, and they rush forward with a force that astonishes me. Now, I require that we review our preliminary ground again, with a broader scheme of commentary and illustration. Otherwise, I may miss opportunities I now see in hindsight, chances that will never return. Do you mark what I say?"

As Bardo stooped to retrieve his scattered manuscript, Tito's slight movement caught the jealous ear of the old man. Tito might have shrugged at the prospect ahead, but impatience was not his nature. Furthermore, he had been trained in that rigorous, exhaustive erudition that defined the age. With Romola nearby, he was buoyed by waves of pleasant sensation that rendered even the arduous task effortless.

"Certainly," Tito agreed. "You wish to expand your commentary on certain passages we have cited."

"Not merely so," Bardo interjected, his voice sharpening. "I intend to include occasional *excursus*, where we discuss authors I have studied deeply, for I may die before completing a separate work. Nor is this a season for scholarly integrity to remain idle. We must fear not only rash ignorance but men like Calderino, who, as Poliziano has demonstrated, resort to impudent falsifications of citation to serve their vanity and crown their errors with triumph. Therefore, my Tito, it is ill-advised to let this opportunity slip. Let us return to the point where we cited Thucydides. First, however, I wish you to accompany me through all my notes regarding the Latin translation by Lorenzo Valla. That incomparable Pope Nicholas the Fifth, honored by my personal acquaintance when I was young and known then as Thomas of Sarzana, paid him five hundred gold scudi—a sum, I do not say unjustly, given the honor. Yet, although Valla holds dubious fame, he is revered for his severe scholarship, prompting the epigrammatist to joke that since he descended to the shades, Pluto dares not speak ancient tongues. Thus, it is imperative that his name not serve as a stamp warranting false goods. I propose an *excursus* on Thucydides, wherein my critiques of Valla's text shall find their proper place. My Romola, you will fetch the necessary volumes—you know them well—from the fifth shelf of the cabinet."

Tito rose simultaneously with Romola. "I will fetch them, if you will direct me," he said, following her hurriedly into the adjoining chamber. Here, the walls were lined with books arranged in perfect order.

"There they are," Romola indicated, pointing upward. "Every book rests exactly where it was when my father ceased to see them."

Tito stood beside her, hesitating before reaching for the books. They had never shared this room together before.

"I hope," she continued, turning her gaze fully upon him with a look of grave confidence, "he will not tire you. This work brings him such happiness."

"And me as well, Romola—if you will allow me to say I love you—if you will deem me worthy of even a little love."

His words were a soft murmur. The dark, beautiful face, drawn closer than ever before, regarded him with beseeching tenderness.

"I do love you," Romola whispered. Her expression retained its simple majesty, yet her voice had never before sank to such a whisper. To both of them, it seemed they gazed at one another for a long time before her lips moved again. Yet, it was but a moment before she spoke: "I know *now* what it is to be happy."

Their faces met, and the dark curls intertwined for an instant with the rippling gold. Swift as lightning thereafter, Tito stepped onto a projecting ledge of the shelves and retrieved the needed volumes. Both were content to remain silent and separate; that first blissful experience of mutual consciousness was all the more exquisite for being unperturbed by immediate sensation.

Everything had unfolded as rapidly as the irreversible mingling of waters, and even the eager, jealous Bardo showed no sign of impatience.

"You have the volumes, my Romola?" the old man asked as they returned. "Now, prepare your pen. As Tito marks off the scholia we decide to extract, it is vital you copy them without delay—number them carefully to correspond with the numbers Tito writes in the text."

Romola always possessed a task granting her a share in this joint endeavor. Tito positioned himself at the *leggio*, where he both wrote and read, while she stationed herself at a table just in front, ready to hand anything her father required or relieve him of a finished volume. They had occupied these positions since the work began, yet today it felt new. So different was it now for them to sit opposite one another; so different for Tito to take a book from her hand as she lifted it from her father's knee. Yet, there was no subtlety employed to secure an extra glance or touch. Each woman fashions in her own image the tokens of love offered to her, and Romola's deep, calm happiness enveloped Tito like the rich, quiet light of evening, dissolving all unrest.

Two hours passed, and they ceased work as the light began to fail, when the door swung open. A figure entered, strangely incongruous with the flow of their thoughts and the solemn atmosphere of the room. It was a short, stout woman with black eyes, perhaps fifty years of age, wearing a black velvet *berretta* adorned with pearls. Beneath it, surprisingly massive black braids crowned her slightly bulging forehead, falling in rich, plaited curves over her ears. An equally striking carmine tint colored the upper region of her fat cheeks, contrasting with the surrounding sallowness. Three rows of pearls rested on her neck, along with a lower necklace of gold, lying on the horizontal cushion formed by her throat. The embroidered border of her trailing black velvet gown and her long, drooping sleeves of rose-colored damask were slightly faded, yet to the initiated eye, they conveyed the satisfying assurance of being the splendid result of six months' labor by a skilled artisan. The rose-colored petticoat, with its dimmed white fringe and seed-pearl arabesques, displayed a similar pleasing reflection. A handsome coral rosary hung from one side of an inferential belt, which emerged into certainty with a large clasp of silver wrought in niello; on the other side, where the belt again became obscure, hung a *scarsella*, or large purse, of crimson velvet, stitched with pearls. Her little, fat right hand, appearing as if molded from paste and distorted by partial baking, held

The afternoon of September the seventh hung lazily over Florence, a languid interval occurring more than two months since Romola and Tito had finally laid bare their hearts to one another. Tito had just stepped out of Nello's shop, finding the barber sprawled upon his bench in a deep slumber. A cap rested heavily over the barber's eyes; one leg was drawn up in relaxation, while the other dangled loosely toward the floor, dragging behind it a manuscript volume of verse whose leaves lay crushed beneath the weight. In a corner sat Sandro, utterly absorbed in a solitary game of *mora*, his left fingers responding to the arithmetic dictates of his right with a solemn, rapt intensity.

With the utmost gentleness, Tito snatched up the lute. Bending over the sleeping barber, he plucked the strings softly, singing a melody that seemed to wake the soul itself:

Nello was as easily roused as a bird startled from its nest. With instant reflex, the cap fell from his eyes, and he sprang upright. "Ah, my Apollino!" he exclaimed, rubbing his eyes. "I am somewhat tardy with my siesta on this sweltering day. This stems from failing to sleep naturally, instead indulging in a draught of potent poetry. Listen to me, as I begin to align my words with their initial letters, like a *Trovatore*. That is a symptom of my failing health: I fear the fine wine of my understanding is draining from the spigot of authorship, leaving me an empty cask smelling only of dregs, much like many a genius I know. Pray, what is it, my Orpheus?" Stretching his arms to their full length, Nello then curled them inward, seizing Tito's curls in a playful grip. "What do you seek from your well-trained Nello? I detect a coaxing strain in that soft music. Let me peer into the needle's eye of your desire, as the sublime poets dictate, so I may thread it."

"That is merely a tailor's metaphor for the sublime poet's vision," Tito replied, letting his fingers drift lightly across the strings once more. "Yet you have guessed the cause of my eager impatience to see you awake. I crave a fresh application of your art—not upon my chin, alas, but upon the *zazzera*, which is currently as knotted as Florence's politics. Your comb works with admirable skill, flattering the skin and kindling the animal spirits in that region. A touch of your delicate orange-scent would be entirely appropriate, for I am bound for the Scala Palace and must present myself amidst radiant company. The young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici will be there, accompanied by a certain Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena, whose wit moves with such speed that I see no path to surpass it save by the fragrance of orange blossoms."

Nello had already seized his comb, flourishing it as he gently guided Tito backward into the chair, draping a cloth around him. "Speak not of rivalry, my dear young lord: Bernardo Dovizi is a keen youth who will never cast a net to catch the wind. However, he bears a resemblance to his brother, Ser Piero—that sharp-muzzled weasel whom Piero de' Medici employs to slip through small holes for him. Nay, you eclipse all rivals and may soon touch the heavens with your forefinger. They tell me you have carried enough honey to sweeten the sour temper of Messer Angelo; indeed, he has declared you less of an ass than might have been expected, considering your close understanding with the Secretary."

"And between ourselves, Nello mio," Tito murmured, "Messer Angelo possesses greater genius and erudition than I can find in all the Florentine scholars combined. It suits them well to praise me now, when Poliziano is bowed down by grief, sickness, or some other affliction. I might attempt a flight with a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito, but regarding Poliziano, he is a large-beaked eagle who would swallow me, feathers and all, without noticing the loss."

"I shall not contradict your modesty, if you prefer it so," Nello smiled. "Yet do you not expect us clever Florentines to repeat the same truths daily, as we must if we always spoke the absolute truth? We cry down Dante and cry up Francesco Cei simply for the sake of variety. If we acclaim you as a new Poliziano, Heaven has ensured it shall not be quite such a great falsehood as it might otherwise be. And are you not a model of virtue in this wicked city? With your ears double-waxed against all siren songs that would lure you away from the Via de' Bardi and from the great work destined to astonish posterity?"

"Posterity, in truth, whom it will likely astonish as the universe is astonished, by the sheer impossibility of discerning what the plan actually was."

"Yes, something akin to that prophecy circulated recently. Cristoforo Landino remarked that the excellent Bardo belongs to that class of scholars who lie prostrate in their learning, like cavaliers trapped in heavy armor, and then grow angry because they have been overriden—a pithy remark, it seems to me, that was not harvested from his own garden. For of all men, for feeding one with an empty spoon and gagging one with vain expectations through long discourse, Messer Cristoforo is the pearl. Behold, you are now perfectly attired." Nello withdrew the cloth. "Impossible to add further grace! But is love always to be sustained by learning, eh? Soon I shall have to dress the *zazzera* for the betrothal—is it not true?"

"Perhaps," Tito said with a smile, "unless Messer Bernardo recommends that Bardo require me to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the cart of the Zecca before I may win my Alcestis. Yet I confess he is correct in judging me unworthy of Romola. She is a Pleiad who may grow dim by marrying any mortal."

"*Gnaffè*," Nello chuckled, "your modesty finds its proper place. Yet Fate appears to have measured and chiseled you for the niche left empty by the old man's son, who, by the way, Cronaca informed me, is now at San Marco. Did you know?"

A faint electric shock coursed through Tito as he rose from the chair, though it remained imperceptible to the outside world. He immediately stooped to retrieve the fallen book, busy flattening the crumpled leaves as he spoke. "No; I believed he was at Fiesole. Are you certain he has returned to San Marco?"

"Cronaca is my authority," Nello shrugged. "I do not frequent that sanctuary, but he does. Ah," he added, taking the book from Tito's hands, "my poor Nencia da Barberino! It jars your scholarly sensibilities to see the pages dog-eared. I was lulled to sleep by the well-rhymed charms of that rustic maiden—'prettier than the turnip-flower,' 'with a cheek more savory than cheese.' But to acquire such a well-scented notion of the *contadina*, one must lie upon velvet cushions in the Via Larga—not venture out to watch the Fierucoloni stumbling into the Piazza della Nunziata this evening after sunset."

"And pray, who are the Fierucoloni?" asked Tito, seemingly indifferent as he settled his cap.

"The country women who journeyed from the mountains of Pistoia and the Casentino, and heaven knows whence else, to keep vigil within the church of the Nunziata, selling their yarn and dried mushrooms at the *Fierucola* (the Little Fair), as we term it. They make a curious spectacle, brandishing paper lanterns and howling their hymns to the Virgin on this eve of her nativity—if you possessed the leisure to behold them. No? Well, I have had sufficient of it myself, for there is wild business in the Piazza tonight. One might receive a stone or two upon the ears or shins without solicitation, and I have never favored such pressing attention. *Addio*."

Tito departed carrying a subtle undercurrent of unease, his visit concluding sooner than anticipated. The boy-cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, the youngest of the red-hatted fathers who would later present his broad, dark cheek to posterity as Pope Leo X, had been detained by his favorite pastime of the hunt and failed to appear. It was nearly half an hour before sunset when Tito left the doors of the Scala Palace, intending to proceed immediately to the Via de' Bardi. He had not traveled far, however, when, to his profound astonishment, he saw Romola advancing toward him along the Borgo Pinti.

She wore a thick black veil and a black mantle, yet it was impossible to mistake her figure or her stride. Beside her walked a short, stout form, which Tito recognized as Monna Brigida, despite the unusual plainness of her attire. Romola had not been raised in strict devotional observance, and the occasions upon which she ventured abroad beyond the loggia on the roof of her house were so rare, and so thoroughly discussed beforehand due to

The Peasants' Fair.

The thronging mass and the jarring medley of sounds that assaulted him at the Piazza's threshold recalled to Tito what Nello had whispered concerning the *Fierucoloni*. With a strange, almost pleasurable sensation in the pushing and shoving, he plunged deeper into the crush. The chaotic noise served to fill the voids of his time, numbing the bleak calculations of his future that haunted him as he envisioned drifting alone in search of some undefined fortune. Even the thought of tracking down Baldassarre flickered briefly in his mind.

Through the opposing archways, people struggled to enter, while above them, paper lanterns tethered to wooden poles waved erratically in the breeze. A crude, monotonous chant formed a persistent thread beneath a cacophony of screams, whistles, mocking songs from boys' piping voices, drumbeats, and tinkling bells. Periodically, one of the dim, floating lights vanished in a crash, struck by a stone hurled with vague intent, followed immediately by shrieks and renewed shouting. Yet, on the fringes of this swirling tumult, certain groups observed the Nativity of the Virgin with a more disciplined reverence than the fitful stone-throwing elsewhere.

Ragged men darted sharp glances around while their tongues ran on merrily, hawking games to country folk on generous terms. Two figures masked and elevated on stilts, having snatched lanterns from the crowd, swayed the lights like meteors as they strode about. At a small covered stall, a shrewd merchant conducted a profitable trade in hot *berlingozzi*, a favorite flour-based delicacy. Another man stood atop a barrel, backed firmly against a pillar of the loggia before the Foundling Hospital (*Spedale degl' Innocenti*), peddling efficacious pills invented by a physician from Salerno, guaranteed to ward off toothache and drowning. Nearby, against another pillar, a tumbler showcased his feats on a miniature stage. Meanwhile, a handful of apprentices, dismissing the leisurely guerrilla warfare of stone-throwing, engaged in a concentrated private contest of their beloved Florentine sport at the narrow entrance of Via de' Febbrai.

Obligated to navigate chance gaps in the crowd, Tito found himself momentarily beside the trotting procession of barefoot, hard-heeled *contadine*. He gazed upon their sun-dried, bronze-toned faces and their strange, fragmented attire—garbed in obsolete stuffs and faded with hereditary grime. To the city dwellers, they appeared like a weary ancestry returning from a pilgrimage undertaken a century prior. These were the sturdy, sparsely fed peasant women from the Pistoian mountains, entering with the fruits of a year's labor in a modest bundle of yarn strapped to their backs. In their hearts beat that meager hope of good and that vast, dim fear of harm, matters they entrusted to the Blessed Virgin. On this Eve of her Nativity, her miraculous image, painted by angels, would have its veil drawn, allowing its potency to flow unobstructed.

Elsewhere, driven toward the plaza's edge, Tito encountered those more stationary vendors who had positioned themselves for safety behind their backs. Among them, he recognized Bratti. The pedlar leaned against a pillar, his lips pursed in disdainful silence, casting cold, superior glances at everyone who approached while clutching tightly to a serge covering that hid the contents of his basket. Surprised by such an unusual demeanor for an anxious trader, Tito stepped closer. As two women approached Bratti's basket with curious looks, the pedlar pulled the covering tighter and diverted his gaze. It was too provoking; one of the women was compelled to ask what lay within.

"Before I answer that, Monna," Bratti said, "I must know if you intend to buy. I cannot display wares of my caliber in this fair for every fly to land on and depart unpaid. My goods are too choice for that. Furthermore, I have but two left, and I have no wish to soil them. Given the rumors of pestilence circulating among the wise, there is a likelihood they are worth their weight in gold. No, no: *andate con Dio*."

The two women exchanged glances.

"And what may be the price?" asked the second.

"Not within what you are likely to possess in your purse, *buona donna*," Bratti replied in a tone of compassionate superiority. "I advise you to trust in Messer Domeneddio and the saints; poor people can do no better for themselves."

"Not so poor!" cried the second woman indignantly, producing her money-bag. "Come, now! What say you to a *grosso*?"

"I say you may secure twenty-one *quattrini* for it," Bratti stated coolly. "But not from me, for I lack such small change."

"Two, then?" the woman demanded, growing exasperated as her companion eyed her with envy. "It will hardly exceed two, I think."

Following further bidding and mercantile coquetry, Bratti feigned an air of concession.

"Since you have set your mind upon it," he said slowly, lifting the cover, "I would be loath to cause you harm. Maestro Gabbadeo used to remark that when a woman desires something and fails to obtain it, she falls into greater danger of the pestilence than before. *Ecco!* I have but two left. Let me tell you, the companion to these is on the finger of Maestro Gabbadeo, who has departed for Bologna—a physician as wise as any at any gate."

The precious objects were two clumsy iron rings, hammered into the shape of ancient Roman rings, occasionally unearthed. The rust upon them and the completely obscured nature of their alleged power were so convincing that the *grossi* were paid without complaint. The first woman, lacking such handsome coins, eventually drove a bargain with some of her yarn after much feigned reluctance on Bratti's part, departing in triumph with the remaining ring. Bratti concealed his basket, now filled with miscellaneous items presumably acquired under similar circumstances, and, moving from his pillar, unexpectedly encountered Tito, who, if given time, would have preferred to avoid recognition.

"By the head of San Giovanni, now!" Bratti exclaimed, drawing Tito back to the pillar. "What a stroke of luck. For I was discussing you this morning, Messer Greco; I said he has ascended among the *signori* now—and I am glad of it, for I was at the bottom of his fortune. However, I can rarely catch a word with him, for he is not to be found lounging on stones anymore—he isn't! But it is your luck, not mine, Messer Greco, save for some trifling gratuity to satisfy me for my trouble in this transaction."

"You speak in riddles, Bratti," Tito retorted. "Remember, I do not sharpen my wits, as you do, by haggling over iron rings: you must be plain."

"By the Holy 'Vangels! it was an easy bargain I extended. If a Hebrew secures thirty-two percent, I trust a Christian may secure a little more. Had I not borne a conscience, I should have obtained double the money and double the yarn. But, speaking of rings, it is your ring—that very ring upon your finger—that I could find a buyer for; yes, and a buyer with a deep purse."

"Truly?" Tito asked, examining his ring and listening intently.

"A Genovese gentleman bound directly for Hungary, as I understand. He visited my shop recently, scouring it to see if I possessed any antiquities whose value I underestimated; I warrant he thought I carried a pumpkin on my shoulders. He had been searching every shop in Florence. He wore a ring—not exactly like yours, but of similar fashion. Discussing rings, I mentioned I knew a fine young man, a particular friend, who owned a ring of that sort. He asked, 'Who is he, pray? Tell him I will pay his price.' And I thought of pursuing you to Nello's tomorrow. In my opinion, Messer Greco, you are not the type to watch the Arno turn to broth and stand idly by without dipping a finger."

Though Tito had missed not a single word, his mind had been racing. Why retain the ring? It had been merely a sentiment, a fleeting fancy, that had stopped him from selling it alongside his other gems. If he had been wiser and sold it, he might perhaps have evicted the identification Fra Luca sought to establish. True, it had been removed from Baldassarre's finger and placed on his own as soon as his youthful hand grew large enough, yet there was no valid spiritual merit in such superstitious scruples regarding inanimate objects. The ring had facilitated his recognition. Tito had begun to detest recognition, a demand from the past. This foreigner's offer, if genuine, presented an opportunity to discard the ring without the bother of hunting for a purchaser.

"You speak with your customary wisdom, Bratti," said Tito. "I have no objection to hearing what your Genovese will offer. But when and where shall I expect to meet him?"

"Tomorrow, at three hours after sunrise, he will await you at my shop. If your wit possesses the sharpness I have always attributed to you, Messer Greco, you will demand a heavy price; for he cares not for money. It is my belief he purchases for someone else, not for himself—perhaps for some great lord."

"It is well," said Tito. "I will attend your shop

When Romola reached the threshold of San Marco, a friar awaited her, standing like a sentinel in the twilight. Monna Brigida slipped silently into the adjacent church, and Romola was ushered through the cloisters to the chamber where the invalid lay confined—a precinct barred to women save for those immediate attendants.

As the heavy door swung open, the dim light filtering from outside merged with the flicker of two tapers set behind a wooden frame beside the bed. There lay Fra Luca, his face wasted to a skeletal map of bone, crowned with the shorn gold of a tonsure. His deep-set hazel eyes were fixed upon a small crucifix held close to his chest. Propped up with desperate effort, he seemed suspended between earth and air. Beside the bed stood another monk, his black cowl pulled low, who retreated toward the shadows as Romola stepped inside. Behind them, high on the frescoed wall, loomed the pale, agonized visage of Christ, while beneath it, ghostly faces of sorrow gazed out from the painted depths.

Then, her eyes met Fra Luca's as they lifted from the cross. A sharp, crushing pang of recognition seized her, binding this emaciated, holy stranger to the memory of her fair young brother.

"Dino!" she cried, her voice a raw whisper of pain. Yet she did not approach; she stood rigid, holding her distance at two paces. An invincible repulsion coiled within her at the sight of him robed and hooded. To Romola, this monastic guise seemed the brand of treacherous abandonment—the mark of a superstition that could twist filial duty into groveling piety. Her father, a man of stark, honest integrity who had raised her in the shadow of the Church's influence, had instilled in her a quiet defiance of ecclesiastical claims. To her mind, the Church belonged to the noisy, chaotic multitude from which they had always separated themselves. How, then, could she reconcile her brother's path with anything but indignant, cold contempt? Still, a thread of old love clung to her heart, and though her stance remained immovable, a yearning search burned in her eyes, seeking a spark of the boy she had lost.

Yet Fra Luca's countenance offered no mirror to her turmoil. He regarded the returned sister, now a woman in full bloom, with the detached, distant gaze of a spirit visiting its former home.

"My sister!" he murmured, his speech broken yet distinct. "It is well thou hast not delayed further. I have a message for thee, and my time is brief."

Romola took a tentative step forward. She expected words of penitence, a confession that would soothe her father's loneliness. Surely, the guilt of these long years of desertion would bring tears, understanding, and forgiveness. Dino would speak of his father's blindness, of the dark voids he had endured, of the quiet life now in the house where he had been born. The end would be tender, regretful.

"Romola," Fra Luca began, "I have seen a vision concerning thee. Three times in these last two months it has appeared to me, growing clearer each time. Thus, I traveled from Fiesole, convinced this was a mandate from heaven. I perceive a promise of mercy in this, that my breath remains to—"

A ragged gasp cut him off.

Chill swept through Romola's heart. This was a vision, then—a relic of those bitter tales her father had often cited. Indignation surged in her throat.

"Dino! Did you think to send some word to my father? You deserted him when his sight failed; you made his existence a wilderness. Does your conscience feel nothing? No repentance? What sorcery is this religion of yours, that places visions above natural obligations?"

Her hazel eyes turned slowly toward her, resting with a terrible, silent gravity for several moments, as if weighing the cost of an answer.

"No," he said finally, his voice low, passionless, sounding less like human speech and more like a spirit speaking through a vessel. "No, I have never repented for fleeing the suffocating, poisonous air of sin that choked me, threatening to drown my senses like strong wine. My father could not hear the Voice calling me night and day; he knew not of the demons that dragged me back from the path I chose. He lived amidst human sin and misery without seeing them. He was like one gathering shining stones in a mine, unaware that the whole world above was perishing with plague. I spoke, and he listened with scorn. I told him that the studies he cherished were either trivial toys or required to be heated by the fires of worldly ambition and carnal lust, for those were the very substances of the poetry and history he demanded I consume."

"Did my father not lead a pure and noble life, then?" Romola burst out, the words tearing from her before she could check them. "He sought no honors; he was truthful; he denied himself every luxury; he lived as the ancients sages did. He never wished you to serve worldly ambitions or fleshly lusts. He raised you to live by the purest maxims of philosophy, the very truths he breathed into you."

She spoke the lines of her upbringing, perhaps by rote, but with a fierce, burning conviction. Her cheeks flushed pink, her body trembling with the force of her protest. Again, Fra Luca was slow to answer, regarding her impassioned face with eyes devoid of warmth.

"What were those maxims of philosophy to me?" he asked softly. "They bade me be strong when I felt weak; they told me, like Saint Benedict, to roll in thorns and welcome smarting wounds as a deliverance from temptation. The Divine Love had sought me, penetrating me until a vast need arose within me, like a seed demanding room to expand. Though I had been raised in careless indifference to the true Faith, and though I had not studied the dogmas of our religion, it seized me like a rising flood. I sensed a life of perfect love and purity, free from hungry longing for pleasure, free from tormenting doubts, free from the fear of suffering. Before I knew the histories of the saints, I foresaw their ecstasy. For the same Truth permeated even pagan philosophy: that it is a bliss accessible to man to die to mortal needs and live in the life of God as the Unseen Perfection. To reach it, I must forsake the world. I must sever all affections and hopes tied to what passes away. I must relate to my fellow-men only as souls connected to the Eternal."

"That need drove me constantly," he continued, pausing as his breathing grew labored. "It visited me in visions when my mind grew weary of the empty words recording the passions of the dead. It came to me after I had yielded to sin and turned away in loathing from the scent of the hollow cup. In those visions, I saw the meaning of the Crucifix."

He stopped, his chest heaving violently for a minute or two. Romola said nothing; her mind recoiled, finding no purchase on the unearthly nature of this brother. As useless as trying to grasp a shadow, any contact with his logic seemed impossible. When he spoke again, the violent heaving of his chest had subsided.

"I felt whom I must follow. Yet, I saw that even among the servants of the Cross who claimed to have renounced the world, my soul would be stifled by the fumes of hypocrisy, lust, and pride. God did not choose me, as He chose Saint Dominic and Saint Francis, to wrestle with evil within the Church and the world. He commanded me to flee. I took the sacred vows and I fled—to lands where danger, scorn, and want bore me like angels to repose upon the bosom of God. I lived as a hermit, I tended to pilgrims, but my task was short. The veil separating me from my eternal rest has grown exceedingly thin. I returned to Florence that—"

"Dino!" Romola interrupted, the image of her father's suffering life striking anew with a desperate desire for reunion and absolution. "You *did* wish to know if my father lives?"

"—Yes. That before I depart, I might urge my brothers to study the Eastern tongues, as I never did, and pursue ends greater than I achieved. I find them already devoted to this labor. And since I have returned, Romola, I have felt I was sent partly to thee—not to restore bonds of earthly affection, but to deliver the heavenly warning conveyed in a vision. For I have seen it thrice. Through all the years since the Divine Voice first called me, while I still walked the world, I have been taught and guided by visions. For in the painful weaving of our waking thoughts, we can never be certain we have not mingled our own errors with the light we pray for; but in visions and dreams, we are passive, and our souls become instruments in the Hand of God. Therefore, listen, and speak not again—for time is fleeting."

Romola's mind flinched violently from the prospect of listening. Her indignation had faded, not because it was resolved, but because the chasm between her brother and herself seemed to widen into an abyss. Just as Fra Luca spoke, the figure of another monk crossed the chamber and stood on the opposite side of the bed, his cowl drawn low.

"Kneel, my daughter," a rich, resonant voice declared, startlingly different from Fra Luca's frailty. "For the Angel of Death is present, awaiting the delivery of Heaven's message. Bend thy pride before it

A Florentine Joke.

The dawn broke early on the morning following Tito's return from Bratti's shop in the winding alley of the Ferravecchi. The Genoese stranger had departed with the onyx ring, leaving behind fifty florins in Tito's pocket. For a fleeting moment, a shadow crossed his mind: if Fortune, in one of her cunning turns, had spared him the necessity of fleeing Florence, perhaps it would have been wiser to keep the ring. It bore the weight of peculiar memories and predilections, symbols of a past he had tried to bury. Yet, it was a trifling loss, barely worthy of emphasis, and in that instant, Tito's faith in fortune had evaporated. The feverish, desperate excitement of the previous night's alarms had cooled into a heavy, regretful lethargy. He found himself wishing he had never courted disgrace by shrinking from the obligations his neighbors demanded, for he cherished the pleasures that flowed solely from their esteem.

Actions, however, are like children born to us; they grow and move independently of our will. While a child may be silenced, a deed cannot be strangled; it possesses an indestructible vitality, thriving both within and outside our consciousness. This terrible, autonomous life of his actions pressed hard upon Tito for the first time, a weight he had not yet fully anticipated.

Bound for his lodging in the Piazza di San Giovanni, Tito deliberately avoided the Mercado Vecchio, his nearest route, lest he encounter Tessa. He was not in the mood to seek anything, save the first sign of how his circumstances might shift. The square itself, bathed in the warm, golden sunlight of a clear autumn morning where the dew still lingered, invited a languid idleness unburdened by fatigue. It was a festive day; the soft warmth seemed to seep into the bones with an invitation to lounge and observe. Around the octagonal Baptistery, stalls were unfurled with fruits and blossoms, while laden mules stood patiently, absorbed in their feedbags, their drivers perhaps having slipped inside the sacred doors to kneel before the Virgin Mary on her Nativity. Upon the broad marble steps of the Duomo, scattered clusters of beggars and chattering idlers formed a tapestry of daily life: an elderly crone with white hair and a weather-beaten countenance coaxed a round-capped infant to feel the warmth of the stone beneath its tiny bare feet, while a nearby dog sniffed the scene with suspicion; further along, two shaggy-headed boys leaned forward to watch a small, pale cripple carving a visage into a cherry-stone; and atop the wide platform, men knotted and unknotted ribbons in laughter-filled chatter, or stood in tight couples, gesticulating with eager intensity.

Yet, the largest and most vital assembly of loafers drew Tito's attention. It was the peak hour for Nello, and in this balmy season, when clients thronged, most men preferred the shade of the shop's pretty red-and-white awning to the stifling confines of narrow chambers. Though it is hardly a sublime posture—to sit with a lathered chin thrown backward, having one's nose treated as a handle—it remained the fashion of Florentine respectability. Astonishingly, men regarded one another with grave seriousness when all were thus attired. It was also the hour when yesterday's gossip was freshest, and the barber's tongue flourished in tandem with his razor. The deft interplay of blade and sponge seemed propelled by a common spring. Tito foresaw that escape from the circle was impossible; he would have to smile, retort, and maintain perfect composure. Very well, it was merely the trial of swallowing bread-and-cheese pills. To let the mere anticipation of discovery paralyze him was the mark of a man with weak nerves.

Just as he settled into this resolve, a hand descended upon his shoulder. No prior fortification could shield him from the jarring, unpleasant sensation of that sudden contact. As he turned his face, the shock betrayed itself, but the owner of the hand, possessing an aura of such malignant charm, broke into a light laugh. He was a youth of Tito's own age, with keen features, a small, closely clipped head, and a close-shaven lip and chin suggesting a mind stripped of non-essential material. His keen eyes burned with hope and friendliness, qualities that often precede bitter closure for those who later close their eyes to a disappointed world. At this juncture, however, prophecy spoke of Niccolò Machiavelli as a man of boundless promise, destined to repair the shattered fortunes of his ancient lineage.

"Why, Melema, what evil dream plagued you last night that you mistook my light grasp for that of a *sbirro* or worse?"

"Ah, Messer Niccolò!" Tito replied, instantly regaining his poise. "It must have been an excess of sluggish blood in my veins this morning that shuddered at the approach of your wit. But the truth is, I have endured a bad night."

"That is ill fortune, for you are expected to shine without obstruction today in the Rucellai Gardens. I take it you are attending?"

"Messer Bernardo honored me with an invitation," Tito said, "yet I shall be occupied elsewhere."

"Aha! I recall—you are in love," Machiavelli observed, shrugging. "Otherwise, you would never harbor such inconvenient engagements. We are to feast on peacock and ortolans beneath the loggia amidst Bernardo Rucellai's rare trees. There will be the finest spirits of Florence and the choicest wines. Alas, since Piero de' Medici attends, the choice spirits may well drown in the capricious verses of impromptu rhymesters. I detest that game; it is a contrivance for the triumph of petty minds, who are always most inspired by the smallest occasions."

"What of Piero de' Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?" asked Nello, whose slender frame currently dominated the Herculean bulk of Niccolò Caparra.

That renowned artisan in iron, last seen with bared muscles and a leathern apron in the Mercato Vecchio, wore holiday attire this morning. Seated submissively while Nello skipped around him, applying lather, seizing his nose, and scraping with magical swiftness, Caparra resembled a lion clad in linen and tunic, preparing to enter society.

"A private secretary shall never ascend in the world if he couples the great and the small in such a manner," Nello continued. "When great men are forbidden to wed their sons and daughters as they please, small men must not expect to marry their words as they please. Have you heard the news, Domenico Cennini, that Pagolantonio Soderini struck Ser Piero da Bibbiena a blow for urging Piero de' Medici to interfere with the union between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi? He is to be exiled as ambassador to Venice as punishment?"

"I know not which I envy most," Machiavelli mused, "the offense or the punishment. The offense will render him the most popular man in all Florence, while the punishment will send him among the sole people in Italy who understand how to govern their own affairs."

"Yes, if Soderini remains long enough at Venice," Cennini remarked, "he may catch the Venetian fashion and bring it home. The Soderinis have long been allies of the Medici, but this affair may open Pagolantonio's eyes to the utility of our old Florentine trick: discarding the old harness when it galls, provided we have not utterly lost the knack in these last fifty years."

"Not we," declared Niccolò Caparra, reveling in the freedom of his restored lips. "Eat eggs during Lent, and the snow will melt. That is my counsel when the folk grow noisy over their cups at San Gallo, discoursing of insurrection (*romor*). Never scheme a *romor*; you might as well attempt to fill the Arno with buckets. When there is sufficient water, the river will be full, and that awaits the readiness of the torrent."

"Caparra, that oracular wisdom stems from my excellent shaving," Nello grinned. "You could never have uttered it with that dark rust upon your chin. *Ecco*, Messer Domenico, I am now ready for you. By the way, my learned scholar," Nello continued, noting Tito's movement toward the door, "here has been old Maso seeking you, but your nest lay empty. He returns presently. The old man appeared mournful and hurried. I trust there is no calamity in the Via de' Bardi."

"Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo's son is dead," interjected Cronaca, who had just stepped forward.

Tito's heart leaped—had the death occurred before Romola saw him?

"No, I had not received word," Tito replied, maintaining a composure commensurate with the occasion. He turned and leaned against the doorpost, feigning resignation to staying. "I knew his sister had gone to visit him. Did he pass away before her arrival?"

"No," said Cronaca. "I was in San Marco at the time and witnessed her emerging from the chapter-house with Fra Girolamo. He informed us that the dying man's breath was preserved, almost by miracle, so that he might disclose matters to his sister."

Tito felt his destiny crystallize. Once again, his mind raced over the circumstances of his departure from Florence, and a plan formed to retrieve his funds from Cenn

High above the bustling streets, atop Bardo's house, a loggia stretched like a crown over the quadrangular walls. Where it faced the road, sturdy columns bore up the roof; elsewhere, a wall of arches opened to the air, framing a view over the huddled, uneven rooftops of the neighborhood toward the distant hill of Bogoli. Here, hidden from prying eyes, Titus waited. The sunlight struck him with an uncomfortable intensity, a physical manifestation of the ruin descending upon his hopes. He had never truly believed that Romola's affection could withstand the shock of his revelation, nor did he possess the arrogance to think her love stemmed from ignorance of his secrets. Yet, as he imagined her approaching in her radiant, earthly beauty, softened by the warmth of her hazel gaze, a selfish desire seized him. He wished she were lesser, something closer to the ground, just so he might hold her tight and kiss her deeply before their inevitable separation. Their past intimacy had been sparse—a lingering glance, a fleeting kiss, a pressed hand—and he had often hungered for solitude with her. Now, finally alone, he expected her closeness, yet found her standing at a rigid, inexplicable distance.

A sharp beat in his chest marked the movement of the door. Romola appeared. Time seemed to fracture into lightning; he felt rather than saw the halo around her brow, the pleading wetness of her eyes, and the cry of love bursting from her lips—"Titus!"

In that same instant, she was in his embrace, sobbing violently against his shoulder.

How desperately she had longed through the watch-lit hours to behold that face again! The terrifying new image of death, the bewildering doubts seeded by the account of a life alien to her understanding, the haunting vision that seemed to echo in her own soul—it had all sharpened her awareness. It was Titus who had first poured the warm stream of hope into her heart, who had blunted the jagged edges of grief regarding her brother. There was no one else to share this burden. Restrained all morning before her father, the dam within her now burst. To the world, proud and self-possessed, she was a fortress; yet in her love for Titus, she became as open and unguarded as a child. She had been content with the quiet glances of the past, but now, needing to cling to him, nothing hindered her.

"My Romola! My goddess!" Titus murmured, his voice thick with passion, as he cradled her gently and kissed the thick, golden waves of hair at her neck. He was in paradise. Disgrace, shame, separation—they held no sway anymore. This happiness was so overwhelming that the knowledge of her deception could not tarnish it; indeed, he rejoiced in her delusion. After all, concealment had been wisdom. All he regretted was his superfluous fear, or perhaps the dread had been worth paying for the sake of this sudden, rapturous union.

The sobbing subsided, and Romola lifted her head. Silence fell between them, a space filled with the sweet, bewildered wonder of young love. Her long, white hands rested upon his dark curls, while his fingers, stained with the streaming gold of her hair, touched her cheek. Each was so beautiful to the other; they shared a mutual consciousness undisturbed by the world. A fresh, cold pressure of sadness settled upon Romola's heart, making her linger in this silent, soothing proximity. Titus dared not press his lips to hers, for any movement would be a change, a disruption of the spell.

"Titus," she whispered eventually, "it has been utterly painful, but I must tell you everything. Your strength will aid me to resist impressions that reason cannot shake away."

"I know, Romola—I know he is dead," Titus replied. His long, lustrous eyes offered no trace of the countless wishes he harbored for that death to have occurred sooner, if mere longing held any power. Romola read her own pure thoughts in their dark depths as clearly as one reads letters in a happy dream.

"So changed, Titus!" she exclaimed, pain piercing her. "To think it was Dino. And so strangely hardened! Not a word to Father, nothing but a vision he wanted to impart. Yet it was so pitiful—the struggling breath, the eyes that seemed to look toward the crucifix, yet failed to see it. I shall never forget it; it feels as though it will stand between me and everything I shall ever behold."

Her heart swelled anew, forcing her to pause, yet the urgent need to unburden her mind compelled her to stifle the rising anguish. Resuming her speech, her thoughts had drifted slightly.

"It was strange, Titus. The vision concerned our marriage, yet he knew nothing of you."

"What was it, my Romola?" Titus asked, guiding her to sit upon the nearby bench. A shadow of fear crossed him, lest the vision somehow implicated Baldassarre, prompting this sudden shift in posture.

Romola recounted every detail from her entry into San Marco, omitting not a single word of her brother's utterances, which burned themselves into her memory like hot iron. But when she reached the end, she faltered. The remainder remained too vivid to speak, and she gazed into the distance, almost unaware of Titus's presence. His mind, however, lay at ease; the vague vision had swept over him like a white mist, leaving no scar. He sat in silence, waiting.

"I took it," she continued, as if he had read her thoughts, "the crucifix. It is down below in my bedroom."

"Now, my Romola," Titus entreated, "banish these ghastly thoughts. It was merely an ordinary monk's vision, born of fasting and fanatical ideas. Surely it weighs nothing with you."
