"No, Titus; no. But poor Dino... *he* believed it to be a divine message. It is strange," she mused, "this life of men possessed by fervid beliefs that appear madness to others. Dino was not a vulgar fanatic. And that Fra Girolamo—his very voice seems to have penetrated me with a sense that there is some truth in what moves them, a truth of which I know nothing."

"It was only because your feelings were highly wrought, my Romola," Titus explained. "Your brother's state of mind was no more than a form of that theosophy which has plagued excitable, dreamy minds throughout all ages; the same ideas your father's old adversary, Marsilio Ficino, pores over in the New Platonics. Only your brother's passionate nature drove him to act out what others merely write and speak of. As for Fra Girolamo, he is simply a narrow-minded monk with a gift for instilling terror. Any words, any voice, would have shaken you at that moment. Once your mind rests, you will judge such matters as you always have."

"Not about poor Dino," Romola countered softly. "I was angry with him; my heart seemed to close against him while he spoke. But since then, I have thought less of what was in my own mind and more of what was in his. Oh, Titus! It was terribly pitiful to see his young life ending thus. That yearning look at the crucifix as he gasped for breath—I can never forget it. Last night, I stared at the crucifix for a long time, trying to imagine how it could help him, until, by the lamplight, the suffering face seemed to shed pity."

"My Romola, promise me you will resist such thoughts. They are fit for sickly nuns, not for my golden-tressed Aurora, who appears made to scatter all such twilight fantasies. Try not to think of them now; we shall not long be alone together."

His final words were spoken in a tone of tender beseeching, and he turned her face toward him with a gentle touch of his right hand. Romola's eyes had been fixed absently on an arched opening, missing the distant hill; she had remained in the chapterhouse, staring at the pale images of sorrow and death.

Titus's touch and gentle voice called her back. In the warm sunlight, she beheld that rich, dark beauty which seemed to gather around it all images of joy—purple vines festooned between elms, corn ripening under vibrating heat, bright-winged creatures darting among flowers, round limbs beating the earth in gladness with raised cymbals, light melodies chanted to the thrilling rhythm of strings—all signs of Nature reveling in her force. A strange, bewildering transition struck her: from those pale specters of sorrow and death to this vibrant youthfulness, akin to a sun-god ignorant of night! What thought could reconcile the worn anguish on her brother's face, that straining reach for something invisible, with this satisfied strength and beauty, proving they belonged to the same world? Was there no reconciliation, save only a blind worship of clashing deities, first in mad joy, then in wailing? For the first time, Romola felt this questioning need like a sudden, dizzying lack, a void demanding something to grasp. It was an experience shorter than a sigh, for the eager theorizing of ages is compressed, like a seed, into the momentary want of a single mind. No answer met the need, and it vanished before the rushing return of young sympathy for the glowing, loving beauty that shone upon her in new radiance, like dawn after turning away from the gray west.

"Your mind lingers apart from our love, my Romola," Titus said with a soft,

As Tito emerged from the Via de' Bardi, a profound sense of relief washed over him. The immediate danger had passed, leaving behind a quiet, almost suspicious tranquility. His mind, unburdened by the urgent gaze of Romola and her father, drifted back to those harrowing hours of uncertainty. He recalled how, in that fog of fear, he had acted contrary to his better judgment, surrendering his ring. Now, the weight of that deception felt lighter, yet it gnawed at him with a subtle irritation. To admit he had sold the gem simply to avoid trouble would look like sordidness to Romola and others who knew its value. Instead, he would have to fabricate a noble pretext—a grand gesture of generosity—to justify the loss. At this moment, however, Tito harbored a weary disdain for all pretense. Fortune had spared him the consequences of his deceit, and he felt a grudging gratitude toward fate, believing it owed him as much freedom as it had granted. Who truly suffered from his lapse? The repercussions for Baldassarre remained murky and distant. What mattered now was shedding every invisible chain that tethered him to Romola, even if those bonds were merely the remnants of his own guilt. He realized, with a chilling clarity, that his current joy in being untethered was slowly numbing the very sensibilities needed to prevent future entanglements.

Yet, compared to these heavy moral knots, the sale of the ring seemed trivial. Even more minor was the illusion that little Tessa clung to. Would her small, hopeful heart shatter upon learning the truth? He debated whether to seek her out alone and dispel her fantasy immediately, or let time and chance unravel it gently. The image of her—sweet, pouting, innocent, and round—was impossible to ignore. Perhaps he could bring her a gift, a token of affection, to gauge how harsh her stepfather had become since her mother's death. Alternatively, he could reveal the truth now and introduce her to Romola, hoping together they could secure her a brighter destiny. But no, the shadows of the Cerreto episode and his own complicity in letting Tessa depart under false pretenses must remain buried from Romola forever. Once exposed, there would be no undoing the damage. Besides, even a child possessed a sharp tongue; if Tessa discovered her "love" had been a lie, she might turn bitter. Yes, she likely loved him, and perhaps it was wiser to sever that thread entirely. After all, a country girl might dwell on such trifles until a rustic lover won her over, breaking her silence and revealing the truth of her freedom. Thus, *Dunque*, farewell, Tessa! May you fare well. Tito resolved that the scandalous affair of the Cerreto marriage would bear no fruit for him. Resolutions born of self-interest often prove fragile, yet he clung to his promise regarding the fifty-five florins earned from the ring. He had devised a clever plan to account for the missing jewel to Romola, a scheme that would also serve as a shield against the monastic reveries that plagued her mind and distressed him. With this objective in focus, he turned toward the Via Gualfonda to locate Piero di Cosimo, the preeminent master of fantastical mythology whose skills Tito required.

Arriving at the courtyard where Piero resided, Tito noted the heavy iron knocker, now swathed in thick wool and secured with intricate cords. Recalling the painter's notorious habit of stuffing his ears against noise, Tito was unsurprised by this makeshift defense against the clamor of visitors. He approached the door, first tapping modestly with his knuckles, then shaking it with increasing vigor. To no avail. Just as Tito prepared to retreat, blaming himself for squandering time on this errand, a small girl emerged from the shadows carrying a basket of eggs. She ascended the steps, stood on tiptoe, and slid up a small iron plate set into the grooved wood. Peering through the aperture, she called out in a piping voice, "Messer Piero!"

Moments later, the bolts gave way. The door swung open, and Piero appeared, dressed in a red nightcap and a loose brown serge tunic with sleeves rolled to the shoulders. He cast a startled glance at Tito but, offering no greeting, extended his hand to accept the basket from the child. Re-entering the house, he soon returned with the empty basket, asking, "How much to pay?"

"Two grossoni, Messer Piero," the girl replied, "they are all freshly boiled, my mother says."

Piero extracted coins from a leather pouch at his belt, handed them to the maid, and watched her trot away, casting upward glances filled with awe at the wealthy young gentleman.

However, Piero's countenance shifted instantly when he addressed Tito directly. "What business brings you to my door, Messer Greco? I saw you this morning at Nello's. Had you asked me then, I could have told you that I welcome no stranger without prior knowledge of his intent and agreement."

"Pardon, Messer Piero," Tito said, his demeanor unflappable and genial. "I acted without due reflection. My mind was occupied solely by your remarkable talent for devising whimsical conceits, and a sudden urge to commission something similar brought me here."

Piero's nature was so eccentric that such a rebuff was rarely perceived as an insult, yet even if Tito had suspected malice, his instinct for winning favor outweighed any spark of resentment. Piero made a habitual grimace, stretching the corners of his mouth and pressing down his brows to obscure his true feelings beneath a mask of stoicism.

"And pray, what is this necessity?" he asked after a brief pause. Though outwardly indifferent, his heart beat with a latent hunger for the creative challenge implied.

"I require a delicate miniature drawn from poetic fables, one that you surely possess the genius to synthesize. It must be painted on a wooden triptych box—the interior to be plain gilded, the exterior adorned with your design. The subject is a favorite of Florence: the Triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne, though I wish to treat it anew. Ovid provides the inspiration. Young Bacchus, seated in a vessel, his brow bound with grape clusters, a spear entwined with vines in his grasp. Dark ivy must coil around the masts and sails; the oars themselves shall be thyrsi, wreathed in flowers. Leopards and tigers crouch before him, dolphins play beside the ship. Beside him stands Ariadne, fair-haired and crowned with gold, rendered immortal—though absent from Ovid's tale, you will envision it. Above them, the young Loves, masters of your craft, shoot arrows tipped with roses..."

"Sufficient!" Piero interrupted sharply. "I possess Ovid in the vernacular. Seek the passage yourself; I dislike being suffocated by another's thoughts. Enter."

Piero guided Tito into the first chamber. Upon the open hearth rested a basket of eggs, surrounded by shattered shells and a mound of ash, creating a jarring contrast to the opulence nearby. A low bedstead of carved ebony lay draped with a fragment of rich Oriental carpet, resembling a step from a Madonna's throne. Adjacent stood a massive *cassone*, its sides and lid decorated with painted scenes. Beyond, the room was sparsely furnished, dominated by plaster casts, wooden steps, easels, and rough crates, all draped in cobwebs.

The adjoining space was even vaster, yet more densely cluttered. Piero evidently celebrated the Feast, for the double doors beneath the window, which admitted the painter's light, stood agape, revealing a garden—or rather, a tangled thicket. Fig trees and vines sprawled wildly among nettles and hemlocks, while a towering cypress rose from a stifling mass of yellow mulberry leaves. This damp, unruly vegetation seemed to seep into the room itself. In one corner, amidst a chaotic pile of carved marble fragments and rusted armor, tufts of long grass and feathery fennel had taken root. A large stone vase, tilted precariously, appeared to spill forth the ivy streaming around it. Hanging from the walls were sketches in pen and oil depicting fantastical sea monsters, dances of satyrs and maenads, Saint Margaret's deliverance from the dragon, Madonnas bathed in supernatural radiance, botanical studies, and grotesque heads. Irregular shelves held scattered books nestled among drooping ears of corn, bullock's horns, dried honeycombs, stones encrusted with rare lichens, skulls, bones, peacock feathers, and broad bird wings. From the filth of the floor rose life-sized effigies: one clad in the robes of a Vallombrosan monk, bizarrely topped with a helmed visor; another smothered in brocade and skins. Amidst this eclectic still life, speckled and white pigeons strutted or perched, too tame to flee at the intrusion of men. Three plump toads crawled in close friendship near the threshold, while a white rabbit, presumably the model for the creature frightening Cupid in the central canvas depicting Mars and Venus, twitched its nose contentedly atop a box of bran.

"Now, Messer Greco," Piero said, gesturing for Tito to sit on a low stool near the door, while he loomed over him with folded arms, "do not attempt to view everything simultaneously, like some omniscient deity. Tell me precisely the dimensions of this triptych."

Tito indicated the desired measurements, and Piero marked them onto a sheet of paper.

"Now, for the book," Piero

Messer Bernardo del Nero remained as stern and unyielding as Romola had anticipated regarding the postponement of the marriage until Easter. In this affair, Bardo stood wholly under the sway of his shrewd and practical friend. Yet, even Bernardo, though immune to the personal charm that captivated others, could not entirely ignore the seductive argument of success—a force invariably potent with worldly men. Tito was swiftly ascending the heights of high society, gaining particular favor with the young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, who had already suggested including him in his learned entourage for an impending journey to Rome. This bright Greek youth, possessed of a tongue ever ready yet never quarrelsome, became increasingly coveted at the lavish suppers of the Via Larga and the Florentine games where he did not strive to excel, but rather admired the incomparable skill of Piero de' Medici with the utmost grace.

By an unbroken sequence of cause and effect, Tito's reputation as an agreeable companion in "magnificent" circles rendered his learning and talents appear even more radiant. He was genuinely accomplished enough to render any exaggeration of his abilities harmless. To the older Bernardo, old prejudices and attachments began to outweigh the newer, weaker bias against the young Greek stranger, who was perhaps a trifle too supple. To the ancient Florentine, it was impossible to dismiss the weight of standing well with the foremost families of the city. Since Tito was now fully embraced by that circle whose views constituted the unquestioned standard of social value, it seemed irrational to deny that the prospect of such a son-in-law for Bardo and such a husband for Romola offered no further checks to satisfaction.

Undeniably, Tito's arrival marked the dawn of a new life for both father and daughter, surpassing even the initial promise. The blind old scholar, whose proud truthfulness had never allowed him to participate in the commerce of feigned and preposterous admiration—the thread woven through all learned discourse alongside its opposite, vituperation—had gradually fallen into neglect even among his fellow citizens. When mentioned, the usual sentiment was that while his blindness and the loss of his son were pitiable, he was tiresome in contending for the value of his labors; his discontent appeared somewhat inconsistent in a man who had formerly been open to religious indifference and had refused various offers of patronage solely on the slight condition that he accept holy orders, which patrons deemed essential for supporting scholars. But since Tito's advent, the monotonous echo of Bardo's name had ceased. It was understood that the old man had laid aside his plaints, and the fair daughter was no longer to be locked away in dowerless pride, waiting for a *parentado*. The winning manners and growing favor of the handsome Greek, destined to become both son and husband, transformed the new interest into something thoroughly friendly. It became common for visitors to enliven the quiet library. Elderly men, prompted by some indefinable urge to renew former acquaintances when an old friend becomes the subject of fresh talk, arrived in droves. Young men whom Tito had graciously permitted to visit once found it easy to return when they met him on his way to the Via de' Bardi; placing a hand upon his shoulder, they fell easily into conversation with him.

For it was delightful to behold Romola's beauty. She sat with a certain grandeur, spoke with gravity, smiled with modesty, and seemed to cast around her an odor of queenliness, just as the old Firenzuola described the majesty of a great woman. Under this genial breath of admiration and homage, she seemed to unfold like a strong white lily. To her, all else paled before her new, bright life intertwined with Tito's love.

Indeed, Tito had become the instrument strengthening the hope in Bardo's mind that, before his death, he might secure the long-desired safety for his library. He feared it would not be merged into another collection, nor transferred to a body of monks to bear the name of a monastery, but would remain forever the Bardi Library, available for the use of Florentines. While the old habit of trusting in the Medici could not vanish while their influence remained the state's strongest lever, Tito, having won the ear of Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, might accomplish more than Messer Bernardo toward securing this desired interest. Tito could demonstrate to a learned audience the peculiar value of Bardi's collection. Speaking with sanguinity of such a result, Tito sought to cheer the old man, conscious that Romola repaid these gentle words to her father with a sort of adoration that no direct tribute to herself could have elicited.

This question of the library became the subject of numerous discussions between Bernardo del Nero and Romola after Christmas passed and the marriage prospects drew nearer—but always conducted outside Bardo's hearing. For Bardo clung to a vague, undisturbed belief that his property, aside from the library, was sufficient to meet all demands. He would not, save under a momentary pressure of angry despondency, admit to himself that the will disinheriting Dino left Romola heir to nothing but debts, nor that he required anything from patronage beyond the assurance of a separate locality for his library in exchange for a deed gifting it to the Florentine Republic.

"My opinion is," Bernardo told Romola during a consultation beneath the loggia, "that since you are to marry and Messer Tito will possess a competent income, we should begin to wind up your affairs. We ought to ascertain exactly the sum necessary to save the library from being touched, rather than allowing the debts to accumulate any longer. Your father requires nothing but his shred of mutton and his macaroni daily, and I believe Messer Tito may undertake to supply that for the remaining years of his life; it may serve in lieu of the *morgen-cap*."

"Tito has always known that my life is bound up with my father's," Romola replied. "And he is kinder to my father than I am; he delights in making him happy."

"Ah, he is not made of the same clay as other men, is he?" Bernardo remarked, smiling. "Your father believed he could shut woman's folly out of you by cramming you with Greek and Latin. Yet, you proved as ready to believe in the first pair of bright eyes and the first soft words that reached you as if you knew nothing by heart but Paternosters, like other Christian daughters."

"Now, godfather," Romola said, shaking her head playfully, "as if it were merely bright eyes and soft words that make me love Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito too because he is so good. I see it, I feel it, in everything he says and does. And if he is handsome, too, why should I not love him the better for that? It seems to me beauty is part of the finished language by which goodness speaks. You know you must have been a very handsome youth yourself, godfather"—she looked up with one of her happy, loving smiles at the stately old man—"you were about as tall as Tito, and you had very fine eyes; only you looked a little sterner and prouder, and..."

"And Romola likes to keep all the pride to herself?" Bernardo teased, not impervious to this charming coaxing. "However, it is well that in one way Tito's demands are more modest than those of any Florentine husband of fitting rank we might have found for you; he wants no dowry."

Thus, matters were settled between Messer Bernardo del Nero, Romola, and Tito. Bardo assented with a wave of the hand when Bernardo informed him that it would be wise now to begin selling property and clearing debts, accustomed as he was to view financial matters as a thick wood that his imagination never penetrated, let alone traverse. Meanwhile, Tito set about winning Messer Bernardo's respect, employing his ready faculties to inquire into Florentine money-matters, the secrets of the *Monti* (public funds), the valuations of real estate, and the profits of banking.

"You will soon forget that Tito is not a Florentine, godfather," Romola observed. "See how he is learning everything about Florence."

"It seems to me he is one of the *demoni*, who belong to no particular country, child," Bernardo replied, smiling. "His mind is a little too nimble to be weighted with all the stuff we men carry about in our hearts."

Romola smiled, too, bathed in happy confidence.

The final week of Carnival had swollen Florence to a fever pitch. The streets, usually thronged, now roared with a chaotic energy born of masks and madness. Processions wound through the lanes, chanting songs mandated by Lorenzo the Magnificent, while the *rigoletto*, a favored round dance, kicked up dust beneath a pale, frost-laden sky. Practical jokes ranged from harmless confections to the lethal art of stone-throwing. The boys and striplings, the most volatile element of the crowd, had become as loud and uncontrollable as cicadas in summer heat. Their privilege was to block passers-by with poles, demanding tribute for suppers and bonfires, culminating in a standing spectacle of stoning. The injuries varied, sometimes fatal, lending a checkered, barbaric quality to the festivities. A painter seeking truth here would produce a canvas of such grossness and brutality that it would require a wall to shield it from view, save for the historian's purpose of documenting a reforming zeal often misjudged for its narrowness. Yet, amidst this, lingered a more innocent, picturesque merriment, fueled by the quick animal spirits and sensitive nerves of the people, devoid of the heavy stupidity of the north or the stealthy ferocity that turns southern brawls into daggers.

High morning had broken, yet the carnival spirit lingered in a languid recapitulation of the night's jests. Titus Melema moved with a brisk, determined stride along the road to the Via de' Bardi, accompanied by Young Bernardo Dovizi. They spoke animatedly of topics utterly unrelated to the sights and sounds pressing past them along the Porta Santa Maria. Even as they gestured and smiled, they cast frequent, rapid glances around them. At the bend toward the Lung'Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte, Titus sensed a presence he desperately wished to avoid. His mind was preoccupied with a singular, life-altering event: his betrothal, set for that evening. The decision had been sudden. Though preparations—chiefly Titus pouring his florins into the refurbishment of Bardo's sparsely furnished dwelling—had been underway, the union was meant to await Easter, following Titus's year of probation under Bernardo del Nero. However, an urgent proposition arrived: Titus was to join Cardinal Giovanni in Rome to assist Bernardo Dovizi in organizing a library using his superior knowledge of Greek. With advancement clearly laid out before him, Titus had become insistent. He argued that the betrothal must precede his departure; the delay before marriage would be minimal upon his return, and crucially, if he and Romola were outwardly and inwardly pledged, with rings exchanged and contracts signed, no power—not even Messer Bernardo's—could nullify his claim. Romola's feelings aligned with his, and the elders' consent was secured.

Now, Titus hastened to snatch a morning visit from Romola, needing final words before the evening ceremony. It was a time when recognition was ill-suited to his haste, let alone by Tessa. Unmistakably, it was Tessa he spotted moving toward the river bend, her expression timid and forlorn. An uncomfortable current ran through Titus; he knew she had seen him and would surely follow. There was no escape along the direct road by the Arno and over the bridge. Yet, she dared not approach him while he was not alone, and Titus resolved to keep Dovizi beside him until they reached Bardo's door. He quickened his pace, weaving new threads of conversation, but the sensation of Tessa trailing behind grew stronger, a persistent irritation tempered by a pang of pity. This compassion made his desire to vanish unseen without causing a scene a difficult resource. Still, he persevered, guiding his companion to the door and managing his farewell without turning his face toward where a pair of blue eyes might watch. Ascending the stone steps, he tried to banish the thought that Tessa might indeed be watching, telling himself she may not have seen him, or if she had, would not follow.

Yet, perhaps because certainty was elusive, when Titus emerged from the doorway, he moved with a quick step, feigning unconsciousness of anything on either flank. Human eyes, however, capture a wide angle without permission. Titus knew, before a hand fell heavily on his arm, that a small figure in a white hood stood near the threshold. It was a real grasp, not a timid touch; poor Tessa, seeing his swift exit, had lunged forward with desperation. When he stopped and turned, her face wore a mask of fright, as if she dreaded the consequences of her boldness.

"Tessa!" Titus exclaimed, his voice sharper than she had ever heard it. "Why are you here? You must not follow me—you must not haunt doorways waiting for me."

Her blue eyes widened with tears, speechless. Titus feared worse than ridicule; if he were seen in the Via de' Bardi with a country girl gazing pathetically at him, it would be disastrous. Bernardo del Nero—or someone equally dangerous—might appear at any moment. Even outside the day of his betrothal, the scene would be awkward. Yet, to drive her away with harsh words would be brutal, impossible. He recalled his foolish entanglement with the Cerretani, a quiet affair buried for months that now bloomed as an untimely weed of vexation. He could not speak harshly, so he spoke quickly.

"Tessa, I cannot, I must not talk to you here. I will go to the bridge and wait for you. Follow me slowly."

He walked swiftly to the Ponte Rubaconte, leaning against the wall of a quaint little house, staring toward the approach where Tessa would arrive. To soften a harder heart than Titus's, the sight of her would have been enough; her round face was paler and more saddened than when he had parted from her at the Nunziata. Fortunately, the bridge was rarely frequented, and it was nearly empty. He wasted no time.

"Now, Tessa, I have little time. Do not cry. Why did you follow me? Never do it again."

"I thought," Tessa whispered, struggling against a sob, "that you would not be long before you came to take care of me again. The stepfather beats me, and I cannot bear it. Always when I come for a holiday I walk to find you, and I cannot. Oh, please, do not send me away again! It has been so long, and I cry because you never come. I cannot help it; the days are so long, and I do not mind the goats or kids..."

Sobs rose rapidly, great tears falling. Titus felt compelled to comfort her. Sending her away was necessary, immediate. But how could he tell her truths that would leave her in hopeless grief? He saw new troubles looming, yet the present moment was too pressing to weigh distant consequences.

"Tessa, my little one," he coaxed in his familiar, tender tone, "you must not cry. Endure the stepfather a little longer. I will return to you. But I am going to Rome now—a long, long journey. I shall return in a few weeks, and then I promise to see you. Promise me to be good and wait."

It was the well-known voice; the sound alone soothed Tessa. She looked up with trusting, tear-glittering eyes, sobbing despite her efforts to obey.

"Promise me, my Tessa."

"Yes," she whispered. "But you won't be long?"

"No, not long. But I must go now. Remember, Tessa: if anyone knows you see me, you will lose me forever. Go straight home now, never follow me again. Wait for me. Good-bye, little Tessa: I *will* come."

There was no alternative. He turned to leave, refusing to look back to gauge her bearing, for he had no time. Once across the Ponte Rubaconte and into the Via de' Benci, where he could not see the bridge, he knew Tessa would obey. The difficulty for that day was ended, yet returning to him at such a moment, when deception was inevitable, left an unpleasant stain on his memory. However, Titus's mind was saturated with a hopeful, first love, buoyed by ambitions shining bright. The future necessity of grieving Tessa was akin to the distant cry of a suffering animal in a thicket, unnoticed by a merry cavalcade in a sunny plain. On his second crossing of the bridge, the thought of Tessa diminished his happiness not at all. Well-muffled in his mantle, perhaps more for privacy than warmth, he ascended the stone steps of Bardo's house, leaping two at a time.

"Where is the damigella?" he asked hurriedly of Maso.

"In the library," Maso replied. "She is ready, and Monna Brigida and Messer Bernardo are there with Ser Braccio, but the rest of the company has not arrived."

Titus requested a few moments alone and awaited Romola in the *salotto*. He entered a room starkly contrasting the library's pallid, somber hues. The walls were brightly frescoed with "caprices" of nymphs and loves sporting among flowers and birds under a blue sky. Beside red leather seats and a central table stood two tall white vases and a young faun playing the flute, modeled by a promising youth named Michelangelo Buonarotti. The room felt like an open, sunlit space.

It was the seventeenth day of November, 1494. Nearly eighteen months had passed since Tito and Romola were bound together in the radiant light of Easter, when confetti rained down upon them like a celestial promise of sweetness for their shared journey. Since that springtime union, however, the landscape of Florence had shifted beneath their feet. Just as every blossom depends on the deep, invisible flow of sap within the tree, the private fortunes of the couple hung precariously upon the grand, turbulent tides of Italian politics.

Only a week prior, the ancient spirit of the republic had surged back into the hearts of its citizens. A thunderclap of alarm rang from the great bell in the palace tower, summoning the populace to arms. Men emerged wielding rusted spears, farming tools, and improvised clubs to drive out the Medici. The Gate of San Gallo slammed shut on Piero, the arrogant exile, as he fled toward Bologna with his mercenaries in disarray, and sealed off his younger brother, the Cardinal, who escaped the city disguised as a humble Franciscan friar. Heads were placed on stakes, homes were sacked in accordance with old traditions, and the shameful caricatures of former conspirators painted on public walls were scraped away. The "tyrants" were ejected from their golden nests in the Via Larga, and the Republic, though bruised, regained the use of its own will.

Yet, barely a week later, the great palace in the Via Larga stood transformed, draped in a canopy of unprecedented color. Streets were roofed with unfamiliar fabrics; banners poured from windows like flags of truce; carpets and tapestries covered every step where "exceptional feet" might tread. If these decorations were proof of joy, then Florence was indeed a city rejoicing in the anticipation of its new guest. The vibrant stream of ornamentation flowed from the palace, winding past the Cathedral, sweeping through the Piazza della Signoria, crossing the Ponte Vecchio, and extending toward the Porta San Frediano, the gateway looking toward Pisa. There, near the gate, a high platform and canopy awaited the Signoria. Messer Luca Corsini, a doctor of law, felt his heart flutter with the weight of a Latin oration he was destined to deliver. Every chief elder prepared himself, smoothing his chin and adjusting his silk *lucco*, ready to march in solemn procession. Young nobles scrutinized their new tunics, tailored in the French manner, hoping to impress the stranger with a peculiar, courtly grace. Meanwhile, a vast assembly of the clergy—from the Archbishop in his blinding splendor to the trains of black, white, and grey monks—consulted early in the morning. They debated how best to marshal their relics, sacred banners, and consecrated jewels, ensuring their movements harmonized perfectly with the arrival of the illustrious visitor at three o'clock in the afternoon.

What an unexampled guest! He had crossed the Alpine passes with an army Italy had never beheld before. With him marched thousands of terrible Swiss mercenaries, hardened to fight for love, hate, or coin; a host of gallant cavaliers proud of their lineage; and an unprecedented infantry where one man in a hundred carried an arquebus. Worst of all, he brought bronze cannons, hurling iron balls instead of stones, drawn by horses rather than oxen, capable of firing twice before a city could even begin to repair the first breach. Some compared this newcomer to Charlemagne, the legendary restorer of Florence and vanquisher of degenerate kings; others likened him to Cyrus, the liberator of the chosen people and restorer of the Temple. For he arrived with glorious designs: to march through Italy amid the jubilations of a grateful populace, to settle all conflicting complaints at Rome, to seize the Kingdom of Naples by virtue of hereditary right, and from there, to launch the crusade against the Turks, cutting them down or converting them to the faith of Christ. Such a scheme seemed fitting for the "Most Christian King," head of a nation strengthened by the cunning of Louis XI, whose death in terror had paved the way for such power. This figure, an antitype of both Cyrus and Charlemagne, was none other than the young Charles VIII of France.

On a general level, hardly anything could appear more grandiose or better suited to awaken memories of divine dispensation than this event. A widespread conviction held that the advent of the French king and his horde was an occurrence at which marble statues might sweat, phantom warriors might fight in the air, and beasts might give birth to monsters. It did not belong to the ordinary order of Providence but was, in a peculiar sense, the direct work of God. This conviction rested less on the sheer magnitude of the foreign invasion than on the moral emotions of the age, shaped into presentiments by the times. These emotions found their most remarkable voice in a single man.

That man was Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco in Florence. On a September morning, when rumors of the French army entering Italy still echoed in men's ears, he preached in the Cathedral from the text, "Behold I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth." He believed this revelation was guided by supreme authority, continuing the exposition of Genesis begun the previous Lent. He interpreted the "flood of waters"—an emblem of both avenging wrath and purifying mercy—as the divinely indicated symbol of the French army. His audience, comprising some of the finest spirits of the age, the most cultivated minds in the most cultured Italian city, believed him too, listening with shuddering awe. Savonarola possessed a power rarely paralleled, of impressing his beliefs upon others and swaying varied minds. Four years prior, he had proclaimed from the chief pulpit of Florence that a scourge was descending upon Italy, intended to purify the Church. Savonarola appeared to believe—and his wavering followers largely agreed—that he held a mission akin to the Hebrew prophets, and that the Florentines, receiving his message, were a second chosen people.

While the gift of prophecy in others resembled a farthing candle, illuminating only small corners of destiny with gossip, in Savonarola it burned like a mighty beacon, shining far ahead to warn and guide humanity. To the soberest minds, the supernatural quality of his insight gained strong attestation from the peculiar conditions of the age.

At the close of 1492, following the death of Lorenzo de' Medici, Italy had enjoyed a peace and prosperity unthreatened by immediate danger. There was no fear of famine, for the seasons had been bountiful with grain, wine, and oil. New palaces rose in every fair city, and villas sprouted on pleasant slopes. Those who held more than their share showed no fear for the many who held less. Citizens' armor grew rusty, and populations seemed to grow tame, licking the hands of masters who paid for ready-made armies as readily as goods from Smyrna. Even the fear of the Turk had faded, leading the Pope to find it more profitable to accept bribes for prospective poisoning than to plan for conquest or conversion.

Altogether, this world—with its partitioned empires and a roomy, universal Church—seemed a handsome establishment for the lucky and the wise. It was a realm where lust, obscenity, lying, treachery, oppression, and murder were pleasant, useful, and, when properly managed, not dangerous. Adorning this substantial delight of tyranny and avarice was the patronage of polite learning and the fine arts, allowing flattery to be spoken in the choicest Latin and sublime artists to paint both the holy and the unclean with impartial skill. Though the Church was said to be never more disgraced in its leadership nor show fewer signs of renewal, it prospered beyond any past era. The heavens above were fair and smiling; below, there were no signs of earthquake.

Yet, as we have seen, there was a man in Florence who, for two years and more, had preached that a scourge was at hand. He believed the world was not framed for the lasting convenience of hypocrites, libertines, and oppressors. From amidst those smiling heavens, he had seen a sword hanging—the sword of God's justice—speedily descending with purifying punishment upon the Church and the world. Seventeen years prior, in brilliant Ferrara, the contradiction between men's lives and their professed beliefs had pressed upon him with crushing force, destroying his appetite for the world and driving him into the cloister at twenty-three. He believed God had entrusted the sacred lamp of truth to the Church for the guidance and salvation of men, but he saw that the Church, in its corruption, had become a sepulcher hiding the lamp. As scandals multiplied and hypocrisy gave way to impudence, questions arose: Had the world lost its righteous Ruler? Was the Church finally forsaken? No, assuredly. In the Sacred Book, the past served as a mirror for the future, showing that when the wickedness of the chosen people became a cry, the judgments of God inevitably descended. Reason itself declared that vengeance was imminent; what else would suffice to turn men from their obstinacy in evil? Unless the Church were reclaimed, how could the promises be fulfilled that the heathens should be converted and the whole world subject to the one true law? He had seen his beliefs reflected in visions, a mode of seeing frequent with him since youth.

But the real force of Girolamo Savonarola's demonstration lay in his own burning indignation at the sight of wrong, his fervent belief in an Unseen Justice ending that wrong, and an Unseen Purity for which lying and uncleanness were an abomination. To his ardent, power

The sky above Florence hung heavy with a slate-grey pall, yet the Piazza del Duomo wore a mask of festive azure. Banners unfurled at the corners of the Baptistery caught the faint light, casting shadows over marble steps that were already trampled by the throngs of the faithful. It was the hour for Advent sermons, a solemn duty that demanded the city's attention regardless of the gloom outside. Yet, not all who moved through the square hurried toward the cathedral doors. High and low walked with a restless pace, their movements dictated by errands rather than devotion. While some lingered near the entrance, content to miss the preacher, others stood apart in clusters, their expressions far removed from the lazy enjoyment of a holiday.

At the corner where the Piazza met the Via de' Cerretani, the air grew somber, the artificial brilliance of the square giving way to the dull, grey morning light. There stood a ragged assembly of workers, their clothes stained with the dyes and dust of daily labor, many armed with tools that doubled as weapons. Among them was Ser Cioni, a notary whose voice had once been dismissed with scorn in the Mercato, but now spoke with the complacent humor of a man riding high. To the gathered crowd, he promised a terrifying truth: "Never talk to me of bloodthirsty Swiss or fierce French infantry," he declared, his tone cutting through the damp air. "They might as well be trapped in mountain passes as walking our streets. Peasants have shattered the finest armies of our condottieri when forced into steep precipices. Tell me truly, Florentines, need we fear any army within our own walls?"

A man named Oddo, his hands and forearms permanently tinted crimson by the vats he tended, stepped forward. A small hatchet hung at his belt. "True enough, Ser Cioni," Oddo replied, his voice rising. "Those French cavaliers who paraded in their stiff doublets last week saw little but a meal waiting for them. I was carrying cloth past Ognissanti when I saw my masters looking down on us, pitying our inability to strut like proud roosters. 'Stick out your stomachs,' I told them, 'for I carry a meat-axe that will slide right in.' Then the great bell of the Palazzo Vecchio sounded—the *vacca muglia*—and chaos erupted. We raised a barricade, trapping them, and our *Ciompi*, led by one brandishing a great scythe, cut off the plumes from a nobleman's helmet. It was glorious! Though, alas, Piero de' Medici never returned. What a pity; we might have left him with neither legs nor wings to fly home."

"Aye, and let us hope he stays put," agreed a young butcher, his knife sheathed at his hip. "Piero looked as spooked as a hunted chicken when we pelted him. A coward, unless he has horses to hide behind. But we shall swallow no Medici again, whatever the King of France demands."

"And those cannons?" interjected Goro, a stout man whose grievances had fattened him over two years. "San Giovanni defend us! If Messer Domeneddio truly cares for us, why send the French to Naples instead? By God, they could have marched through Bologna or Arezzo. We wouldn't have minded Arezzo!"

"Fools!" Ser Cioni snapped, attempting to steer the conversation toward the glory of Florence, but his words were drowned out by a sudden cry: "Look there!"

All eyes swiveled toward the Via de' Cerretani. Advancing with a brilliance that clashed with the grey street was Lorenzo Tornabuoni, accompanied by a French nobleman dressed in splendor, his white linen embroidered with gold. With them walked two other Florentines of high birth, but leading the procession on the Frenchman's left was a figure who needed no brocade to command attention. He wore only black, a mantle of mourning that would soon be exchanged for red as he prepared to walk in the Latin Secretary's procession. This was Tito Melema.

Eighteen months had passed since last seen, yet a subtle shift marked him. The final shedding of youthful innocence had settled over him, replaced by the cold, calculated self-possession of a man fully adopted into the machinery of state. His features remained soft, his eyes clear, but beneath the surface lay an indefinable transformation, like the shifting hues of dawn. He smiled, listened, and explained with effortless grace, masking a profound internal change. Beside him, the Frenchman sought instructions on ceremony for his return to Signa, pausing finally to survey the Piazza where the royal procession would halt.

Though the party drew stares, the gaze was not universally warm. Whispers circulated regarding the Frenchman's hoof-shaped shoes, delicate flatteries of royal excess, while sharp, muttered insults directed at the Medici were carefully lowered to avoid detection. Lorenzo Tornabuoni, however, possessed the skill of a master actor, disguising any annoyance with a practiced smile. Tito, aided by his alien perspective, viewed the local tensions with detached amusement, using his fluency in French and familiarity with the South to charm the visitors.

As the group reached a point offering a view of the Duomo, they paused. Festoons adorned the central door, prompting a debate. Tornabuoni summoned Piero di Cosimo, who lounged idly before Nello's shop. An animated discussion ensued, punctuated by Piero's pungent observations, which Tito translated with literal precision. The absurdity of the exchange turned even the grumpiest onlookers into amused spectators, their faces wearing expressions of half-smile, half-humiliation. For Tito, this was a triumph; he reveled in the approving glances, basking in the warmth of the crowd's laughter as the grey light faded around them.

Yet, in the shadowed lanes beyond the square, a darker current flowed. Oncomers, clad in rags and devoid of festive finery, approached with grim determination. Their focus was drawn to the conspicuous French attire, specifically the strange, oversized footwear. Pressing closer, a growing number of non-admiring citizens surrounded the foreigners. Leading this tide were three men in scanty clothing, their hands bound tightly by cord, a rope looped around their necks and bodies to restrain any violent movement. Their guards were French soldiers, who used broken Italian and the threat of strangulation to urge their charges to beg.

Two of the prisoners complied, kneeling and holding out their bound hands with piteous tones: "For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us something for our ransom! We are Tuscans; we were captured in Lunigiana."

But the third man, the eldest, remained stubbornly silent. Unlike his younger companions, who appeared sturdy and mendicant despite their poverty, this older man had crossed the threshold of age, likely fifty or sixty. His beard, grown long in neglect, and his straight, thick hair framed a bald crown, both turning nearly white. His frame, though emaciated, stood firm and upright, radiating a strange, isolated energy. Dark eyes and strong brows burned with intensity against a face of yellow, bloodless skin, deeply wrinkled and tangled with grey strands. Yet, a fitfulness haunted his gaze; after flickering with fierce scrutiny of the windows and passersby, his eyes would fall again, lost and wandering. His lips were motionless, his hands clenched resolutely downward. He refused to beg.

The sight sparked growing exasperation among the Florentines. Initially, many had dropped coins, some out of automatic piety, others out of a superstitious awe of the French military, believing payment ensured safety. But as the procession moved deeper into the city, this compliance evaporated. A chorus of angry exclamations rose, intelligible enough to the foreign ears without translation. "French dogs!" "Bullock-feet!" "Snatch their pikes!" "Cut the cords and let them run! They'll flee faster than geese!"

The soldiers, who relished their insolence, found themselves checked by the sheer volume of the crowd. With a strong desire to draw steel, they were hamstrung by the necessity of maintaining a tight grip on their prisoners. Hurrying now toward a nearby inn, hoping for shelter, they were met by the dyer, Oddo. Seeing the tumult, Oddo snatched his axe from his belt and ran forward, followed by the butcher and the rest of the workers, leaving Goro to retreat into a narrow alley.

Oddo's appearance, with his blood-red arms and uplifted axe, acted as a catalyst. He did not strike immediately but stood as a stirring symbol of resistance, a living banner of the street. From the crowd emerged Lollo, a conjuror's impish lad, dancing and jeering before the innocent children who formed the bulk of the mob. Lollo cared little for the prisoners' plight; he coveted the chance to display his dexterity. Seizing a moment as the crowd jostled more violently, he leaped forward. In an instant, he sliced the cord binding the eldest prisoner.

"Run, old one!" Lollo whispered, piping the word into the man's ear. Immediately, he set an example, sprinting away with the frantic agility of a startled bird.

The old man's senses were not dulled by age. The idea of escape had haunted him constantly, and the shifting mood of the crowd gave him fresh hope. He broke free instantly. The Florentines, sensing opportunity, rushed between him and his captor. He fled into the Piazza, but the sound of heavy boots

"You are easily frightened, though," Piero remarked, his lips curling in a fresh sneer. "Though my likeness falls short of the original, the old fellow certainly wore a tiger's gaze. I intend to return to the Duomo and study him again."

"It is hardly pleasant to be seized by a lunatic, if indeed he is one," Lorenzo Tornabuoni replied smoothly, offering a polite defense for Tito. "Yet, he may merely be a rogue. Let us observe; I believe we must ascertain whether we possess sufficient authority to quell this unrest between our citizens and these foreigners." Addressing the Frenchman directly, he added, "We must determine if we can halt this disturbance."

With swords unsheathed, the three men stepped forward, flanked by a silent ring of onlookers. Tito followed suit. The initial paralysis of terror within him yielded quickly to the frantic calculations that mortal peril forces upon the cowardly. He needed to know what others knew regarding Baldassarre.

The mob of men and lads, eager mostly to jeer and torment the soldier rather than wound him seriously, parted instantly at the sight of gentlemen bearing drawn steel. The French soldier was questioned. He and his comrades had merely escorted their captives into the city to solicit ransom. Two were Tuscan soldiers captured in Lunigiana; the third, an elderly man, had accompanied a group of Genoese who had clashed with French foragers near Fivizzano. Though the elder appeared mad, he posed no threat. The soldier knew nothing further, unable to comprehend a single word the old man muttered. Tito listened intently, yet remained deaf to all surrounding noise until Tornabuoni addressed him specifically.

"Will you accompany us, Melema? Or, since Messere departs for Signa, might you not wisely align yourself with the current fashion and attend the Friar? He speaks with the force of a torrent at flood today. It is a duty we must all perform to preserve our Medicean standing. *I* myself would go, if only time permitted."

Tito's countenance had regained its hue, and he managed a display of cheerful composure.

"Certainly, I rank among the admirers of the inspired orator," he smiled. "Regrettably, however, I shall remain engaged with the Segretario until the procession commences."

"I shall enter the Duomo to view that savage old man once more," Piero declared.

"Piero mio," Lorenzo interjected gently, "show him kindness and bring him to one of the traveler's hospitals. The monks may determine if he requires confinement."

As the party dispersed, Tito turned toward the Palazzo Vecchio, where he awaited Bartolommeo Scala. The distance was brief, yet for Tito, it stretched into eternity, akin to the fleeting, agonizing minutes of a waking dream. Every street corner and public square held layers of memory, foreboding, and torturous anxieties that could have filled many months of history. A cold serpent seemed to coil around his limbs.

Baldassarre lived, and he resided in Florence—a living vengeance that would not rest, just as a coiled viper refuses to settle until it has crushed its prey. In Baldassarre's nature, injury demanded immediate retribution. His love and hate burned with a feverish intensity that enslaved every fiber of his being, driving him to sacrifice himself to his passions as if worshipping a capricious god. Yet, Baldassarre had loosened his grip and vanished. Tito understood this perfectly: the vengeance was being measured, calculated to ensure absolute success.

Had Tito not uttered those damning words—"He is a madman"—could he not have conjured the mental fortitude required to deny knowing the man? Had he claimed positive proof of Baldassarre's demise, who could contradict him? Only Fra Luca, who was dead, and the crew of the companion galley that delivered the news of the pirate encounter. The odds of Baldassarre crossing paths with any survivor of that shipwreck were infinitesimal. Tito felt a bitter pang of regret; a timely, carefully crafted lie might have spared him catastrophe. Yet, fabricating such a falsehood in the heat of a convulsive shock would have demanded a mastery of self that he did not feel he possessed. Those words had erupted from him like a sudden birth, conceived and nurtured in the darksome recesses of his soul.

Tito was subject to an inexorable law of human nature: we prepare for sudden acts through the repeated selection of good or ill, slowly forging the character that guides our next move.

His sole hope now rested on Baldassarre failing to find his revenge. And then, a thought more actively cruel than any he had previously entertained struck him: Could his own unpremeditated words contain a grain of truth? Enough truth, at least, to shield him against any declaration Baldassarre might later make? The old man looked strangely wild, his eyes burning with an eager, tormented light. Perhaps suffering had driven him to madness. If so, the vengeance seeking to inflict disgrace might be thwarted.

Yet, there existed a form of vengeance that no clever deception could parry. Baldassarre belonged to a stock for whom the thrust of the dagger was as instinctive as the spring of a tiger's paw. Tito shrank back in shuddering horror from the prospect of disgrace, but he also harbored a deep-seated, physical dread inherent to his soft, pleasure-loving nature—a terror that prevented him from facing wounds and death as a mere release from shame. Immediately, his mind raced to discover some hidden armor, some stratagem that could protect him from a wrath no subtlety could deflect.

He marveled at the potency of the fearful passion that gripped him. It felt as though a blight had suddenly struck, transforming the vibrant joy of youth into a landscape of pure pain.

A final resource remained. He could turn back, seek out Baldassarre again, confess everything—to Romola, to the entire world. But he never considered this path. True repentance, severing all ties to evil, demands more than the paralyzing grip of selfish fear. He possessed no conviction that truth offered strength or safety; instead, he clung entirely to his ingenuity and his capacity for dissimulation. With the initial shock of betrayal fading, he hoped to brace himself for any emergency through cool deceit and defensive guile.

A peculiar fact marked Tito's experience at this critical juncture: no direct plan to eliminate Baldassarre ever crossed his mind. Every other possibility ran through his consciousness, even flight from Florence itself, yet he never devised a scheme to remove his enemy. His dread spawned no active malice, nor did he wish to cause pain to any mortal. He had simply sought to make life easier for himself, to navigate his human lot in a way that pinched nowhere. That choice, made repeatedly at various turns, had landed him in precarious positions. The question now stood not whether he should share the burden of destiny with his suffering fellows, but whether the totality of his lies and deceptions would suffice to prevent him from being crushed by the inevitable consequences of his habitual choices.

Within the Great Cathedral.

As Baldassarre forced his way back, his wrists bound tight and a heavy hemp cord looped around his neck and torso, pushing aside the heavy curtains, he froze. The air inside the Duomo struck him with the shock of a physical blow. He had expected the hollow, silent grandeur he knew so well—a vast nave stripped of life save for cold, stone idols and flickering tapers, where the faint murmur of a monotonous chant drifted from the distant choir. That was how churches appeared to a man who sought them only for shelter, not for worship.

Instead, he found a sea of humanity. Hundreds, perhaps thousands, filled the space from the nave to the choir. They were of every rank: robed magistrates and ladies of noble birth sat alongside rough-hewn artisans and peasants in coarse wool. All were turned upward, their faces illuminated by a breathless, hushed silence directed toward the pulpit set at the angle where the aisle met the choir.

Upon the raised platform stood a Dominican friar. He possessed features of stark intensity and hair the color of midnight. In his hand, he held a wooden crucifix, moving it with a rhythmic, commanding cadence as he preached.

For the first few minutes, Baldassarre took no note of the man's words. Though his entry had been silent, several pairs of eyes near the threshold had turned sharply toward him, fixed with suspicion. Yet, the rope around his neck marked him clearly as an escaped prisoner. By custom, the church was a sanctuary; his advanced age and the wild, ragged misery etched onto his face invited pity rather than fear. As he stood perfectly still, his gaze drifting aimlessly from the vast assembly to the worn stones beneath his feet, the observers lost interest, returning their attention to the sermon that commanded the room.

Among those eyes that had initially scanned him were Romola's. She had entered late through a side door, positioned so that she held a direct line of sight to the main entrance. She had watched Baldassarre with a long, piercing attention. Something about the grey hairs framing his temples, combined with the distinctive marks of suffering carved into his face and the constriction of the cord, struck a deep chord within her. Her entire life had cultivated a sensitivity toward the sorrows of the aged, and this man invoked it instantly. She fancied that their gazes had locked briefly in that initial moment of recognition, but Baldassarre did not truly see her. His awareness of her presence was merely a fleeting flash, a spark instantly consumed by the ferocious turmoil of emotions ignited by his recent encounter with Tito.

Images from a fractured past surged through him like delirious visions, twisted by a gnawing thirst and sheer anguish. No coherent thought for the future could take root amidst this internal storm. The closest approximation to reflection was a bitter realization of his own enfeeblement, coupled with a vague, sweeping resolve toward total distrust. Suddenly, his body began to vibrate, attuned to a sound that resonated like the thundering echo of his own inner rage. A voice, penetrating his very bones with an accent of triumphant certainty, proclaimed—"The day of vengeance is at hand!"

Baldassarre shuddered and looked up. Too distant to discern the preacher's features clearly, he saw only the silhouette of a man standing with his right arm outstretched, lifting the crucifix high. Yet, he gasped for those threatening words again, as if they were a promise of paradise. A pause followed, deliberate and heavy. Slowly, the preacher lowered his arm. He laid the crucifix gently upon the edge of the pulpit and folded his arms across his chest, gazing out at the multitude as if challenging every single soul to meet his gaze.

"All ye in Florence are my witnesses, for I spoke not in a corner," the friar declared, his voice ringing with authority. "Ye are my witnesses that four years ago, ere signs of war or tribulation, I preached the coming of the scourge. I lifted my voice as a trumpet to the prelates, princes, and people of Italy, declaring: 'The cup of your iniquity is full.' Behold, the thunder of the Lord gathers, destined to shatter the vessel. Your sins, which taste like sweet wine to you, shall be poured out upon you, becoming molten lead.

"And you, O priests, who mock and say, 'Ha, ha! There is no Presence in the sanctuary—the Shechinah is void, the Mercy-seat bare: we may sin behind the veil, and who shall punish us?' To you I said, 'The presence of God shall be revealed in your temple as a consuming fire. Your sacred vestments shall become winding-sheets of flame. For sweet music there shall be shrieks and hissing; for soft couches, thorns; and for the breath of wantons, pestilence. Trust not in your gold nor silver, trust not in your high fortresses. Though walls of iron and castles of adamant, the Most High shall instill terror into your hearts and weakness into your councils, until you are confounded and flee like frightened women. He shall break countless mighty men and raise others in their stead. For God can endure no longer the pollution of His sanctuary; He shall thoroughly purge His Church.'"

"And since Scripture declares that God reveals His purposes to His prophets alone, He hath chosen me, His unworthy servant, making His design manifest to my soul through the living Word and the works of Providence. By the ministry of angels, these truths have been revealed to me in vision. His word possesses me so completely that I am but a branch of the forest when the wind of Heaven blows through it; I cannot remain silent, even if I am to be mocked by the scornful. For four years, I have preached in obedience to the Divine Will. In defiance of ridicule, I have proclaimed three things entrusted to me by the Lord: first, that *in these times God will regenerate His Church*; second, that *before regeneration comes the scourge over all Italy*; and third, that *these things will come quickly*."

"But hypocrites, who cloak their hatred of truth with a veneer of affection, have told me, 'Come now, Frate, abandon your prophecies; it suffices to teach virtue.' To them I answer: 'Yes, you speak in your hearts that God dwells afar off, and His word is no more than parchment written by dead men, dealing not as in ancient days—rebuking nations, punishing oppressors, striking down unholy priests as He smote the sons of Eli. But I cry again in your ears: God is near, not distant. His judgments do not alter. He is the God of Armies; the warriors who march to battle are His ministers, just as storm, fire, and plague. He drives them by the breath of His angels, and they descend upon the chosen land which has forsaken the Covenant. And thou, O Italy, art the chosen land; hath not God placed His sanctuary within thee, and hast thou polluted it? Behold, the ministers of His wrath are upon thee—they stand at thy very doors!'"

Savonarola's voice had climbed to a peak of impassioned force before falling abruptly into silence. He dropped his hands, clasping them quietly before him. This silence, rather than signaling restlessness, acted as a potent spell over the crowd. Through the vast expanse of the cathedral, men and women sat like living statues, faces uplifted, until the voice returned in clear, low tones.

"Yet there is a pause—even as in the days when Jerusalem was destroyed, there was a pause that the children of God might flee. There is a stillness before the storm: lo, darkness hangs overhead, yet not a leaf trembles; the winds are stayed, that the Voice of God's warning may be heard. Hear it now, O Florence, the chosen city in the chosen land! Repent and forsake evil; do justice; love mercy; purge all uncleanness from among you, that the Spirit of Truth and Holiness may fill your souls and breathe through every street and dwelling. Then the pestilence shall not enter, nor the sword pass over you unhurt.

"For the sword hangs suspended from the sky; it quivers, ready to fall! *The Sword of God upon the earth, swift and sudden!* Did I not tell you years ago that I beheld the vision and heard the voice? And behold, it is fulfilled! Is there not a King with his army at your gates? Does not the earth quake beneath the tread of horses and the rolling wheels of swift cannon? Is there not a fierce multitude capable of laying bare the land as with a razor? I tell you, the French King with his host is the minister of God: He shall guide him as a hand guides a sharp sickle, and the joints of the wicked shall melt before him, mown down as stubble. He who flees shall not escape, he who escapes shall not be delivered. And the tyrants who built their thrones from the vices of the multitude, and the unbelieving priests who traffic in souls and fill the sanctuary with fornication, shall be hurled from their soft couches into burning Hell; and the pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand aloof, crying, 'Lo, these men have brought the stench of a new wickedness into the everlasting fire.'"

"But thou, O Florence, take the offered mercy. See! The Cross is held out to you: come and be healed. Which nation in Italy has received a token like yours? The tyrant is driven out; those who held a bribe in their left hand and a rod in their right

Outside the Duomo, the air hummed with the aftermath of the sermon, a heavy, vibrating silence broken only by the rustle of robes and the shifting weight of thousands. While Baldassarre had been seized by the terrifying voice of Savonarola, he remained oblivious to the shadow falling upon him from behind. Standing just within reach was Piero di Cosimo, the painter, who had slipped past the throng not to listen to the fiery orations, but to witness the fate of the escaped prisoner.

As the preacher and his captivated audience surrendered themselves to a wordless, collective emotion, the newcomer stepped forward. With a gentle, almost casual touch, Piero placed a hand on Baldassarre's arm. The painter turned slowly, the tears still tracing slow paths down his cheeks, though a vigorous sigh marked the end of his emotional outburst. Speaking in a low murmur, he offered, "Shall I cut your cords for you? I've heard the tale of how you were taken."

Baldassarre did not answer at first. His gaze narrowed, suspicious and wary, fixed on the officious stranger. Finally, after a beat of hesitation, he whispered, "If you will."

"Better step outside," Piero suggested softly.

Again, Baldassarre looked askance. Sensing the distrust, Piero smiled, drew a small knife from his sleeve, and swiftly severed the bonds around the prisoner's wrists. As he worked, the artist mused that the notion of the man's madness was hardly improbable; there was something so singular, so wild, etched into the expression of his face. *If he causes trouble,* Piero thought, *he will be tied up again quickly enough. For now, let the poor devil breathe.*

"You fear me," Baldassarre observed, his voice an undertone. "You wish to keep secrets from me."

Baldassarre crossed his arms, savoring the return of muscular sensation after days of captivity. His look of suspicion softened, replaced by a quiet, steely resolve. "No, I have nothing to tell."

"As you please," Piero replied, shrugging slightly. "Though perhaps you seek shelter? We Florentines are renowned for our hospitality to travelers with torn doublets and hollow bellies. There is a hospital for the poor near the gates, and if you wished, I could guide you there. You need not fear the French soldier anymore; he has been dispatched."

Baldassarre nodded, accepting the offer in silence. Together, they walked away from the church.

As they moved along the Via dell' Oriuolo toward the Gate of Santa Croce, Piero broke the quiet. "Would you not sit for a portrait? I am a painter, and I would pay well to capture your likeness."

A flicker of old caution returned to Baldassarre's eyes. He shook his head decisively. "No."

"A pity!" Piero said curtly. "Very well, then, walk on. You will find the Porta Santa Croce ahead, and outside it lies the hospital for strangers. So, you decline my further aid?"

"I thank you for what you have already done. I require nothing more."

"It is as it should be," Piero murmured, turning away with a philosophical shrug.

*Mysterious old tiger,* the artist reflected as he retraced his steps back toward the Piazza del Duomo. *Truly a subject worthy of the brush. Ugly, carved with deep lines, as if the plowshare and the harrow had tilled over his heart. A stark, grim counterpoint to my own bland, smiling Messer Greco—the Baccus Triumphans who wedded the beautiful Antigone against all reason and history. Ah! One feels the scholar's blood curdle uncomfortably at the sight of that old fellow's claw!*

By the time Piero re-entered the square, the vast multitude that had listened to Fra Girolamo was beginning to disperse. The urgency with which they hurried toward their separate destinations testified to the profound impact of the preaching, a spell that had momentarily suspended the ordinary rhythms of daily life. The artist leaned against the sharp angle of the Baptistery, watching the departing tide. He delighted in the kaleidoscope of garments and the intense, characteristic profiles of the crowd—faces that seemed to echo the timeless observations of Masaccio, fifty years prior, and the unflagging realism of Domenico Ghirlandaio.

This morning, however, was peculiar. The congregation, usually a mosaic of the city's factions, had displayed a completeness of representation rarely seen. There were men of high birth, accustomed to public office, who had emerged as fierce enemies of the Medici and ardent supporters of popular rule. Among them stood figures of authoritative presence, like Francesco Valori, handsome yet hot-tempered, intoxicated by the immediate divine mandate to liberate Florence in their own image. Beside them stood others like Soderini, possessing less of the fiery zealotry and more of the statesman's wisdom. Then there were the brave, un-doctrined lovers of sober republicanism, like Piero Capponi, who preferred the sword to the argument and cared little for ideology so long as the Medici were ousted.

At their sides walked the doctors of law, whose study of Accursius had not extinguished their spiritual ardor; Messer Luca Corsini himself, who would one day raise his learned arms in the streets to hurl stones for religion and freedom. Intermixed with these dignitaries carrying their black luccio or fur-lined mantles were those with contemplative, sensitive features: scholars bearing the names of Strozzi and Acciajoli, already inclined toward the cowl; artists driven to higher ambitions by Savonarola's fire, like the young man who had recently surpassed himself in his fresco of the Divine Child within the friar's bare cell—a youth unconscious that he would soon don the habit himself and become known as Fra Bartolommeo. There was the mystic poet Girolamo Benevieni, likely rushing to deliver news of the Friar's impending arrival to his friend Pico della Mirandola, a man who would never see another dawn. There were women of noble birth, clad in scrupulously plain dress, their grace derived from restraint rather than ornament. And dominating the scene, perhaps more than any other stratum, were the genuine *popolani* of the Major and Minor Arts, conscious of their purses shrinking under the threat of war taxes. Most striking of all, however, was the long, winding stream of poorer tradesmen and artisans. Their faith in the Friar's divine message ranged from the crude, undiscriminating trust of the oppressed, seeing him as the friend of the poor and the scourge of the luxurious rich, to the eager, ravenous hunger of the sedentary worker for every subtlety of biblical interpretation, illuminated by a pale flame that promised a science beyond the board where he stitched.

Yet scattered among these devoted disciples were many who were not his followers at all. Some were Mediceans, already bowing to the prevailing spirit of the popular party out of fear and policy, feigning deference. Others were sincere advocates of a free government who viewed Savonarola merely as an ambitious monk—half sagacious, half fanatical—who had leveraged his position to become a powerful instrument of the masses, a social fact that could not be ignored. Even among his bitterest enemies, members of the old aristocratic anti-Medicean party determined to reclaim the reins for the chief families, or licentious youths who detested him as the killjoy of Florence, found their attention drawn. The sermons in the Duomo had become political incidents, captivating the ears of curiosity and malice as much as faith. Men of ideas, like the young Niccolò Machiavelli, went to observe and report to friends in country villas; men of appetite, like Dolfo Spini, sought to hunt down the Friar as a public nuisance who made game scarce, lying in wait for grounds of accusation.

Perhaps no preacher had ever exerted such massive influence as Savonarola, nor worked with such heterogeneous material. The secret of his sway lay in the highly mixed character of his preaching itself. To Baldassarre, wrought into an ecstasy of self-martyring revenge, the sermon had been a call to vengeance. Yet for others, Savonarola's words struck the very finest susceptibilities of human nature, while simultaneously gratifying low egoism, tickling gossiping curiosity, and fascinating timorous superstition. His need for personal predominance, his labyrinthine allegorical interpretations of Scripture, his enigmatic visions, and his false certitude regarding Divine intentions never ceased, in his own large soul, to be ennobled by a fervid piety, a passionate sense of the infinite, an active sympathy, and a clear-sighted demand for the subjection of selfish interests to the general good. But for the mass of his audience, all the potency of his preaching lay in his strong assertion of supernatural claims, in his denunciatory visions, and in the false certainty that lent his sermons the immediacy of a political bulletin. Having once held that audience in his mastery, it became necessary to his nature—and necessary for their welfare—that he maintain it. The effect was inevitable: no man struggles to retain power over a mixed multitude without suffering vitiation; his standard must inevitably descend to meet their lower needs rather than rising to his own best insights.

The mysteries of human character were seldom presented in a way better suited to checking the judgments of facile knowingness than in the person of Girolamo Savonarola. Yet, if one regards his life as a drama wherein great inward modifications accompanied outward changes, one might afford him a reverence that requires no shutting of the eyes to fact. Up to this

By six o'clock, the majority of Florence breathed a sigh of relief as the spectacle of the new Charlemagne finally concluded. While the rolling drums, blaring trumpets, and thundering hooves along the Pisan road mingled with the ringing of excited bells, creating a majestic crescendo for those perched on turreted rooftops, the scene itself was shrouded in twilight. Without sunlight to illuminate the banners, spears, and silken surcoats, nor a thick dust cloud to obscure them, the approaching troops stood out with stark, terrifying clarity. Tall Scottish archers, ponderous Swiss halberdiers, agile Gascon climbers, and heavy cavalrymen who seemed embodiments of knight-errantry—all appeared formidable yet purposeful. To the onlookers, there was solace in knowing this array of strength existed solely to punish the enemies of God. With hearts fortified by this conviction, viewing the splendor of the nobility, the jewel-adorned hilts, the embroidered scarves bearing symbols of piety and valor, the golden chains, and the transcendent canopy borne aloft by chosen youths above the Most Christian King became a distinct, albeit solemn, pleasure. As an old chronicler once noted, simply, "_it was great magnificence._"

For the Signoria, however, standing on their platform against the gates, the grandeur was obscured by a rising unease. They waited to march forth, led by their orator, to greet the mighty guest. Had Messer Luca Corsini been able to deliver his prepared Latin welcome without obstruction, the moment would have been triumphant. Yet, rain began to fall, breeding impatience in both men and horses that shattered the delivery of his carefully studied periods. The scholarly representatives were forced to abandon their formal address, offering a clumsy, impromptu greeting in French. This sudden chaos, however, proved fortuitous for Tito. Stationed behind the Signoria among the secretaries, he found himself thrust forward by the press of horses and officials.

"Somebody step forward and speak a few words in French," Soderini urged.
No man of high standing dared risk another failure. "You, Francesco Gaddi—you can manage it." But Gaddi, doubting his own composure, hesitated. Pushing Tito aside, he whispered, "You, Melema."

Tito stepped forward instantly. With the natural grace of deference that suited him as easily as walking, he delivered the necessary few words in the name of the Signoria. Then, with a fluid courtesy, he yielded the space for the King to pass. His presence of mind, which had deserted him during the terrifying crisis of the morning, served him perfectly now. It was the mark of an excellent liveried servant to remain steadfast when danger was invisible. When later commended for his timely service, Tito laughed off the praise as trivial, allowing Gaddi to claim the credit for the improvised welcome. There was no wonder at Tito's popularity; the standard by which men judge others is most often their own vanity.

Yet, other aspects of the day's reception had proven far more disastrous than anticipated. If the Florentine procession of clergy and layfolk had followed their intricate plans, they would not have found themselves choked into a makeshift route through back alleys, meeting the King only at the Cathedral. Furthermore, if the young monarch, seated beneath his canopy with lance resting on his thigh, had resembled a true Charlemagne rather than a hastily modeled grotesque, the imaginations of his admirers might have been better served. One wished the scourge of Italian wickedness and "Champion of the honor of women" possessed a less miserable leg, fewer than the normal number of toes, a mouth less reptilian in its slit, and a nose and head of less exorbitant proportion. Nevertheless, the thin leg rested upon cloth of gold and pearls, and the face was merely a small interruption amidst black velvet, gold, blazing rubies, and the brilliant embroidery of the canopy. Still, "_great magnificence_" it was.

The people cried out "_France, France!"_ with an enthusiasm matched only by their subsequent tearing of the canopy to pieces, an act performed according to immemorial custom. Royal lips duly kissed the altar, and despite the myriad mishaps, the King and his retinue were lodged in the Palace of the Via Larga. The nobles and gentry dispersed to their great houses, while the terrible soldiery encamped in the Prato and surrounding fields. The business of the day was done.

But the streets still presented a startling aspect, unseen by Florentines before under the November stars. Instead of a gloom broken only by feeble lamps before saintly images or streams of reddish light from open doorways, every house window glowed with suspended lamps.
