Men walked along with security and ease comparable to daylight—"great magnificence."

Through these illuminated thoroughfares, Tito Melema walked at approximately eight o'clock, returning home. He had labored throughout the day under the weight of hidden anxieties, eventually slipping away unnoticed from the post-prandial gaiety. Now, alone, he hoped to confront his circumstances and adjust himself to the probable outcomes, thereby shedding his childish fears. If only he had not lacked the presence of mind to recognize Baldassarre during that shocking encounter! It would have been happier for him in every regard. He still winced at the knowledge that he was deliberately inflicting suffering upon his father; he would have vastly preferred that Baldassarre prosper and find happiness. But there remained no second path now; conflict was impossible. All that remained was to protect himself.

As he moved from the Piazza di Santa Croce along the Via dei Benci, nearing the turn into the Borgo Santa Croce, his ear caught a sound that was not the merriment of evening revelry, but the vigorous labor of the anvil. Tito gave a slight start and quickened his pace, the sounds suggesting a welcome thought. He knew they emanated from the workshop of Niccolò Caparra, a favorite haunt for all Florentines who admired curious and beautiful ironwork.

"What keeps the giant at work so late?" Tito wondered. "All the better for me. I can finish this little piece of business tonight instead of tomorrow morning."

Preoccupied, he paused momentarily in admiration as he approached the workshop. The wide doorway, situated at the truncated angle of a great block of houses, was surmounted by a loggia roofed with fluted tiles and supported by stone columns with roughly carved capitals. Framed in red light by the outlines of the tiles and columns stood the grand figure of Niccolò in black relief, his huge arms moving in a rhythmic rise and fall, alternately hiding and revealing the profile of his firm mouth and powerful brow. Two smaller, ebony-like figures—one at the anvil, the other at the bellows—accentuated his superior massiveness.

Tito darkened the doorway, standing in silence, for it was futile to speak until Niccolò should deign to pause and notice him. Only after the smith had hammered the head of an axe to the proper sharpness and dismissed it did Niccolò acknowledge him.

"What is it, Messer Tito? Business?"

"Assuredly, Niccolò; otherwise, I should not venture to interrupt you while working past hours, as I interpret that as a sign that your work is pressing."

"I've been at the same work all day—making axes and spear-heads. Every fool who passes my shop puts his pumpkin-head inside to ask, 'Niccolò, will you not come and see the King of France and his soldiers?' And I answer, 'No: I do not wish to see their faces—I wish to see their backs.'"

"Are you forging arms for the citizens, then, Niccolò, so they may have something better than rusty scythes and spits in case of an uproar?"

"We shall see. Arms are good, and Florence is likely to need them. The Frate tells us we shall regain Pisa, and I agree with the Frate; but I would gladly know how that promise is to be fulfilled if we do not secure plenty of good weapons. The Frate sees far ahead, I believe. But he does not see birds caught by winking at them, as some of our folk attempt to imagine. He sees sense, not nonsense. But you are a bit of a Medicean, Messer Tito Melema. Ebbene! I too have been one in my time, before the cask began to run sour. What is your business?"

"Merely to inquire regarding the price of that fine coat of mail I observed hanging here recently. I intend to purchase it for a certain personage who requires protection of that sort beneath his doublet."

"Let him come and buy it himself," Niccolò replied bluntly. "I am particular about what I sell and to whom. I prefer to know my customer."

"I understand your scruples, Niccolò. But this is defensive armor; it cannot hurt anyone."

"True: but it may give the wearer a false sense of security, prompting him to desire to hurt someone if given the chance. No, no; it is not my own work. It is the fine craftsmanship of Maso of Brescia. I would be loath for it to cover the heart of a scoundrel. I must know who is to wear it."

"Well, then, to be plain with you, Niccolò mio, I want it myself," Tito admitted, sensing persuasion was futile. "The truth is, I am likely to undertake a journey—and you know what travel entails in these times. Do you suspect _me_ of treason against the Republic?"

"No, I know

The Young Wife.

As Tito hurried across the bridge, the new armor hidden beneath his cloak, Romola paced the length of the ancient library, her thoughts fixed entirely on his return. It was a day when almost every window in Florence gaped open to witness the arrival of the French King and his nobility, yet Romola's shutters remained drawn. Since her father's sudden and terrifying death three months prior, she had not attended a single festivity.

"He asked if Tito were coming to write," Bardo had murmured earlier that evening, his voice trembling with a mixture of hope and exhaustion. "I told him yes, though he was at a supper at the Cardinal's. Perhaps he brings news of the library?"

But the hope had curdled into a frantic urgency. Bardo, flushed and pale in turns, had declared he must proceed without his son-in-law. "Get the pen, Romola!" he had cried, his voice cracking. "He has brought me no new text, yet I must speak on the New Platonists. If I die today, nothing will be done."

Romola had knelt instantly, pen in hand, expecting his voice. Instead, the room fell into a silence so profound it became a physical weight. When she finally spoke, offering encouragement, he did not answer. The silence stretched, unbroken, until it seemed to swallow the air itself. Later, looking back on that hour, Romola felt a sting of shame for her own thoughts. In the first shock of grief, she had secretly whispered, *Perhaps my life with Tito will be more perfect now.*

That dream of a triple existence, united with her father and her husband, had never fully materialized. It was as if a rainbow shower of sweets contained invisible seeds of bitterness. She had realized with a cruel clarity that no human could endure her father's monotonous, exacting demands forever. Her own patience, forged by deep sympathy, had eventually cracked under the strain. Before their marriage, Tito's efforts had seemed effortless, buoyed by the novelty of the task. But now, the irksomeness of the burden had grown unbearable.

Yet, she refused to blame Tito. To do so would be unjust. He remained gentle, tender, and sweet in his excuses, lingering with playful fondness before departing for whatever social engagements called him. His nature was different from hers; he craved the vivid gaiety of the world, the admiration of crowds, which she, absorbed in her father's legacy, had willingly renounced. Their natures diverged widely—he lighter, less proud, quicker to forgive—while she found her affections becoming increasingly singular and consuming. She told herself this was the natural order of man and woman, that her capacity for absorption was greater, and that she must accept his inevitable flight from the gloom of study.

A sense of guilt, however, gnawed at her as she contemplated her father's death. Was it not sinful to feel a glimmer of relief that his importunate claims on her heart were silenced? Now, free from the division of her duties, their young lives should flow together in one pure current. Yet, the promise to fulfill her father's supreme wish regarding the library weighed heavily upon her soul. That piety was the only atonement she could offer for the shadowy joy she felt at his passing. The laborious, dark existence—the "seed-time without a harvest"—must end. What remained of it was the collection of manuscripts and antiquities, the fruit of fifty years of toil, now secured from creditors thanks to Bernardo del Nero's advance of a thousand florins.

But the political landscape had shifted violently. Within ten days, all hope in the Medici circle had evaporated. The famous collections in the Via Larga faced dispersal; French agents eyed the antique gems as spoils of war, and the Florentine State, drowning in debts and preparing for conflict over Pisa, was likely to sell rather than preserve. For Romola, educated in a secluded world of books, these turbulent shifts mattered less than the fate of her father's dream. Her newfound interest in the public sphere, sparked by the French invasion and the fall of the Medici, burned hot and focused. She needed to know the truth, not through Tito, whose insights into the machinery of state often softened the edges of reality, but for herself.

This resolve led her to the Duomo that morning. She had heard Fra Girolamo preach. While the sermon's fiery rhetoric and denunciations of sin had initially struck her like distant thunder, a strange, piercing emotion had seized her when Savonarola invoked martyrdom. She had wept, feeling a resonance with a spiritual fervor that transcended her daily life, akin to hearing actual vibrations of music rather than a mere memory of sound. Yet, that emotion, powerful as it was, lay outside the sanctuary of her domestic world. As Tito returned, she suppressed the impulse to discuss it, fearing it might isolate her from him.

Waiting for hours, her heart pounding, she finally heard the great door of the courtyard creak open. Hastening to the stone steps, she caught sight of him ascending the staircase. Eighteen months had altered her face, softening the lines, lending it a pleading, beseeching quality that made her appear infinitely more lovely than on the day of their wedding. Tito, too, seemed changed, his features more defined, his demeanor heavier.

"My Tito, you are tired," she greeted him gently, noting the fatigue etched around his eyes. "A fatiguing day?"

Maso followed them into the ante-chamber, and soon they were alone in the library. The fire crackled warmly in the great dogs, a welcome sight to the weary traveler. Tito sat down heavily, throwing aside his cap, and shivered.

"Romola, I implore you," he said, his voice lacking its usual lilt, "stop sitting in this library. Our rooms are warmer, pleasanter."

Romola felt a sharp pang of hurt. He had never been so indifferent, so cold. She watched him anxiously, searching for an explanation in the shadows of his expression. "You know I am cataloguing the manuscripts on the new plan Father wished for," she reminded him softly. "You have no time to assist, so I must work closely."

Instead of engaging, Tito closed his eyes, rubbing his hands over his face and hair. He felt a terrible alienation rising within him, a repulsion toward the mind of the woman he loved, though he could not name it. He was vexed with himself for behaving so poorly, unable to summon the affectionate warmth he desired. "I am not well, Romola," he muttered, "do not be surprised if I am peevish."

Kneeling beside him, Romola laid her arm across his chest, smoothing back his hair. Suddenly, she recoiled with a start, her gaze sharpening with alarm. "What is that under your tunic, Tito? It feels as hard as iron."

"It is iron," he admitted quietly, revealing the chain-mail armor he wore beneath his clothes. "I bought it unexpectedly. I must wear it constantly for some time."

"What threatens you?" Romola clutched his arm, her voice trembling. "Did you encounter danger today?"

"Everyone is threatened unless they are enemies of the Medici," Tito said, attempting a smile to break the tension. "My armor protects me from covert attacks."

"But my godfather," Romola insisted, "he is in far greater danger than you. Why does he take no precautions?"

"It is because I am of lesser importance," Tito explained, his smile fading. "Men like Bernardo are shielded by wealth and influence. I am a Greek, unavenged by anyone, and constantly suspected of being an envoy."

Romola shrank from the thought of a specific threat, dreading the degradation of such a fear in her husband. "Have you received special threats?" she whispered.

"Yes," Tito replied firmly, cutting short her inquiry. "Do not mention it to your godfather, or you will have broken my confidence."

"I will not," Romola promised, her face flushing with shame. "But it makes you so wretched, Tito. Seeing you in this heavy shell... it seems like a story of enchantment, as if a malignant fiend has hardened your skin."

"And you would rather see me exposed to peril?" Tito teased gently, trying to restore the thread of their intimacy. "If you do not mind my being stabbed, why should I mind? I will give up the armor."

"No, Tito, no!" she cried, shaking her head. "I am merely fanciful. Do not listen to me. Are crimes like this becoming common in Florence? My father and Godfather always said otherwise."

"They will become frequent," Tito said grimly, "bred by bitter hatreds."

Silence fell between them again. Romola, unwilling to press the subject further, forced a cheerful tone. "Tell me, what happened today? Did everything go well?"

"Tremendously well," Tito laughed, the sound hollow. "First, the rain ruined Luca Corsini's tedious oration. Then, a quick-witted speaker gave the French King the briefest possible welcome in broken French. After that, a storm of gifts, jewels, and trappings poured in. There was strutting, prancing, confusion, and shouting. The King smiled from ear to ear. I was at the Tornabuonis; there was feasting and playing. I shall tell you all about it tomorrow."

"But is there any hope?" Romola asked, her face falling. "Will Florence avoid fresh trouble? Will the Republic remain peaceful?"

Tito shrugged. "Florence

Four days after the tumultuous arrival of the French, Romola set forth toward the dwelling of Piero di Cosimo on Via Gualfonda. The journey took her through arteries of the city thick with tension. On either side of the street, crowds gathered: French soldiers, arrogant and newly arrived, stared down at the citizens of Florence, while Florentines returned the gaze with a mixture of resentment and wary defiance. There was no warmth here, no mutual admiration. The invaders, unaccustomed to seeing themselves as anything less than the apex of European power, wore their superiority like armor. Yet the Florentines, having initially courted these "Most Christian" guests with feigned hospitality, now found their patience wearing thin. They resented the condescension, the "ogling" of their womenfolk, and the underlying message that Florence was merely a prize to be won.

Beneath the surface of polite ceremony lay a cold calculation. King Louis, having entered the city with lance in rest, spoke openly of leaving a viceroy behind and restoring the Medici family to power. To the Florentines, this was not the act of a humble servant of God, but of a conqueror treating a free city as a spoil. Though the official offense of Florence was limited to the unpopular policies of Piero de' Medici, the spirit of the republic refused to bow. This resolve was evident in the clandestine councils within the Old Palace and now manifested openly on the broad banners waving above the piazzas, mocking the insolence of the French. For a woman of high birth, the streets were treacherous terrain; yet Romola, veiled in deep black and accompanied by the faithful old Maso, moved with a quiet security.

Her destination was driven by a pressing need. A copy of her father's portrait, painted by Piero in the guise of Oedipus, remained unfinished. Piero was a man of erratic temperament; his dedication to a commission wavered with his whims. Sometimes, left without urgent pressure, he would abandon a canvas for months, hiding it away in a corner or chest until it seemed lost forever. Though Romola was a favorite of his and he often indulged her desires, she knew that affection alone could not guard against his capricious nature. Having promised completion weeks prior, he had likely forgotten the pledge. Romola's anxiety stemmed from a profound desire to preserve the memory of her blind father. She feared that without a physical likeness, his image would fade from her mind, leaving a void in her devotion. Love, she believed, required perfect loyalty; it could not rest content unless it achieved a completeness that included the faithful keeping of promises.

By special favor, she was permitted to enter Piero's studio without prior warning. Lifting the iron slide, she called out in a soft, flute-like voice, recalling the manner of the little maid who once summoned Titus. The response was immediate. When the door swung open, Piero explained his haste with a bluntness that offered no comfort.

"Ah, Madonna Romola, is it you?" he exclaimed, stepping back. "I thought my eggs were coming; I wanted them."

"I bring you something superior to hard-boiled eggs, Piero," Romola replied with a smile, removing her veil. From Maso, she retrieved a small basket brimming with cakes and *confetti*. "Maso has secured this for you."

Stepping past him into the interior, she continued gently, "I know you appreciate such treats when they arrive without trouble. Confess, do you?"

"Yes," Piero admitted, folding his arms and gazing down at the sweets as she displayed them. "They come to me as readily as the light does." His eyes then rose to meet hers, filled with a painter's acute appreciation as her heavy hood, pulled back by the weight of her veil, revealed her hair. "And they have arrived with the light today."

"But I understand why you are waiting for them," he observed, a knowing glint in his eye. "To keep your tongue from chiding me. Very well, step into the next chamber. I have altered the picture since you last saw it, though it remains incomplete. Be warned, however; I take great care never to make a promise I cannot keep."

"'Who promises and does not perform / Never sends his soul to heaven,'" he quoted, reciting the Tuscan proverb with a theatrical flair.

Beyond the threshold, the studio opened onto a wild, overgrown garden. Piero stood before an easel, working. However, he was not touching the portrait of Oedipus commissioned by Romola. That canvas stood propped against the far wall, neglected. As he reached to lift it, intending to move it into better light, he inadvertently obscured it and revealed another work hidden beneath—a fresh oil sketch of Tito.

Within recent days, Piero had added a startling detail to this smaller study. Because it hung recessed from the larger composition, the change had gone unnoticed until now. As he shifted the canvas to examine a specific area, the new element was fully exposed. Romola gasped, flushing with shock.

"That is Tito!"

Piero spun around, offering a silent shrug of vexation at his own lapse in organization. He was troubled by his forgetfulness.

Romola studied the sketch, her brow furrowed in puzzlement and alarm. "What a bizarre image! When did you paint this? What signifies it?"

"A mere whim," Piero said, removing his skull-cap and scratching the back of his neck with a gesture designed to mask deeper emotion. "I required a handsome countenance for this piece, and your husband's features were ideal."

Moving closer to the canvas, he bent down, presenting the back of the painting to Romola, and feigned a casual inspection before deciding to store it away as unworthy of display. Yet Romola, haunted by the memory of the chain-mail armor, struck by a terrifying coincidence linking Tito to images of dread, stepped forward.

"Do not hide it! Let me view it again. The figure with the rope around his neck... I saw him. I saw you approach him in the Duomo. What compelled you to include him alongside Tito?"

Seeing no path to evasion, Piero yielded a fraction of the truth. "It was pure chance. The man was fleeing, scrambling up the steps, and seized hold of your husband. He must have tripped. I happened to be present, saw the spectacle, and deemed the ragged old fellow a suitable subject. It is worthless, truly; merely a freakish daub." With an air of final dismissal, he pushed the sketch onto a high shelf. "Come, view the Oedipus."

In doing so, he had betrayed his caution. His eagerness to conceal the sketch had confirmed her deepest fears: there was indeed something unsettling, something detrimental to Tito's reputation, inherent in the scene's genesis. Stung by pride and delicacy, Romola recoiled from further inquiry. To question further might imply a suspicion she dared not harbor, lest it tarnish her husband's honor. In a voice barely above a whisper, she asked, "He was a strangely pitiful prisoner. Know you anything else of him?"

"No further knowledge," Piero replied quickly. "I merely guided him to the hospital. Observe now; the face of Oedipus is nearly complete. Tell me your thoughts."

Romola devoted herself entirely to the portrait, standing in a prolonged silence before it. Finally, she spoke. "Ah, you have accomplished what I desired. You have bestowed upon it the look of listening. My dear Piero..." Her eyes shimmered with gratitude. "...I am deeply thankful."

"Now there lies what I cannot endure in women," Piero muttered, turning impatiently and sweeping aside the clutter on the floor. "You pour forth emotions where none are needed. Why should you be grateful to me for a painting you paid for, especially when I have delayed it? If I paint, surely it is for my own pleasure and credit to execute it well, is it not? Must you thank a man for not being a rogue or a fool? 'Tis sufficient if he thanks Messer Domeneddio for granting him that distinction. Women imagine walls are held together with honey."

"You crusty Piero! I forgot how irritable you are," Romola laughed softly through her tears. Reaching into the basket, she offered him a particularly crisp sweetmeat.

Piero accepted it with the grace of a proverbial bear dreaming of pears; he genuinely relished the gift, though accustomed to masking his joys and sorrows behind a shaggy exterior. "It is excellent, Madonna Antigone," he remarked, reaching for another. He had subsisted solely on hard eggs for two weeks. Opposite him, Romola felt her mounting anxiety suspend momentarily, buoyed by the sight of this simple, naive delight.

"Adieu, Piero," she said eventually, placing the basket down. "I promise not to express thanks if you finalize the portrait swiftly and masterfully. I shall tell you then that you were bound to succeed for your own sake."

"Prithee," Piero said curtly, assisting her with practiced dexterity to wrap her mantle and veil securely around her form.

As soon as the door clicked shut behind her, a sigh escaped him. "Thank goodness she asked no further questions regarding that sketch," he murmured to himself. "I should be ill at ease if she surmised I considered her fine husband a fitting model for a coward. I played it light; she will not broach the matter again."

Yet Piero proved too sanguine, a common failing of open-hearted men attempting subtle deception. As Romola walked home, the image of the sketch pressed upon her

"The old fellow has vanished," Nello observed, his tone casual yet tinged with curiosity, "went on towards Arezzo the next morning. I suspect he couldn't stand the stench of the French after being their prisoner. I stopped by the hospital to ask if those monkish cooks had discovered whether he'd lost his wits. They said he was perfectly sane, merely aloof, staring at a vineyard twenty miles away as if it grew on his wall. He was a mysterious old tiger. I've always wished to know more about him."

These words were spoken in Nello's shop on the twenty-fourth of November, a mere week after the French entry. The air hummed with anticipation; Florence braced itself for a decisive political shift. Every public square teemed with idlers, and every shopkeeper without a deputy stood guard at his threshold, thumbs hooked in belts, watching the street like cats waiting for a mouse. Artisans drifted past like fallen leaves, ready to bolt at the slightest stir.

Inside, Nello strummed a lute, seated casually on a wooden board near the window, his gaze fixed on the piazza.

"Ah," he declared, setting down his instrument with a flourish, "I would not trade the sight of those French soldiers lumbering in their broad boots after their fleeing prisoners for a gold florin. It happens whenever I leave this central hub to shave some magnificently chin-furnished gentleman. If I step out, chaos ensues in my piazza."

"You should have been there," Piero di Cosimo interjected, his voice sharp as a scalpel, "to witness your favorite Greek trembling as if Satanasso had seized him by the throat. I delight in seeing your perpetually smiling Messeri stripped bare by a sudden gale. Consider the color of a man's liver when he resembles a bleached deer the moment a stranger grabs him."

"Piero, save your vinegar for your own omelets!" Nello retorted warmly. "What fault does my *bel erudito* bear for starting when he felt claws upon him and saw a raving madman beside him? Your scholar is not like those brutish Swiss and Germans, whose minds are built for battering-rams and stomachs big enough to swallow a cannonball before dawn. We Florentines value far more than that crude notion of bravery, which amounts to hiring dullards by the dozen. Once men realized they possessed more brains than oxen, they yoked the beasts to pull for them; and when we Florentines discovered our superior intellects, we employed them to fight for us."

"Treason, Nello!" a voice cried from within the shop, where Francesco Cei leaned forward eagerly. "That contradicts the State's doctrine! Florence is forging weapons, and the last authenticated vision from the Friar depicted Mars standing atop the Palazzo Vecchio, resting his hand on Saint John the Baptist's shoulder, who offered him honeycomb."

"It is well, Francesco," Nello replied smoothly. "Florence possesses several thick-skulled individuals capable of bombarding Pisa, while the finer spirits remain at home to think and shave. As for our Piero, if he prizes valor so highly, let him take his largest brush as a spear and his palette as a shield, challenging the widest-mouthed Swiss he encounters in the Prato to single combat."

"*Va*, Nello," Piero grumbled, "your tongue rolls like a mill driven by a swollen Arno, regardless of whether there is grain to grind."

"Plentiful grain, I assure thee," Nello countered. "It would be as absurd to expect a weather-beaten painter like thee to crave a javelin thrust as it is to anticipate a man whose wit has been honed by classical studies enjoying his handsome visage torn by a wild beast."

"There you go again, assuming folks will march into sacks because you label them 'hosen'," Piero shot back. "Who mentioned a wild beast or an unarmed man charging into battle? Fighting is a trade, not mine. I might avoid danger, but I could face it if it sought me out."

"Why do you fear the thunder, then, my Piero?" Nello pressed, eager to corner the accuser. "One understands why a man trembles at trifles that shake others less—you hide with the rats the moment a storm brews."

"That stems from a specific sensitivity to loud noises; it reflects neither my courage nor my conscience."

"And Tito Melema may share a peculiar sensitivity to being seized unexpectedly by prisoners escaping French guards. Men are born with aversions; I cannot abide the scent of mint. Tito was born with an antipathy toward elderly prisoners who stumble and clutch. *Ecco!*"

A ripple of laughter swept through the room, revealing that Piero's dislike for Tito held no sway among his companions. The painter, contorting his features into an unreadable grimace, stuffed tow into his ears in a gesture of indignant disdain, while Nello continued triumphantly.

"No, my Piero, I cannot afford to disparage my *bel erudito*; nor can Florence, with her scholars shedding feathers at the tender age of forty. Our Phoenix Pico ascended straight to Paradise, as the Friar informs us; and the incomparable Poliziano departed but two months ago—well, let us hope he joins the eminent scholars of the Malebolge rather than descending into hell."

"Intriguingly," interjected Francesco Cei, "have you heard that Camilla Rucellai has surpassed the Friar in prophecy? Two years ago she foretold Pico would die in the season of lilies. He passed in November. 'Not the time of lilies,' scoffed the critics. 'Go to!' replies Camilla; 'I meant the lilies of France, and lo, they lie close beneath your noses.' I say, *Euge, Camilla!* If the Friar can verify that any vision of his has been fulfilled with such precision, I shall declare myself a Piagnone tomorrow."

"You are somewhat too flippant regarding the Friar, Francesco," observed Pietro Cennini, the scholar. "In these turbulent weeks, we are all indebted to him for preaching peace and quelling factional strife. Those who wish to see the populace slip the Friar's leash now possess little discernment. Should the Most Christian King stubbornly refuse to sign a fair and honorable treaty today, Fra Girolamo is the man we must trust to bring him to reason."

"You speak truly, Messer Pietro," Nello agreed. "The Friar stands as one of Florence's firmest anchors—at least, according to the most respectable chins I have the honor of shaving. Yet, young Messer Niccolò remarked not long ago, and perhaps Francesco implies the same, that meanings in visions stretch as magically as Dido's bull-hide. To me, a dream signifies whatever follows it. As Franco Sacchetti noted, a woman dreams of a serpent biting her, smashes a drinking cup the next day, and cries, 'Lo! I thought something would happen—it is plain now what the serpent meant.'"

"But the Friar's visions differ from that sort," corrected Cronaca. "He does not merely predict what will occur—that the Church will be scourged and renewed, and the heathens converted—but declares it shall happen swiftly. He is no slippery charmer crafting loopholes; he is—"

"What is this? What is this?" Nello shouted, leaping from his perch and thrusting his head through the doorway. "People are flooding the piazza, screaming. Some catastrophe must have unfolded in the Via Larga."

"Aha!" Nello burst out, stepping outside laughing and waving his cap. Delight surged through him.

The entire company rushed to the door. News from the Via Larga was exactly what they awaited, though the manner of its arrival left them stunned. Seated above the shoulders of the crowd on a bench seemingly plucked from the street sat Tito Melema, smiling with amused detachment at the coercion he endured. His cap had slid off, dangling by the *becchetto* loosely wound around his neck. Upon spotting the group at Nello's door, he raised his fingers in beckoning recognition. Within a heartbeat, he leaped from the bench onto a cart laden with bales stationed in the open space between the Baptistery and the Duomo's steps, while the populace swarmed around him with the noisy fervor of chickens awaiting feed.

Yet, silence fell instantly when he began to speak in his clear, mellifluous voice.

"Citizens of Florence! I possess no warrant to proclaim news save your own will. But the tidings are good and shall harm none in recounting them. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty honorable to Florence. Yet you owe this boon to one of your citizens, who uttered a phrase worthy of ancient Rome—you owe it to Piero Capponi!"

Instantly, a roar of voices erupted. "Capponi! Capponi! What did our Piero say?" "Ah! He refused to be sent from Herod to Pilate!" "We knew Piero!" "*Orsù*! Tell us, what did he say?"

As the clamor subsided slightly, Tito resumed.

"The Most Christian King demanded a trifle too much—was obstinate—and finally declared, 'I shall order my trumpets to sound.' Then, Florentine citizens! Your Piero Capponi, speaking with the voice of a free city, proclaimed, 'If you sound your trumpets, we will ring our bells!' He snatched the parchment detailing dishonorable conditions from the secretary's hand, tore it into fragments, and turned to depart the royal presence."

The Avenger's Secret.

For the first time since his escape, Baldassarre stood within the Piazza del Duomo. A fierce longing burned within him to hear the monk preach again, yet he recoiled from returning to the very ground where he had appeared half-naked, with matted hair and a rope around his throat, labeled a madman. The sting of those words—"surely some madman"—spoken by Tito, remained fresh and venomous. It was not merely their cruelty that wounded him, but the sudden, bitter realization that he might indeed be powerless to prove them false. Alongside his burning desire for vengeance, a cold, calculating fear had taken root: the suspicion that his ability to exact that revenge was precarious. It felt as though Tito had been guided by a diabolical whisper, revealing Baldassarre's deepest shame to the traitor. He was not mad, for he retained the terrible, lucid awareness of his shattered faculties; he measured his own feebleness with a grim precision. Yet, as the first surge of vindictive rage coursed through him, a primal caution followed, akin to a wounded beast or an insect pausing in a tremor of distrust. This wariness, this refusal to take any step that might betray his condition, was precisely why he had spurned Piero di Cosimo's friendly overtures.

He had practiced similar caution at the hospital, answering the brothers' inquiries only with the lie that he had been captured by the French on his journey from Genoa. However, his age and the peculiar cadence of his speech, suggesting a background far removed from the ordinary mendicants sheltered there, prompted the monks to extend extraordinary charity. They gifted him a coarse woolen tunic against the chill, a pair of peasant shoes, and a handful of *danari*, the smallest Florentine coin, to aid his travels. Early the next morning, he began the road toward Arezzo. But at the first small town, he halted and spent two of his coins at a barber's shop. He demanded to be shaved and have his tangled hair cut short, restoring it to its former style. The barber produced a small hand-mirror of polished steel. It had been years since Baldassarre had gazed into glass, and as his eyes met the reflection, a jarring thought pierced his mind. "Is he so altered that Tito truly does not know me?" The thought arrested his impulsive currents with a painful shock; his hand trembled like a leaf as he reached to retrieve the mirror from the barber's grip. He needed to see himself before the transformation was complete.

The barber, noting his trembling, held the glass steady.

"No," Baldassarre whispered, the word heavy with resignation. "I am not so changed." He recognized the deepening lines etched into his brow, the same rough, leathery skin marked by the ravages of time, appearing now like a withered rind. The shaggy white beard offered no disguise to eyes that had studied him closely for sixteen years, eyes that should have searched for him with the fervor of a lover seeking a lost kin among the dead. There was a difference in his gaze, yes—but it was a difference that should have made recognition all the more startling. A known voice is always more chilling when heard as a cry. Doubt was a luxury he could not afford; he felt certain Tito knew him. Pushing the mirror away, he forced the stagnant waters of his spirit to rush anew, reigniting the engines of hatred and vengeance.

Turning back toward Florence, he avoided the city until dusk. Seeking solitude, he diverted from the highroad and sat by a tranquil pool shaded by alders still clinging to yellow leaves. It was a calm November afternoon, and the still surface of the water beckoned as a potential mirror. He sat on the bank, leaning forward to scrutinize his reflection with an intensity he had lacked courage to muster before the barber.

Was there a madness lingering in his eyes? Something akin to the confusion he often felt within?

Now, under the scrutiny of his own gaze, his expression was one of intense purpose. Yet, what of the long, lonely hours when the fog returned? When he sat in a dark void, haunted by whispers that mocked his efforts to catch them, and visions that drifted away the moment he reached for them? In those moments, his countenance must bear the mark of continual frustration and bewilderment. Even worse, when the clouds briefly parted, offering a glimpse of hope, only to roll back together and leave him helpless as before—then his face must have shown a blank, terrifying confusion, as if struck blind.

"Can I prove anything?" he asked the silence, his heart heavy. "Can I even begin to speak with the confidence that my thoughts will not unravel?" Would anyone believe that a mind once filled with rare knowledge, busy with profound contemplation, and eloquent in speech, had been reduced to this? That vast store of understanding had slipped away, like water poured from an urn into the boundless sea? Or did it remain, trapped behind some internal barrier, waiting for a crack to burst free?

He clung desperately to the latter hope. Since the day he first limped from his straw cot, feeling a new, suffocating darkness beneath the sun, his mind had undergone a tumultuous shift. Some changes were gradual, others fleeting. As his physical strength returned, so did his self-mastery and the energy of his will. He had reclaimed the memories of his life intimately woven with emotion, yet the gnawing sensation of lost knowledge plagued him constantly. But occasionally, when strongly agitated, he seemed to momentarily inhabit his former self entirely, as an old man might briefly recall the vigor of his youth. He saw Greek texts clearly again, felt his mind move freely among familiar concepts. It was but a flash, and the encroaching darkness made it seem more horrific, but could it not return and stay long enough for him to execute his revenge? To devise a torment so exquisite that a mere arm could never inflict it?

Rising from his hunched posture, Baldassarre folded his arms and focused every ounce of his remaining mental fortitude onto the plan he must immediately adopt. He needed time for knowledge and opportunity, and during that wait, he required a means of survival without begging. His greatest dread was being perceived as a foolish, helpless senile old man. No one must know that half his memory was gone; the lost strength might return, and even a brief resurgence could suffice.

He knew how to acquire the information regarding Tito. The name "Bratti Ferravecchi" had become etched into his mind through constant repetition. A man in Genoa, having seen Tito's ring, had informed Baldassarre that the jewel had been purchased in Florence from a young Greek, well-dressed and dark-complexioned, at the shop of a rag-and-bone dealer named Bratti Ferravecchi, located in the street also known as Ferravecchi. This revelation sent a violent shockwave through Baldassarre. Until then, he had clung tenaciously to his faith in Tito, refusing to believe he had been willfully abandoned. Initially, he had reasoned, "My piece of parchment has not reached him; hence I toil still at Antioch. But he searches; he knows where I was lost; he will trace me." Later, at Corinth, he had convinced his captors to secure insurance against any inquiries regarding his fate at Antioch, thinking joyfully, "Here, at last, he must find me." But before a year had passed, illness had seized him, leaving him so shattered that he was deemed worthless save for the ransom that never materialized. Sitting helpless in the morning light, supporting his head with blank eyes and lips moving uncertainly, he had looked so much like a hopeless imbecile that his owners gladly released him to a Genoese merchant who showed compassion for an Italian soul.

Months later, aboard a galley traversing the Aegean and the coast of Asia Minor, Baldassarre regained his physical strength, yet upon landing in Genoa, he was consumed by a weariness so profound he wished he had died at Corinth. Only one possibility staved off suicide: that Tito lived, perhaps in imprisonment or destitution. If Tito lived, there remained a faint, deferred hope that Baldassarre might find his son again, clasp his hands, and meet his face-to-face with the one being who remembered him as he had been before his mind broke. It was in this fragile state of feeling that he encountered the stranger wearing Tito's onyx ring. Though Baldassarre could not describe it beforehand, the sight of it awakened dormant fibers, and he recognized it instantly. The fact that Tito, nearly a year after their separation, lived in apparent prosperity in Florence, selling a gem he ought not to have parted with until the last extremity, was a reality Baldassarre shrank from comprehending. He preferred to be stunned by it rather than to analyze it distinctly, focusing instead on the joy of seeing eyes that remembered him. "At least I am not alone in the world," he murmured. "And I shall never be alone, for my revenge walks beside me."

With the resolve of an avenger, he bent down to examine his reflection in the pool again, viewing himself not with the tenderness due a forsaken elder, but with the hard curiosity of an instrument serving a higher master. Human passion, whether low or high, reaches a point where it ceases to be purely egoistic, becoming a fire within the being to which everything else is merely fuel.

"I was a foolish lover," he recited silently, his voice echoing in the hollow of his chest. "I worshipped a woman, believing she could care for me. Then I took a helpless

"My dear Romola," Tito began the following morning, two days since his impassioned appeal in the Piazza del Duomo, "grand company awaits me today. The Milanese Count returns, accompanied by the Seneschal de Beaucaire, the favorite confidant of the King of France. I suspect you hold little relish for the hollow rituals required by such glittering magnates, figures we shall scarcely behold again. Since they intend to inspect our antiquities and the library, perhaps you should suspend your labors and accompany me to visit Cousin Brigida?"

A subtle hope flickered within Romola's gaze at these words, and she nodded immediately. Yet, moments later, returning draped in her hood and mantle, she observed, "Ah, what a mighty exhalation Florence shall let loose when the gates swing wide and the final French footfall vanishes beyond them! Even you, my Tito, wear the shadow of fatigue beneath your patience. Confess it now. Indeed, your brow burns with fever."

Tito leaned forward over his desk, his quill scratching steadily across parchment. She reached out to rest her palm upon his forehead, intending a tender farewell gesture—a movement so habitual that he typically raised his chin, reclined slightly, and gazed upward at her with unguarded warmth. But now, though her hand hovered near his hair, he remained immovable, as if encased in a helmet of cold iron. Instead of lifting his gaze, he maintained his composure, speaking lightly as the ink flowed.

"The French depart from Florence with the swiftness of wasps abandoning a ripened pear the moment they have tasted its sweetness."

Sensing the void where his customary affection usually bloomed, Romola withdrew her hand and declared, "I am departing, Tito."

"Farewell, my sweet one. I remain to await the visitors. Take Maso with you."

Yet Tito did not lift his eyes from the page. Romola left the chamber without further word. In the quiet rhythm of matrimony, the faintest tremor can mark an epoch; for the first time, she acknowledged internally not merely that Tito had altered, but that his alteration extended specifically toward her. Was the fault hers? Perhaps, if not for the tangible evidence of the armor and the painting, which whispered of some external catastrophe shrouded in mystery.

As soon as Tito was certain she had crossed the threshold, he set down his pen and lifted his gaze, seeking solace in the solitude of his papers and fragments of marble. A sting of shame pricked him; he regretted his inability to summon his usual gentleness, to look upon her as before. If circumstances allowed, he would have surpassed his former kindness. Yet, he could not instantly conquer a visceral recoil, a shrinking born of those same qualities that drove him to cherish her. Looming before him lay a path fraught with grievance, requiring him to endure harsh confrontations to secure her pardon. Tito, by nature, abhorred defiance or insolence; every inclination bent toward preserving her tenderness. Though free from the paralyzing scruples of sentiment—having swiftly disproven their lack of practical utility—the absence of such scruples did not shield him from the agony of offense. Unscrupulousness dispels many troubles, yet it cannot cure toothache, nor heal wounded vanity, nor banish the crushing loneliness that plagues the world unless countered by robust health and a righteous, loving soul. At this moment, Tito felt acutely that no strategy could spare him the pain of the inevitable collision with Romola. No softening of manner could buffer the blow toward which he drifted, urged on by the terror of the approaching storm.

A secret conviction had gnawed at him for weeks: his stubborn adherence to Bardo's wishes regarding the library, under current duress, resembled a sentimental folly, robbing both himself and Romola of vital benefits. Had not the events of the past week forged a new urgency, coupled with a rare opening, this realization might have remained dormant. Only after witnessing Baldassarre regain his sanity, and only when he feared flight from Florence, did he resolve to exercise his legal right to sell the library before the chance of sale to the French and Milanese bidders slipped away. For if exile became necessary, he refused to depart as a beggar. Accustomed to a life of ease, he sought to carry with him the means to sustain it. Above all, he wished to carry Romola, and above all, he wished to carry no stain of infamy. Prosperity had whetted his appetite for the pleasures attendant upon elevated station; the notion of fleeing under disgrace, even laden with jewels, appeared to him not as temptation, but as a grotesque alternative. He could not render himself independent of those Florentines who treated him with mere courtesy, nor, certainly, of Romola. She was the spouse of his first love, and he still adored her; she was integral to his existence, a piece of furniture from which he shrank to part. He winced under her judgment, unsure how far her feelings had recoiled. While the absolute sway a husband holds over a wife often tempts betrayal that a lover dares not attempt, it proved insufficient to quell Tito's profound unease. This heavy stone, too burdensome for his will, prevented him from raising his head to meet her eyes. Her radiant countenance brought the prospect of struggle too sharply into focus. Yet, resistance was futile; if they were to leave Florence, funds were essential. Indeed, Tito could not construct a life satisfying to his mind without a considerable sum, and the obsession with arranging his existence to his satisfaction had been the root of all his transgressions. He would have faced any sacrifice, provided it lacked unpleasantness.

The distinguished guests arrived and departed; the negotiations concluded. That evening, however, no serious matters were broached. Tito displayed a buoyancy and chatterfulness unprecedented in recent memory, recounting tales and descriptions of the French visit to Romola. She perceived an effort in his liveliness, interpreting it as a penitent awareness of the wound he had inflicted that morning, and inwardly ached at the growing distance between them—the presence of an unspoken offense.

The following day, Tito remained absent until late hour. To Romola, it marked a significant day; Piero di Cosimo, spurred by the fear that his actions might have caused her sorrow in the past week, had dispatched her father's portrait. She propped it against the back of his ancient chair and gazed upon it for some time when the door opened behind her, revealing Bernardo del Nero.

"It is you, godfather!" Romola exclaimed, hastening toward him. "How I wish you had arrived sooner! Twilight is already descending."

"I stopped briefly to bring you tidings, knowing Tito had not yet returned," Bernardo replied. "The French King departs tomorrow; indeed, 'tis high time. Another clash occurred this morn between our citizens and his soldiers. Yet, there is now hope that the city regains order and trade resumes."

"That brings joy," Romola said. "But it seems precipitous. Tito seemed to believe yesterday the King would linger for some time."

"He has been thoroughly harried, hence the haste," Bernardo smiled. "His own generals quarreled fiercely, and finally, our Signoria unleashed the city's mastiff, Brother Girolamo. The Cristianissimo, startled by that thunderclap, issued orders to move. I fear discord may follow his departure, but at least all parties rejoice at ending Florence's suffocation under military occupation. The Friar barked with effect this time." He paused, then asked, "And what is this?" As Romola, clutching his arm, guided him before the portrait, he continued.

"Do you not require your spectacles, godfather?" Romola asked anxiously, wishing him to see exactly what she beheld.

"No, child, no," Bernardo answered, removing his cap and seating himself with dignified upright posture. "At this distance, perhaps my aged eyes surpass yours. Old eyes resemble old memories; they are sharpest for things distant in time."

"It is preferable to having no likeness at all," Romola offered apologetically, after a brief silence. "It resembles him less than the vision I hold in my mind, yet that image may fade with years." Resting her arm upon his shoulder, drawn strongly by their shared reverence for the dead, she spoke.

"I am not sure," Bernardo mused. "I almost perceive Bardo as he was in youth, more clearly than this canvas depicts him in age. Your father possessed a great fire in his eyes when young. What I could never fathom was how a spirit so fiery, seemingly more impatient than my own, could devote itself to books and dwell among shadows for a lifetime. Nevertheless, he poured his heart into it."

Bernardo shrugged slightly, but Romola heard a resonance in his voice that mirrored her own.

"And he was disappointed unto the end," she murmured involuntarily. Instantly fearing her words might sound like an accusation against Tito, she hurried on, "If we could but witness his longest, dearest wish realized precisely to his mind!"

"Then so we shall," Bernardo said kindly, rising and donning his cap. "Times are clouded now, yet fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? Should the wheel turn sufficiently, I may yet serve as Gonfaloniere before I breathe my last; and no creditor shall touch these treasures." He glanced around the room, then turned to her, patting her cheek. "And you need not fear my demise; my ghost claims naught. I have arranged for that in my will."

Romola seized the hand resting on her cheek and pressed it

The following dawn, Romola shared the general fervor of the Florentines regarding the departure of the French army. Yet, beneath this public joy, a private, half-superstitious hope stirred within her. She clung to the faint belief that the heavy anxieties concerning Tito, which had arrived alongside the foreign guests, might dissipate with them. Though the French had lingered for barely eleven days, Romola recalled that period as one of profound, acute unhappiness unknown to her prior life. Since their arrival, Tito had donned a hateful armor of caution; though she could not logically discern why their exit should erase the root of his fear—and though the vision of the prisoner seizing him, as depicted in Piero's sketch, haunted her whenever she considered the cause—nevertheless, she reasoned that with the soldiers gone, she would finally be free of something so tightly bound to her sorrow.

Enveloped in her mantle, she stood upon the upper loggia, gazing toward the bridges where the troops and the royal retinue were passing en route to the Porta San Piero, facing Siena and Rome. Even after the gates had fallen shut, she returned to her post, eager to vibrate with the resonance of the great bell-peal. By the time dusk settled and she descended into the library, she lit her lamp with a stern resolve to conquer the agitation that had rendered her idle all day, intending to resume her copying of the catalogue. Tito had departed early, and she planned to leave the study once he returned, seeking refuge in the charming salon adorned with dancing nymphs and birds—a retreat he had demanded since finding the library too chilly and somber.

However, to her astonishment, Tito entered before she had undertaken any work. A surge of concern crossed her mind; how bleak the vast darkness must seem to him, with a solitary oil lamp flickering at the far end and the fire nearly extinguished. She hastened toward him.

"Tito, dearest, I knew not you would return so soon," she exclaimed, nervously raising her white arms to loosen the *becchetto* around his neck.

"Am I then unwelcome?" he asked, offering one of his brightest smiles, though he playfully held his head back from her embrace.

"Tito!" she cried, the word tinged with pretty, loving reproach. Then, he kissed her tenderly, stroking her hair as was his custom, seemingly unmindful of removing his mantle. Romola quivered with delight. All the turmoil of the day had sharpened her senses to the return of this habitual tenderness. "It shall return," she murmured to herself, "the ancient happiness will perhaps return. He is like himself again."

Yet Tito was laboring intensely to appear as his former self; his heart beat violently with anxiety.

"If I had anticipated your return so swiftly," Romola said, helping him disrobe, "I would have staged a small festival for this joyful tolling of the bells. I did not intend to remain in this library when you arrived."

"Nay, never mind, sweet," he replied lightly. "Cease to worry about the fire. Come, sit down."

A low stool stood beside his chair, Romola's customary seat during their conversations. Resting her arm upon his knee, as she had done upon her father's lap, she looked up at him. He had thus far failed to notice the portrait hanging nearby, and she refrained from mentioning it, contemplating it with growing intensity.

"I have savored the clamor of the bells for the first time, Tito," she began. "I delighted in being shaken and deafened by them; I imagined myself a Bacchant possessed by divine fury. Do not the people look exceedingly joyful tonight?"

"Joyful, indeed, in a sour and pious fashion," Tito observed, shrugging. "Truth be told, those remaining in Florence have scant cause for jubilation. The most sensible grounds for gladness would surely be a flight from the city."

Tito had struck the desired note effortlessly, without apparent premeditation. He spoke without emphasis, yet his gravity compelled Romola to ask anxiously, "Why, Tito? Are there fresh troubles?"

"No occasion for fresh woes, my Romola. Three powerful factions lie within the city, poised to tear each other apart. Were the Frate's party strong enough to silence the others—which seems probable—life would become as pleasant and amusing as a funeral. They already simmer with plans for a Great Council; whoever sings the sacred Lauds the loudest shall be deemed most eligible for office. Furthermore, the city, drained by this immense subsidy to the King of France and the war to recover Pisa, faces a dismal prospect without the tyranny of fanatics. In sum, Florence shall be a delightful haunt for those who entertain themselves by visiting crypts and flagellating themselves; for all else, the exiles fare best. For my part, I have seriously contemplated that it would be prudent to depart Florence, my Romola."

She started. "Tito, how could we leave Florence? Surely you do not think I could abandon it—at least, not yet, not for a long while." Her voice grew cold and trembled; she found speech difficult. Tito must perceive the reasons swirling in her mind.

"That is entirely a fabric of your own imagination, my sweet one. Your secluded existence has led you to overvalue a few trifles. Remember, before our marriage, I told you I wished to reside somewhere other than Florence. Had you seen more lands and more people, you would understand what I mean when I say that something in the Florentines resembles their biting spring winds. I prefer people who take life less eagerly; and it would benefit my Romola greatly to witness a new life. I should like to bathe her briefly in the soft waters of forgetfulness."

He leaned forward, kissing her brow and laying his hand upon her fair hair again. Yet, she felt his caress no more than if he had kissed a mask. Agitated by the chasm widening between their minds, she remained oblivious to the contact of his lips.

"Tito, it is not because I imagine Florence to be the most agreeable place in the world that I refuse to quit it. It is because I... because we must fulfill my father's wish. My godfather is aged; he is seventy-one. We cannot forsake him."

"It is precisely those superstitions, clinging to your mind like obscuring clouds, that constitute a major reason for my desire to locate us two hundred leagues distant from Florence. I am compelled to guard you against your own will. If those dear eyes, so tender in appearance, deceive themselves, I must see for them, saving my wife from wasting her life in impracticable dreams."

Romola sat silent and immovable. She could not blind herself to the direction of Tito's words: he sought to persuade her that the library could be deposited in a monastery or otherwise disposed of, severing their tie to Florence. She resolved never to submit her mind to his judgment on this matter of filial duty; inwardly, she prepared to endure any pain required for resistance. However, this determination remained latent in these initial moments, crushed by the heart-rending realization that she and Tito were now openly divided in their desires. He welcomed her silence; despite his fears regarding the strength of her feeling, it was impossible for a man confined to the narrowness of mere intellect, devoid of deep passion, not to overestimate the persuasiveness of his own arguments. His conduct appeared harmless to himself, and his imagination failed to reveal exactly how it would appear to Romola. Continuing in the same gentle, remonstrative tone, he pressed on.

"You know, dearest—you whose clear judgment has always been evident to you—that the notion of isolating a collection of books and antiquities, attaching a single name to them forever, offers no valid, substantial good for its object: and, moreover, is liable to be thwarted in a thousand ways. Witness the Medici collections! For my part, I deem it even blameworthy to harbor such petty notions of appropriation. Why should anyone reasonably rejoice that Florence possesses the fruits of learned research and taste more than any other city? I comprehend your sentiment regarding the wishes of the dead; but wisdom imposes limits upon such sentiments, lest lives be endlessly squandered in futile devotion—much like praising deaf gods eternally. You devoted your life to your father while he lived; why should you demand more of yourself?"

"Because it was a trust," Romola said, her voice low but distinct. "He trusted me, he trusted you, Tito. I did not anticipate you would feel otherwise about it—or feel differently—but I did expect you to acknowledge that."

"Yes, dearest, naturally I would feel it where your father's genuine welfare or happiness was concerned; but there is no question of that now. If we believed in purgatory, I should be as anxious as you to have masses said; and if I believed it could now pain your father to see his library preserved and utilized in a manner differing from his intent, I should share the rigidity of your views. But a little philosophy ought to teach us to liberate ourselves from those air-woven fetters mortals bind upon themselves, spending their lives in misery under the mere illusion of weight. Your mind, which seizes ideas so readily, my Romola, is capable of distinguishing between substantial good and these brain-forged fantasies. Ask yourself, dearest, what possible good these books and antiquities can achieve, stowed together under your father's name in Florence, compared to what they would accomplish if dispersed or transported elsewhere? Nay, is not the scattering of such treasures among hands that懂得 how to value them a means of extending their utility

Baldassarre wandered through the labyrinthine streets of Florence, seeking the cheapest shelter available. His feet, weary from travel, were drawn inevitably toward the uneven terrain outside the Via de' Bardi, known locally as the Hill of San Giorgio. Here, the ground sloped downward, offering a vantage point that pierced the city's roofline to reveal the distant hills and valleys usually hidden from view. Bratti had mentioned that Tito resided nearby, but for Baldassarre, the promise of a quiet corner atop the hill outweighed any specific address. The narrow, claustrophobic alleys of the city had intensified his sense of isolation and fractured memory; he craved the open sky.

As he climbed, he spotted a patch of jagged, uncultivated earth scarred by a landslide from the late thirteenth century. Rising from this broken ground stood a peculiar, truncated tower-like building with fluted tiles, adjacent to a small outhouse wedged against a ruin. Beneath the shade of a dying mulberry tree, an ancient, stooped woman untied a goat with two kids. A portion of the outbuilding housed livestock, while the other remained vacant save for scattered straw and tools. It was exactly what he needed.

Approaching the old woman, Baldassarre realized immediately that hearing was not her strong suit. Despite his shouts, she offered only muffled denials—"No lodging"—shaking her head with the patience of the deaf. Undeterred, Baldassarre persisted, explaining his status as a destitute foreigner who could not afford hostels and merely sought a bed of straw for the night, promising a few pennies weekly. After a moment of dubious contemplation, the woman produced a hatchet and pointed to a log half-buried under debris. "Chop that up," she instructed, "and you may sleep in the shed for one night."

Baldassarre agreed and fell to work. As he chopped, a figure watched from within the upper floor. Through a shutter closed tight against the chill, a small, round aperture revealed the eye of a child. This was Tessa. Perched precariously on a wobbly stool, she strained to catch every movement. Though forbidden to form acquaintances by her guardians, the allure of the stranger—the possibility of sharing a secret, of tasting freedom from her rigid routine—proved too powerful. She recalled her mother's warnings: *Obey, or your happiness turns to lead.* Yet, obedience paled before the thrill of a transgression. With a heart pounding with illicit joy, she descended the stairs, dressed in her finest green serge gown adorned with a red sash and silver pins, clutching a single gold ring that marked her preparation for a feast.

Descending with a lantern and a wooden bowl filled with macaroni flavored with cheese, Tessa kicked gently at the outhouse door. Inside, Baldassarre, lost in melancholy reverie, blinked in surprise at the intrusion. Before he could protest, Tessa burst forth, mimicking the deaf woman's volume. "I've brought you supper!" she cried, placing the bowl on his lap and positioning herself beside him. "Eat while I stay!"

Her assumption that he was deaf was charming in its innocence. Baldassarre, starving and moved by her earnest demeanor, accepted the food. As he ate, Tessa, forgetting her initial hesitation, leaned close, whispering conspiratorially, "Do you like it?" When he confirmed he heard her perfectly, her delight knew no bounds. She launched into a stream of chatter about her life: the kindness of Monna Lisa, the freedom from labor, the goats, and the mules she missed. Then, emboldened by his silence, she unveiled a heavy secret. "I have a little baby," she confessed, her voice dropping to a dramatic hush. "Born at Christmas. I'm married, but you must not tell anyone!"

Baldassarre, a man of the world, found her revelation both absurd and poignant. He guessed her husband's identity based on her terror, suspecting the notorious stepfather Nofri. Tessa erupted in laughter at his mistake, clapping her hands. "No, indeed! But I must not tell you what he is—he is not like Nofri!" She spoke of her husband with a mixture of reverence and longing, praying for his return, and described her infant with a tenderness that contrasted sharply with the grim reality of her household. She promised to bring the child the following day.

Before dawn, Tessa returned to the house, recounting her adventure to Monna Lisa. The old woman feigned reluctance, citing the displeasure of Messer Naldo, the master of the estate, yet eventually conceded. "If I let him stay and carry burdens for me, you must keep silent," she grumbled. Tessa nodded vigorously, declaring she would confide only in her "bimbo" (baby). They speculated on Naldo's whereabouts, fearing his return but hoping he remained absent until Baldassarre departed.

The next morning, Tessa rose with renewed industry, eager to share her "precious" gift. However, Monna Lisa had already tasked Baldassarre with fetching water, disrupting Tessa's plans. She hurried down the hill, insisting he sit on the straw to enjoy the sun and broth she had prepared, refusing further labor. Soon, she returned carrying the infant swaddled in cloth.

"This is my baby," she announced, seating herself beside Baldassarre. "Look how lively it is! Like the little Gesu." She held the bundle out, inviting him to touch it, though forbidding a kiss. "You might hurt him." Baldassarre, whose voice carried a strange, underlying threat beneath its apparent gentleness, took the child. "Poor thing!" he murmured, his gaze piercing. To him, this guileless girl appeared less as a friend and more as a creature trapped against a cruel world, someone who would require vengeance.

"Oh, do not pity me," Tessa protested, reclaiming the baby. "My husband is beautiful and good." When Baldassarre asked for his name, paralyzed by the weight of the question and the desire to remain anonymous, Tessa simply shrugged. "It matters not," she said kindly. "You are a stranger. Come back whenever you wish; we will be good to you."

As she departed, leaving the lantern aglow in the twilight, Baldassarre stared after her. To him, she was a fleeting rainbow against a storm-blackened sky, beautiful yet elusive, offering warmth to a man drowning in sorrow, unaware that her innocent curiosity had just entangled him in a web far deeper than mere hospitality.

No Place for Repentance.

Messer Naldo returned sooner than anyone anticipated, arriving on the evening of the twenty-eighth of November, merely eleven days after his previous visit. This swift return indicated he had not ventured far beyond the mountains. A scene previously witnessed in the Via de' Bardi helps illuminate the impelling force that drew his steps toward the hill of San Giorgio.

When Tito first secured this dwelling for Tessa upon his return from Rome, more than eighteen months prior, he convinced himself he acted solely under the constraint of his innate kindness following the unfortunate incident that led poor Tessa to mistake him for her husband. While it was undeniable that his benevolence was directed toward a pretty, trusting creature whom one could not approach without an urge to caress, it was equally true that Tito harbored feelings of kindness toward her independent of any calculated gain. Indeed, charming as her beauty and chatter were in idle moments, he might have preferred freedom from her company; for he was not in love with Tessa. For the first time in his life, he was consumed by an entirely different woman, whose mere presence vibrated through his hours. Every spark of youthful ideal passion he possessed had been ignited by Romola. His fiber was too refined, his intellect too bright, to succumb to the habits of a coarse pleasure-seeker. Yet, he had spun a web around himself and Tessa that he felt powerless to break. In the initial moments following the mock marriage, he had been driven to leave her under an illusion by a distinct calculation of his future needs. However, since that critical juncture, the web appeared to spin itself autonomously, a growth over which he held no sway. The elements of kindness and self-indulgence were indistinguishable in a soft nature like Tito's. The annoyance he had felt regarding Tessa's pursuit on the day of his betrothal, coupled with his sincere intention to reveal the truth, served as sufficient proof that his ultimate decision to maintain her deception and provide her a home stemmed from overwhelming compassion. In these days of his first devoted attachment to Romola, he required a self-justifying narrative. He had learned to find solace in the fact that she was deceived about certain matters. Yet, every profound feeling constructs its own conscience, possesses its own piety; just as the bond between a son and mother may survive amidst the foulest stench of depravity, so Tito could not yet rest easy committing a secret offense against his wedded love.

Consequently, he exercised extreme caution in preserving the secrecy of this transgression. Monna Lisa, who, like many of her station, rarely left her confines except for specific shops or annual confession, knew nothing of his true identity or whereabouts. She merely knew that he compensated her handsomely to ensure her comfort and cared little for the details beyond her duties, save that she had grown fond of Tessa and derived satisfaction from the cares for which she was paid. An air of mystery clearly enveloped the situation, given that Tessa was a peasant girl (*contadina*) while Messer Naldo was a gentleman. To Monna Lisa, however, he might easily have been a real husband. Tito had thoroughly terrified Tessa into silence regarding the circumstances of their union, warning her that breaking that silence meant never seeing him again. Furthermore, Monna Lisa's deafness rendered it impossible to convey information without premeditation, thus saving Tessa from any accidental revelations, unlike her careless confidences to Baldassarre. For a long time, Tito's visits were so infrequent that it appeared he traveled extensively between them. These journeys were primarily motivated by a desire to verify that all was well with Tessa. Although he invariably found his visits more pleasant than the anticipation of them—always rediscovering the charm of that pretty, ignorant, loving trust—he had not yet developed a pressing need for them. Nevertheless, he was resolved, if possible, to preserve the simplicity upon which this charm depended. He intended to keep Tessa a genuine *contadina*, refusing to place the wildflower among conditions that would strip it of its grace. He would have been appalled to see her dressed in attire inappropriate to her rank; the zest of her speech would vanish if new relations entered her life, if her world expanded, and if her pleasures lost their childish innocence. Her squirrel-like enjoyment of nuts at leisure defined the standard of the luxuries he provided. Through this strategy, Tito preserved Tessa's charm from being sullied, and simultaneously, by a convenient coincidence, spared himself from exacerbating expenses that were already becoming burdensome to a man whose funds were wholly dedicated to his acknowledged lifestyle.

Briefly, this had been the history of Tito's relationship with Tessa until a very recent date. It is true that on occasion, prior to Bardo's death, the knowledge that Tessa resided up the hill, offering a chance for an agreeable hour, had induced him to escape the slight weariness of caring for the old man. Had there been no positive engagement, he might otherwise have endured the fatigue patiently and shared Romola's burden. However, the moment when he first felt a genuine hunger for Tessa's ignorant lovingness and blind faith in *him* had arrived only quite recently, distinctly marked by circumstances as memorable as the onset of a disease that permanently vitiated sight and hearing. It was the day he first encountered Baldassarre and purchased the armor. Returning across the bridge that night, clutching the coat of mail, he felt an unconquerable dread of an immediate encounter with Romola. Like her, she knew little of the actual world; like her, she trusted him. Yet, he possessed an uneasy consciousness that behind her frank eyes lay a nature capable of judgment, and that any misplaced trust of hers sprang not from a brutish inability to discern, but from a nobility that might prove an alarming touchstone. He desired a measure of ease, a repose from self-control, after the agitation and exertion of the day. He sought a place where he could adjust his mind to the morrow without concern for his current behavior. And there existed a sweet, adoring creature within reach whose presence was as safe and unconstraining as that of her own kids—one who would believe any fable and remain utterly unimpressed by public opinion. Thus, on that evening, when Romola waited and listened for him, he turned his steps up the hill.

No wonder, therefore, that the steps followed the same course on this evening, eleven days later, after he had recoiled under Romola's first outburst of scorn. He could not wish Tessa in his wife's place, nor refrain from wishing that his wife be thoroughly reconciled to him; for it was Romola, not Tessa, who belonged to the realm where the larger desires of an ambitious man with effective faculties must inevitably reside. Still, he sought refuge from a standard disagreeably rigorous, from which he could not liberate himself merely by dismissing it as folly; and Tessa's small soul was that inviting refuge.

It was scarcely past eight o'clock when he ascended the stone steps to the door of Tessa's chamber. Usually, she heard his entry and rushed to greet him, but not tonight. Upon opening the door, he discovered the cause. A single dim light burned above the dying fire, illuminating Tessa in a kneeling posture by the head of the bed where the infant lay. Her head had slumped onto the pillow, and her brown rosary, which usually hung above the pillow over the picture of the Madonna and the golden palm branches, lay loosely grasped in her right hand. She had fallen sound asleep over her beads. Tito stepped lightly across the small room and sat down close beside her. She had likely heard the opening of the door as part of her dream, for she had not been looking at him two moments before she opened her eyes. She opened them without a start, remaining quite motionless as she gazed at him, as if the awareness that he smiled at her suppressed any impulse to disturb that happy passivity. Yet, when he placed his hand beneath her chin and stooped to kiss her, she declared—

"I dreamed it, and then I said it was a dream—and then I awoke, and it became true."

"Little sinner!" said Tito, pinching her chin, "you have not recited half your prayers. I shall punish you by refusing to look at your baby; it is ugly."

Tessa disliked those words, even though Tito was smiling. A pouting distress marred her countenance as she said, bending anxiously over the child—

"Oh, it is not true! He is prettier than anything. You do not think he is ugly. You will look at him. He is even prettier than when you saw him before—only he is asleep, and you cannot see his eyes or his tongue, and I cannot show you his hair—and it grows—isn't that wonderful? Look at him! It is true his face is very much the same when he sleeps; there is not so much to see as when he is awake. If you kiss him very gently, he will not wake: you want to kiss him, is it not true?"

He soothed her by granting the small bundle a butterfly kiss, then placing his hand on her shoulder and turning her face toward him, he remarked, "You prefer looking at the baby to looking at your husband, you false one!"

She remained kneeling, resting her hands on his knee and gazing up at him like one of Fra Lippo Lippi's round-cheeked adoring angels.

"No," she said, shaking her head; "I love you always best, only I want you to look at the bambino and love him; I used only to want you to love me."

"And did you expect me to return so soon?"

For several days, Tito saw little of Romola. When he returned the next morning, his voice was gentle, suggesting that it would be prudent for her to clear away her few personal effects from the library before the agents arrived to pack up the antiquities. Leaning forward, he kissed her brow and advised her to retreat to her chamber, where the small painted tabernacle stood, so she might escape the clamor of unfamiliar footsteps. She assented quietly, offering no outward sign of emotion. To Tito, the night seemed a single, interminable vigil. Though her frame remained strong, sensation had dulled into a continuous, heavy ache, as if her spirit had been stunned and bruised. He perceived that she was ill, yet he dared speak no words of comfort. Only when he noticed her hand and brow turned ice-cold did he dare move, fetching a fur-lined mantle and draping it softly over her shoulders. In every brief interval between his returns, the scene repeated itself: he strove to soothe her through silent acts or soft words, while she appeared to have lost the capacity to speak to him or even look upon him.

"Patience!" he murmured to himself. "She will recover, and forgive at last. The bond between us must endure." When the struck person lingers in silence and feigns nothing occurred, the striker often drifts effortlessly into the role of the aggrieved party; he feels no wound himself and becomes acutely aware of his own benevolent conduct despite having delivered the blow. Yet Tito was not naturally inclined toward grievance. His nature bent constantly toward reconciliation, and he would have endured much just to feel Romola's hand rest once more upon his head, as it had that morning when he first recoiled from gazing at her.

He found it easier to wait patiently for the restoration of his domestic peace because his life beyond the walls grew ever more captivating. There exists a course of action that is, in truth, the gradual unfolding of a whole character, yet it usually traces back to a single, decisive impression as its apparent origin. Since that moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito, seated upon bales, had tasted a sharp delight in knowing he could please the ears of men with any phrase he chose, his imagination had drifted continuously toward a form of political activity that Florence's turbulent times surely demanded. However, a fresh terror of Baldassarre, awakened simultaneously, had lain like an immovable rock blocking that path, driving him to sell the library as a precaution against fleeing Florence—a step he now found unnecessary. That dread had nearly vanished. He must still wear his armor, prepare himself for tests of coolness and wit, but he was no longer compelled to abandon his home for distant fortunes. His father had rejected the offered atonement, forcing Tito into open defiance; an aged man, stripped of memory, in a foreign land, was too weak to withstand such opposition.
