Now, Tito's latent desires began to manifest as explicit thoughts. As the freshness of youthful passion faded, life increasingly took on the appearance of a game for him, blending skill and chance with equal measure. The game available in Florence promised to be swift and thrilling—a struggle of revolution and parties, brimming with unspoken actions where brilliant ingenuity, discarding all inconvenient beliefs save that "ginger is hot in the mouth," discerns the path of superior wisdom.

Scarcely had the French guests departed when Florence erupted into agitation, akin to a colony of ants disturbed by the removal of a shadow, scrambling to repair their encampment. "How shall we raise funds for the King of France?" they asked. "How shall we handle the stubborn Pisan rebels?" "Above all, how shall we reform our governance to ensure magistrates are chosen and laws voted upon sound principles?" Until these questions found answers, trade risked stalling, and the vast body of workers, excluded from citizenship and denied the vote, threatened to grow restless. Something had to be done.

First, the great bell tolled, summoning the citizens to a parliament in the Piazza de' Signori. When the crowd was packed tight, hemmed in by armed guards at every exit, the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight Priors—emerged and stood before the stone lion on the platform of the Old Palace. They proposed that twenty leading men of the city be granted dictatorial authority for one year, empowered to select all magistrates and reorganize the state framework. The people shouted their assent, feeling themselves the electors of these Twenty. This form of "parliament" was an ancient Florentine custom, designed to make the will of the few appear as the choice of the many.

The shouting in the square ceased quickly, but the debates within the palace raged on. Should Florence adopt a Great Council in the Venetian style, where all officials and laws were elected by a broad body of qualified citizens, ignoring rank and party? Or should it follow a narrower, less popular scheme, preserving the hereditary influence of noble houses from being diluted by the votes of merchants? Lawyers argued day after day, far into the night. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini presented compelling arguments for the popular model; Messer Guidantonio Vespucci countered with equally valid reasons for a more aristocratic form. It was a debate over whether to boil or roast meat, a matter prejudged by the disputants' palates; their eloquence might have continued endlessly without deciding anything other than delaying the meal. The majority inside the palace, already holding power, sided with Vespucci, believing change should be moderate. Conversely, the majority outside, aware of their powerlessness and deep grievances, feared nothing so greatly as change.

Yet, a force existed outside the palace, slowly turning the vague wishes of that outer majority into a determined will. That force was the preaching of Savonarola. Driven both by a spiritual compulsion to guide the people and by the urgent needs of politicians who could pass no measures without his support, he rapidly shifted in his daily sermons from general exhortations to specific directives. He moved from urging listeners to subdue private passions for the public good to declaring exactly what sort of government they must possess to achieve it. He progressed from "Choose whatever is best for all" to "Choose the Great Council," and finally, "The Great Council is the Will of God."

To Savonarola, these propositions were indistinguishable. The Great Council was the only viable means to express a public will large enough to counter the corrupting influence of partisan self-interest; it was a plan that could render honest, impartial public service at least possible. The purer Florence's government became, the safer it would be from the schemes of men who profited from the moral decay of others, and the closer the Florentines would approach the character of a pure community, fit to lead the renovation of the Church and the world. Fra Girolamo's mind never rested until it reached that sublime end; the objects toward which he worked always possessed the same moral grandeur. He harbored no private malice and sought no petty gratification. Even in the final, terrible days, when shame, torture, and the dread thereof exposed every hidden flaw in his soul, he could tell his relentless judges: "Do not wonder if it seems to you that I have told but few things; for my purposes were few and great."

More than three weeks passed before the final contents of the library were packed and removed. Instead of closing herself off to the world, Romola observed the entire process with a vigilance born of grief. Exhaustion following violent emotion often breeds a dreamlike denial of reality's causes; yet, as the workmen departed each evening, Romola lit her handlamp and wandered slowly through the chaos of straw and wooden crates. She paused at every empty pedestal, every familiar object laid low, driven by a bitter, desperate need to confirm that there was a sufficient reason for her love having vanished and the world becoming barren. For several nights in succession, she returned to this ritual. It was no longer merely to assuage herself, but because this vivification of pain and despair surrounding her father's memory remained the sole vital thread in her affections. By the twenty-third of December, she knew the last packages were departing. She rushed to the loggia at the summit of the house, unwilling to miss the final, crushing pang of watching the slow wheels traverse the bridge.

The sky was overcast, approaching dusk. The Arno flowed dark and trembling; the hills wore a mournful expression. Florence, encircled by its stone towers, possessed a silent, sepulchral aspect, cast by unbroken shadows upon the city viewed from above. Santa Croce, where her father lay interred, was shrouded in that same gloom. Slowly creeping across the bridge and ascending the narrow street, the white cargo moved like a cruel, deliberate fate, carrying her father's lifelong hope to be buried in an unmarked grave. Romola felt less as though she were witnessing this event herself than as if her father, helpless beneath the imprisoning stones, were conscious of it, unable to reach out her hand to tell him that he was not alone.

She stood motionless even after the load had vanished, indifferent to the biting cold, soothed by the gloom that seemed to cloak her like a mourning vestment, silencing the discord of joy. Suddenly, the great bell in the palace tower rang out a mighty peal—not the hammer-clash of alarm, but an agitated clamor of triumph. One by one, every other bell in every other tower caught the vibration and joined the chorus. As the swelling crescendo filled the air until it seemed composed of sound alone, little flames, vibrating as if ignited by the noise, burst forth between the turrets of the palace and along the girdling towers.

That sudden clanging, those leaping lights struck Romola like sharp wounds. They were the triumph of demons celebrating the success of her husband's treachery and the desolation of her life. Scarcely three weeks prior, she had been intoxicated by the ringing of those very bells; in the gladness of Florence, she had heard a prophecy of her own joy. Now, however, the general jubilation appeared cruel to her. She stood aloof from common life—from that Florence shouting its loud exultation to deafen the ears of sorrow and loneliness. She could never again join hands with gladness, save with those whom the hard nature of joy inevitably forgot. In her bitterness, she felt that all rejoicing was mockery. Men chanted paeans with souls heavy with melancholy, then scanned their neighbors' faces to verify whether joy truly existed. Romola had lost her faith in the happiness she once thirsted for; it was a hateful, smiling, soft-handed entity, possessing a narrow, selfish heart.

She fled down from the loggia, pressing her hands against her ears, and hurried across the antechamber when she was startled by an unexpected encounter with her husband, who came seeking her. His step was elastic, and a radiance of satisfaction, unusual in him, illuminated his countenance. "What! The noise was a trifle too much for you?" he asked, noticing how closely she held her hands to her ears. He took her gently by the wrist, drew her arm within his, and led her into the salon adorned with dancing nymphs and fauns. Continuing to speak, he declared, "Florence has gone quite mad with acquiring its Great Council, which promises to terminate all evils under the sun, particularly the vice of merriment. You may well appear stunned, my Romola, and you are chilled. You must not remain so late under that windy loggia without wrappings. I was coming to inform you that I am suddenly summoned to Rome regarding some learned business for Bernardo Rucellai. I depart immediately, joining my party at San Gaggio tonight, so we may start early in the morning. I need give you no trouble; my packages are already prepared. It will not be long before I return."

He knew he could expect nothing from her but quiet endurance. He dared not venture to kiss her brow that evening, contenting himself with pressing her hand to his lips before leaving. Tito sensed that Romola was a more unforgiving woman than he had imagined; her love was not that sweet, clinging instinct, stronger than all judgment, which constituted the great charm of a wife. Yet, this petrified coldness seemed preferable to a passionate, futile opposition. Her pride and her capacity to recognize where resistance was useless offered a certain convenience.

But when the door closed behind Tito, Romola shed the look of cold immobility that descended upon her like an inevitable frost whenever he approached. Internally, she was far from a state of quiet endurance. The days passing since the scene that divided her from Tito had been days of active planning and preparation for the fulfillment of a purpose.

The first task she undertook was summoning old Maso. "Maso," she said, her tone resolute, "we embark on our journey tomorrow morning. We shall now be able to overtake that first convoy of cloth while they await at San Piero. Attend to the two mules tonight, be ready to set off with them at dawn, and wait for me at Trespiano."

She intended to travel with Maso as far as Bologna, then send him back bearing letters to her godfather and Tito, informing them that she had departed and never intended to return. She had devised her flight so thoroughly that its secrecy would be absolute, her broken love and shattered life concealed from vulgar scrutiny. Bernardo del Nero had been absent at his villa, eager to evade political suspicions by returning to his favorite occupation of tending his lands, and she had settled his debt without a personal audience. He remained ignorant that the library had been sold, left to conjecture that some sudden stroke of fortune had enabled Tito to raise such a sum. Maso had been included in her confidence only to the extent that he knew her journey was a secret; doing precisely what she commanded was the paramount desire of his withered, wintry age.

Romola did not intend to sleep that night. Once the door was fastened, she took her taper to the carved and painted chest containing her wedding garments. The white silk and gold lay there, along with the long white veil and the pearl circlet. A great sob rose within her as she beheld them; they seemed the shroud of her dead happiness. In a tiny gold loop of the circlet, a sugar-plum had lodged—a pink hailstone from a shower of sweets. Tito had detected it first and remarked that it should forever remain there. At certain moments—and this was one of them—Romola was swept, by a sudden wave of memory, back into the era of perfect trust. She felt again the presence of the husband whose love rendered the world as fresh and wonderful to her as to a child sitting in stillness among sunny flowers; she heard the gentle tones, saw the soft eyes devoid of deceit, and breathed again that vast freedom of the soul that arises from faith in the being nearest to oneself, greater than oneself. In those fleeting instants, tears invariably rose: the woman's lovingness felt something akin to what a bereaved mother experiences when tiny fingers seem to lie warm upon her bosom, yet feel like marble to her lips as she leans over the silent bed.

However, something else lay in the chest beside the wedding clothes: it was something dark and coarse, rolled tightly in a bundle. Turning her eyes away from the white and gold to the dark bundle, her tears began to subside as her hands touched the serge. That coarse roughness anchored her fully in the present, from which love and delight had fled. She unfastened the thick white cord and spread the bundle upon the table. It was the grey serge dress of a sister belonging to the Third Order of Saint Francis, living in the world but devoted chiefly to deeds of piety—a figure the Florentines customarily termed a *Pinzochera*. Romola intended to don this dress as a disguise and resolved to wear it immediately, ensuring that if she required sleep before morning, she would awaken in perfect readiness to depart. She removed her black garment, and as she thrust her soft white arms into the harsh sleeves of the serge mantle and felt the hard girdle of rope cut into her fingers as she tied it, she welcomed those rude sensations. They accorded with her newfound scorn for that thing called pleasure, which rendered men base—that dexterous contrivance for selfish ease, that shrinking from endurance and strain when others bowed beneath burdens too heavy for them, a burden which now united her with her husband. Then she gathered her long hair, drew it tightly away from her face, bound it in a great, hard knot at the nape of her neck, and took a square piece of black silk, tying it in the fashion of a kerchief close across her forehead and under her chin; over that, she drew the cowl. Lifting the candle to the mirror, she assessed her transformation. Surely her disguise would be complete to anyone who had not lived intimately near her. To herself,

The Tabernacle Unlocked.

A sharp rap at the door broke Romola's slumber. The room was already flooded with the cold, hard light of dawn, and Maso stood waiting for her travelling-wallet. When she unbarred the door, the old man started visibly; the vision he had anticipated—the graceful figure crowned with living gold of hair—had vanished, replaced by the heavy folds of a grey mantle and a pale, shadowed countenance hidden beneath a dark cowl.

"It is well, Maso," Romola said, striving for a voice of absolute calm to steady his nerves. "The wallet is packed. Go on quietly, and I shall not be far behind. Once you have crossed the gates, you may walk slower; I doubt not I shall catch up to you before you reach Trespiano."

She let him pass and drew the bolt home. Then, her hand sought the key resting beside her bed—the original lock of the little painted tabernacle. Tito had failed to drown it in the river, and it had remained tucked away in the corner of his embroidered sash, worn over his purple tunic. Years ago, Romola had discovered it there, keeping it close without use, finding a quiet comfort in knowing it was within reach. Now, she turned to the cabinet where the shrine stood, pushed near the window where the morning sun illuminated the faded colors. She gazed at the figures with a fresh, bitter revulsion: the triumphant Bacchus, clutching the crowned Ariadne amidst clusters of grapes and vines; the Loves scattering roses over a wreathed vessel; the cunning-eyed dolphins in the rippling sea. All framed by a flowery border, mimicking a paradise garden. Yet here, on this chilling morning, they offered no sanctuary, only a pitiable mockery. *Foolish Ariadne,* she thought, *how could she believe that gaze held the secret of her life?* "She looks strangely transformed now," Romola murmured. "She would look ill at ease among these vines."

She picked up the mirror, staring at her reflection in the stark daylight. The image struck her with such jarring force that she lowered it, feeling a shrinking akin to her earlier flight from the joyous painting. To see her own face, veiled and hooded, terrified her anew with the dread of being dragged into the fold of some wretched superstition—to become part of the howling fanatics and weeping nuns whom she had despised since childhood. With a tremor in her hands, she inserted the key, unlocked the shrine, and withdrew the crucifix without looking closely at it. She threaded a cord through its ring, fastened it around her neck, and buried it deep within the bosom of her mantle. *"For Dino's sake,"* she whispered to the empty room.

Still, the tasks remained. The letters Maso was to carry back to Bologna needed writing. They were brief, yet final. For Tito, she penned: "Tito, my love for you is dead; and therefore, so far as I was yours, I too am dead. Do not try to enforce any laws to fetch me back; it would bring you no happiness. The Romola you married can never return. I need say nothing further after what I told you when we last spoke. If you thought those words born of transient anger, know now that they mark an irreversible change. I request that my bridal chest be sent to my godfather, who gave it me. It holds my wedding clothes and portraits of my parents." She folded the ring inside the parchment, addressing Tito's name to the outside.

For Bernardo del Nero, her "second father," she wrote: "Dearest Godfather, if staying could have served your life, I would not have fled. But now I am gone. Ask not the reason, and if you love my father, hinder anyone from seeking me. I could not endure life in Florence, nor can I bear to tell why. Cover my fate in silence. I have requested that my bridal chest be sent to you; its contents will reveal the truth. Please entrust all things belonging to my mother to my cousin Brigida, and beg her to forgive me for failing to bid her farewell. Farewell, my second father. The best gift I possess remains the memory of your kindness."

With the letters and the crucifix secured in her mantle, Romola felt the weight of departure lift. No soul stirred in the house. Descending the stairs and stepping onto the silent street, she moved with the ghostliness of a grey phantom. Though her heart hammered violently, she relished the sensation of her firm tread on the broad paving stones—the swift motion of a resolution finally unleashed. The urgent drive to complete this act, coupled with the fear of obstacles, drowned out sorrow. As she approached the Ponte Rubaconte, the church of Santa Croce seemed less a looming presence in her view than the widening yellow streak of dawn cutting through the grey mist. Unless she hastened, she risked meeting eyes.

Her direct route led straight to the Borgo Pinti, then along the walls to the Porta San Gallo, passing through the Piazza di Santa Croce. Yet she walked with relentless speed, refusing to glance toward the church. The thought that curious eyes might fix upon her, or indifferent minds speculate on her private ruin, caused her to recoil as from physical torture. She felt degraded by the act of her husband from which she suffered so deeply. Yet, no windows watched her; no eyes noticed the tall grey sister moving with the firm step and proud, hooded attitude of a nun. Her path skirted the early traffic, and when she neared the gate, a dispute over tolls for egg baskets created a perfect distraction for her exit.

Beyond the houses of the Borgo, she was free from the last fringe of Florence. Above her, the sky stretched wide and open. She had entered a new existence—one of loneliness and endurance, yes, but of freedom. She had been strong enough to sever the bonds accepted in blind faith. Whatever came next, she would never again feel the warmth of hated lips or the stifling breath of an odious mind. The biting winter air, the bare, sombre hills, were welcome in their severity; they held no ghosts of beauty or joy, gods she had forever renounced.

Suddenly, however, the light surged with fierce intensity, casting long, shifting shadows across the road. It seemed the sun intended to banish the gloom. Perhaps the divine presence is never felt more acutely than in moments when light instantly animates the shadows. A profound awe, inherent to this momentous act, grew in Romola as she stood in the sudden radiance of golden glory and confronted the long, shrouded shadow of herself that was henceforth unescapable. So far, she had encountered only occasional country folk with mules, and the winding road had kept Maso's location obscure. But having passed Pietra and climbed onto rising ground, she raised the brim of her cowl to peer ahead.

Instantly, she lowered it again. Before her stood not Maso, but two monks advancing within a few yards. The hood had acted as a penthouse over her brow, blinding her to anything above eye level; thus, for the last moments, she had stared only at the gleam of the path and her own tall, spectral shadow.

Regret seized her. Monks were particularly unwelcome in her guise; they might demand pious passwords unknown to her. She forced a mask of unconscious awareness until the black hems of their mantles swept past. The encounter made her pulse race unpleasantly. There lay a gnawing unease in her religious disguise, a shame at this studied concealment, sharpened by the effort to remain invisible despite their likely scrutiny.

Yet the monks, walking downhill, would soon vanish. Seeing a large flat stone leaning against a cypress tree atop a grassy embankment, the slight shock of the meeting spurred her to sit and rest. Turning her back on Florence, she resolved not to look back until the monks were lost from sight. Lifting the edge of her cowl slightly, she spotted Maso and his mules in the distance, a gap not insurmountable, for the old man would surely linger, expecting her. For now, she paused. Free and alone, she breathed the crisp air.

The Awakening of Vengeance.

Tito Melema's journey to Rome, a move that smoothed the path for Romola's eventual departure, had been decided upon with startling abruptness at a supper held merely the previous evening. Tito had approached that gathering with high spirits, anticipating delicate meats, choice wines, and a circle of distinguished company. The venue was the renowned Orto de' Rucellai, the Rucellai Gardens, hosted by Bernardo Rucellai, a paragon of Florentine grandeur. His very name bore a symbolic weight, derived from *roccella*, a lichen of the Greek islands and Canaries that, when soaked in sunlight, yields a vivid purple dye known as *oricello*. Just as this humble plant, through the labor of merchants centuries prior, had transformed into a source of immense wealth for the Rucellai line, so too had Bernardo elevated his station. He had wedded a sister of Lorenzo de' Medici, served as an ambassador to France and Venice, and held the office of Gonfaloniere. A patron of the arts and learning, he had adorned the façade of Santa Maria Novella with black and white marble, cultivated gardens of rare trees, and hosted the Platonic Academy following Lorenzo's death. A writer of Latin and antiquarian lore, Bernardo stood as a monument to human potential, a figure so virtuous that nature herself might despair of creating another.

Lorenzo Tornabuoni had delivered Bernardo's invitation to Tito, though with a gravity that hinted at serious political machinations beneath the guise of social entertainment. To Tito, however, the gathering promised the furtherance of party aims through the medium of exquisite flavors and aged Greek wine. Aware that Bernardo was one of the Elect Twenty currently steering Florence, Tito felt assured of the event's importance. Yet, he harbored a lingering doubt regarding the intellectual caliber of the assembly. Having attended various somber dinners in the Rucellai grounds before, particularly those dominated by tedious philosophical discourses where guests were expected to swallow entire cosmologies, Tito feared the company might be both dull and pedantic.

It was a dark night when Tito traversed the streets, his form becoming visible only when he stepped from the shadow of one lantern into the flickering light of another. To any observer watching closely, the tall, graceful figure cloaked in a mantle was invariably accompanied by a stark contrast: a short, elderly man clad in a rough serge tunic and a felt hat. Their conjunction might easily be dismissed as coincidence, for the streets were crowded. However, when Tito halted at the side gate of the Rucellai gardens, his companion ceased moving as well. A servant, distracted by a prior errand, held the small portal open. Tito slipped inside quickly, offering his name, and vanished into the torch-lit thicket of evergreens. His follower entered immediately behind.

"Your name?" the servant asked, turning slightly.

"Baldassarre Calvo," the old man replied instantly.

"You are not a guest; all guests have passed."

"I attend Messer Tito, who has just entered. I shall wait in the gardens."

The servant hesitated, glancing around. "I received orders to admit guests only. Are you a servant of Messer Tito?"

"No, friend, I am not a servant. I am a scholar."

There is a specific cadence of voice, a tone of quiet conviction, that commands respect even from the most suspicious. The porter yielded immediately, allowing Baldassarre to step into the garden, where the heavy iron door clanged shut and locked behind him, sealing him among the shimmering bushes. Those firm, rapid answers marked a profound transformation in Baldassarre since their last encounter, a meeting that had ended with a shattered dagger.

That metamorphosis had begun in the aftermath of Tito's departure from Baldassarre's humble dwelling. At that moment, Baldassarre sat paralyzed by the aftershocks of a violent emotional outburst—a state where physical weakness is often paired with a crystalline clarity of thought. As if the dissipating storm of passion had swept away the mist, leaving a pristine sky, his mind was suddenly lucid. His limbs felt numb, his body cold, his hands trembling, yet he was not lost in darkness. Instead, the vague shadows of his usual existence had been replaced by the sharp, unyielding images of the past. He relived a life that had felt like a long, bitter preparation for the present moment.

For several minutes, he was so consumed by these visions that he forgot his surroundings. Then, the clarity extended outward, anchoring him firmly in the reality of the street outside. He recalled Monna Lisa and Tessa. Yes, *he* was the mysterious husband; he possessed another wife in the Via de' Bardi. Now, it was time to retrieve the broken dagger and vanish, leaving no trace. Hiding his frailty was the only semblance of power he retained. Leaning forward, he grasped the jagged shards of steel. Beside him lay a large, open manuscript—an unusual volume of Pausanias. Moonlight spilled across the page, illuminating the large Greek letters at the head: *ΜΕΣΣΕΝΙΚΑ. ΚΒ′.*

In times past, Pausanias had been a familiar friend to Baldassarre, yet recently, those pages had appeared as meaningless black scrawls on a wall, devoid of sense. But tonight, the moonbeam acting as a lens conjured the world anew. The letters bloomed into the vibrant history of Messenia, its desperate struggle against Spartan oppression. Baldassarre snatched up the book, though the light was too faint for further reading. It mattered not; he knew the chapter by heart. He read it silently, seeing the stoning of the traitor Aristocrates—the entire populace casting him out to rot unburied, erecting a pillar of verse to declare how time had restored justice to the unjust.

Words swarmed within him, vibrating with the resonance of a thousand memories. He forgot his age; he could have shouted aloud. The light had returned, the mother of knowledge and joy! In this surge of exultation, his limbs regained their strength. He leaped to his feet, clutching the broken blade and the book, and emerged into the broad moonlight.

A biting frost hung in the air, yet Baldassarre felt no chill. Only a radiant glow of conscious power warmed his blood. He paced the open spaces of the high ground, gazing down upon the sleeping city, crowned by mountains and guarded by silent towers. He beheld the pale gleam of the Arno and the valleys fading into snowy peaks, feeling himself the master of this vast domain. This mental empire, available to all in moments of singular clarity, was amplified for Baldassarre by the long nights when memory had been little more than a hollow echo. The city, once a weary labyrinth, now submitted to his will. His mind raced through its affairs with lightning-fast conjectures, reviving the sensation of a man who truly understood cities, whose vision was honed by vast experience, and who delighted in holding everything within the grasp of language.

Names! Images! His mind flooded through this treasury without pause, like an heir accepting a vast legacy. Yet, amidst this rushing tide, a dark deity presided in the inner sanctum of his consciousness—a god who seemed forgotten only during the preparations of sacrifice. Once the initial triumph of recovering power settled, his thoughts coalesced around Tito. That fair, slippery viper could no longer escape him. Thanks to the struggles of justice, the heart that usually refused tenderness now possessed sensitive fibers capable of being pricked by the sharp point of anguish. The soul that bowed to no moral right now bowed to the supreme lord of mortals: Pain.

He could now penetrate every secret of Tito's life. Some he already knew, but the failure of the broken dagger, which had seemed like a crushing defeat, was merely the prelude to true achievement. That sudden, blinding rage had shaken away the obstructions that had suffocated his soul. Twice before, his memory had returned in flashes triggered by sudden excitement—once defending himself from a rabid dog, once scrambling up a cliff to escape drowning waves.

Yet, a shadow lingered. If this light faded tomorrow, returning the dreary blankness, what would become of him? Though the light felt stronger and steadier now, the fear of regression gnawed at him. Could the dark fog return before dawn? This anxiety, though painful, felt like the genesis of renewed strength. He dreaded the idea of sinking further under the strain of this vigilant vigil on the hill, expending his final reserves. Seeking a sheltered corner, he nestled against a heap of warm garden straw and succumbed to sleep.

When he awoke, the sun had risen. The first moments were filled with a strange bewilderment: was he a man of dual identities? Had he woken to the life of a dim-sighted heir to a fallen greatness, or to the life of recovered power? Surely the latter, for the events of the night surged back with undeniable force: the recognition of the page in Pausanias, the flood of facts and names, the panoramic vision that had filled him with the glorious amazement of a Maenad waking atop a mountain.

He picked up the book again, reading and remembering simultaneously without effort. A name evoked deeds; a deed summoned a name. He encountered tales of unpardonable crimes, yet also stories of guilt that succeeded. Sanctuaries existed for swift-footed criminals; baseness wore armor, and the weapons of justice sometimes broke against it. So what then? If baseness could hoard all the goods of the earth and even seize the keys of hell

A Supper in the Rucellai Gardens.

As Tito stepped into the elegant pavilion, his keen observation instantly confirmed his suspicion: this gathering was a political assembly, disguised merely as a celebration of camaraderie. In the era of Florence, where wealth had spawned a peculiar reverence for *Madonna della Gozzoviglia* and Saint Buonvino, it was widely believed that fine food and vintage wine sharpened the mind's grasp on political truths. Only those with "invited stomachs" could truly discern their loyalties with the clarity afforded by post-prandial leisure. While the Florentines were typically sober, the Rucellais, among the city's few magnates who maintained a magnificent household, spared no expense tonight. High philosophical treatises were out of place; the atmosphere demanded the bust of Plato, recently transferred from Lorenzo's villa to gaze down approvingly on the scholars, and the frescoes of the cardinal virtues adorning the walls.

Plato's marble visage, crowned with laurel, had long supervised the Academies' gatherings, particularly on the thirteenth of November, the saint's reputed birthday. Tonight, however, the company was not merely discussing the Master's doctrines with moderation; they were indulging in them with a fervor that bordered on excess. Among them sat Pico della Mirandola, a former Quixotic genius now humbled by a consuming quest for inner perfection; Marsilio Ficino, the diligent reared-up philosopher whose mind, fed exclusively on Platonism, seemed softened by the diet; Angelo Poliziano, the age's supreme literary spirit, whose verses flowed with living blood; and Leon Battista Alberti, the venerable patriarch, a robust mind of universal scope, both practical and theoretical. These valiant workers, whose names might fade from daily records, formed the bedrock of an intellectual inheritance that, like the ploughing of past generations, sustained the soil beneath their feet.

Bernardo Rucellai, the host and patron, stood in the prime of life, barely forty, radiating a cautiously dignified hauteur. He possessed a purity of Latinity that Erasmus deemed uncatchable, a tongue that refused to yield a single syllable to foreign scrutiny. He greeted Tito with particular favor, seating him between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, two accomplished young lions of the Medicean faction.

While the silver basin of scented water circulated, allowing the company to cleanse their hands, the air filled with the perfume of fresh white damask from France, and rings glittered on slender, white fingers under the flickering wax-lamps. Conversation drifted to the familiar, harmless topics of the day. Someone mused what Dante, if resurrected, would make of silver forks replacing iron ones, and the consensus was unanimous: posterity's habits would shock the ancients if the latter could witness them. As silver spoons teased delicacies designed to precede serious discourse—morsels of liver cooked to a melting point—the eye lingered on the enamel centers of the brass service. Attention turned to the silver dish for *confetti*, a masterpiece attributed to Antonio Pollajuolo, whom papal patrons had enticed away from Florence to the opulence of Rome.

"I recall," interjected Niccolò Ridolfi, a middle-aged gentleman whose negligent ease masked a lifelong conviction of superior rank, "how our Antonio grew bitter regarding the chiseling and enameling of these metal trinkets. He declared a furious shift to painting, reasoning that 'the artist who puts his work into gold and silver puts his brains into the melting-pot.'"

"And surely that was a prophetic warning," Giannozzo Pucci remarked. "If this war with Pisa continues, spreading to our other towns, it will not only be our silver dishes that disappear. I suspect Antonio's silver saints around the altar of San Giovanni will one day vanish from the faithful's eyes to be worshipped more devoutly as coin."

"The Friar prepares us for this," Lorenzo Tornabuoni observed dryly. "He tells the populace that God rejects silver crucifixes alongside starving bellies, insisting the Church is best adorned with the gems of holiness and the golden hue of brotherly love."

"A sound doctrine of war finance," Bernardo Rucellai replied with a crisp wit. "Yet politics wait for the wine to wash them down; they are too heavy for roast and boiled meat."

"Yes," Niccolò Ridolfi agreed. "Luigi Pulci would insist this boiled kid requires an impartial palate. I remember a day at Careggi when Luigi, in his rattling vein, claimed nothing corrupted the tongue like opinion. 'Opinion,' he cried, 'spoils the saliva—that is why men embraced pepper. Scepticism alone is a philosophy that tastes nothing.' 'Nay,' poor Lorenzo de' Medici countered, 'you are mistaken, Luigi. Here sits Matteo Franco, an untainted sceptic craving hotter sauce than anyone.' 'Because he holds a strong opinion of himself!' flashed Luigi. 'That is the primal egg of all other opinion. Is he a sceptic? He believes in the immortality of his own verses. Such a logician as that preaching friar who mapped the pavement of Hell! Poor Luigi, his mind was steel, sharp enough to cut whatever it touched.'"

"And yet a tender soul," Giannozzo Pucci murmured. "His words sounded like soap bubbles, yet he was as temperate as a butterfly."

For a time, the light chatter and solid victuals held sway. After the roasts, the indispensable capon and game appeared, capped by the crown of a well-laid table: a peacock prepared à la Apicius. Cooked with feathers intact, it was presented so artfully that it resembled a live bird resting peacefully. Great skill was required of the official carver to rotate the classical, albeit insipid, bird on its back, exposing the plucked breast for slicing, unless a guest possessed the rare independence to decline the "expensive toughness" for the "vulgar digestibility" of the capon. Few dared refuse. Tito, quoting Horace, scattered his slice in minute particles; Bernardo Rucellai offered a learned digression on the ancient price of peacock eggs but feigned non-eating; and Niccolò Ridolfi balanced a mouthful on his fork while recounting Luigi Pulci's tale of a Siennese man who deceived guests by boiling a wild goose in its feathers to mimic a pheasant.

Little peacock was consumed, yet the mere spectacle conferred a profound satisfaction, a marker of status accessible only to the wealthy. To slight the bird's flesh or question the institution at a time when Fra Girolamo preached that luxury was not the duty of the rich would have been rash.

Beyond the circle of warmth, light, and savory aromas, the chill obscurity of the garden hid a solitary figure. Baldassarre walked in narrowing circles, pausing to peer through the windows, which glowed like brilliant jewels against the gloom. He heard the laughter, saw Tito gesticulate with careless grace, and heard his voice weaving through the merry confusion. Baldassarre's mind was a harp string stretched to breaking. He prepared himself for the moment of entry, deriving a savage pleasure from watching Tito's unconscious gaiety prepare its victim for torture. Yet, the men seated among the branching tapers and flashing cups knew nothing of the pale, fierce face watching from without. Light could serve as effectively as darkness as a curtain.

As the conversation deepened, shedding its triviality, the sense of citizenship pressed heavily upon the minds of the guests. The disturbing influence of Fra Girolamo dominated the air. Before the stewed fish was removed, his name surfaced, and despite Bernardo Rucellai's prior restraint, the talk turned openly political. Gossip rippled through the room regarding the first day's voting in the Palazzo: the fiery, domineering nature of Francesco Valori, who seemed to believe his austere virtue entitled him to absolute rule, and the evident alignment between Soderini's speeches for the Great Council and the Friar's sermons.

"My view," Niccolò Ridolfi asserted, "is that the Friar possesses a longer head for public affairs than Soderini or any of the Piagnones. Rest assured, Soderini speaks as his mouthpiece more than the Friar acts as his."

"No, Niccolò," Bernardo Rucellai corrected gently. "I disagree. The Friar has an acute mind, seeing clearly what serves his ends, but it is unlikely that Pagolantonio Soderini, a veteran of affairs who has studied the Venetian Council, owes his ideas to a monk. No, Soderini loads the cannon; I grant you, the Friar provides the powder and strikes the match. He masters the people, and the people are beginning to master us. Behold!"

"Well," Lorenzo Tornabuoni ventured, now that the servants had departed and only wine circled, "regardless of Soderini's debt, *we* owe the Friar the general amnesty accompanying the Council scheme. We might have survived without the terror of God or the moral reforms passed by a majority of black beans. That proposal allowing Medicean heads to remain safely on our shoulders, sparing us the confiscation of our properties in fines, earns my warm approval. I am convinced nothing short of the Friar's dominance could secure it. He stands as firm as a rock on promoting peace. I have spoken privately with him."

A murmur of surprise rippled through the far end of the table, but Bernardo Rucellai nodded silently, inviting Tornabuoni to proceed.

"Yes," Tornabuoni continued, "I was granted

When Romola seated herself upon the rough stone beneath the cypress, a profound and terrifying silence seemed to envelop the landscape. The familiar walls of Florence receded behind her, replaced by the vast, unbounded sky and the sombre, brooding mountains that loomed in the distance. Between her and those dark peaks lay a deep depression in the road, a physical gulf mirroring the chasm opening within her soul. Her husband, Tito, rode away toward Siena, leaving her alone in this desolate stretch, moving further from him with every passing hour. For the first time, the air felt heavy not with the oppression of the city, but with a raw, elemental solitude. There was no human law here, no eye watching, no hand to guide her. She felt entirely exposed to the earth and the heavens, stripped of her social armor.

Then, a voice cut through the stillness, close and intimate, startling her like a sudden gust of wind.

"You are Romola de' Bardi, the wife of Tito Melema."

She knew that voice instantly. It had resonated through her spirit on occasions before, vibrating with an authority that bypassed reason. Because she recognized it, she did not turn to see the speaker, nor did she lift her eyes. Instead, she sat frozen, a tremor running through her limbs—a mixture of superstitious awe and a fierce, internal rebellion. A monk, clad in his black habit, dared to intrude upon her flight, to interrupt her private agony. Yet, as he spoke, she felt as though some ancient, crowned god had descended, seizing her with tangible, flesh-and-blood fingers.

"You are fleeing from Florence in disguise," the voice continued, unwavering. "I hold a mandate from Heaven to halt you. You are not permitted to escape."

A surge of indignation flamed within Romola. How dare this man presume to dictate her course? With deliberate slowness, she refused to turn, resenting the scrutiny of his gaze.

"What authority do you possess to address me, or to obstruct my journey?" she demanded, her voice steady despite the turmoil within.

"The authority of a messenger," the monk replied firmly. "You have donned religious attire not out of piety, but as a mask. You sought to pass unseen, but Providence ensured you were seen. It was revealed to me who you are: a woman attempting to flee the station God has assigned you. You desire to bury your true name and your true calling, replacing them with a fabrication of your own choosing, bound only by your own caprice. I have been commanded to summon you back. Return to your appointed place, my daughter."

With every word, Romola's resolve hardened. She feared that if she showed even a flicker of weakness, she would succumb to paralysis. Irritated, she spoke again.

"I shall not return. I recognize no jurisdiction in priests or monks over my actions. You wield no power over me."

"I understand," the monk said softly, "that you have been raised in contempt for obedience. Yet, it is not I, a poor friar, who seeks to bind you. It is Truth itself that commands you. And Truth cannot be evaded. You stand at a fork: either submit to it, and it shall become your guide; or resist it, and it shall weigh upon you like a millstone for eternity. You will submit, my daughter. My faithful servant returns with the mules shortly, and you shall accompany them back to Florence."

Suddenly, Romola sprang to her feet, her eyes blazing with defiance. She faced the speaker directly. It was Fra Girolamo; she knew him well. They were nearly equal in height, their faces level. She had opened her mouth to hurl a torrent of accusations, but the words died in her throat. Fra Girolamo regarded her with a calm, piercing gaze. It was a look so unfamiliar, so devoid of judgment yet brimming with a strange, intense attention, that her anger evaporated, replaced by a shameful humility.

There was no mystic radiance about Savonarola's face, nor any conventional beauty. It was a visage carved by stern mental habits and rigorous bodily discipline. Yet, the effect of his glance upon Romola was profound. It conveyed a sense of genuine interest and care, detached from any personal affection. It was the first time she had encountered a look where simple human fellowship manifested as an unbreakable bond. Half the vocation of a spiritual leader lay in such a gaze, and Romola found herself unable to challenge his authority any longer. She stood silent, staring at him.

He spoke again, his tone gentle yet cutting.

"You boast loudly of your liberty, my daughter. Yet, who is more base than a debtor who believes himself free?"

His words stung, and a pallor washed over Romola's features.

"And you are fleeing from your debts," he continued, his voice rising slightly. "The debt of a Florentine woman; the debt of a wife. You are turning your back upon the lot ordained for you, seeking to carve out a new destiny. Can a man or a woman truly choose their duties? No more than they can select their birthplace or their parents. My daughter, you are running from the presence of God into the wilderness."

As the heat of anger drained from her, a new sensation took its place: a vague, terrifying intuition of the power inherent in submission, *if* this man indeed possessed a valid law. But it was absurd—he could not possibly know the depths of her inner torment. Yet, she could not simply refuse guidance. Constrained by a desperate need, she pleaded, trying to maintain a veneer of reverence while fighting back.

"My father..." she whispered, the title slipping from her lips unbidden. "You cannot comprehend the reasons driving me away. Only I can know them. Only I can judge. Great sorrow has consumed me, and I am resolved to depart."

"I perceive enough, my daughter," Savonarola replied, his expression softening. "My understanding of you is clear. You are unhappy in your marriage. However, I am not a confessor, and I seek no secrets sealed by the sacrament. I act upon a divine warrant, independent of such knowledge. You were warned by a message from Heaven, delivered in my very presence, prior to your wedding. You had the lawful opportunity to remain free, yet you chose the bond. By deliberately breaking it—and speaking to you as a pagan, for if the sanctity of matrimony holds no sway over you, I treat you as one outside the covenant—you are violating a sacred pledge. Why will you complain of others' wrongs when you yourselves commit the gravest sin a woman and citizen can perpetrate? Secretly withdrawing from a promise made before God and your fellow men. Breaking the most fundamental law binding humanity together: faithfulness to the spoken word. Is this the wisdom you have acquired by scoffing at the Church? Not to discern the bare duty of integrity, where the Church would teach you to see not merely integrity, but religion?"

Blood surged to Romola's cheeks, and she shrank back as if struck by a blow. "I would not have assumed a disguise," she began, but her voice faltered. She could not finish; the implication in the friar's words—that her conduct mirrored Tito's—shook her foundations.

"To sever that pledge, you flee Florence," he pressed gently. "Florence, the only place where men and women exist to whom you owe the debt of a fellow citizen."

"I never would have left Florence," Romola murmured, her voice trembling, "so long as there remained any hope of fulfilling a duty to my father there."

"Do you acknowledge no tie save that of a child to a father of the flesh?" Savonarola asked sharply. "Your life has been lived in blindness, my daughter. You have dwelt with those perched high on hills, gazing down upon the lives of their neighbors with disdain. I hear their hollow discourses, filled with fancied wisdom of the past, while they scorn God's work in the present. Perhaps you were taught of pagan women who lived for the Republic, yet you have never felt that you, a Florentine woman, ought to live for Florence. If your own kin wear a yoke, will you slip away rather than struggle to lighten it? There is hunger and misery in our streets, and yet you declare, 'I care not; I have my own sorrows.' The servants of God strive for justice, peace, and charity so that the hundred thousand citizens among whom you were born may be ruled rightly. Yet you regard this as trivial, like a bird spreading its wings to seek food wherever it pleases. You have scorned the teaching of the Church, my daughter. As if you, a wilful wanderer chasing your blind desires, were not inferior to the humblest Florentine woman who stretches out her hands with her people, craving a blessing for them; who feels a sisterhood with the neighbor kneeling beside her, stranger though she be; who contemplates the mighty purpose God has for Florence and waits, enduring, because the work is grand and she feels herself small."

"I was not departing for ease or indulgence!" Romola cried, lifting her head, compelled to defend herself. "I went away to hardship. I expect no joy; it has departed from my life."

"You seek your own will, my daughter," Savonarola countered, his voice firm. "You seek some good other than the law you are bound to keep. But how shall you find good? It is not a matter of choice; it is a river flowing from the foot of the Invisible Throne, tracing the path of obedience. Man cannot choose

"Rise, my daughter," Father Girolamo finally spoke, his voice cutting through the silence. "Your servant waits with the mules not far hence. 'Tis time I press onward toward Florence."

Romola rose from her kneeling posture. That stillness had become a sacrament to her, a ritual cementing the passive longing into which she had lately stepped. With a single act—the surrender of her plan to flee her husband—her will felt shattered, leaving her needing direction even for trivial matters. She lifted the brim of her hood and spied Maso and the second Dominican standing with their backs turned upon the hill's edge, some ten yards away. Yet she gazed past them, fixing her eyes on Savonarola, silent and expectant, as if the command for Maso to retreat must issue from Savonarola's lips alone, not hers.

"I shall fetch them," he said, reading the quiet plea in her gaze. "And I commend you to the Brother who accompanies me. You seek guidance, seeking that wisdom which hath seemed folly unto you. A primary gateway to this wisdom is the sacrament of confession. You require a confessor, child, and I entrust you to the care of Fra Salvestro, a brother of San Marco in whom I hold deep faith."

"I wish for guidance from you alone, Father," Romola answered, her brow furrowed with anxiety.

"My daughter, I serve not as your confessor. My calling withdraws me from duties requiring constant contact with layfolk, interfering with my specific obligations."

"Then shall I never speak to you privately?" Romola asked, her voice trembling with sudden agitation. "If I falter, if—" She broke off, seized by a sharp alarm. Her newfound fortitude in renunciation threatened to crumble if the immediate, personal influence of Savonarola were severed.

"My daughter, if your soul requires the Word spoken privately by my own mouth, let me know through Fra Salvestro, and I shall meet you in the sacristy or the choir of San Marco. I shall not cease to watch over you. I will instruct my brother regarding you, directing him to lead you into that path of labor for the suffering and the hungry to which you are called as a daughter of Florence in these days of dire want. I desire to behold you among the weaker and less knowing sisters as an apple tree stands among the trees of the forest, that your fairness and natural gifts may serve merely as a lamp through which the Divine Light shines all the more purely. Now, I go to summon your servant."

Once Maso had been dispatched ahead, Fra Salvestro stepped forward, and Savonarola led Romola toward him. She had felt an inner shrinking beforehand, a dread of a new guide who was a total stranger. Yet to resist Savonarola's counsel now would be to assume an attitude of independence at a moment when every ounce of her strength must be drawn from the renunciation of self-will. Consequently, her entire bent of mind leaned toward doing what was painful rather than what was easy. She bowed reverently to Fra Salvestro before looking upon him directly; but when she raised her head and beheld him fully, her reluctance curdled into a palpitating doubt.

There exist men whose presence alone infuses trust and reverence; then there are those to whom we must carry our trust and reverence pre-formed, ready-made. This distinction struck Romola with blinding clarity as Savonarola receded from view, replaced by Fra Salvestro Maruffi. It was not that Fra Salvestro possessed any manifest repulsion in face or manner, nor any air of hypocrisy or tinge of coarseness. His visage was handsomer than Father Girolamo's, his frame slightly taller. He was the long-standing confessor to many among Florence's chief personages, having accrued vast experience as a spiritual director. Yet his face bore the vacillating expression of a mind incapable of concentrating strongly upon one great emotion or belief—a trait fatal to influencing an ardent nature like Romola's.

Such an expression does not necessarily mark insincerity; rather, it stamps a shallow soul. One finds such souls sincerely striving to fill a high vocation, earnestly composing their countenances to utter sublime formulas, only to find their muscles twitch or relax in spite of their beliefs, as prose intrudes where poetry ought to reign. Fra Salvestro harbored a peculiar liability to visions, seemingly dependent on a constitution prone to somnambulism. Savonarola believed in the supernatural character of these visions, whereas Fra Salvestro himself had originally resisted interpreting them thus, even rebuking Savonarola for his prophetic preaching. Another proof, if one were needed, that the relative greatness of men cannot be gauged by their tendency to disbelieve the superstitions of their age. Between these two, there could be no question which was the great man and which the small.

The disparity between them was measured accurately by the shift in Romola's feelings as Fra Salvestro began to address her with words of exhortation and encouragement. After her first angry resistance to Savonarola had passed, she had lost all memory of the ancient dread lest any influence drag her into the circle of fanaticism and sour, monkish piety. But now, once again, the chilling breath of that terror stole over her. It could have no decisive effect against the impetus her mind had just received; it was akin to the closing of grey clouds over a sunrise, rendering her return path monotonous and sombre.

And perhaps of all sombre paths, the one we tread after traversing it with strong resolution is that which most severely tests the fervor of renunciation. As they re-entered the city gates, light snowflakes fell around them. As the grey sister hastened homeward from the Piazza di San Marco, crossing the bridge again and turning into the broad door of the Via de' Bardi, her footsteps left dark marks upon the thin carpet of snow, and her cowl fell laden and damp about her face.

She ascended to her chamber, cast off her serge habit, destroyed the letters of parting, restored all her precious trifles to their places, unbound her hair, and donned her usual black dress. Instead of undertaking a long, stirring journey, she was to sit down in her accustomed seat. Snow beat against the windows, and she stood alone.

She felt the dreariness, yet her courage ran high, like that of a seeker who has stumbled upon new signs of gold. She was about to thread life by a fresh clue. She had poured all the energy of her will into renunciation. The empty tabernacle remained locked, and she placed Dino's crucifix outside it.

Nothing broke the outward monotony of her solitary home until night arrived like a white ghost at the windows. Yet it proved the most memorable Christmas Eve of her life, this one of 1494.

October 30, 1496. The air was crisp, carrying the gentle promise of autumn, yet beneath the surface of the clear sky lay a storm far more terrifying than any wind. The Florentines paid little heed to the land breezes; their minds were consumed by the gales at sea, tempests that seemed to conspire against the Friar's assurance that Providence watched over their city. From the shores of Leghorn, ships laden with Marseilles grain and soldiers had been driven back, leaving Florence in a stranglehold of starvation and military vulnerability. Hunger stalked the streets like a ghost, and the borders of the state were exposed to invasion.

France, under a King who had swept through Italy like a new Charlemagne, had recently withdrawn, reportedly broken by the grief of a lost child. In response, a "Holy League" had formed, led by Pope Borgia, ostensibly to expel the French "barbarians" from Naples. Yet, to the sharp-eyed observers, this coalition resembled nothing so much as a pack of wolves agreeing to drive out rivals only to tear each other apart over the carcass. And Florence, standing alone against this tide due to her stubborn adherence to the French alliance, was viewed not as a partner, but as the most desirable meal. While Pisa fought savagely for liberty, bolstered by Venice, Milan, and the German Emperor Maximilian, Florence sat isolated, cut off from the sea and surrounded by enemies.

The government, influenced heavily by the followers of Savonarola, responded with a mixture of vigor and compassion. They raised funds and recruits, refusing the traditional cruelty of shutting out the starving peasants and beggars who flocked to the city like birds fleeing a frozen north. Instead, they opened their gates wide, inviting the destitute into the belly of the beast. Yet this benevolence drew fire from the disaffected, who argued that Florence should join the League to secure peace rather than invite chaos, and that feeding the alien poor weakened the state against foreign foes.

Distress grew with every passing day, fueling louder murmurs in the streets. To compound the crisis, Fra Girolamo, obeying a mandate from Rome, had remained silent at the pulpit for over a month. Only after the news of the failed grain shipment reached the Signoria did they summon him back. Two days prior, Savonarola had returned to the Duomo, delivering a bold sermon wherein he wagered his own habit—if God did not save Florence for their piety, he would strip it off. Yet, as the morning broke on the thirtieth, no sign of divine rescue had appeared.

Desperation gnawed at the city's resolve. Some whispered that the miraculous *Tabernacle* of the Madonna dell'Impruneta, a powerful intercessor known to deliver Florence from drought, plague, and war, needed to be brought in. For centuries, the city had trusted in this "Mother rich in sorrow," pouring wealth and devotion upon her shrine. Though the Tabernacle had already been escorted from Impruneta and placed in the church of San Gaggio outside the walls, it had not yet entered the city proper. The morning sun rose on unchanged misery, where hospitals overflowed, courtyards served as infirmaries, and the bodies of the dead, buried by the various fraternities, lined the roadsides.

Among the many women moving through the streets in plain garb, attending to the sick and feeding the hungry, one figure stood out unmistakably: Romola de' Bardi. Cloaked in simple black serge, with a veil hiding her hair save for bands of gold rippling across her brow, she walked from the Ponte Vecchio toward the Porta Santa Maria. Her path was suddenly blocked by a bier carried by the Company of San Jacopo del Popolo. The brothers paused to inspect a load, while a cluster of idle workers, their faces gaunt and sharpened by hunger, gathered around, speaking in a confused murmur.

"He's dead!" one cried. "Messere Domeneddio has taken him!"
"It would be kind to stretch out our legs and walk ahead with our heads," another joked grimly. "Standing upright with hunger is no support."
"A beggar turned out of Siena," someone noted. "Or perhaps a prisoner from the Stinche?"

There was speculation whether the man was truly dead or merely collapsed from lack of food. One brother suggested giving him wine. Before the decision could be fully acted upon, Romola stepped forward. With a deft motion, she loosened a small flask at her belt and used a tiny ivory implement to pour a few drops of wine into the prostrate man's mouth. The liquid worked quickly; the man gasped, swallowing eagerly. More followed, and slowly, the old man's head turned toward her, his eyes opening with a vague, returning consciousness.

A jolt of profound recognition struck Romola. Those wild, dark eyes peering from a sallow, deep-lined face, framed by a long, white beard, were unmistakable. They were the same eyes she had seen in the Duomo years ago, belonging to the escaped prisoner who had terrified Tito and haunted the sketches in Piero's studio. A tremor seized her heart. Here, finally, was the secret that might be more bitter than anything she had endured: the man her husband had wronged. An impulse to flee surged within her, a visceral reaction to the horror of the encounter, yet a deeper, imperious need to stay rooted her feet. She knew, with the intuition of keen feeling, that this man was connected to her husband's crimes. Her hands trembled, but the muscle memory of her charitable work guided them as she supported his neck with her left arm and prepared to offer more wine with her right.

Baldassarre, the prisoner, turned his gaze onto her for the first time. The seclusion of his imprisonment and her recent flight had kept them apart, erasing any specific memory he might have held of the fair, golden-haired wife described in rumors. Now, seeing her clearly, those descriptions vanished like mist.

"The steps of San Stefano," Romola suggested gently. "We must move him there so we do not block the street. You may proceed with the bier."

They moved but thirty yards, and by the time they reached the stone steps of the church, Baldassarre managed to sit up, propping himself against the doorway. The charitable brethren continued on their rounds, but the crowd of onlookers swelled. The atmosphere shifted from casual curiosity to a tense, watching silence as the reality of his survival settled.

Romola sat beside him, offering a piece of soft bread from her basket. "Can you eat this now? Perhaps later you will be able, if I leave it for you. I must go to the hospital, but I will return if you wait. Do you understand? Wait for me."

Her words echoed in his enfeebled mind. "Come back," he repeated dreamily.

As she offered him the bread, a rough-looking man in a red night-cap shoved past the others to elbow his way closer. "Is this bread for those who cannot swallow, Madam?" he sneered. Another voice chimed in, mocking the idea of feeding thieves. "If every criminal is to be revived with wine and bread, the Ciompi had better go fill themselves in the Arno while the waters are plenty."

The circle tightened, the gaunt, starving men pressing in, their coarseness cutting through the polite distance Romola usually maintained. She held out her basket, containing the bread and a small horn-cup of wine, soaked with the liquid. Until now, she had ignored their taunts, focusing solely on the dying man. But now, rising to her feet, she looked around at the crowd. Instinctively, the men nearest to her stepped back, as if sensing a boundary they dared not cross.

"Hunger is hard to bear," Romola said, her voice steady despite the strain on her face, which bore the marks of prolonged fasting. "You have the power to take this bread if you wish. It was saved for sick women and children. You are strong men; if you do not choose to suffer because you are strong, you have the power to take everything from the weak. Take the bread from this basket if you will, but I shall watch by this old man. I shall resist anyone who tries to take bread from *him*."

Silence fell over the group. The man in the red night-cap looked foolish, backing away and jabbing his elbow into his neighbor's ribs in a gesture of mock rebuke. The young man in the fine cloth tunic melted away into the crowd. Just as the tension began to dissipate, the armed servitors of the Signoria arrived to break up the obstruction blocking the street.

The man called Cecco, who had retreated up the church steps, turned back. Seeing the threat of a mace, he approached Romola with a sudden shift in demeanor. "Madam, if you wish to continue your errands, I will take charge of the old man."

Cecco was a wild-looking figure, clad in a ragged, variegated tunic, his arms bare and sinewy, his face bruised by hardship. He spoke quickly, trying to assure her. "Do not distrust me, Madam. I am not handsome, but I have an old mother who eats my porridge. I buy candles for the Virgin. Look, he is eating his sop. He is hale enough; soon he will stand as well as the best of us."

Romola hesitated, reading his intent, but eventually nodded. "Thank you for your offer, friend." Leaning down to Baldassarre, she whispered

The Unseen Madonna.

Returning from the hospital, an hour past, Romola chose a path that widened toward the river, distancing herself from the Ponte Vecchio before turning back toward it. Her intention was to hasten to San Stefano and seek Baldassarre, though dread held her back; yet, in abandoning him, she feared she would lose a vital tether to her own existence.

As she neared the junction where the Por' Santa Maria curved to her right and the Ponte Vecchio lay to her left, she stumbled into a throng that suddenly sank to their knees. Without hesitation, Romola joined them. The Great Cross of the Duomo was passing, leading a solemn procession. Having arrived later than anticipated, she was forced to wait until the cross had vanished behind the buildings.

Rising, the shift from kneeling to standing, coupled with the sudden silence, made her acutely aware of her exhaustion. Beside her, a hosier offered kind words: "Madonna Romola, you must be weary. Gian Fantoni would be pleased to offer you a seat in his home; his door is right there. Let me open it. You know he loves God and the Friar as we do. His house is yours."

Accustomed to such fraternal address from citizens whose faces she knew from the Duomo, Romola accepted. For her, the concept of 'home' had shifted away from the lonely solitude of the Via de' Bardi toward the towering circuits of Florence itself, a city that seemed to greet her at every street corner with appeals and friendship. Gladly, she stepped through the open door, guided by the hosier to an upstairs window. There, a stout woman in the plain garb of the Piagnoni, accompanied by three children, received her with profound reverence, seating her amidst bright draperies. From this vantage point, Romola observed not only the procession moving slowly between the houses on the bridge but also the river and the Lung' Arno stretching toward the Santa Trinita.

The procession advanced in sadness and stillness. No wailing chants broke the silence; only the tramp of feet and the soft sweep of woolen robes echoed. First came a long train of Florentine youth, their faces young and unblemished by beard, bearing aloft the white image of the youthful Jesus, crowned with golden glory, standing beside the tall cross where thorns and nails awaited. Following them were the Companies of Discipline, bound by vows of self-chastisement and piety. Their garments concealed heads and faces save for the eyes, transforming individual citizens of all ranks—shopkeepers, accountants, statesmen—into anonymous symbols of a shared vow. Each company wore a color and a badge, but the complete shrouds erased personal identity, leaving only an expression of collective fellowship.

In stark contrast, the multitude of monks stood out as distinct individuals despite their common tonsures and frocks. A white stream of reformed Benedictines flowed first, followed by the vast numbers of the Frati Minori, or Franciscans, clad in grey with knotted cords around their waists and wooden sandals beneath bare feet. Perhaps the largest order in Florence, they were known for their zeal, their love for mankind, and their hatred of the Dominicans. After the grey came the black of the Augustinians of San Spirito, men of cultivated countenances who had inherited Boccaccio's library and formed Florence's most learned circle during an age of scarcity. Then followed the white-over-dark habit of the Carmelites, and finally the unmixed black of the Servites, an order founded by seven merchants who abandoned worldly gain to worship the Divine Mother.

Now, the hearts of the onlookers quickened, beating either with hatred or love. A stream of black and white mantles emerged from the bridge—black cloaks over white scapularies. All knew these were the Dominicans. Those from Fiesole led the way, one black mantle parting another, one tonsured head following another. Suspense hung heavy in the air. The mantles were coarse, many threadbare or ragged, for the Prior of San Marco had reduced his fraternities to the strictest poverty and discipline. Yet, amidst this sea of worn cloth, one mantle stood out, slightly less tattered than the rest, topped by a tonsured head that a stranger might find unremarkable unless they recognized it from bronze medals depicting the Sword of God, or from scenes of his arrival guarded by an armed retinue, or from the inward flame that transformed him as he surveyed a rapt multitude.

As Savonarola drew near, silence reigned absolute. Even those wishing to speak hesitated, lest their voices break the solemn tramp of the feet and the faint rustle of garments. Nevertheless, his ear—and others'—caught a mingled hiss, a sound longing to become curses, intercut with murmurs yearning for blessings. Perhaps the preponderance of hissing caused two or three of his disciples in the foreground to fall to their knees as if divinity itself were passing. This movement of silent homage spread like a subtle shockwave along the streets, leaving some unmoved while compelling others to bow deeply. Yet hatred gathered intensity as well; as Savonarola ascended the Por' Santa Maria, Romola saw someone at an upper window spit upon him.

Monks returned—the Humble Brothers of Ognissanti, glorious workers in the wool trade—and others of the Vallombrosan and Benedictine varieties, their varied forms marking a spiritual renewal long past. Finally, the shaven crowns ended, giving way to the train of untonsured secular priests.

Then followed the twenty-one incorporated Arts of Florence, arrayed in long lines with banners proclaiming their distinct functions, from bakers to judges and notaries. After them marched the secondary officers of state, ascending from lesser to greater, until the line of seculars was broken by the Canons of the Duomo. They carried a sacred relic: the very head of San Zenobio, immortal bishop of Florence, enclosed in silver, whose virtues were believed to have saved the city a millennium prior.

Here lay the nucleus of the procession. Behind the relic marched the archbishop in a gorgeous cope, sheltered by a canopy, followed by the mysterious, hidden Image. First veiled by rich brocade curtains enclosing an outer painted tabernacle, and within that, by an ancient shrine never opened in living memory, lay the Vision of the Pitying Mother. Found ages ago in the soil of L'Impruneta, she had uttered a cry as the spade struck her. Historically, the unseen Image rarely entered the Duomo without lavish gifts preceding it—veils, curtains, mantles offered by emulous men and communities. The richest, it was said, was woven by a poor abbess and her nuns, who, lacking funds, crafted a mantle of gold brocade with their prayers, embroidering it with supplications until two beautiful youths spread white wings and vanished into the blue sky to present it to the Blessed Virgin in the great Piazza.

But today, no gifts preceded the tabernacle. No donations were to be made except to the poor. This was the counsel of Fra Girolamo, whose preaching never urged gifts to invisible powers but only aid for visible need. Altars had been raised at various church points, laden with oblations for the destitute. Not even a torch was carried. Surely, the hidden Mother cared less for torchlight and brocade than for the wail of the hungry. Florence was in extremity; she had done her utmost and could only wait for a divine intervention beyond human power.

Fra Girolamo, the friar in the torn mantle, had promised help would surely come. Many, hearts faint, clung to his word rather than the virtues of the unseen Image. Yet, many fiercely hearted rejoiced secretly, hoping the Friar's promise might prove false.

Slowly the tabernacle moved forward, knees bending in reverence. Profound stillness enveloped the scene, for the train of priests and chaplains from L'Impruneta stirred no passion. The procession was nearing its close with the Priors and the Gonfaloniere. The long train of companies and symbols, possessing a silent music that stirred the mind like a chorus, was fading from view. Now, a faint, yearning hope struggled against the accustomed despondency.

Romola's heart swelled, half with foreboding, half with the enthusiastic fellowship that the last two years had made habitual to her, as natural as clothing to the vain and idle. Giving a deep sigh, as if released from long mental tension, she remained on her knees, overcome by languor. Suddenly, something brightly colored flashed from between the houses onto the distant bridge. Instantly, Romola sprang up, stretching out her arms, leaning from the window. As the black drapery fell from her head, the golden gleam of her hair and the flush of her face seemed illuminated by a single, brilliant light.

A shout erupted simultaneously. The last troops of the procession paused, and all faces turned toward the distant bridge. But the bridge was already passed; the horseman pressed on at full gallop alongside the Arno. His bay horse, streaked with foam, appeared all white from the speed of its motion. Its cap flew loose beside its red *becchetto*, and the rider waved an olive branch in his hand. It was a messenger—a bearer of good tidings! The blessed olive branch spoke far and wide. Yet the impatient people could not wait. They surged to meet the oncomer, seizing his horse's reins, pushing and trampling.

Now Romola saw clearly that the horseman was her husband, Tito, who had been sent to

As the throng swept past, Romola slipped down into the street and hurried toward San Stefano. Cecco had drifted with the crowd to the Piazza, leaving Baldassarre standing solitary by the church doors. The priest held a horn-cup, waiting, yet a profound transformation had overtaken him. Where once lay the vacant, dreamy gaze of a half-conscious mind, there now burned a fierce, concentrated intensity. As Romola approached, offering a word, that look flared upon her with the sudden, terrifying recognition of a caged beast spotting prey slipping safely beyond the bars.

Romola started at the glare, her immediate thought leaping to Tito. *Has he seen him?* she wondered. As the hatred in Baldassarre's eyes scraped against her skin, a fragile hope ignited within her: perhaps this man was the criminal, and her husband innocent. If she could confront Baldassarre with Tito, if she could see the truth laid bare, her mind would finally rest.

"If you will come with me," she said gently, "I can offer you shelter and food until you regain your strength. Will you?"

"Yes," Baldassarre replied, the word sounding like a muttered prayer rather than a response to her. "I must get my strength. I need it."

"Then come!" She extended her hand, guiding him away from the noisy square toward the quieter path by the Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte.

"I suspect you are not of Florence," she ventured as they crossed the bridge.

He glanced around, silent and wary. His suspicion was heightened by the physical frailty that clouded his mind, making the fog of his confusion thicker. Yet, something in her gentle, steady gaze compelled him to answer.

"No," he said cautiously. "I am no Florentine. I am a lonely man."

Noticing his reticence, Romola held her tongue, fearing to push him away if he desired solitude. As they walked, her heart heavy, the faint hope that he was merely a victim of circumstance began to dim. If an old man like him harbored malice, what terrible secret did it conceal?

Silence stretched between them until they reached the entrance of the Via de' Bardi. Suddenly, Baldassarre stopped, turning with a jerky motion as if struck by a blow. Moments later, Romola halted before the open courtyard, where straw littered the ground and four or five ill folk lay prostrate. Tiny children crawled among them, pale and weak, gnawing at straws.

"If you will enter," Romola whispered, her voice trembling, "I will find you a corner and bring you more food."

"No," Baldassarre refused, though he stood frozen, paralyzed by the weight of his confused thoughts. "I cannot go in."

"What can I do for you?" she asked, desperation edging her tone. "Take this money so you may buy sustenance. It will be abundant soon."

She drew coins from her *scarsella*, holding out a palm full of *grossi*. She intended to give him more than she ever would to any stranger. He stared at the metal, then nodded slowly.

"Yes," he murmured.

She poured the coins into his hand, and he clutched them tight. "Tell me," she pleaded, her voice breaking, "what will you do next?"

Baldassarre had already turned, walking back toward the bridge. Crossing it, he marched straight up the Via del Fosso, pausing before the shop of Niccolò Caparra. Without hesitation, he pushed inside. Niccolò was absent, having joined a procession of armorers, leaving only an apprentice behind. Weapons hung everywhere, gleaming in the dim light. Baldassarre's eyes, starving for bread, fixated on steel.

Though Niccolò might have refused to arm a prisoner, the boy sold a sharp hunting-knife for three *grossi* without a second thought. It was small, easily concealed within Baldassarre's tunic. As he walked on, feeling a phantom surge of power, he realized the edge could deliver a fatal thrust even from an aged arm. Perhaps the armor his enemies wore would not always be present; perhaps, in those long months of imprisonment, their baseness had grown complacent. The knife had been purchased with the traitor's own coin. Justice balanced.
