And if he secured enough food, he could think clearly again. First, he needed to think of where to hide, lest Tito drag him back to prison. With that resolve, he navigated the narrowest alleys, buying meat and bread, then sat beneath a loggia to eat. The bells rang out in joyful, swelling peals, shaking the air with celebration. To Baldassarre, they sounded only like the roar of a mighty tide rising against him.

Romola watched him vanish around the corner of the Piazza de' Mozzi. A mixture of relief and self-reproach swirled within her. Had she sought the truth more vigorously? Could she have relieved his suffering? Yet, if the truth involved a grievous sin committed by her husband, what choice remained to a wife? Was it not her duty to remain silent, to shield him? These intricate threads of feeling tangled in her weary mind, untangled for now.

Instead, she turned to the court to share the good news. Closing the door to muffle the sound of the bells, she threw back the black drapery covering her head so the women might see her face. Standing amidst the straw beds, she announced that corn was coming and that the bells sang for joy. The sick lifted themselves, the children crawled forward, pulling at her skirts, eager to touch her. Exhausted as she was, she sat down on the straw, letting the pale faces cluster around her basket.

"The Holy Virgin has pity on us!" the feeble voices cried.

"It was the procession!"

Romola rose eventually, too drained to force a smile, promising to return with dinner. "Bless you, Madam!" the chorus echoed, a soft sound indistinguishable from their earlier thanks to the invisible Madonna.

Romola listened to the fading echoes of their gratitude with deep affection. Unlike those who found comfort in the mere routine of charity, her early training had kept her distant from such labors. Only the inspiration of her deepest sorrows had drawn her near. Now, however, this work of sympathy had become her anchor, the single clear path through the darkness. Even as the chasm between her and Tito widened, fueled by her futile attempts at reconciliation, doubts gnawed at her regarding the validity of their bond, and a revulsion rose against the narrow superstitions of Savonarola's followers, she found stability here.

Whatever cast doubt on her faith, her service to the citizens grounded her. She remembered the words of Fra Girolamo: her place had never been empty; it was filled with her love and labor. Florence needed her. As her own grief pressed harder, she found a deeper gladness in the memory of easing burdens for others. All the ardor of her nature, which could no longer flow solely into the tenderness of a wife and daughter, had transformed into a fervent empathy for the collective life of humanity. She had ceased to seek happiness for herself; the sole aim of her existence appeared to be the alleviation of sorrow.

Her spirit, constantly renewed, drew strength from Savonarola. Though she recoiled from the stale, wearisome visions and allegories repeated by lesser imitators, her affinity for his passionate sympathy and lofty aims remained undimmed. His burning indignation against the corruption of the Church and State kindled a fire within her. His focus on the liberty and purity of Florence, linked to a universal regeneration, awakened in her a new awareness of history as a vast, unfolding drama. Through her daily contact with the poor, this abstract consciousness hardened into a concrete, self-denying purpose. She thought little of dogmas, shunning close reflection on the Friar's prophecies of imminent scourge. Instead, she had submitted her mind to his, entering communion with the Church to satisfy a moral hunger that her previous education had left unquenched.

Fra Girolamo's voice had given her a reason to live, distinct from personal pleasure or affection—a reason demanding fuel far greater than she possessed internally. Her obedience to every office of the Church was a form of vigilance, a waiting to see if fresh strength might arrive. The pressing question before her was not theological debate, but the preservation of that unselfish flame that allowed a life of sadness to remain a life of active love.

Her trust in Savonarola's superior nature formed the bedrock of her courage. It was not intellectual force that blinded her to his eccentricities, nor visionary acumen that ignored his flaws; it was simply the negation of high sensibilities. Moved profoundly by the grandeur of his energy, she listened patiently to every prophecy and doctrine delivered in the vehicle of his ardent faith. No soul remains truly desolate so long as there exists a human being worthy of trust and reverence. For Romola, this trust in Savonarola was like a rope securely fastened to her path, rendering her steps elastic and sure. Should that rope snap, no firmness of the earth beneath her feet could prevent her from stumbling, or falling.

Following the serendipitous arrival of the olive-bearing courier—a favor unbidden by fate—Tito's agenda shifted toward matters of deliberate design. Pausing before the Palazzo Vecchio, he waited for the return of the *Dieci*, the board managing external and military affairs, to formally present the fruits of his clandestine journey to Pisa. This mission served as a prologue to an open embassy led by Bernardo Rucellai, aimed at securing a peaceful accord between Florence, Emperor Maximilian, and the League.

Tito's aptitude for diplomacy had been thoroughly vetted. As he delivered his findings with exhaustive detail and precise articulation, Bernardo del Nero, then a member of the *Ten*, could not suppress his admiration. Yet, beneath this professional courtesy, Bernardo's ancient hostility toward Tito had resurfaced and deepened following the sale of the library. Though Romola had remained silent regarding the transaction, leaving Bernardo to interpret her quietude as a command not to broach the subject, the elder man was certain that violating her father's wishes had inflicted a profound, blighting sorrow upon her. Observant as ever, Bernardo detected further signs that her marriage lacked happiness.

"Ah," he murmured internally, "this undoubtedly explains her turn toward Fra Girolamo and the *Piagnoni*—a shift I never anticipated from her nature. Women, unhappy and barren, inevitably drift into either madness or a religion so straitened they bear the weight of heaven alone on their shoulders. And as for my poor child Romola, as I often warned, the excessive cramming of Latin and Greek has left her scarcely more than a woman who spends her days pricking her fingers with needles. As for her husband, a tool with a smooth handle, employed wherever needed, I pray that the Tornabuonis and their ilk do not find their fingers severed. Well, well, *solco torto, sacco dritto*—many a full sack comes from a crooked furrow; and he who commands only honest men shall receive scant wages."

Holding firmly to the conviction that the moral qualities of political agents could not be sifted, Bernardo abstained from any overt interference against Tito. Beyond the sacred privacy owed to Romola, Bernardo possessed no direct accusation save Tito's Greek origin and his personal dislike of the man. The duplicity of feigning allegiance to the popular government while harboring Medicean sympathies was common to Tito and half the Medicean faction alike, though Tito practiced it with superior skill. Thus, Bernardo maintained a cold reserve, which Tito returned with scrupulous, distant respect. To the citizens of Florence, any service rendered to Romola's husband was viewed as an acceptable tribute to her godfather.

Once discharged at the Old Palace, Tito felt the day's official duties complete. Exhausted and scorched by the long ride, he chose not to return home immediately. There lingered in his satchel (*scarsella*) and in his mind certain burdens he wished to shed, yet he must do so with such guile that his intentions appeared accidental rather than sought. Walking from the palace with an air of languid indifference, he drifted toward the Piazza del Duomo. Although the procession had concluded, the bells still rang, and the populace moved restlessly through the streets, craving a more tangible outlet for their jubilation. While the new discipline, proclaiming Christ the special King of the Florentines and mandating Christian pleasures, sought to curb excess, a secret, vibrant hunger persisted in many youths for the vigorous catharsis of stone-throwing.

As Tito traversed the square, he could not evade recognition as the bearer of the olive branch. To dispel the inevitable ovation, particularly the barrage of eager inquiries, he adeptly deflected attention by announcing that Meo di Sasso, the true messenger from Leghorn, was imminent and could surely be found near the Porta San Frediano. He offered far more knowledge than he actually possessed.

Liberated from these importunities, Tito entered the Piazza del Duomo, casting wide, careless glances around the space while secretly scanning for a presence that might offer a desired opportunity. The termination of the procession at the Duomo promised an unusual concentration of loungers and conversationalists near Nello's shop, and as he had hoped, the crowd was thick. Near the north gates of the Baptistery, a group leaned against the iron rails. Spotting precisely what he sought, Tito affected an even greater indifference, pretending to recognize the tallest figure in the group only by chance as he passed, tossing the end of his *becchetto* over his left shoulder with a casual flick.

Yet the tall, broad-shouldered individual thus greeted looked like a man of considerable consequence. He wore a richly embroidered tunic, displaying a lavish amount of linen in the latest French fashion, and at his belt hung a sword and poniard of exquisite craftsmanship. His hat, adorned with a red plume, seemed a defiant protest against the somber gravity imposed upon Florentine dress by the *Piagnoni*. Indefinable traces of youth accentuated the coarseness of his face and the substantial diameter of his waist. His eyes held a rude, desecrating stare toward all men and things, an offense to a refined mind akin to a foul odor or a glaring light.

He and his companions, likewise young men clad in expensive attire and bearing arms, exchanged jokes with an ostentatious laughter designed to prove that their mirth remained unassailed despite any underlying suspicions. There were ample grounds for such suspicion; this broad-shouldered man with the red feather was Dolfo Spini, leader of the *Compagnacci*, or Evil Companions. These were the dissolute young men of the old aristocracy, enemies of the Mediceans and the popular government, but above all, bitter foes of Savonarola. Dolfo Spini, heir to the great house with the loggia over the Santa Trinita bridge, had organized these youths into an armed band, swearing themselves champions of extravagant suppers and the sinful pleasures of the flesh against the reforming pietists who threatened to render the world so chaste and temperate that existence itself would become unbearable, save for the misery of the alternative. Earlier that morning, he had loudly proclaimed that Florence faced famine and ruin solely due to its blind adherence to the Frate's counsel, asserting that salvation lay only in joining the League and driving the Frate from the city—to send him to Rome, where he rightfully belonged, obeying the Pope's summons long overdue. Consequently, it was suspected that Messer Dolfo Spini's heart burned with no pure joy at the unexpected succors arriving in apparent fulfillment of the Frate's prophecies. Furthermore, the laughter ringing out anew as Tito joined the group near Nello's door failed to dispel this suspicion. Leaning against the doorpost in the center of the circle was a close-shaven, keen-eyed figure named Niccolò Machiavelli. Though young, he had already penetrated the minute secrets of human egoism.

"Messer Dolfo's head," Machiavelli remarked, "is more of a pumpkin than I thought. I gauge a man's dullness by the devices he trusts to deceive others. Your dullest beast of all is he who grins and claims he minds not a whit just after having his shins kicked." Smiling as the circle opened to admit Tito, he continued, "If I were a trifle duller, now, I should pretend to be fond of this Melema, who holds a secretaryship that would fit me perfectly—as if ill-paid Latin could love better Latin that is better paid! Melema, you are a pestiferously clever fellow, very much in my way, and I am sorry to hear you have secured another stroke of good luck today."

"Questionable luck, Niccolò," replied Tito, placing a friendly hand on Machiavelli's shoulder. "I have gained nothing from it yet but being grabbed and breathed upon by wool-combers, while I am as soiled and battered by the ride as a letter-carrier from Bologna."

"Ah! You crave a touch of my art, Messer Oratore," said Nello, stepping forward at the sound of Tito's voice. "I perceive yesterday's stubble on your chin. Come, come—consign yourself to the priest of all the Muses. Sandro, quick with the lather!"

"In truth, Nello, that is precisely what I most desire at this instant," said Tito, taking a seat. "That was why I turned my steps toward thy shop instead of returning home directly after completing business at the Palazzo."

"Yes, indeed, it is not fitting that you should present yourself to Madonna Romola with a rusty chin and a tangled *zazzera*. Nothing that lacks delicacy ought to approach the Florentine lily; though I see her constantly wandering like a sunbeam among the rags lining our corners—or perhaps she resembles a moonbeam now, for I noted yesterday, when I encountered her, that she looked as pale and worn as that fainting Madonna of Fra Giovanni's. You must attend to this, my bel erudito: she observes too many fasts and vigils in your absence."

Tito gave a melancholy shrug. "It is too true, Nello. She has been depriving herself of half her proper sustenance every day during this famine. But what can a husband do? Her mind has been ignited into flame. A husband's influence is powerless against the Frate's."

"As every other influence is likely to be, including that of the Holy Father," interjected Domenico Cennini, who had entered with Tito. "Pray, have you gleaned anything at Pisa regarding

The air grew heavy with the promise of rain, a dark and threatening cloak settling over Florence. As twilight deepened, Romola returned from the Hospital of San Matteo, accompanied by Maso and a lantern that cast a wavering circle of light around them. Just as they emerged from the gloom of the streets, her husband, Tito, stepped forth from the Monastery of San Marco. He had barely spent the day in her company, having left shortly after arriving in the Via de' Bardi. Without hesitation, he dismissed Maso, whose measured, hurried steps irritated Tito, and offered to escort Romola home.

To Tito, this attentiveness was merely an official duty, performed only when clearly expected. Between them, however, lay a chasm too wide ever to bridge. Their relationship was not one of friction or argument, but of a terrible, static alienation. They navigated life like ships passing in the night, adhering strictly to a pre-arranged script. If Tito desired supper in the old library, now transformed into a banquet hall, Romola arranged it with perfect efficiency; in return, he supplied the necessities for her charitable works, though he never asked how they were used. Yet, despite this functional harmony, they avoided eye contact. Tito feigned a belief that she preferred solitude, playfully attributing to her a nature that shunned his company, all the while keeping his distance from her chambers.

In the fervor of her early reconciliation, after deciding against flight, Romola had tried to restore a bond through open speech. She sought a moral understanding, a foundation of honesty. To Tito, however, salvation from estrangement required something else: a return to the effusive tenderness she had long ago lost. He desired a wife who loved blindly, forgetting their differences, but for Romola, tenderness was impossible where trust was absent. She could remain submissive, gentle, masking any trace of repulsion, but to fake love was a betrayal of her conscience. She was acutely aware of the cost: her husband was a stranger, alienated by the silence between them.

This distance forced her into a position of vigilance regarding secrets he would never willingly share. Regarding politics, Tito assured her that the Medici cause was hopeless and that he served the popular government with genuine conviction—a stance she now shared. Yet, subtle, odor-like impressions gnawed at her peace of mind concerning his dealings with San Marco. She was torn between the fear of confirming her suspicions and the urgent impulse to watch, lest some hidden harm unfold that she might otherwise have prevented.

As they walked, Tito broke the silence. "The business of the day is not finished," he said, his voice smooth. "I will walk you to the door, my Romola, and then I must fulfill another commission. It will take an hour, perhaps longer, before I can return to rest, as I desperately need it."

He chatted lightly of his visit to Pisa, guiding her toward a quiet loggia where a lamp burned before a painting of the Virgin. Suddenly, the street, previously empty, filled with the murmur of many feet and confused voices.

"We shall not reach home dry if we stay in the open," Tito said quickly, hastening her up the steps of the loggia with a start that betrayed his alertness. "Shelter awaits."

"Surely it is pointless to wait for such a drizzle," Romola remarked, surprised.

"No," Tito replied, his eyes fixed on the flickering lamplight. "I felt it growing heavier. Wait a moment."

His mind, trained by constant caution, had spotted the danger before the crowd fully materialized. Through the glow of the lamp, he saw the leader of the approaching group wore a red feather and a sword-hilt that glittered dangerously. This man was Dolfo Spini, the captain of the *Compagnacci*, the very last person Tito wished to meet alone with Romola. Earlier that day, Tito had met Spini, and now, following a premeditated plan, he was to meet him again.

Tito had delivered letters to Savonarola—forged documents, meticulously crafted. One bore the signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples, a powerful influence in Rome favoring the friar. The letters claimed the Cardinal, unwilling to enter Florence due to strong reasons, wished to consult Savonarola at San Casciano, ten miles from the city, before riding out the next morning in humble priestly garb to meet him on the road. The trap was laid: Spini and his band were to ambush the friar at a lonely spot five miles from the gates, seize him along with his companion monk, and hand him over to Milanese cavalry waiting near San Casciano to transport him to Roman territory.

Tito calculated the odds. The Friar was sharp, often guarded, and unlikely to fall for a scheme requiring him to travel alone with only one monk. Yet, the Friar's mind was consumed with the hope of convening a General Council and securing papal support against the Pope. The lure of immediate service from the Cardinal, combined with his desperation, might overcome his natural caution in the brief window allowed for decision.

Tito himself had already interviewed Savonarola, declining to show the letters to anyone else. With great skill, he had hinted that they referred to a rendezvous outside the gates, urging the Friar to seek an armed guard from the Signoria and offering his own services to carry the request discreetly. Savonarola had refused, stating that an armed guard destroyed privacy. His eyes flashed with intensity, and Tito was certain the Friar intended to accept the risk.

Tito cared little for the outcome. Whether the Friar fell or rose, Tito would profit. Diamonds and papal patronage awaited if the Friar failed; if he succeeded, Tito's agency would strengthen his standing with both the Friar and Spini, making him invaluable to the Mediceans.

However, Spini was a dangerous partner. Cunning enough to delight in plots, he lacked the self-command necessary for secrecy. A drinker, prone to sudden outbursts of boisterous affection, Spini might betray the conspiracy through a single slip of the tongue or a fraternal embrace in the dark. Tito's greatest hope was that Spini had not yet seen him. But shadows played tricks; Tito, illuminated briefly by the lamp, had been seen by Spini, who, in turn, had caught a glimpse of Tito. Romola, cloaked in black, had remained unseen, standing behind her husband's shoulder in the corner of the loggia.

Hope vanished instantly.

"Hah! My carrier-pigeon!" Spini's harsh voice grated out, though he pretended to speak softly, his hand clamping onto Tito's shoulder. "Why did you run to hide? You didn't know it was comrades coming. Well, I found you; it saves time. How goes the chase tomorrow? Will the bald game rise? Are the falcons ready?"

A surge of rage threatened Tito, burning hot against this bull-faced accomplice, fit for neither leadership nor execution. His lips turned white, his mind racing for a safe exit. If he hushed Spini, it would raise alarms in Romola's mind; she was not easily silenced. If he repelled him angrily, the drunkard might become savage. Tito chose a third path, proving that Romola still held power over him: the power of dread.

He pressed Romola's hand, pretending to offer her a signal, and turned to Spini with a good-humoured smile. "Yes, my Dolfo, you may prepare in security. But take no trumpets with you."

"Don't be afraid," Spini retorted, slightly piqued. "No need to play Ser Saccente with me. I know where the devil keeps his tail as well as you do." He lowered his voice, blundering with a false sense of secrecy. "What! Did he swallow the bait whole? Didn't the prophetic nose smell the hook?"

"The brute won't be satisfied until he has emptied the bag," Tito thought. Aloud, he smiled. "Swallowed it all as easily as you swallow a cup of Trebbiano. Ha! I see torches. There must be a dead body coming. The pestilence has spread, I hear."

"Santiddio! I hate the sight of those biers. Good-night," Spini muttered, hurrying away.

The torches indeed approached, led by a church dignitary, the Vicario dell'Arcivescovo, whom Tito bowed to respectfully. But the mention of death and plague was Tito's shield, designed to dismiss Spini without warning him to leave. The moment Spini departed, Tito turned to Romola.

"Do not let *bestia*'s words alarm you, my Romola," he said quietly, his voice steady. "We will proceed. I think the rain has not worsened."

Romola trembled with indignant resolve. Her trust in his casual demeanor crumbled. "I will not go on," she declared firmly. "I will not move closer to home until I have security that this treachery will not be carried out."

"At least wait until these torches pass," Tito suggested, his self-command unbroken, though a new dislike for a wife who might thwart him arose within him.

The torches passed, followed by the dignitary. Outwardly, everything appeared normal, but inwardly, Romola felt a terrifying division. If stopping this plot required her to throw herself from the loggia with him, she felt she could do it. The discipline of two years seemed nullified; she felt nothing but the abyss separating them.

Darkness returned, obscuring their faces.

Tito's intricate designs had been hopelessly undone by trifling accidents that no mastermind could foresee. Though it was rare for him to stroll with Romola at dusk, fate had conspired to place her beside him precisely tonight, rendering her presence a supreme nuisance. Life proved to be a labyrinthine game where the finest strategies were routinely thwarted by whimsical chance, as unpredictable as falling dandelion seeds.

His distress stemmed not from the collapse of Spini's conspiracy, but from the treacherous tightrope he now faced regarding his warnings. To alert Savonarola immediately would tether his caution so closely to his recent delivery of forged letters that suspicion would inevitably arise. Yet, to confront Dolfo Spini instantly would require confessing the truth—that Savonarola had altered his intentions. Tito knew Spini's sharp intellect would perceive nothing but a reversal, interpreting Tito's actions as a mere "turnabout" designed to sabotage the plot. Conversely, delaying his warning to Savonarola until morning would likely forfeit the chance to stop Spini, leaving the Compagnacci band to return enraged and disappointed. Calculating the risks, Tito resolved to accept the anger of the conspirators, banking instead on his ability to smooth over Spini by attributing the failure solely to the Friar's prudent caution.

Annoyed and irritable, Tito found himself incapable of smiling, a feat requiring unnatural effort. Determined to avoid another encounter with Romola, he bypassed his home and ventured forth alone.

Inside, Romola remained awake throughout the night, refusing to remove her garments. As the rain intensified, drumming a relentless rhythm against the roof, she found solace in the tempest. The stormy heavens appeared as a divine shield against human machinations, forcing even the most ambitious plots into stasis. Yet, her peace offered little respite to her troubled mind. Doubts assailed her on every front: what deceptions might Tito harbor, what secret agendas remained hidden from her gaze? If trust in Tito endangered others, how futile it was to convince herself otherwise. A nagging guilt plagued her conscience; was she not selfishly clinging to her pride when she hesitated to warn men against her husband? *If* he were indeed a criminal, perhaps her duty lay in standing by him in prison—but could a wife stand idly by while her spouse inflicted harm upon the innocent, when prevention within her grasp? Prayer felt impossible; her mind, agitated by frantic speculation, refused the quietude required for spiritual surrender.

As dawn approached, the fury of the rain subsided, replaced by a gentle breeze that scattered the clouds, revealing a stark, clear morning. Paradoxically, this tranquility rendered her inner turmoil more unbearable. Wrapping her mantle tightly around herself, she rushed to the loggia, gazing desperately at the vast landscape, searching for some sign that might dictate her next move, though she saw nothing but rooftops concealing the streets where Savonarola might walk toward ruin.

Could she summon her godfather to intervene, perhaps prompting him to block the gates without needing specific revelations? No, the delay would likely be fatal. With fresh anguish, Romola realized she had gleaned no concrete details from Tito, and it was already past seven o'clock. There was no alternative but San Marco.

Hurrying down the stairs, she ignored the ill in her care and burst onto the street, racing swiftly along Via de' Bardi toward the Ponte Vecchio. She intended to cut directly through the city's heart; the great Piazza offered a slim possibility of intercepting her husband. By some miracle, he might still confirm the Friar's safety, obviating the need for her dangerous journey to San Marco. Upon reaching the courtyard of the Palazzo Vecchio, she peered eagerly into the pillared space, scanning the square, but the familiar figure etched in her youth and scarred by her current suffering was absent. She pressed onward to the Piazza del Duomo.

Already alive with motion, the square bustled with worshippers ascending and descending the marble steps, groups chatting, and vendors bearing their wares. Amidst this throng, Romola spotted him. On his way from San Marco, Tito had diverted into Nello's shop and now leaned casually against the doorpost. As she drew nearer, she observed him standing with an effortless grace, holding his cap, shaking back his freshly combed hair. The jarring contrast between this languid ease and the bitter anxieties he had sowed convulsed her with indignation; the new vision of his unfeeling hardness deepened her terror. She recognized Cronaca and two other habitués of San Marco standing nearby. A sudden resolve flared within her: *"I shall force him to speak before these men."* Her light step carried her forward before he could retreat, just as Cronaca remarked, "Here comes Madonna Romola."

A faint shock rippled through Tito's frame as he realized he stood face-to-face with his wife. She looked haggard from sleepless vigilance, yet a flash of something other than worry ignited in her eyes as she spoke.

"Is the Frate gone beyond the gates?"

"No," Tito replied, feeling utterly powerless before this woman. He summoned every ounce of self-command necessary to maintain a mask of pure surprise.

"And you are certain that he is not going?" she pressed, her voice trembling slightly.

"I am certain that he is not going."

"That is enough," Romola declared, turning up the steps to seek refuge within the Duomo, hoping the sanctuary of stone would calm her agitation.

Tito watched her retreat, a sensation bordering on hatred burning in his eyes as she disappeared behind the pillars.

Amidst the crowd stood not only devoted followers of the Friar but also Ser Ceccone, the notary. At that moment, much like Tito Melema, Ser Ceccone operated as a covert agent for the Medici. Ser Francesco di Ser Barone, infamous simply as Ser Ceccone, lacked learning, handsomeness, success, or generosity. He was a traitor devoid of charm, and consequently, he held no affection for Tito.

The afternoon sun, low and heavy, cast long, slanting shadows across the floor as Tito returned home. In her narrow chamber, opposite the wooden cabinet, Romola sat over her writing materials, her fingers pausing mid-copy as the daylight began to fail. Before her husband could enter, she intended to extinguish the lamp and retreat into the quiet of her solitude, but the sound of his footsteps cut short her preparations.

He did not knock. Instead, he strode straight to her side, bringing with him a weight of intent that shifted the air in the room. Without removing his cap or mantle, he planted himself before her, resting an elbow heavily on the cabinet, his gaze fixed upon her face with a silence that demanded an answer.

Romola, who had spent the day reassured by the Friar regarding Tito's actual safety, now felt the bitter aftertaste of her own doubt. A wave of penitence washed over her, mingled with indignation at having publicly accused a man who cherished privacy above all. She chided herself for her impulsiveness, realizing that the treacherous duplicity inherent in Florentine politics—a truth her godfather often whispered—could easily explain Tito's association with Spini without implying any personal guilt on his part. For hours, she had wrestled with the hope that a humble confession might break the two-year silence that had grown between them, a silence so absolute that Tito remained unaware of her flight and return, just as she was ignorant of the specific terrors that haunted his nights. They lived around a banquet table where death had once shattered the feast, leaving neither the food nor the memory accessible to the other.

As she looked up, her eyes lowered in a submissive glaze born of self-reproach, Tito's countenance halted her words. There was a subtle, terrifying alteration in his features, a shift from his usual mild tolerance to something harder, colder.

"It seems," Tito began, his voice a cool, liquid stream that sent a chill down her spine, "that it is finally time for us to understand one another." He paused, letting the words hang in the dim air.

"That is what I desire most, Tito," she replied, her voice faint. Her pale, sweet face, stripped of its recent anger and replaced by the timidity of self-questioning, seemed to magnify the dark, formidable strength radiating from her husband.

"You took a step this morning," Tito continued, "which you must now recognize as futile. It exposed you to scrutiny and may entangle me in grave practical difficulties."

"I admit I was too hasty," Romola said, finding a firmness in her tone that surprised her. "I am sorry for any injustice I may have caused." She hoped that acknowledging her error would soften his gaze, clearing the path for the truths she sought to speak.

"I wish you to understand, once and for all," Tito said, his voice unchanging, "that such collisions are utterly incompatible with our station as husband and wife. Consider how you were led to this step, lest you repeat it."

"That depends largely on you, Tito," Romola countered, a spark of defiance igniting within her. It was not what she had planned to say, yet the words escaped before she could check them.

"Perhaps you intend to say," Tito answered smoothly, "that I must ensure nothing in future provokes your unreasonable suspicions. Last night, you were frank enough to declare you hold no faith in me. I am not surprised by the exaggerated conclusions you may draw from slender premises, but I must warn you of the fruit of treating such exaggerations as grounds for interference in affairs of which you are ignorant. Are you following my line of thought?"

He waited for a response.

"Yes," Romola stammered, her cheeks flushing with a hot, irreplaceable resentment at his superior, icy tone.

"Then consider this," Tito pressed. "Before long, some stray word or incident may set your imagination to weaving fictions of crime against me. You might then rush to the Palazzo Vecchio, alarm the Signoria, and set the entire city ablaze with panic. Can you foresee the consequence? Not merely the disgrace of your husband, to which you approach with such reckless courage, but the arrest and ruin of many of Florence's leading men, including Messer Bernardo del Nero."

Tito had arrived at a decisive juncture, and he had crossed the threshold. The color drained from Romola's face, leaving her lips pale—a rare transformation for a woman usually buoyed by spirit rather than fear. Tito sensed his victory.

"You might flatter yourself," he went on, "that you are performing a heroic act of liberation. Yet, you might as well attempt to pick locks with flowery rhetoric as apply such naive notions to the intricate machinery of Florentine politics. The question is no longer whether you can believe in me, but whether, now that you are warned, you will dare to rush headlong, like a blind man clutching a lantern, into complex affairs of which you know nothing."

Romola felt her mind gripped in a vice by Tito's iron will; the possibilities he outlined rose before her with terrible, crystalline clarity.

"I am too rash," she confessed. "I will strive not to be so."

"Remember," Tito insisted, his tone relentless, "that your act of distrust this morning might have borne far more fatal fruits than the sacrifice of your husband, a fate you have learned to contemplate without blinking."

"Tito, it is not so!" Romola burst forth, rising and moving closer to him with a desperate resolve to speak the truth. "It is false that I would willingly sacrifice you. It has been the greatest effort of my life to cling to you. I fled in anger two years ago, yet returned because I was bound to you more deeply than to anything on earth. But it is useless. You shut me out from your mind. You treat me as a being too unreasonable to share in the knowledge of your affairs. You will reveal nothing to me."

She appeared like a good angel pleading with a sinner, bending her face toward him with wide, imploring eyes and laying her hand upon his arm. Yet, Romola's touch and gaze failed to stir a single fiber of tenderness in her husband. The good-humored, tolerant Tito, capable of little hatred or impatience, accustomed to gentleness toward the world, felt himself growing strangely hard toward this wife whose presence had once been his strongest anchor. Beneath his soft disposition lay a masculine effectiveness of intellect and purpose, an energy akin to the sharpness of a blade, working steadily without needing great momentum. Romola possessed an energy of her own that thwarted his, and no man, unless exceptionally weak, endures being thwarted by his spouse. Marriage must be either a union of sympathy or a contest of conquest.

No emotion flickered across Tito's face as he heard Romola mention her departure for the first time. His lips merely hardened slightly as he offered a faint, cold smile.

"My Romola, when certain conditions are ascertained, we must accept them. No amount of wishing can fill the Arno, as your people say, nor transform a plum into an orange. I have not observed that prayer holds much sway in such matters. You are constituted to possess strong impressions inaccessible to reason; I cannot share those impressions, and consequently, you have withdrawn all trust from me. You have changed toward me, and thus, I have changed toward you. It is useless to look backward. We must simply adapt ourselves to these altered conditions."

"Tito, it would not be useless for us to speak openly," Romola said, her voice trembling with the exasperation of striking one's fist against a lifeless, immovable stone. "It was the sense of deception within you that changed me, and that separation keeps us apart. And it is not true that I changed first. You changed toward me the night you first donned that chain-mail armor. You had some secret from me—it concerned that old man—and I saw him again yesterday. Tito," she continued, her tone shifting to an agony of entreaty, "if you would once tell me everything, whatever it may be, I would not mind the pain. Perhaps then there will be no wall between us! Is it not possible that we could begin a new life?"

For the first time, a flash of genuine emotion crossed Tito's face. He stood perfectly still, yet the flash seemed to whiten him. He ignored Romola's plea entirely. After a brief pause, he spoke quietly.

"Your impetuosity regarding trifles, Romola, possesses a freezing influence that could chill the baths of Nero." These cutting words caused Romola to shrink, drawing herself up into her customary, self-sustaining posture. Tito continued. "If by 'that old man' you mean the madman Jacopo di Nola, who attempted my life and lodged a strange accusation against me, of which I told you nothing because it would have alarmed you to no avail, know this: the wretch has died in prison. I saw his name listed among the dead."

"I know nothing of his accusation," Romola said. "But I know he is the man I saw with a rope around his neck in the Duomo—the man whose portrait Piero di Cosimo painted, grasping your arm as he saw him grasp it the day the French entered, the night you first wore the armor."

"And where is he now, pray?" Tito asked, though he remained pale, governing his emotions with practiced control.

"He was lying lifeless in the street, dying of starvation," Romola revealed. "I revived him with bread and wine. I brought him to our door, but he refused to enter. I gave him some money, and he

The winter days slipped past Romola like white ships drifting silently along a distant shore—each bearing a hidden cargo of impending change, indistinguishable from the last in their monotony. Tito's whisper had infused her curiosity about public affairs with a chilling dread, leading her to prefer ignorance over knowledge. Though the menacing German Emperor had vanished and Florence's immediate plight seemed eased, a profound distress lingered. Consequently, Romola's active duties showed little diminution; in fact, they served as her sole sanctuary from a mind plagued by uncertainty.

She could not allow herself to celebrate the relief that had arrived in extremity, nor the apparent vindication it brought to the Friar's faction. Francesco Valori, the fiery leader of the *Piagnoni*, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year and was already rushing to enact his radical reforms within his brief two-month tenure. On the surface, this looked like a fortification of liberty and a shield for Savonarola. Yet, Romola's intuition, sharpened by fear, sensed that this triumph was merely an unconscious incubation of mixed germs destined to bloom tragically. By Carnival time, shortly after mid-February, her premonition crystallized into reality: the Medici ceased their passivity and began actively maneuvering for the election of Bernardo del Nero as the new Gonfaloniere.

On the final morning of the Carnival, between ten and eleven, Romola ventured forth as promised toward the Corso degli Albizzi to retrieve her cousin Brigida. Together, they intended to depart from the Via de' Bardi in the afternoon, securing a vantage point in the Piazza della Signoria reserved by Tito. They awaited a spectacle of unprecedented novelty, one that demanded the gaze of every Florentine eye. The *Piagnoni* were determined to dictate the spirit of the holiday. Despite the futile efforts of Dolfo Spini and his associates to resurrect traditional masques and pranks seasoned with indecency, the city declared Christ King, rendering such frivolities impossible.

Romola moved with a languid air, the mindset of an adult accompanying a cherished child on a tour of sights. While the day marked an epoch in carnival history, this phase of reform failed to ignite her enthusiasm. She remained unaware that it was simultaneously an epoch in her own life, wherein another destiny was visibly weaving itself into hers, no longer secret but manifest.

Choosing to traverse the vast Piazza alone first, she took in the panorama. Dominating the square stood a monstrous, multi-colored edifice resembling a colossal fir-tree or pyramid, sixty feet tall. Its shelves widened toward the base until they circled eighty yards. The square teemed with life: slender youths clad in white garments, olive wreaths upon their brows, drifted near the base carrying baskets brimming with vibrant objects. Beside them, mature figures—some in monastic robes, others in the loose tunics and crimson caps of artists—assisted, inspected, or retreated to observe the wondrous assembly from afar. A notable cluster, including Piero di Cosimo, stood on the marble steps of Orgagna's Loggia, separated from the scene by an aura of disdain.

As Romola approached, she scrutinized the myriad items arranged in ascending order from the base to the summit. There were tapestries and brocades of scandalous design, paintings and sculptures deemed liable to incite vice. Boards and tables for divers games lay alongside printing blocks, dice, and gaming paraphernalia. Worldly music scores and instruments of every variety—lutes, drums, cymbals, trumpets—were piled together. Masks and costumes from former carnivals mingled with handsome editions of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarch, and Pulci, works of vanity and impurity. Implements of female coquetry abounded: pots of rouge, artificial hair, mirrors, perfumes, powders, and sheer veils designed to invite leering glances. At the apex sat an unflattering likeness of a mythical Venetian merchant, rumored to have paid heavily for this collection of "marketable abominations." Soaring above him in grotesque ugliness was the symbolic figure of the ancient, depraved Carnival.

This arrangement prepared for a novel kind of bonfire: the Burning of Vanities. Concealed within the pyramid's core was a bountiful supply of dry fuel and gunpowder. On this final evening, the pile was to be ignited to the sound of trumpets, sending the ugly visage of old Carnival tumbling into the flames amidst chants of reformed triumph.

Such a grand gesture could scarcely have been orchestrated without a unique organization initiated by Savonarola two years prior. The mass of Florentine youth was no longer abandoned to their natural inclinations toward mischief and crude dissipation. Trained under Fra Domenico, Savonarola's lieutenant, these lads and striplings—the hope of Florence—were to speak only pure words. Their zeal for an unseen good was to eclipse the tepidity of their elders, knowing no pleasure but that of an angelic nature: singing divine praises and walking in white robes. These fresh-faced troops were the architects of the regenerated merriment, a sacred parody of the old festivals. Where once bonfires consumed earthly delights, now a fire would consume impurity. Where symbolic processions once paraded worldly vanity, new processions displayed white robes, red crosses, and olive wreaths—emblems of peace and innocent joy. Instead of dancing rings beneath open skies accompanied by loose songs, there would be circles of monks and laity united in divine joy, singing hymns. Collections from the streets, formerly for superfluous feasts, were now gathered to feed the hungry, alongside the gathering of the *Anathema*: the vanities destined for the great pyramidal bonfire.

Regiments of beardless inquisitors scoured the houses, urging the surrender of the *Anathema*. Perhaps, after yielding obvious sins, the Madonna of the household still clutched small red spheres imported from the Levant, meant to fake a sudden bloom upon a sallow cheek? If so, she was to cast them into the basket of doom. Or perhaps she wore ringlets and coils of "dead hair"? Then she must bring them to the street door, holding them in her hands, not upon her head, to publicly renounce the *Anathema* that masked respectable aging with a ghastly mimicry of youth. In return, she would hear fresh, youthful voices bestow a blessing upon her home.

These inquisitors, organized into tiny battalions, likely embraced their work with eagerness. Coercing the populace through shame or spiritual peltage into relinquishing cherished possessions is a form of piety readily adopted by the boyish mind. Even if some stubborn sinners threatened whips or cudgels, such defiance added to the excitement. Savonarola himself seemed acutely aware of the difficulties inherent in training these children, weighing the noble yearning for great ends against an imperfect grasp of means, forcing a reliance on supernatural constraint as the only secure hope. Florentine youth had harbored vile habits and foul tongues; initially, hearing them shout "*Viva Gesù!*" seemed an unmixed blessing. Yet, Savonarola eventually confessed from the pulpit that there was "too much shouting" of the sacred name, lest it fall into contempt, ordering a cessation until the next feast.

Nevertheless, as the long stream of robed youthfulness, adorned with little red crosses and olive garlands, had proceeded to the Duomo at dawn to receive communion from Savonarola's hands, it presented a vision of beauty. Doubtless, many of those young souls were storing memories of hope and awe that would protect them from viewing their civic labor as mere vulgarity. Conscious obedience, after all, represents an advance upon lawlessness, and these boys became the generation of men who fought and endured greatly in the Republic's final struggle. Now, in the interim hours between communion and dinner, they were making their final rounds to collect alms and vanities, explaining why Romola observed those slim, white-clad figures circling the base of the great pyramid.

"What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?" a brusque voice murmured close to her ear. "Your *Piagnoni* will render hell a pleasant prospect for us if they are permitted to rule the earth as they please. It takes a cudgel dragged from the mountains to witness painters like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio aiding in the burning of color from life in such a manner."

"My good Piero," Romola replied, lifting her gaze to smile at the grim man, "even you must find solace in seeing these trifles consumed. Observe those gewgaws, wigs, and pots of rouge: I have heard you speak with as much indignation against them as Fra Girolamo himself."

"What then?" Piero snapped, turning sharply upon her. "I never claimed a woman ought to paint a black patch against the background. *Va!* Madonna Antigone, it is shameful for a woman of your hair and shoulders to indulge in such nonsense—leave it to women unworthy of being painted. What! Has the Most Holy Virgin herself not always been attired with care? That is the doctrine of the Church! Talk of heresy, indeed! And I ask you, what would the excellent Messer Bardo have said regarding the burning of divine poets by these friars, who are no better imitation of men than onions with their bulbs turned upward? Look at that Petrarch sticking beside a pot of rouge: do these fools presume that heavenly Laura was a painted harlot? And Boccaccio? Do you mean to say, Madonna Romola—you who are fit to be a model for a wise Saint Catherine of Egypt

That morning, the Piazza was filled with the bustle of the Carnival. Among the throng, a figure approached, instantly recognizable not by the somber black of mourning, but by the vibrant hues of old red, green, and white. This was Tessa. Though she no longer resided on the Hill of San Giorgio following the tumultuous events involving Baldassarre and Tito, she had secured a new abode. Tito, seeking peace for both her sake and his own, had found her a quiet, airy house bordering the vast gardens north of the Porta Santa Croce.

Tessa had not ventured forth without explicit permission. The night prior, Tito had accompanied her. She had suppressed a rising tide of emotion within her breast until she beheld him in a state of radiant tranquility. With one arm encircling the sturdy Lillo and the other resting gently upon her shoulder, he helped steady the toddler Ninna on her wobbly legs. The profound weariness that had previously settled upon his brow and lips when he returned home had completely evaporated. Tessa, quick to master the subtle arts of persuasion required to navigate life with Naldo while retaining a measure of autonomy, understood perfectly how to read the shifting landscapes of her husband's countenance.

Indeed, the charming, gentle-humored spirit of Tito, who had awakened beneath the Loggia de' Cerchi five years ago unburdened by deception, rarely manifested in anyone as fully as in the man currently seated in the straight-backed, carved armchair he had procured for his comfort. In these fleeting moments, Tito felt a swelling sense of relief. He required no guile toward Tessa; her innocence and ignorance rendered her incapable of suspecting his duplicity. The soft, childish coos of "Babbo" were music to his ears. When he contemplated departing Florence, he could never imagine abandoning Tessa and their little ones behind. He harbored a deep, tender affection for these round-faced, wide-eyed creatures who clung to him, untouched by malice. Wherever genuine affection takes root, it blooms like a green leaf or flower, pure and breathing purity regardless of the soil. Conversely, poor Romola, despite her tireless self-sacrifice, inadvertently contributed to the hardening of Tito's nature, chilling it with a disdain that seemed impossible for him to harbor. Yet, Tessa kept the fountains of kindness flowing.

"Ninna is remarkably well-behaved without me," Tessa began, feeling a plea swell in her throat as she guided Ninna to sit on the floor. "I can leave her with Monna Lisa whenever I please. And if she cries in the cradle, Lillo is quite sensible; he immediately goes to thump Monna Lisa."

Lillo, whose large dark eyes appeared even deeper in shade against his light-brown curls, leaped from his father's knee and promptly demonstrated his wit by striking Monna Lisa, who sat at the far end of the room, slowly shaking her head over her spinning wheel. "A wonderful boy!" Tito exclaimed, laughing. "Is he not?" Tessa asked eagerly, stepping closer. "Might I venture to see the Carnival tomorrow, merely for an hour or two? Mightn't I?"

"Oh, you wicked pigeon!" Tito chuckled, pinching her cheek. "Are those your longings? What business does an old woman with two children have with carnivals?"

"But old ladies enjoy seeing sights," Tessa replied, her lower lip trembling slightly. "Monna Lisa mentioned she would like to go, though she is so deaf she cannot hear what follows behind her, and she fears we cannot manage both children."

"No, indeed, Tessa," Tito said, his expression turning grave. "You must not consider taking the children into the crowded streets, or I shall be angry."

"But I have never visited the Piazza without leave," Tessa pleaded in a frightened voice, "since Holy Saturday, and I believe Nofri is dead, for you recall the poor *madre* perished. I shall never forget the Carnival I witnessed once; it was so lovely—all roses, a king, a queen beneath them, and singing. I preferred it to San Giovanni."

"There is nothing comparable anymore, my Tessa," Tito responded. "They intend to construct a bonfire in the Piazza—that is all. However, I cannot allow you to walk alone in the evenings."

"Oh, no! I do not wish to go in the evening. I only desire to witness the procession by daylight. Surely there *will* be a procession?"

"Yes, in a manner," Tito conceded. "As lively as a flight of cranes. Do not expect roses and glittering monarchs, my Tessa. Nevertheless, I suppose any assembly of people termed a procession will delight your blue eyes. Furthermore, there is a structure they have erected in the Piazza de' Signori for the bonfire; you might wish to view that. But return home early, and maintain the appearance of a solemn little old matron. Should you encounter men adorned with feathers and swords, steer clear of them; they are exceedingly fierce and prone to decapitating elderly women."

"Santa Madonna! Whence do they emerge?" Tessa gasped, though Tito's laughter reassured her. "But I shall keep away from them. Only..." she whispered, leaning close to Naldo's ear, "...if I might take Lillo? He is very sensible."

"But who will thump Monna Lisa if she remains unheard?" Tito asked, struggling to suppress a smile while maintaining a serious facade. "No, Tessa, you could not safeguard Lillo amidst a crowd, and he is too heavy for you to carry."

"It is true," Tessa admitted with a touch of sadness. "He enjoys running off. I forgot that. Then I shall go alone. But now, observe Ninna; you have neglected her too much."

Ninna was a blue-eyed tot, at that teetering, tumbling stage of development—a solid, reliable entity akin to a loaded die that invariably finds its base, warranting accurate prediction. Tessa snatched her up, and when Tito finally bestowed due attention on the infant's recent dental developments, Tessa whispered, "Shall I purchase some confetti for the children?"

Tito extracted a handful of small coins from his purse (*scarsella*) and deposited them into her palm.

"That will procure endless treats," Tessa declared, delighted by the abundance. "I shall scarcely mind going without Lillo so long as I bring him something."

Thus, Tessa departed toward the great Piazza where the bonfire awaited. She considered the February breeze insufficiently cold to necessitate additional covering beyond her green woolen gown. A mantle would have been oppressive, concealing a new necklace and a new clasp, mounted in silver—the sole ornamental gifts Tito had ever bestowed upon her. Tessa gave no thought to displaying her figure, for no one had ever complimented it; however, she was convinced her jewelry represented the finest ever worn by the wealthiest peasant woman. She adjusted her white hood to ensure the front of her necklace was prominently displayed. These ornaments, she reasoned, must inspire respect for her status as the wife of a man capable of affording them.

She trotted along cheerfully in the February sunlight, preoccupied with purchasing sweets for her little ones to fill her small basket, oblivious to any observers. Yet, her descent from her upper story into the street had been noted. She was being followed by a man who had frequently waited in vain to determine if Tessa indeed resided in the house to which he had once trailed Tito. Baldassarre carried a package of yarn, a constant employment to earn his meager sustenance and keep the sacred flame of vengeance alive. He had made this detour, as he often did, specifically to pass the house where he had shadowed Tito at night. His prolonged imprisonment had sharpened his timidity and conviction regarding a diabolical fortune favoring Tito. He dared not pursue him openly, fearing exposure to ridicule and a return to prison unless concealed within a crowd or darkness. He felt an instinctive horror that if Tito's gaze fell upon him, he would again be castigated, stripped of his weapon, and imprisoned helplessly. His fierce resolve had become as stealthy as a serpent, relying on a single strike of the fang. Justice remained weak and friendless; he could not again hear the voice echoing promises of vengeance in the Duomo. Though he had returned repeatedly, that voice appeared stifled by cunning, strong-armed wickedness. For a considerable time, Baldassarre's primary objective had been to verify whether Tito still wore armor, for at last, his fading hope would have been satisfied by a successful thrust on this side of the grave. Yet, he would never again risk his precious knife. It was a weary existence, waiting for a chance to answer this question by brushing Tito's back in the crush of the street. Since then, the knowledge that his sharp steel was useless, and that his only hope lay in some novel scheme, had weighed heavily upon his enfeebled mind. A dim vision of securing one of the two wives to assist him haunted him continually, only to slip away again. The wife who had lived on the hill was no longer there. If he could locate her anew, he might seize a thread of a project and forge a path toward greater clarity.

And this morning, he had succeeded. He was certain now of her location. As he walked, slightly bowed under his burden of yarn, yet keeping the green-and-white figure in his sight, his mind dwelled upon her and her circumstances, as feeble eyes scrutinize lines and colors, striving to interpret them into coherent meaning.

Tessa traversed various long streets, encountering no sign of the Carnival other

As Romola spoke of someone expecting her, she referred to her cousin, Brigida. Yet, the depth of her relative's need remained entirely unsuspected by her. Together, returning toward the Piazza, they had spotted a gathering of lads halting before Tessa. Upon approaching close enough to witness the simple country girl's distress, Romola paused, telling Brigida, "Wait for me a moment, cousin." To this, Monna Brigida replied hastily, "No, I cannot proceed. Come with me to Boni's shop; I shall return there."

In truth, Monna Brigida harbored a dual torment. On one side lay a keen consciousness of the "vanities" adorning her person—the rouge, the false hair, the pearl embroidery. On the other surged a profound alarm that the *Piagnoni* (Weepers) might be correct in their conviction that these adornments damaged the soul. Their grave perspective hung in the air like a pervasive odor, distorting reality until nothing tasted as it once had. Standing next to her youthful and beautiful cousin, who lived a life suggesting rigorous moral demands, the fifty-five-year-old widow found her usual sources of satisfaction threatened. Her joy had long been derived from how she viewed herself and how she believed others perceived her appearance. Maintaining buoyant spirits under such shifting tides required a vast reservoir of imagination.

Recently, Brigida had fought frequent battles at her toilette. Was it truly worthwhile to apply rouge and braid her hair if her soul could prosper better without them? However, when she raised her hand-mirror and beheld a sallow visage with sagging cheeks and crow's-feet impossible to disguise through lip-smiles, her resolve crumbled. She parted her grey hair, arranging it simply around her face in the manner of the *Piagnoni*, only to feel her courage evaporate. Monna Berta, though only fifty-two, possessed a superior vitality that would surely erupt into laughter at Brigida, calling her an ancient hag. Such an insult, delivered by a younger woman, would cut deeply. Furthermore, every non-*Piagnona* would likely shrink from her, and men would address her with the deference reserved for grandmothers. At fifty-five, she reasoned, a woman was not yet truly old; merely a touch of makeup sufficed to mask age. Thus, the rouge, the braids, and the embroidered *berretta* returned. Satisfied with this familiar transformation, she felt secure. Today, however, as she prepared for the *Piagnone* Carnival, the internal struggle between fear and longing proved too great. Seeking refuge in the druggist's shop to avoid the collectors of the *Anathema*, she hoped to bypass the ordeal while Romola remained absent. Yet, her retreat lacked sufficient speed. Even before she turned away, she had been sighted by the white-robed boys trailing behind the crowd that had wheeled toward Tessa. Perhaps their eagerness to seize her stemmed from the knowledge that another member of their troop had recently confronted a figure bearing the *Anathema* more visibly.

Among these youths, several belonged to the youngest cohort, undergoing special training. A small boy of ten, adorned with an olive wreath above cherubic cheeks and wide brown eyes, embodied a specific kind of childhood solemnity. His imagination was seized by a hovering awe of existence, sensing that great consequences hinged on every act of goodness or wickedness. Despite this reverence, his longings leaned toward mischief and mastery. He was the first to intercept Monna Brigida, placing himself squarely in her path. Anger flared within Brigida, and she scanned the surroundings for an exit, but none existed nearby. A flight now would only exacerbate her plight.

It was not the cherubic toddler who first addressed her, but a youth of fifteen gripping the handles of a wide basket.

"Venerable mother!" he began, his voice earnest. "The Blessed Jesus commands you to surrender the *Anathema* you bear. That cap embroidered with pearls, those jewels securing your false hair—they must be given up and sold for the poor. Cast the hair itself aside as a lie worthy only of the fire. Surely, beneath your silk mantle, you wear other treasures?"

"Yes, lady," chimed in the youth at the opposite handle, who had memorized many of Fra Girolamo's sermons, "these burdens are too heavy for you. They weigh heavier than a millstone, dragging you toward damnation. Will you deck yourself in the hunger of the poor and boast of carrying God's curse upon your brow?"

"In truth, you are old, *buona madre*," intoned the little boy in a sweet soprano. "You appear frightfully ugly with the red on your cheeks and that glistening black hair, along with those fine trinkets. It is only Satan who takes pleasure in seeing you. Your Angel grieves; He wishes you to wipe away the red."

The small fellow snatched a soft silk scarf from the basket and extended it toward Monna Brigida, offering it as her guardian angel desired. Her anger and humiliation rapidly surrendered to a spiritual terror. Monna Berta and the usual cloud of high-society observers were nowhere to be seen. Instead, she was encircled by young monitors clad in white robes, wreaths, and red crosses. Their uncanny candor and the sheer awesomeness of their unusual garb struck her with dread. With Fra Cristoforo of Santa Croce absent to bolster her skepticism of Dominican teachings, she was helplessly gripped by a vague, overwhelming sensation that a supreme warning had arrived. Unconsciously considering any alternative action, she accepted the offered scarf and, with trembling submission, wiped her cheeks.

"It is well, madonna," declared the second youth. "It is a holy beginning. Once you have stripped your head of these vanities, the dew of heavenly grace shall descend upon it." As his words grew infused with mischief, he reached for one of the jeweled pins fastening her braids to the *berretta*. He plucked it free. The heavy black plait cascaded down over Monna Brigida's face, pulling the rest of her headgear forward. This served as a final catalyst for hesitation; she raised her hands frantically, undone the remaining fastenings, and cast her beloved crimson-velvet *berretta*, with its unparalleled embroidery of seed-pearls, into the basket of doom. She stood revealed as an unrouged woman, her grey hair pushed back from a countenance where the deep lines of age had decisively conquered *embonpoint*.

Yet, the *berretta* was not permitted to rest in the basket. With impish fervor, the youngsters lifted it, holding it up pitilessly, allowing the false hair to dangle.

"Behold, venerable mother," proclaimed the taller lad, "how ugly falsehoods you have liberated yourselves from! And now you resemble the Blessed Saint Anna, the Mother of the Holy Virgin."

Thoughts of immediate entry into a convent, vanishing from the world forever, raced through Monna Brigida's mind. Nothing remained possible for her save the guarding of her soul. Of course, spectators laughed, needing no glance around to confirm their amusement. Perhaps, she mused, it would prove better to be forced to contemplate Paradise. But the notion that the cherished, accustomed world was no longer within her grasp summoned a cluster of hard tears, just enough to moisten her elderly eyes. Through a mist, she discerned a large, rough hand clutching a red cross, suddenly thrust before her over the shoulders of the boys, accompanied by a strong, guttural voice.

"Only four *quattrini*, madonna, blessing included! Purchase it. You will find comfort in it now that your wig is gone. *Deh!* What do we sinners accomplish all our lives? Making soup in a basket and receiving but the scum for our stomachs. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four *quattrini*; the profit amounts to no more than the scent of a coin, and it goes to the poor."

Confused and vision blurred, Monna Brigida moved toward the further submission of extracting money from her embroidered pouch, currently concealed by her silk mantle, when the circle surrounding her—which she had not yet conceived of escaping—suddenly parted. Into the gap stepped a figure as welcome as an angel releasing bolts from a prison cell.

"Romola, look at me!" cried Monna Brigida in a pitiful tone, extending both hands.

The white-robed troop was already drifting away, possessing a faint awareness that their zeal regarding the headgear had been excessive, thereby affording a dispensation from any further demands for penitential offerings.

"My dear cousin, do not be distressed," said Romola, overcome with pity yet barely restraining a smile at the sudden apparition of her kinswoman in a genuine, natural guise, so strikingly contrasted with all former memories. She removed the black drapery from her own head and draped it over Monna Brigida's shoulders. "There," she continued soothingly, "no one will notice you now. Let us turn down the Via del Palagio and go directly home."

Hastening away, Monna Brigida clutched Romola's hand tightly, as if seeking a firmer assurance of her actual presence.

"Oh, my Romola, my dear child!" exclaimed the short, stout woman, hurrying with frequent steps to match the majestic stride of her companion beside her. "What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!"

"Yes, yes; buy a cross!" echoed the guttural voice. Rough

To Romola, the chaotic whirlwind of the Carnival offered little more than a shadowy duty: the care of her cousin Brigida, whose frailty and surrender to age required a gentle, steady hand. Yet, beneath this domestic quietude, the coming Lenten season loomed like a storm cloud, charging her mind with a feverish intensity.

Bernardo del Nero had been elected Gonfaloniere. Through immense effort, the Medicean faction had secured a fragile victory, a triumph that deepened Romola's foreboding. She sensed a shadowy machinery at work, a scheme brewing in the silence of the next two months that threatened to bloom either into catastrophic success or ruinous betrayal. With every gray dawn that crept into her chamber, the same chilling dread returned, a ghost that clung to her as she traversed the cobblestones en route to the early sermon in the Duomo. There, however, amidst the throng, the cold shiver faded, replaced by the adrenaline of battle—the same way soldiers cease to fear death when the clash of steel begins.

Inside the vast cathedral, Romola stood within a tempest of passion that echoed far beyond Florence's walls. Savonarola preached the final series of Lenten sermons permitted before exile, aware that excommunication hung like a sword above his head. He had crossed the Rubicon, ready to defy it. His words were a terrible mirror, reflecting the rot of the Church without sugar-coating or euphemisms. With growing conviction, he heralded the advent of renovation—a time of universal uprising against corruption. His gaze flickered between two futures: sometimes he envisioned himself as the trumpet of a glorious revolution, shaking the corpse of the Church into new life; at others, he saw only the path to martyrdom, viewing this mortal coil merely as a watch until the dawn after death.

Even the minds of the skeptical, like Machiavelli, who read the Friar's character through a lens devoid of nobility, could not ignore the gravity of his stance. But for Romola, whose kinship with his fervor convinced her of his genuine magnanimity, the crisis felt intimately personal. It infused her daily labors with a transcendent glow. Famine still gnawed at Florence, distress grew weary of bearing, and plague hovered on the horizon. Walking among the sick, the starving, and the murmuring crowds, Romola found solace not in pity alone, but in the belief of a heroism striving toward sublime ends. Her acts of compassion, though feeble like dew upon weeds, served as preparation for a harvest yet unseen.

Yet, the majestic music of the Friar's voice contained dissonant notes. From those initial days of radiant hope—when he preached peace, charity, and the coming of a French deliverer—a sharp turn had occurred. Political intrigue proved too stubborn to forget; the dream of French salvation had collapsed, turning into a wound. Hostility, both petty and monumental, now besieged the Prophet with renewed vigor.

Consequently, the spirit of contention seeped into his sermons. Urged by the clamor of the crowd, he shifted from denying the desire for miracles to asserting that Divine power would soon validate his prophecy through a sign. As his moods swayed rapidly between visions of triumphant good and the grim reality of prevailing evil, his threats against tyrants and corrupt clergy gathered momentum, fueled by personal exasperation and indignant zeal. In the career of a great orator yielding to the heat of the moment, the internal war between selfish and unselfish emotions is laid bare, written in fire.

While these jarring tones troubled Romola, they resonated profoundly with another listener: Baldassarre. Having discovered the Frate's return, he attended frequently, drinking in the threats of a voice that sounded like justice itself. He watched Romola with a predator's patience, hoping for a shift in circumstances—or perhaps a softening in her spirit—that would justify an interview. Twice their eyes had met in the nave; once, his dark gaze had fixed upon hers. She wished to avoid him, yet she awaited his reappearance like a harbinger of doom. Whatever revelation he held was now a secondary worry compared to the crushing weight of the present.

As the stirring Lent drew to a close, April neared its end, and nothing materialized to fulfill Romola's premonitions. Rumors swirled from Rome regarding Piero de' Medici, but Bernardo's tenure was nearing expiration. It was only on the twenty-seventh, as she ventured out on her errands of mercy, that fate intervened. A messenger from Camilla Rucellai, one of Florence's foremost "seers," demanded her immediate presence on matters of the highest urgency.

Romola recoiled. She despised the shrill volubility of these illuminated women, and Camilla specifically terrified her due to rumors of revelations hostile to Bernardo. Her instinct screamed refusal, a desire to block out knowledge that would only heavy her burdens. Yet, habit had forged a chain around her impulses; she had learned to obey external claims rather than follow her own rebellious heart. Reproaching herself for cowardice, she complied and walked straight to Camilla.

She found the nervous, grey-haired woman in a chamber dressed like a convent cell. Thin fingers gripped Romola as she sat, and a loud whisper struck her like a blow, causing a physical shrinking that made sitting impossible. Camilla possessed a vision, revealed to her by Romola's Angel: secrets concerning her godfather, Bernardo, which, if exposed, could save the Republic. As she spoke, her voice climbed in pitch, urging Romola to obey her Angel and sever ties with the enemy of God.

Romola's nature was massive and slow-moving, usually calm and self-possessed, but she held a constitutional hatred for the shallow excitability of women like Camilla, whose faculties seemed entirely consumed by fantasy. Before Camilla finished, Romola sprang up, attempting to wrench her arm free. It was useless. The prophetess held on like a crab, and Romola's resistance only spurred her into a shrill declaration of further visions to corroborate the first. Christ Himself had appeared, commanding her to send orders to certain officials to cast Bernardo del Nero from the window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Even Fra Girolamo, she claimed, knew of it and dared not deny its divine origin.

"And since then," Camilla cried, her treble voice straining upward, wild eyes fixed on Romola's face, "the Blessed Infant has placed a wafer of sweetness on my tongue, token of His pleasure!"

"Let me go!" Romola roared, her voice deep with fury. "God grant you are mad! Else, you are detestably wicked!"

Her violent struggle finally broke Camilla's grip. Wrenching free, Romola fled the room, rushing down the street without pause until she reached the church of the Badia. She slipped behind the curtain under the ancient stone arch, finding a sanctuary shut off from the noise and haste of the world, a place where thoughts of eternal peace persisted amidst turmoil.

Kneeling on the step before Filippino Lippi's serene Virgin, visiting Saint Bernard, she waited for the inner tumult to subside. The thought that pressed hardest on her was Camilla's claim to know Savonarola's countenance of her wicked folly. Romola did not believe the Friar had sanctioned the murder of Bernardo as a divine command; she was certain there was falsehood or error in that charge. Savonarola had grown severe in his views on resisting malcontents, yet strict law and order were the bedrock of his politics. If he truly foresaw the fatal effects of such visions, and distrusted spirit-seeing women so deeply, why did he not publicly denounce these revelations that cast darkness over the Supreme Will?

The answer tore at her with painful clarity: he was fettered internally by the consciousness that such revelations were inseparable from his own visions, and externally by the fear of raising a cry against himself, even among his own party, as a suppressor of Divine inspiration shared with his confidential seer, Fra Salvestro.

Kneeling with her face buried, Romola endured a sickening moment. The enthusiasm that had been her only energy now seemed bound up with vain dreams and willful blindness. Her mind drifted back to the sturdy worldly sense, the dignified prudence, and the untheoretic virtues of her godfather, who might be treated like Agag for holding a more restricted form of government and refusing to forget old ties to the banished Medici. But with this thought arose the presentiment of a plot to restore the Medici. Then, again, she felt the popular party was half-justified in its suspicion. To keep Florence pure and avert vicious rule was a sacred cause, and the Frate was right there. But at this moment, her assent was given unwillingly. Her heart recoiled from a righteousness allied to so much narrowness, a judgment that measured men by superficial symbols rather than the humanity within.

Affection and respect clung to her godfather, linking her to the memories of her father, who also opposed the division of men into sheep and goats based on political or religious markers. Ideas, no matter how powerful, remain weak agents unless dissolved in the solvent of feeling. The great struggle of thought is always foreshadowed in the struggle of the affections, seeking justification for love and hope.

Had Romola's intellect been less perceptive, her early loving associations would have forbidden her acceptance of Savonarola's exclusivity. She had simply felt that his mind suggested a deeper, more efficacious truth than any other, granting her patience for his flaws so long as

"I desire to speak with you," Baldassarre said, breaking the silence as Romola regarded him with a quiet, suspended hope. It was evident he had trailed her, waiting patiently for this exact moment. "At last, you intend to learn the truth."

"Yes," she replied, offering a compliance that mirrored the penitent bowing of a soul under sentence. "Yet you seek a place where no ear may catch our words?"

"Where *he* shall not stumble upon us," Baldassarre murmured, glancing nervously over his shoulder. "Out, into the open air, away from the crowded streets."

"I occasionally visit San Miniato at this hour," Romola offered. "If you will, let us depart together. The path is long, yet it affords solitude."

He bowed slightly. Romola turned and walked. To a lesser spirit, venturing alone with a man whose countenance bore the marks of the madness Tito accused him of, would have been terrifying. But Romola harbored no fear for her own safety; rather, she welcomed the distance, viewing it as a buffer against the next blow fate intended to strike.

As twilight deepened and the sun dipped low in the western sky, she halted on rugged ground beneath the shadow of ancient cypresses, scanning the slopes for Baldassarre. He approached, weary, and collapsed onto a ledge of stony earth. His robust frame, once so vigorous when he first appeared coiled with the rope in the Duomo, now sagged. The climb had drawn out a trembling frailty in his step, and his gaze held a vacant, helpless mistiness.

"The ascent is steep," Romola said, her tone soft with compassion as she sat beside him. "Pray, tell me if hunger has sapped your strength."

He twisted his head, fixing his stare upon her, yet when the moment of speech arrived, his tongue failed him. He struggled to form the words that carried his burden, leaving Romola motionless, lest any sign of impatience betray her heart. To the untrained eye, he appeared merely a common, neglected old man. Yet, having grown accustomed to the sorrows of the downtrodden, she pondered deeply the weight of his troubles. Slowly, a focus returned to his eyes, sharpening into a sudden, emphatic resolve.

"Aye!" he cried abruptly. "You would have been my daughter!"

A flash of crimson swept across Romola's cheeks, vanishing almost instantly, leaving her lips pale and parted, like stone carved in terror. In that instant, the veil lifted. She read the history written in that single phrase. Before she could curb the impulse, her mind, honed by intimate knowledge of Tito's nature, leaped to the conclusion. This face, these eyes—they belonged to the man who had raised Tito.
