Her emotional reaction stirred Baldassarre. For the first time, his words struck their mark. He pressed forward, his voice gaining heat and conviction, his hand clamping onto her arm.

"You carry blood of high lineage, do you not? You listen to the preacher; you despise baseness—the kind that grins and triumphs. Do you hate your husband?"

"Oh, God!" Romola whispered, her voice barely audible, consumed by the ghosts of the past and blind to Baldassarre's pressing inquiry. "Was he truly your son? Or did you take him as a babe, as Tito claims?"

"He knows the truth!" Baldassarre exclaimed, tightening his grip as if physical contact fueled his fading light. "Do you aid me?"

"Yes," Romola answered, misunderstanding the gravity of his plea. She laid her warm palm gently over his rough, grasping hand, tears welling in her eyes. "O, it is pitiful! Speak, sir—you were a great scholar, a teacher. What became of you?"

She fell silent. Tito's accusation of insanity echoed in her mind, and where were the traces of former refinement? Still, she possessed enough self-mastery to keep her hand steady, sitting quietly, listening with newfound vigilance.

"It is lost! All is gone!" Baldassarre lamented, his voice heavy with despair. "They would not listen, for he lied, calling me mad, and had me dragged to prison. Now I am old, and my mind returns to nothing. The whole world stands against me."

He paused, his eyes sinking as if drowning under a wave of melancholy, before lifting them again with a desperate fervor. "But *you* stand with me. He won your love, yet he has betrayed you. You hate him. Indeed, he won *my* love, too! He was beautiful and gentle, while I was a lonely soul. I took him when they beat him. He slept in my arms as an infant, and I watched his growth, pouring every ounce of my knowledge into him. I gave him all I had—money, books, jewels. He wore my gems, sold them, and left me in bondage. He never sought me. When I returned, broken and wretched, he denied me. He called me a lunatic."

"He told us his father perished, drowned," Romola said softly, her voice faint. "Surely, he must have believed it then. O, he could not have been so vile *then*!"

A vision rose of Tito in those early days, when she knew no evil of him save as a child sees poison in flowers. A deep, aching regret softened the horror, bringing a sob that tore through her.

"Aye, you are young, and tears flow freely," Baldassarre chided, his patience thinning. "But tears extinguish the fire within, and it is the fire that drives us. They serve no purpose. Harken to me."

Romola started slightly. Once more, the specter of his madness flickered through her mind, checking the flood of belief. Was this merely a madman plotting murder? Yet, beneath the surface, a profound sympathy anchored her trust, stronger than fear.

"Speak," she urged, as gently as her trembling voice allowed, "how did you lose your memory, your learning?"

"I fell ill," he replied, his gaze drifting. "Time means nothing to me. I remember only sitting in the sun amidst stones, surrounded by darkness. Slowly, a longing took root—a desire for something I could not name. When I sailed upon the waters, I understood what I craved: the return of the Boy, the restoration of my thoughts. I was locked outside of them all, and now I remain outside. I feel nothing but walls and shadows."

His words drifted into a reverie, and he sank into silence, burying his head in his hands. As before, Romola's faith in him drowned out her caution. The pity in her heart resonated with an old, familiar pain. Did not her own father miss Dino, mourning a future lost?

"It returned once," Baldassarre continued, breaking the silence. "I held sway over all things. I beheld the world anew, my treasures, my library. I thought I held him in thrall, so I went to expose him where the lights gleamed and the trees stood. But he lied again, branding me mad, and they hauled me away to prison... Wickedness is mighty, and he wears armor."

Fierceness flared in his voice, returning with its former intensity. He seized her arm again.

"But you will assist me? He has betrayed you, too. He has another wife, bearing children. He convinces her he is her husband, and she is a simple, helpless creature. I will reveal her dwelling."

The first shock that coursed through Romola was a surge of righteous anger. The indignity of the woman's position cut deepest. Baldassarre, instinctively attuned to her spirit, sensed her alignment.

"You hate him," he insisted. "Is it not true? There is no love between you. I know women can hate, and you possess proud blood. You abhor deceit, and you can embrace revenge."

Romola sat paralyzed by the storm of conflicting emotions. She felt no sensation of the bruising grip on her arm.

"You shall devise a plan," Baldassarre whispered urgently. "I have memorized that you are his lawful wife. You are a noblewoman. You seek the preacher of vengeance; you will uphold justice. Yet, you must think for me. My mind wanders—everything slips away, save the fire. That fire is God; it is Justice; it cannot die. Do you believe this? If they will not hang him for robbing me, you must strip him of his armor. You will leave him defenseless, and I shall strike him down. I bear a knife, and my arm retains sufficient strength."

He slid his hand beneath his tunic, drawing forth the hidden blade, running his thumb absently along the edge, as if the tactile sensation kept his ideas alive.

To Romola, every passing hour seemed to promise greater difficulty. Her judgment, swift and vigorous, refused to succumb to futile pleas of reason against a man in Baldassarre's fractured state. She chose silence, refusing to answer his final appeal, instead seeking to buy time by deflecting his urgency with a question of her own concern. Her voice trembled as she spoke.

"You claim she is foolish, helpless—that other wife—who believes him to be her true spouse. Perhaps he is her husband, perhaps he wed her before marrying me."

"I cannot say," Baldassarre admitted, pausing in his examination of the knife, his expression bewildered. "My memories are broken. I know only

A purpose, forged in the silent heat of the afternoon and swelling within her like a tributary joining a mighty flood, drove Romola forward. It was not merely a resolve, but a compulsion of spirit. Ignoring the encroaching shadows of the streets and refusing the cautious escort of Maso, she crossed the bridge where the river lay black beneath the fading crimson of the sunset. With unerring speed, she sought the Old Palace. Whether she met her husband there mattered not; probabilities held no sway over her urgent need to unburden her soul. Blind to the corridors ahead, unaware of the specific turns she took through the pillared courts or up the broad staircases, she demanded an usher for the Gonfaloniere. She gave her name, begged for a private audience, and pressed onward.

Soon, the chamber opened to reveal the frescoed saints and the flicker of newly lit tapers. Before solitude could take hold, the door swung open and Bernardo del Nero entered. His white hair stood erect atop his silken *lucco*, a crown of dignity undimmed.

"Romola, my child, what is this?" he exclaimed, his voice thick with anxious surprise as he shut the door behind them.

She uncovered her head and moved toward him in silence. He laid a heavy hand upon her shoulder, pulling her slightly away so he might inspect her features. Her countenance was ravaged by exhaustion and prolonged torment; her hair hung loose and disordered. Yet, a fierce excitement burned in her eyes, a fire that had conquered physical weakness.

"What has he done?" Bernardo asked abruptly. "Speak, child. Tell me everything. Cast aside pride. I am your father."

"It is not concerning myself—it is nothing about me," Romola replied quickly. "Dearest godfather, it concerns you. I have heard whispers... some I dare not speak aloud. But you stand in peril within these walls, and in peril everywhere. Fanatics seek to harm you, and there are traitors among us. Trust no one. To trust is to invite betrayal."

Bernardo smiled, a faint, weary curve of his lips.

"Have you driven yourself to this feverish state, my poor child," he said, lifting a hand to stroke her hair gently, "to repeat such ancient, worn truths to an old man like me?"

"Oh, no! They are not old truths," she insisted, pressing her clasped hands together with painful intensity, as if the gesture could seal the forbidden words inside. "These are fresh horrors I have known, yet I cannot articulate them. Dearest godfather, you know I am no fool. Why should I come to you without cause? Is it too late to warn you against anyone, *everyone* who appears to serve your interests? Is it too late to cry out, 'Flee to your villa, hide in the countryside before these final three days of office end'? Oh, God! Perhaps it is too late! And if any harm falls upon you, it will seem as though I have struck the blow!"

The final words escaped her involuntarily, a long-suppressed emotion bursting forth in a spasmodic gasp. Immediately, she was startled by her own vehemence and arrested herself.

"I mean," she stammered, "I know nothing concrete. I only know what fills me with terror."

"Poor child!" Bernardo murmured, gazing at her with a quiet, piercing depth for a fleeting moment. Then he spoke softly. "Go, Romola—return home and rest. These fears may be but large, ugly shadows cast by something trivial and harmless. Even traitors require motive; the rats will run only where they smell the cheese, and we know not yet from which direction the scent drifts."

He paused, turning his gaze away with an air of abstraction, until, with a slow shrug, he added, "As for warnings, they avail me little, child. I enter into no plots, nor do I ever forsake my colors. If I march alongside obstinate men who charge blindly at guns and pikes, I must share their consequences. Let us say no more. I have not many years left in my sack for them to steal away. Go, child; return and find peace."

Once more he placed his hand upon her head, caressing her. She could not resist clinging to his arm, pressing her brow against his shoulder. This godfather's affection felt like the final remnant of that youthful filial life which now appeared so radiant to her, even amidst its troubles, for those struggles remained unstained by malice.

"Is silence best, my Romola?" the old man asked.

"Yes, for now; yet I cannot know if it will remain so forever," she answered, hesitating, her eyes lifting in an appeal.

"Well, you possess a father's ear while I breathe, and a father's home," he said, lifting the black drapery to fold it tenderly around her head. "Remember that." Opening the door, he gestured for her departure. "Hasten away. You resemble a black specter, but you will be safe enough."

That night, when Romola finally succumbed to sleep, it was profound and deep. Agitation had reached its zenith; she required strength before she could endure further suffering. Despite her rigorous habits, she slumbered well past sunrise.

Awakening, she was greeted by the thunderous roar of cannon. Piero de' Medici, backed by thirteen hundred men, stood before the gate facing Rome.

Maso, returning with many details of dubious reliability, informed her of the situation. A countryman had arrived before dawn to alarm the Signoria; otherwise, the city would have fallen to surprise. His master was absent, having been summoned to the Palazzo long ago. Romola dispatched the old man once more to gather intelligence while she climbed repeatedly to the loggia, straining her eyes to detect either the dread advance or signs of a successful repulse. Maso reported that the great Piazza swarmed with armed men, and that many leading citizens, suspected of sympathy with the Medici, had been summoned and detained. Some of the populace seemed indifferent to Piero's entry, even whispering that the Signoria itself had invited him; others claimed the soldiers from Pisa were arrayed against him.

In the memory of those morning hours, few things stood out to Romola as distinct external realities rising clearly above the tumultuous waves of retrospect and anticipation. She knew she had walked to the Badia at the appointed hour despite the street alarms, and she knew she had waited there in vain. However, the scene she had witnessed emerging from the church, standing on the steps as the doors closed behind her for the afternoon recess, returned to her mind with the vivid clarity of a remembered dream.

A transformation had passed over the faces and tones of both armed and unarmed folk lingering or hurrying through the streets. Though the guns fired again, the noise now provoked laughter rather than fear. She soon grasped the cause of this shift. Piero de' Medici and his horsemen had turned their backs on Florence, galloping with desperate speed along the road to Siena. This truth came from a substantial shopkeeper, a *Piagnone* who had not yet lowered his pike.

"It is true," he concluded, his emphasis carrying a bitter edge. "Piero is gone, but those who remained behind were privy to his coming—we all know that. If the new Signoria fulfills its duty, we shall soon learn who they were."

Like a sharp spasm, these words pierced through Romola. Yet the evil they foreshadowed was not yet immediate. As she entered her home again, her most pressing anxiety shifted to the terrifying possibility that she had lost sight of Baldassarre for a long time.

The sun stretched its golden arms across the long days, yet it offered neither the reunion Romola craved nor the finality she feared. There came no word from Baldassarre. Despite the government's vigilant eyes, the shadowy conspiracy remained unexposed. Yet, in lieu of answers, the season delivered two calamities that cut close to her heart: the ravaging Plague and the formal Excommunication of Savonarola.

These twin disasters served to halt Romola's drifting away from the Friar and to bind her anew to the man who had unveiled to her the vocation of duty. How could she traverse the streets of pestilence and sorrow carrying the buoyant joy of her own existence, forgetting that she owed this transcendent moral awakening solely to Fra Girolamo? And how could she stand before the silencing and banishment of a soul whose greatness lay not in error, but in the fierce energy to make Christianity a living reality, without feeling pulled irresistibly to his defense?

Deep into the sweltering heat of June, the Edict from Rome arrived, and weeks later, it was proclaimed solemnly within the Duomo. Romola attended the ceremony, seeking to fortify the sympathy for Savonarola that had become a pillar of her strength. It stood in stark, somber contrast to the vibrant mornings she had known. Gone were the upward-turned faces of citizens, the youthful crowds rising tier upon tier to form a garland of hope around the elders, and the mighty voice that thrilled the heart with visions of struggle. Instead, the great hall echoed with the gloom of evening tapers casting shadows on bare walls. A black and grey throng of monks and clerics stood with bowed, expectant countenances. Only the faint, metallic chiming of small bells broke the monotony of a voice reciting sentences long hung over the churches. Then, the lights faded, and the heavy, shuffling footsteps of the friars departed into a dim silence.

Romola's fervor for the Frate was doubled by the gleeful triumph etched onto the rough features of the crowd and the terrified confusion visible in the eyes of many of Savonarola's staunchest allies. The boundary between obedience and resistance, always a treacherous line, became nearly impassable here. To the devout Florentines, the Church was not a political compromise but a living organism, imbued with divine power to bless and to curse. This conviction was not merely an opinion held by the faithful; it was an inalienable impression, as undeniable as the curve of the sky. Thus, Savonarola's written arguments—that the Excommunication was unjust and therefore void—did not reassure them. On the contrary, they caused them to tremble, perceiving his defiance as a sacrilege cast against a mystical image, a power before which there was no shield.

For Romola, however, whose spirit had never drunk from the traditional wells of communal faith, the Church existed only through Savonarola. His moral force was her sole authority. In his exile, she saw only the menacing thrust of hostile vice: on one side, a man dedicated to public virtue and spiritual purity; on the other, an assault led by alarmed, greedy, and murderous self-interest, personified by Rodrigo Borgia, now elevated to the throne of infamy as Pope Alexander VI. While neutral observers might see nuance, Romola needed the clarity of conviction. This Excommunication stripped away the petty antagonisms of local politics, presenting Savonarola simply as a champion appealing to Christendom against a tyrannical abuse of ecclesiastical power. He became a standard-bearer leaping into the breach. Life appeared clearest when the heart beat fast at the sight of generous self-sacrifice; in those moments, doubt vanished, and the soul believed in its capacity to achieve the highest prize. A new current of this enthusiasm carried Romola through the difficult summer.

She had said nothing to Tito regarding her recent meeting with Baldassarre or the terrible revelations he had made. What purpose would agitated words serve? Would they yield the truth, or merely a cold sneer at her sympathy for an assassin? Baldassarre was clearly helpless. Her true fear was not that he would harm Tito, but that Tito, driven by terror of the injured man who haunted his dreams, would devise a new scheme to eliminate Baldassarre. Until she saw him again and learned the whole truth about his "other wife"—whether she were indeed the woman to whom Tito was first bound—she could take no decisive action.

The possibilities surrounding that other wife, striking at the very core of her hereditary pride, mixed with a newly embittered suspicion. These thoughts ate away at the lingering tenderness of her past with Tito, poisoning the memory of their union. An irresistible belief in the veracity of Baldassarre's tale caused her to shrink from Tito with a horror so profound it might have compelled a passionate confession if he had not been away from home. Like many wealthy citizens during the plague, Tito spent his leisure hours in the countryside. The charming villa of Melema welcomed guests, and since Romola had refused to flee the city, he had found no necessity to establish a country residence of his own.

Finally, in the waning days of July, the weight of these public troubles began to lift. The Plague had receded, and Savonarola's cause gained hope through a favorable magistracy that wrote urgent letters to Rome, pleading for the revocation of the Excommunication. Yet, though Romola's robust frame succumbed to the languid exhaustion following her continuous exertion, her restless mind refused stillness save during the sultry hours. In the cool of the morning and evening, she walked constantly, varying her path with a vague, desperate hope: if Baldassarre lived, perhaps she would find him. Maybe some sickness had paralyzed his memory, causing him to forget where she dwelt, or even her existence itself. That was her most sanguine explanation for his absence. But the probability that weighed heaviest on her soul was far darker: that he had perished of the Plague.

The Other Wife.

The morning air, though initially gentle, had begun to press heavily upon Romola as she turned from the walls near San Marco toward the intersecting lanes of Santa Croce. The Borgo La Croce lay in a profound stillness; she walked listening to the echo of her own steps on the sun-drenched pavement until, rounding a bend, she spied a small child, barely three years old, clad only in a white shirt. He paused mid-waddle, turning a circle of reddish-brown curls atop a square, sturdy boy's back. As he faced her, his dark eyes widened with tears, his lip trembling, his fat brown fists clutching his shirt in helpless panic. A tall, shadow-casting figure loomed behind him, sending the boy's fear into a climax of loud, rolling sobs.

Instinctively, driven by a tender, maternal impulse, Romola uncovered her head. She stooped, wrapping her arms around the shivering child, pressing her cheek against his. "You have lost yourself, little one," she cooed, kissing him gently. "But never mind! We shall find the house again. Perhaps Mamma will meet us." She sensed he had fled while his mother's gaze was elsewhere and believed he would soon be pursued.

"Oh, what a heavy, heavy boy!" she murmured, attempting to lift him, but failing. "Come, you must toddle back by my side." The boy's lips remained sealed in an awed silence, his grip on the shirt unyielding, yet his free hand yielded readily to her soft, strong palm.

"You *have* a Mamma?" Romola asked, looking down at the solemn little man with a yearning ache. He offered no sound. Under different circumstances, a girl might have chattered, but this silent, square-shouldered creature was unmoved.

However, he was keenly aware of his destination. At the turn before San Ambrogio, he dragged Romola toward the house, his brow furrowed. "Ah, that is the way home, is it?" she smiled. He merely thrust his head forward, pulling urgently for speed. After another sharp turn, they emerged onto a street opening to garden grounds. Here, the boy halted, tugging her toward stone stairs. He showed no desire to let go, and she, unwilling to abandon him without ensuring his safety, ascended. The light faded as they climbed, giving way to a sudden brightness at an open doorway. Through a small lobby and another door, they entered a room where no sound greeted her arrival.

At the far end, sitting on a low chair by a cradle, was Tessa, her head lolling, fast asleep. By a window, with her back to the entrance, sat Monna Lisa, silently preparing salad. Romola caught this still tableau in an instant before Lillo snatched his hand away and ran to his mother, leaning his head back against her arm, regarding Romola with serious scrutiny.

As Lillo nudged her, Tessa awoke, blinking in bewilderment. But her gaze landed on the stranger, and she sprang up, flushing deeply, trembling, unable to speak or move forward.

"Ah! We have met before," Romola said, stepping forward with a smile. "I am glad it was *your* little boy. He was crying in the street; I suppose he had run away. So we walked together a little, and then he knew where he was, and brought me here. But you had not missed him? That is well, else you would have been frightened."

The shock of discovering Lillo missing overwhelmed Tessa. Her color drained and returned as she seized her son's arm, rushing to Monna Lisa with a half-sob in the old woman's ear. "Oh, Lisa, you are wicked! Why did you stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran away ever so far!"

"Holy Mother!" gasped Monna Lisa, dropping her spoon. "Where were *you*, then? I thought you were there, watching him."

"But you *know* I fall asleep when I rock," Tessa retorted pettishly.

"Well, well, we must keep the outer door shut, or tie him up," Monna Lisa sighed, her voice thick and meek. "For he'll be as cunning as Satan before long, and that is the holy truth. But how did he come back?"

This question brought Tessa back to the present, her blush returning. Turning to Romola, she bowed her head. "Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back." Moving closer, she added, "It is my shame he was found in only his shirt; he kicked and refused his clothes this morning, and the Mother, poor soul, will never hear of him being beaten. But what can an old woman do without a stick when the lad's legs grow so strong? Let your nobleness look at his legs."

Lillo, sensing the inquiry, hitched his shirt up to display his olive-round legs with dispassionate curiosity. Romola laughed, shaking and kissing him, a gesture that eased Tessa's tension. For when Naldo had recounted the Carnival incident to Tessa, asking who the celestial lady might be, Naldo had replied, "Not exactly, my Tessa; only one of the saints," refusing further comment. Thus, in Tessa's dreamlike blend of childhood impressions, Romola became vaguely associated with the painted saints in the churches. With good beings unlikely to punish her as Nofri or the Devil did, Tessa felt a growing relief.

"Ninna is in the cradle," Tessa said, gesturing to the sleeping infant. "She is pretty, too."

Romola approached the cradle as Monna Lisa returned to her salad. "Ah! She is waking; look, her blue eyes are open," Romola noted. "You must take her, and I will sit in this chair—may I?—and nurse Lillo. Come, Lillo!"

She seated herself in Tito's chair, extending her arms. Lillo hesitated, pointing accusing fingers at her with a mix of puzzlement and anger. "That's Babbo's chair," he declared, fearing the consequences if his father returned to find a stranger there.

"But Babbo is not here, and I shall go soon," Romola assured him, noting with surprise the daintiness of Tessa's attire despite her contadina garb. "Come, let me nurse you as he does." Lillo consented, sinking into the lap, exploring her dress and hands for ornaments beside the rosary.

Tessa, having coaxed Ninna from her peevish waking, sat near Romola's knee, arranging the baby, jealous that the strange lady paid attention to Lillo as Naldo did.

"Lillo was going to be angry because I sat in Babbo's chair," Romola said, bending to kiss Ninna's foot. "Will he come soon and want it?"

"Ah, no!" Tessa exclaimed. "You can sit in it a long while. I shall be sorry when you go. When you first came to care for me at the Carnival, I thought it wonderful; you came and went so fast. And Naldo said, perhaps you were a saint, and it made me tremble a little, though saints are very good, I know; and you were kind to me, and now you care for Lillo. Perhaps you will always come to care for me. That is how Naldo did a long while ago; he came when I was frightened at San Giovanni. I couldn't think where he came from—he was so beautiful and good. And so are you," Tessa concluded, gazing up with devout admiration.

"Naldo is your husband. His eyes are like Lillo's," Romola observed, studying the boy's dark eyebrows. She spoke not as a questioner, but with a quiet certainty that baffled Tessa.

"Ah! You know him!" Tessa paused in wonder. "Perhaps you know Nofri and Peretola, and our house on the hill, and everything. Yes, like Lillo's eyes; but not his hair. His hair is dark and long—" she continued, growing excited. "Ah! If you know, *ecco*!"

She touched a thin red silk cord at her throat, drawing forth a tiny old parchment *Breve*, a horn of red coral, and a long dark curl carefully tied and suspended. She presented these mysteries to Romola, shielding them from Ninna's grasping hands.

"It is a fresh one. I cut it lately. See how bright it is!" she whispered, laying it against Romola's white fingers. "They fade, and then he lets me cut another when his hair grows; and I keep it with the *Breve*, because sometimes he is away so long, and I think it helps protect me."

A slight shiver ran through Romola as the curl touched her skin. At Tessa's first mention of her husband arriving mysteriously, a possibility flared in Romola's heart, quickening her pulse. To one searching desperately for truth, faint suggestions carry weight. Holding the curl, it seemed momentarily a mocking phantom of the lock Romola herself had cut five years prior. Yet she maintained her composure, determined to learn the truth without wounding this trusting, ignorant soul, whose mind remained childlike. "Foolish and helpless:" so far, she matched Baldassarre's description.

"It is a beautiful curl," Romola said, resisting the urge to withdraw her hand. "Lillo's curls will resemble it, perhaps, for his cheek, too, is dark. And you never

There was no room for doubt in Tito's mind regarding his security; indeed, his safety was the direct fruit of three months of meticulous calculation. During this interval, he had anticipated the inevitable exposure of the Medicean conspirators and had spent every moment fortifying his position. He had expanded his network of influence across Rome and Milan, positioning himself as the vital conduit for clandestine intelligence and subtle counter-moves against both the friar and the popular faction. Simultaneously, he had cultivated an image of unwavering loyalty to the popular cause, presenting subtle proofs that his political convictions aligned perfectly with theirs. Yet, paradoxically, he had not withdrawn his services from the Medici; rather, he had become more indispensable to them, seeking exclusive trust through a masterful display of utility.

Maintaining this triple deception was effortless for Tito. The Medici, by admitting the principle of duplicity into their own conduct, had stripped themselves of any moral standard by which to judge a colleague lacking their hereditary bonds, alliances, and intense prejudices. To deceive an enemy was considered honorable strategy; to deceive a friend was viewed as a base betrayal they felt no urge to commit. By exploiting Tito's agile abilities, they remained oblivious to the fact that his lack of traditional attachment—their greatest convenience—was also the absence of the primary guarantee of mutual honor found within their own circle. Furthermore, the *Arrabbiati*, the aristocratic enemies of Savonarola, operated a web of espionage where deep hypocrisy was the currency of the realm, demanding exorbitant fees for silence. Suspecting an agent merely because he played a dual role would have been a failure of logic on their part. Conversely, the *Piagnoni* of the popular party, driven by the directness of energetic conviction, were inclined to believe Tito's professed adherence to their cause precisely because he feigned no religious sympathy.

Consequently, the last three months had been a period of flattering triumph for Tito. His ultimate ambition, however, was not the immediate victory of his allies but the securing of a future post in either Rome or Milan. A growing resolve took hold in him to abandon Florence for one of those great capitals where life was less arduous and the rewards of talent and learning more splendid. Currently, the scales tipped toward Milan; if he could render specific services to Duke Ludovico Sforza within the year, the prospects at the Milanese court would eclipse the allure of Rome.

The revelation of the Medicean conspiracy, therefore, was a scenario Tito had foreseen, though not the manner of its execution. The arrest of Lamberto dell'Antella, carrying a damning letter and harboring bitter rancor against the Medici, was an incalculable variable. Despite the careful pretexts guarding his agency, Tito could not escape implication in such a wide discovery. However, his sharp mind quickly charted the course that would ensure his safety with minimal discomfort. While it is pleasant to remain unblemished, even intact skin remains sensitive to the atmosphere.

His calculations proved accurate. That night, before returning home, Tito had secured the three outcomes he desired: freedom from all proceedings regarding his alleged complicity with the Medici, the retention of his secretaryship for another year, and, crucially, the classification of the price he had paid for these guarantees as a state secret. This price would have seemed exorbitant to many, and Tito himself would have preferred not to pay it.

First, he targeted the mind of Francesco Valori. As one of the Ten under whom Tito held his secretaryship and a member of the special council investigating the plot, Valori was the key. Known as the head of the *Piagnoni*, Valori possessed fine qualities incompatible with nothing, including violent partisanship, an arrogant temper that alienated friends, and a fierce personal animosity toward Bernardo del Nero. In a brief, private interview, sealed by a pledge of secrecy, Tito confessed his agency for the Medici. He spoke frankly of his motives, declaring that he had always deemed their efforts futile and that he sincerely preferred the maintenance of the popular government. He feigned confidence in Valori regarding his personal dislike for Bernardo del Nero. Finally, he presented the critical offer: there existed another Medicean plot, centered on Piero de' Medici gathering forces in Siena and Romagna, for which he could procure documentary evidence by traveling there, provided he obtained certain conditions from the government. For this to succeed, his reputation as a Medicean agent had to remain unshaken, necessitating that his role as a source of information to the authorities be buried in profound secrecy. Before he could incur the unpleasant consequences of acting against his friends, he required absolute assurance of immunity from prosecution and protection from the loss of his office.

To Valori, these propositions did not sound as they do to us today. Valori's mind was not bent on weighing Tito's conduct with nuance; it was intensely focused on procuring an extreme sentence against the five prisoners. He foresaw immense efforts to save them and believed that, for public grounds, the evidence against them needed to be overwhelming enough to alarm all well-meaning citizens to the dangers of clemency. In the legal spirit of the age, evidence was a commodity accessible only through foul means. Torturing a few to confess everyone's guilt was a step toward justice, and the appearance of a traitor was often necessary to validate the next step. Lamberto dell'Antella had been tortured to extract what he already knew willingly, yet additional, stronger facts were desired, particularly against Bernardo del Nero, who had thus far refrained from betraying the plot despite failing to discourage it. The welfare of Florence, Valori zealously believed, demanded that Bernardo's guilt be cast in the starkest light. Perhaps Valori was not fully aware that the fervor of his zeal was fueled by hatred. He resolved to accept Tito's proposal, presenting it to his colleagues without revealing Tito's name, and successfully swayed them to his view. Late in the day, Tito was granted an audience with the Special Council. By revealing another plot designed to insure the mastery of Florence for Piero de' Medici, scheduled for mid-August, he caused a deep sensation. He argued that documentary evidence on this subject would clarify the right course above all else. He received a commission to depart for Siena at break of day and, departing from the council chamber, carried with him a written guarantee of his immunity and the retention of his office.

Among the twenty Florentines who lowered their grave eyes upon Tito as he stood gracefully before them, speaking of startling matters with easy periphrasis and an apparently unaffected admission of motives falling short of the highest, several minds were not wholly preoccupied. They silently revised their judgment, concluding that this ingenious and serviceable Greek was henceforth to be utilized for public necessities rather than private intimacy. Unprincipled men were assets, allowing those with scruples to keep their hands relatively clean in a world saturated with dirty work. Indeed, it was unclear to the respectable brains of Florence, unless they shared the Friar's extravagant belief in earthly purity, how life could be conducted in any sphere without employing human instruments whom it would not be unbecoming to kick or spit upon while paying their wages. Some of these same men, passing a tacit verdict on Tito, were shortly to engage in a memorable transaction impossible to carry through without a level of unscrupulosity as decisive as his. Yet, as the poet Pulci had noted for them, "Betrayal pleases many, / But no one likes the traitor ill." The same society erected a gibbet for the murderer and a gibbet for the martyr, offered execrations to dastardly acts and equally loud hisses to words of generous truth or just insight—a mixed condition signaling not hopelessness, but struggling order.

For Tito himself, he was not unaware that he had sunk slightly in the estimation of those who had accepted his services. He possessed a degree of self-contemplation inherent to the habit of acting on well-considered reasons, regardless of their quality. If he could have chosen, he would have declined to see himself disapproved by men of the world. He had never intended to be disapproved, conducting himself so ably that if he opposed others' standards, they would remain unaware. The barrier between himself and Romola had been raised by the impossibility of such concealment with her. He shrank from condemnatory judgments as from a climate to which he could not adapt. However, reality was not as plastic as the hands of cleverness wished, and events had turned inconveniently. He harbored no rancor against Messer Bernardo del Nero; he held a personal liking for Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci. He had served them ably, and if their party had won, he would have deserved high reward. Was he to relinquish the agreeable fruits of life solely because their party had failed? His proffer of additional proof against them would likely have no influence on their fate; in fact, he felt convinced they would escape extreme consequences. Yet, had he not given it, his own fortunes, which promised a magnificent fabric, would have been utterly ruined. What motive could any man possess but self-interest? Florentines, engrossed in petty and precarious political schemes, might attribute no separable self-interest to family pride and tenacious hatreds; a modern simpleton swallowing an old philosophical system might mistake the indigestion it causes for signs of divine influx or an inward monitor; fanatics believing in the coming scourge and renovation might see their interest in a future palm-branch and white robe. But no man of clear intellect allowed his course to be determined by such puerile impulses or questionable inward fumes. Did not Pontanus, poet and philosopher of unrivaled Latinity,

Tito returned swiftly from Siena, only to embark immediately upon another journey, leaving behind the heavy silence of the city until August seventeenth. Two weeks had elapsed since the arrest, and the prisoners remained in their confinement, their fate suspended in a limbo of uncertainty. To Romola, the world outside the prison walls had ceased to exist; her entire reality narrowed to the trembling balance of probability regarding those inside. At times, hope flared brightly. The delay itself became an instrument of survival; the authorities, terrified of the odium attached to condemning five distinguished citizens, hesitated to strike the blow. A sentence of death and confiscation would ignite a rancorous hatred from the families of the accused, falling like a curse upon anyone deemed responsible. Conversely, lenity appeared dangerous, with enemies of the Republic whispering that mercy would be the signal for their assault. Yet, the scales tipped neither way decisively.

The power to judge rested with the Eight, the body governing criminal justice. Their sentence required a majority of six—a threshold known as *set fave*, or "six beans," a phrase that echoed the fragile political pulse of Florence. However, the Eight recoiled from the weight of this responsibility, petitioning the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight Priors—to share the burden. The Signoria, in turn, shrugged off the heat of the matter, proposing an appeal to the Great Council. According to a law championed by Savonarola three years prior, a citizen condemned by the fatal six votes possessed the right of appeal.

Yet, the friends of the accused, driven by a desperate need for time, fiercely resisted the formal appeal. Strict legality demanded the sentence be pronounced before the appeal could be lodged, creating a procedural deadlock. They argued that the sentence itself was premature. This resistance held sway, leading to a convoluted middle path: the case was referred to a massive assembly convening on the seventeenth, comprising all higher magistrates, the Senate of Eighty, and a select circle of citizens.

On this day, Romola, her heart hammered by the fear that her godfather's destiny would be sealed before the session ended, secured leave to visit the prisoners. It was permitted only in the presence of witnesses. Returning home along the Via de' Bardi with her cousin Brigida, she remained in a state of suspended animation, unaware of the council's verdict. Brigida departed quickly to wait for news at a friend's house within one of the magistracies, promising to return with authentic tidings.

Romola collapsed onto the first seat of the bright salon, too agitated, too sick at heart, to care for propriety or the discordant beauty surrounding her. With her back to the door, her head resting in her hands, she waited. Time stretched thin; Brigida's absence felt like an eternity. Finally, the door swung open. Romola braced herself, expecting her cousin, but the figure stepping inside brought a chill that froze the blood.

It was Tito.

Since their parting on that fateful night, Romola had clung to the illusion of his innocence, bolstered by evidence that the Medici still trusted him and sent him on errands into Romagna. The obscurity surrounding the conspirators allowed her to believe he had escaped implicating himself. But now, standing before her, the illusion shattered. Her horror of his cruelty toward Baldassarre cast a shadow over every other act he had performed. It was as if she saw fresh blood on his hands, a stain that could never be washed clean.

Hearing his step on the stone floor, a shudder racked her frame. She could not turn, could not rise. He moved silently, pausing before settling on the bench opposite her. He offered no greeting, no apology. His voice, when it finally came, was cool and detached.

"This meeting corresponds with our parting, Romola. But I understand it is a moment of terrible suspense. I have come, if you will listen, to bring you the relief of hope."

Romola started, shifting her posture but fixing him with a doubtful, searching gaze.

"It will not be unwelcome to you to hear—even though it is I who tell it—that the council is prorogued till the twenty-first. The Eight have been frightened at last into passing a sentence of condemnation, but the demand has now been made on behalf of the condemned for the Appeal to the Great Council."

The doubt vanished from Romola's face, replaced by eager anticipation. "And when is it to be made?"

"It has not yet been granted; but it *may* be granted. The Special Council meets again on the twenty-first to deliberate whether the Appeal shall be allowed. In the meantime, there is an interval of three days. Chances may occur in favor of the prisoners; interest may be used on their behalf."

Romola sprang from her seat. Color flooded her cheeks, her hands trembling violently. For a fleeting second, her revulsion toward Tito faded, swallowed by the urgent necessity of saving lives.

"Possibly," Tito observed, rising as well, "your own intention anticipated what I was going to say. You are thinking of the Frate."

"I am," Romola admitted, surprised. "Has he done anything? Is there something to tell me?"

"Only this. It was Messer Francesco Valori's bitterness and violence that chiefly determined the course of affairs today. Half the men who voted against the prisoners were cowed into it. Yet, there are many friends of Fra Girolamo within this Special Council and without—Piero Guicciardini, for instance, a member of the Signoria who made the stoutest resistance; and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, who, as a *Piagnone*, will not lightly forgive the death of his brother Niccolò."

"But how can the Appeal be denied?" Romola cried, indignant. "When it is the law! Was it not one of the chief glories of our popular government to enact this law?"

"They call this an exceptional case. There are ingenious arguments, certainly, but there is much more loud bluster regarding the danger to the Republic. However, no opposition could prevent the assembly from being adjourned. A certain powerful influence, rightly applied during these next three days, might determine the wavering courage of those desiring the Appeal and check the headlong enmity of Francesco Valori. It has come to my knowledge that the Frate has already intervened, sending a message to Valori in favor of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. I know you sometimes have access to the Frate; it would surely be worthwhile to exercise that privilege now."

"It is true," Romola murmured, her expression abstract, distant. "I cannot believe the Frate would approve of denying the Appeal."

"Tis said by many in the courtyard of the Palazzo, before I left, that it would be an everlasting discredit to Fra Girolamo to allow a government composed almost entirely of his party to deny the Appeal without protest, especially after he boasted in his books and sermons that he was the author of the law." Tito leaned closer, his tone dropping to a confidential murmur. "But between ourselves, with all respect for your Frate's ability, my Romola, he has fallen into the habit of preaching a form of human sacrifice called 'killing tyrants.' Some of his followers are beginning to suspect that this rhetoric is inconsistent with the leniency required in the present case."

*I know,* Romola thought, a pang of sorrow cutting through her. *He is driven into those excesses of speech.*

"He is driven into them," she whispered aloud, her voice thick with emotion. "It used to be different. I *will* ask for an interview. I cannot rest without it. I trust in the greatness of his heart."

Her eyes were fixed on the floor, hazy and unfocused, lacking the sharp consciousness that the words issuing from her mouth were spoken to her husband.

"Better lose no time, then," Tito suggested, his manner suavely smooth as he twirled his cap in his hand, preparing to depart. "And now, Romola, you will perhaps be able to see, in spite of prejudice, that my wishes align with yours in this matter. You will not regard the misfortune of my safety as an offense."

An electric shock coursed through Romola. The full, terrifying consciousness of her husband's presence returned to her. She stared at him, silent and stunned.

"At least," he added, his tone hardening imperceptibly, "you will endeavor to base our intercourse on reasoning other than the fact that an evil deed is possible, therefore *I* have done it. Am I alone to be cast beyond the pale of your extensive charity?"

The feelings that had been suppressed for two weeks surged back, gathering the force of a tidal wave. She spoke with a decision that showed her indifference to consequences.

"It is too late, Tito. Deceit, once begotten, cannot be killed. And now I know everything. I know who that old man was: he was your father, to whom you owe everything—to whom you owe more than if you had been his own child. By comparison, it is a small thing that you broke my trust and my father's. As long as you deny the truth about that old man, a horror rises between us. The law that should make us one can never be obeyed. I, too, am a human being. I have a soul of my own that abhors your actions. Our union is a pretense—as if a perpetual lie could constitute a sacred marriage."

Tito did not answer immediately. When he finally spoke, it was with a calculated caution, born of sudden alarm.

"And you intend to

The morning burned with a crisp, early clarity as Romola resumed her journey toward San Marco. The night prior, through the mediation of Fra Salvestro, she had secured a rare audience with Fra Girolamo within the convent's chapter house. Such meetings were the exception rather than the rule; Savonarola guarded his secluded life with iron resolve, fearing even the slightest pretext for slander, and when permission was finally granted, it was stripped of all mystique. Hence, the hour chosen was deliberate—a time when the outer cloisters bustled with ordinary visitors, ensuring the meeting appeared as mundane as any other administrative duty.

Romola resolved to traverse the beating heart of the city first, seeking to gauge the feverish pulse of public sentiment. Everywhere she looked, the air vibrated with the clamor of gratuitous debate. Every loggia, every shadowed corner of the piazza, every shopfront serving as a gossip station, hummed with a languorous trade that only made political discourse more frantic. It was evident that the factions supporting and opposing the execution of the conspirators were racing against the clock, determined to harness the three-day interval to crystallize the popular mood. Handbills fluttered like black birds overhead; some shouted in massive, bold letters the stark choice between justice and the Republic's ruin, while others, equally enlarged, screamed for the observance of the law and the granting of the appeal. Surrounding these jagged islands of capital letters were vast lakes of smaller script, offering arguments deemed unnecessary to read aloud. At that time, in the first intoxicating flush of triumph following the invention of printing, the prevailing belief was that no persuasion could match the convincing power of a question-begging phrase printed in giant type.

Yet, Romola craved the arguments hidden in the smaller print. Though compelled to hurry, her gaze darted anxiously around her, desperate to snatch a copy before the wind scattered them or the *sbirri* tore them from the walls. For miles, she found only flyers clutched by eager hands or pinned high above reach, until finally, slipping behind San Giovanni with a quickened stride to evade the crowds, she spotted Bratti. He held a stack of pamphlets, peddling them for small coins to the passersby. She knew the humble rhythms of Florence too well for Bratti to be a stranger, and turning to him, she commanded, "Have you two sorts of handbills, Bratti? Let me have them quickly."

"Two sorts," Bratti replied, slowly separating the damp sheets with a deliberation that tested Romola's patience. "'There's 'Law,' and there's 'Justice.'"

"And which sells faster?" she pressed.

"'Justice,'" Bratti admitted. "'Justice' flies off the shelves, so I hiked the price to two danari. But then I recalled that the 'Law' is just as fine merchandise and deserves a fair charge; folk do not value cheap things. If I sold the 'Law' for one danaro, I'd be wronging it. And I'm a fair trader. 'Law' or 'Justice,' it makes no difference to me; they're both good wares. I acquired them gratis, and I'll sell them at a proper profit. But you'll need more than a single pair?"

"No, no," Romola interrupted, unfolding a white *quattrino*. "Here for both." She folded the bills and fled.

Soon she reached the outer cloisters of San Marco, where Fra Salvestro awaited beneath the arcade, oblivious to her approach as he chatted with lay visitors. Under the current regime, which favored the Frate, the timidity that had gripped the faithful after the initial shock of excommunication was slowly dissolving. Among the listeners sat Andrea Cambini, a known satellite of Francesco Valori, gesturing emphatically as he expounded some point. Fra Salvestro listened with an air of trivial curiosity that betrayed how deeply he cared for the news and how lightly for its substance—a trait that always grated on Romola. Yet now, the disjointed fragments reaching her ears suggested Cambini was discussing the fate of the conspirators. Choosing not to interrupt, she waited until she had captured Fra Salvestro's attention, then turned sharply toward the chapter-house door. A lay brother opened it for her, closing it softly behind her.

Inside, the room was adorned with those sad frescoes that had once seemed to weep with her over the death of her brother Dino. Inevitably, a trace of that sorrow returned, but the intensity of her present dread rendered the memory less a recollection than a vague recurrence of impressions, blending with her agitating fears. Her anxiety felt like a resurrection of the fierce longing she had once brought to this spot—to find solace only to be met by marble rigidity. She gave no space to reflection, immediately unrolling the handbills, hoping to read them before Fra Girolamo's arrival. By the time she finished the tract advocating for the observance of the law, the door swung open, and she doubled the papers, standing expectant.

When the Friar entered, she knelt, adhering to custom, but rose instantly as he offered a benedictory greeting, standing opposite him at a respectful distance. Seclusion since his excommunication meant this was the longest interval since she had last seen him. Late months had visibly etched deeper lines into his face, marking the toll of overtaxed intellect and physical austerity. Yet, Romola sensed a shift more profound than mere weariness. Was the serene elevation and pure human fellowship that once moved her spirit absent? Or was it the gnawing sense of his division from her regarding her godfather that stirred dormant wells of alienation, blurring her vision? Perhaps both currents ran deep. Human relations are often dictated by such coincident flows; the unforgivable word or deed rarely strikes until affection or reverence has been eroded by the strain of repeated apologies.

Indeed, Savonarola's gaze held a hardness born of egotistical preoccupation. He divined that her visit concerned the conspirators, a subject on which he had already silenced inner voices that threatened to roar again if encouraged from without. Seated in his cell, correcting the sheets of his *Triumph of the Cross*, it was far easier to rest upon a resolution of neutrality.

"It is a matter of grave import, doubtless, for which you sought见我, my daughter," he began, his tone gentle primarily due to self-control rather than immediate inclination. "I know you do not usually burden yourself with trifles."

"Father, you know the weight before I speak," Romola replied, pouring forth her plea as if forgetting all else. "You know what I cherish—it is the life of the old man I love best in the world. The thought of him has traveled with the thought of my father since I can recall daylight. That is my warrant for coming, even if my journey proved superfluous. Perhaps it does: perhaps you have already decided that your sway over human hearts shall be used to deny the Florentines a right you yourselves helped secure."

"I meddle not with the functions of the State, my daughter," Fra Girolamo stated, strongly disinclined to reignite a debate he had exhausted internally. "I have preached and labored that Florence possess a good government, for sound governance is requisite for the perfection of Christian life; but I keep my hands from specific affairs which are the office of seasoned citizens to administer."

"Surely, father—" Romola broke off. The word escaped almost impulsively, but she halted, struck by the counter-agitation of feeling herself standing in accusation toward the man who had been her guide and strength. In rebelling, she bruised her own reverence.

Savonarola was too sharp not to divine the conflict arresting her spirit—too noble, deliberately, to assume in calm speech the self-justifying evasion into which he was often hurried in public by the crowding impulses of the orator.

"Speak what is in your heart; proceed, my daughter," he said, standing with arms crossed, looking at her with quiet expectation.

"I was about to say, father, that this matter surpasses in gravity many issues about which I have heard you preach fervently. If it were yours to urge that men condemned for offenses against the State retain the right to appeal to the Great Council—and if counting it a glory to have won that right for them—you cannot fail to declare yourself against its denial to the first men who need it? Surely this touches the Christian life more intimately than whether you foresaw the Dauphin's death or Pisa's conquest."

A subtle movement, like a subdued sign of pain, twisted Savonarola's strong lips before he spoke.

"My daughter, I speak as I am permitted to speak. I am not master of the times when I may become the vessel of knowledge beyond common lights. In this instance, I possess no illumination beyond what wisdom grants to those charged with state safety. Regarding the law of Appeal against the Six Votes, I labored to ensure its passage so no Florentine might suffer loss of life or goods through the private hatred of a few momentarily in power. However, these five men, who sought to overthrow a free government and restore a corrupt tyrant, have been condemned with the assent of a large assembly of their fellow-citizens. They initially refused to bring their cause before the Great Council. Thus, they have forfeited the right to appeal."

"How can they have lost it?" Romola cried. "It is the right to appeal against condemnation, and they have never been condemned until now! Forgive me, father, but _this_ is private hatred that denies them the appeal; _this_ is the violence of the few that frightens others.

Midnight had barely claimed the Piazza della Signoria when the moon rose above the Old Palace, yet its silver light offered no comfort. Instead of casting the familiar play of shadow and breadth upon the cobblestones, the moonlight was swallowed by a living ocean of humanity. There was no pavement visible, save for the heads bobbing in a churning tide of eager, struggling bodies. Where usually the silence of the city held back the pattering of feet and the hum of nocturnal wanderers, here reigned a deafening roar. Shouts mingled with curses, boots trampled upon boots, and the accidental clash of weapons cut through the din, drowning out everything but the occasional darting shriek and the heavy, rhythmic tolling of a bell.

Inside the palace, the Council Chamber remained the epicenter of a storm that had raged through days of daylight and torchlit nights. The chief magistracies, the eighty senators, and the select citizens sat locked in debate over the fate of the prisoners: whether an appeal should be granted or if the sentence of death must be executed immediately to forestall the dangers of delay. Their arguments had grown so fierce, so akin to a brutal quarrel, that the clamor from within seeped out to meet the crowd below. For nearly the entire session, the vote had hung in a precarious balance, the Signoria deeply divided. Four magistrates stood immovable for the appeal, defying threats of sack and ruin, until Francesco Valori, in a brief, furious outburst, shattered the stalemate. With ominous clarity, Valori declared that if the Signoria refused to defend the liberties of Florence by executing the five "perfidious" citizens, others would seize the cause, endangering everyone who opposed them. In that moment, the Florentine Cato had triumphed. When the ballots were recounted, the four stubborn "white beans" vanished; nine fatal "black beans" remained, sentencing the five prisoners to immediate death—and, though unspoken, condemning Francesco Valori to a similar fate.

Now, as the Judicial Eight departed for the Bargello to prepare the machinery of justice, the five condemned men were led barefoot and shackled through the palace corridors. This humiliation, orchestrated by their friends, was a calculated gamble to stir the populace by associating such cruel ignominy with the two venerable elders, Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi, and the brilliant young leaders, Tornabuoni and Pucci. Yet the strategy failed to generate pity capable of rescue; instead, it merely fueled a tardy, vengeful fury.

Upstairs, amidst the weight of stone pillars in the courtyard, stood Romola. She waited for the arrival of her godfather, having secured permission through powerful intercession to remain with him until the council's verdict was sealed. Her heart, bent on clinging to the childless old man as her father once would, had silenced all objections. Beside her stood Giovan Battista Ridolfi, Niccolò's younger brother and a devoted follower of Savonarola, who himself walked toward death in bitterness to see his elder brother fall. They awaited the guards who would escort Bernardo to his doom.

Elsewhere in the palace, Tito lingered, unseen by Romola since the evening of the seventeenth. He understood, through the reports of the friar's neutrality, that her pleas had fallen on deaf ears. Surrounded by officials and foreign envoys, Tito maintained a grave, subdued silence, masking a painful internal strife between his public duty and private affection. When a whisper turned to mention his wife, he feigned indifference, claiming that her violent mental agitation and her deep-seated hostility toward the government—which he served—made any approach dangerous.

"The old Bardi blood!" scoffed Cennini with a shrug. "I shall not be surprised if this business shakes *her* loose from the Frate, as it has shaken others."

"It is excusable in a woman," smiled a young French envoy, bowing politely to Tito, "who is doubtless beautiful, to believe that her affections must override the welfare of the State. To think that no head falls unless it belongs to someone's cousin is a view not to be encouraged in men. It weakens Florentine polity."

"That is true," admitted Niccolò Machiavelli, his expression unreadable. "But where personal ties are strong, the hostilities they spawn must be reckoned with. Many of these halfway severities are merely hot-headed blunders. The only safe blows to strike at men and parties are those too heavy to be avenged."

"Nicolò," Cennini warned, "there is a clever wickedness in thy talk sometimes that makes me mistrust thy pleasant young face as if it were a mask of Satan."

"Not at all, my good Domenico," Machiavelli replied, laying a hand on the elder's shoulder. "Satan was a blunderer, an introducer of novelty who met with a stupendous failure. Had he succeeded, we would all be worshipping him, and his portrait would be far more flattering."

"Well, well," conceded Cennini. "I say not thy doctrine is not too clever for Satan; I only say it is wicked enough for him."

"I tell you," Machiavelli pressed, his voice taking on a prophetic edge, "my doctrine is the creed of all who seek an end slightly beyond their own noses. Ask our Frate, our prophet, how his universal renovation is to be achieved: first, by establishing a free and pure government. Since this cannot be accomplished by making all Florentines love one another, it must be done by cutting off every head that stands obstinately in the way. A man who incurs odium by sanctioning a severity that lacks the finality to be complete commits a blunder. And I suspect the Frate has committed exactly that blunder. On this occasion, he might have won great lustre by exerting himself to maintain the appeal; instead, he has lost lustre and gained no strength."

Before anyone could respond, the announcement rang out: the prisoners were leaving the chamber. The majority of the assembly surged toward the doors, jostling for the clearest path to the rear of the prison guard, drawn alike by deep passion and cold curiosity.

Tito remained behind. A native repugnance to sights of death and pain had once made him hope for exile rather than execution five days prior, a hope born of self-preservation. But now, the landscape of his fear had shifted. He felt that the instant decapitation of Bernardo del Nero was the ultimate guarantee of his own security. The new knowledge revealed to him by Romola on the day of his return had instilled a fresh dread of the power she wielded to render his position precarious. If any act of hers succeeded in making him an object of suspicion, he foresaw not only frustration but frustration under unpleasant circumstances. Her faith in Baldassarre had clearly hardened her wavering resolve against further submission; if her godfather lived, she would easily turn him to her side. Romola seemed more than ever an unmanageable fact in his destiny. Yet, if Bernardo were dead, the difficulties that would beset her in opposing her husband would likely become insurmountable to her shrinking pride. Thus, Tito felt a grim ease knowing the Eight had gone to the Bargello to erect the scaffold instantly. Four other men, his intimates and confederates, were to die alongside Bernardo. A man's own safety is often a god that makes grim demands. As he heard the shuffle of many feet and the clanking of iron on stairs echoing from the open doorway, Tito answered the French envoy's questions with a mask of sad resignation to the necessities of the State.

To Romola, those falling sounds were amplified, her senses heightened to a superhuman degree. She required no support; she shed no tears. An intensity of life, transcending both grief and joy, seized her—a state wherein the mind feels kin to the ancient forces that wrought existence before pleasure and pain entered the world. Since her godfather's fate was sealed, her previous inner struggle had collapsed into a total identification with him in these supreme moments. Inwardly, she asserted for him that if he suffered the punishment of treason, he did not deserve the name of traitor; he was the victim of a collision between two forms of faithfulness. It was not given him to die for the noblest cause, and yet he died precisely because of his nobility. He might have been a meaner man and found it easier to avoid this guilt. Romola felt the full force of that sympathy for the individual lot, constantly opposing the rigid formulas by which actions and parties are judged. She was treading the path with her second father to the scaffold, steeling herself to defy ignominy through the consciousness that it was undeserved.

The route to the execution site was fenced in by three hundred armed men, ordered by Francesco Valori. Among the contradictions of this event, none struck harder than the dual alarms: the genuine terror of the popular rage against the conspirators, and the equally real fear of a desperate rescue attempt by a hostile mob. Upon reaching the courtyard of the Bargello, Romola was permitted a fleeting moment to approach Bernardo with his confessor. Even in the crush of the agitated throng, many eyes were fixed on them. The aged man, forgetting that his hands were bound in iron, lifted them toward the bowed golden head, then checked the motion, leaning forward to kiss her. She seized the fettered hands hanging limp beside him and kissed them as if they were sacred relics.

"My poor Romola," Bernardo whispered, his voice low. "I have only to die, but thou hast to live—and I shall not

Eight days had passed since the night that altered everything, and now Romola stood upon the edge of the Mediterranean Sea, gazing at the gentle, rhythmic pulse of the water just beyond the modest fishing hamlet of Viareggio. Once more, she had escaped Florence. This time, however, no commanding voice called her back; the silence around her was absolute. Clad in the grey habit of a religious sister, she wore the disguise not as a shield, but with a chilling indifference, for in the depths of her heart-sickness, she no longer cared for the labels men placed upon her. Within her, a fresh rebellion had ignited, born of a profound despair. She questioned the necessity of clinging to a single identity when every other thread of meaning had unraveled. Where was the consistent duty bound to "Romola"? What power existed to forge the sacred motive men call duty, save through a love that remained steadfast?

Every tie of deep affection had been severed. In her marriage, the most sacred bond of all, she had failed to perceive the mystical union that guarantees permanence, nor the obligation of a voluntary vow. Had she not demonstrated that the causes to which she had pledged her life were ultimately futile? The urge to liberate herself surged again with overwhelming force, yet she knew this freedom would merely trade one catastrophe for another. For a woman who believes her greatest relationship was nothing more than a fatal error, there is no recompense. She had lost her crown. The profound secret of human blessedness had briefly whispered to her lips before slipping forever beyond her grasp.

Her primary anchor amidst this supreme sorrow had vanished. The vision of any lofty purpose, any end to existence capable of sanctifying endurance and elevating mundane labor with divine fire, was now obliterated. All human endeavor appeared to her as dragging at tangled threads; all fellowship, whether in resistance or advocacy, seemed nothing but unfairness and exclusion. Who, after all, was the man who had embodied the highest heroism for her—not the stoic endurance of stone, but the self-offering love of a willing spirit? What was the cause he strove for? Romola had lost her trust in Savonarola, lost the fervent admiration that had previously allowed her to overlook his deviations, focusing only on the grand sweep of his ambition. Now, her sharp sensitivity toward her godfather, which had placed her in opposition to the Friar, revealed the repulsive and contradictory details of his teachings with a painful, hyper-lucid clarity. In the bitterness of her disillusionment, she declared that his quest to renew the Church and the world was a hollow pursuit of a mere title—a name that signified little more than the strategies required to fortify his grip on Florence, often at the cost of questionable acts and words designed to protect his fragile influence from the consequences of his own mistakes. That political reform, once a beacon of new interest, now reduced itself to narrow schemes for the safety of the city, standing in shameful contradiction to his professed blind trust in Divine providence.

It was inevitable that she judge the Friar unjustly regarding a matter of individual suffering, viewed through the lens of personal tenderness, while he viewed it through the rigid prism of theoretical conviction. In his declaration that the cause of his party was the Kingdom of God, she heard only the echo of selfishness. Yet, perhaps such words rarely escape without that baser ring, for they remain the implicit formula of all energetic belief. If such a belief, pursuing a grand and distant ideal, risks descending into a form of demon-worship where devotees send their children through the flames with a readiness that borders on the sacrificial, then tender compassion for the immediate neighbor carries its own perils, tending toward timidity and skepticism regarding the larger aims necessary for life to ascend into true religion. Thus, blinded by her tears, poor Romola drifted further from truth.

No soul who has ever known the shattering loss of faith in a fellow-man whom they have deeply loved and revered will lightly claim that such a shock leaves the faith in the Invisible Goodness unscathed. As high human trust sinks, the dignity of life follows suit; we cease to believe in our own higher selves, for that too is part of the common nature degraded in our thoughts, and all the finer impulses of the soul grow dull. Romola felt the springs of her former pity dry up, leaving her to a barren, egoistic lament. Had not she suffered her own sorrows? And few had cared for her while she had given so freely to others. She had done enough; she had striven for the impossible and grew weary of this suffocating, crowded existence. She longed for a repose in mere sensation, a state she had sometimes dreamed of during the sultry afternoons of her youth, when she imagined herself floating, naive and fluid, within the waters.

The clear waves seemed to beckon. She wished she could lie down upon them and slip from sleep into death. Yet Romola could not directly seek death; the fullness of young life within her forbade such a direct step. She could only wish that death would arrive.

At the point where she paused, the shore curved sharply inward, revealing a small boat with a sail moored nearby. In her longing to glide across the waters turning golden under the level rays of the sun, she recalled a tale from Boccaccio, one she had cherished reading from the *Decamerone* when her father slept. It was the story of Gostanza, a beauty driven by lovesick despair to live no longer, yet lacking the courage to strike down her young life. She had boarded a boat, pushed off into the sea, and wrapped her mantle around her head, hoping to be wrecked, trusting that her terror would render flight from death impossible. The memory had lingered in Romola's mind as a mere thought, failing to blossom into distinct desire, until now. As she paced back and forth, she saw another vessel, dark against the red-gold light, approach the bend where the first boat lay. A single man rowed it toward the shore. Watching him land, unload cargo, and prepare to depart, she realized her window of opportunity was closing—he would leave soon, taking the chance to acquire the smaller boat. Though she had not yet fully admitted to herself that she intended to use it, a sudden, fierce eagerness seized her to secure the possibility, marking the unconscious growth of a thought into a desperate desire.

"Is that little boat yours?" she asked the fisherman, who had looked up, startled by the tall, grey-clad figure, and bowed in reverence to this holy sister wandering alone in the evening solitude.

"It is mine," he replied. "An old craft, hardly seaworthy, though worth repair to any buyer." By the blessing of Saint Anthony, whose chapel stood near the village, his fishing had prospered, and he now possessed a superior vessel, formerly owned by Gianni, who had died. Yet, he had not sold the old boat. Romola inquired of its value. While the fisherman busied himself, she slipped the remaining coins from her meager dinner into a small satchel on the ground. She then watched him furled his sail and asked how to set it if he wished to venture to sea. Pacing up and down again, she waited for his departure.

As the fisherman departed, gathering his few belongings, the image of herself gliding away on those darkening waters grew stronger, transforming into a physical thirst, much like the craving for a cool stream in oppressive heat. To be freed from the burden of choice when every motive was bruised—to commit herself, in a slumber, to destiny which might bring death or awaken a new necessity within her—it was a thought that beckoned her, especially as the soft evening air invited rest in solitude rather than return to the noise and feverish heat of the village.

Finally, the slow fisherman walked away. Soon, the gold of the sun faded into dusk, leaving sea and sky alike devoid of living things, save for the lulling, monotonous lap of waves. Here, the sea held no tide to aid her drifting if she waited for the ebb, but Romola sensed the land breeze rising slightly. She entered the boat, unfurled the sail, and secured it as she had learned in that first brief lesson. Seeing it catch the faint wind, she deemed it sufficient. Loosening the moorings, she tried to urge the craft forward with an oar, pushing it far from the land until the sea turned dark even to the west, and stars began to disclose themselves like a throbbing life over the vast heavens. Finally resting, she threw back her cowl and removed the kerchief beneath it, doubling both under her head for a pillow against one of the boat's ribs. Her youthful head could endure the hard wood.

Thus she lay, the soft night air breathing upon her as she glided silently over the water, watching the deepening quiet of the sky. She was alone now; she had freed herself from all claims, freeing herself even from that heavy burden of choice that presses harder when those claims loosen their guiding hold.

Had she found anything akin to the dreams of her girlhood? No. Memories clung to her like the weight of broken wings that could never again lift flight—memories of human sympathy which, even in pain, left a thirst that the Great Mother had no milk to quench. Romola felt orphaned in these wide expanses of sea and sky. She read no message of love for her in that distant, symbolic script of the heavens, and with a great sob, she wished she were gliding into death.

Drawing the cowl over her head again, she covered her face, choosing darkness over the light of the stars, which seemed to her like the harsh

February 27th dawned with a crisp, decisive air, the currents of the Florentine populace surging unmistakably toward San Marco. It was the final morning of the Carnival, yet while rumors swirled of a grand Bonfire of Vanities staged before the Old Palace, the true gravity of the day anchored the hearts of the city elsewhere. In front of the church, a semicircular barrier held back a swelling tide of humanity, a wall of bodies pressed tight enough to resist the frantic pushing of latecomers seeking entry. Within this confined space, the Dominican brethren assembled in silence, their black mantles a uniform sea against the chaotic energy outside.

Above the church door, a temporary wooden pulpit stood stark and empty. It was destined for a man who had been cast down from the very heights of the Duomo, shunned by the ecclesiastical order, and declared anathema by the citizenry upon penalty of excommunication. This man, who had dared call the Pontiff a thief who bought his chair and a tyrant whose curses were merely broken swords, had proclaimed that obedience to the Papal command was not only unlawful but a sin against Christian life. Yet, despite the Vatican's terrifying threats of divine wrath and interdicts if Florence did not renounce this "pestilential schismatic," the crowds flocked to San Marco. They communed with his hand, their faith suspended between a desperate hope for salvation and a terrifying dread of judgment. Was it possible that this friar, far from Rome, held sway over heavenly lightning greater than the official successor of Saint Peter? If God had chosen him as a prophet to rebuke a High Priest clothed unworthily, surely the signs would be undeniable. For weeks, the people had asked silently, *"What miracle?"* The promise of commerce ruin, the shaking of Italy's balance, the looming shadow of an interdict—the stakes had grown too high for mere rhetoric. Now, the question gnawed at Savonarola: *What proof?* His faith, once iron-clad, trembled under the weight of public scrutiny, wavering between the certainty of yesterday and the uncertainty of tomorrow, yet his voice remained unbroken, speaking for a crowd that demanded a sign before they would follow.

To meet this impatience, the wooden platform had been raised. As the usual friars in black took their places, chanting softly, the multitude's gaze did not yet fix on the empty wood. Instead, eyes darted from face to face, searching for the familiar features of the inner circle, noting which monks hailed from noble houses and which bore kinship with the artisans and shopkeepers forming the bulk of the crowd. Only when the choir was fully seated and the psalmody began to swell did a murmur ripple through the throng: *"Fra Girolamo must be coming now."*

It was not the sound of the chants, but the sudden, profound silence that followed, a paling light falling over thousands of upturned faces, that signaled the arrival. With the last note fading, the pulpit was no longer void. A figure, cloaked entirely in black, stepped forward and knelt, head bowed, face hidden. To the eager multitude, the time seemed to stretch into eternity as the monk knelt.
