The stillness was absolute, charged with a strange, electric awe that unnerved even those ready to stone him; their arms grew weak, unable to raise stones against a man who appeared to commune directly with Heaven.

Then, a subtle vibration ran through the crowd, like a passing wind or the rustle of dry leaves, as if the air itself were waiting. The friar rose, turning slowly toward the sea of faces, and partially drew back his cowl. The monotony of the chant had ceased, swallowed instantly by the force of Savonarola's flashing glance. He looked around, his eyes burning with intensity, silencing the square. Stretching out his hands, they seemed transformed from tools of labor into vessels of pure sensitivity, delicate and translucent, emanating a speech of the soul that masked his fierce, passionate visage. Lines of deep weariness etched his brow and mouth, marking forty-four years of struggle.

As he extended his hands, a wave swept through the front ranks. Men and women fell to their knees. Some knelt with fervor, others with hesitation, fearing that to kneel before an excommunicated man might invite immediate divine judgment. A third group stood firm, their hearts filled with scorn for what they called a theatrical deception, an ambitious comedy played before an impotent audience. Yet, beneath the hatred, there lay a helpless admiration for a fashion that had already conquered them.

Then the voice broke forth, clear and low, uttering the ancient words of absolution: *"Misereatur vestri."* More sank to the earth. As the tone rose, growing louder and clearer, the number of upright heads dwindled rapidly. At the climax, *"Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus,"* the words erupted into a masculine cry, a trumpet blast ringing to the extremities of the piazza, as if protesting the grip of a demon trying to stifle the blessing. Under the sound, every head bowed.

Savonarola himself collapsed onto his knees, hiding his face, consumed by a temporary exhaustion. These great jets of emotion were the price of his existence; he had told the people long ago, "Without preaching I cannot live." But it was a life that shattered him piece by piece.

Moments later, as some began to rise, most remained kneeling, their faces fixed on him. In his hands, he held a crystal vessel containing the consecrated Host.

"You remember," he addressed them, his voice carrying over the hush, "three days ago I besought you. When I hold this Sacrament before you, I ask you to pray fervently to the Most High. Pray that if this work of mine does not come from Him, He will send a fire to consume me, that I may vanish into eternal darkness, away from the Light which I have hidden with my falsity. Again, I beseech you. Make that prayer. Make it *now*."

A breathless silence fell. Perhaps no one truly prayed, save those driven by obedient habit, but every consciousness was seized by the overwhelming sense that Savonarola himself was praying. His voice was not loud, yet it cut through the wide stillness like a knife.

"Lord," he whispered, the words rising in a crescendo, "if I have not wrought in sincerity of soul, if my word cometh not from Thee, strike me in this moment with Thy thunder! Let the fires of Thy wrath enclose me!"

He ceased, standing motionless, the sacred mystery clutched in his hand, eyes lifted to heaven, his whole aspect trembling with a feverish excitement. The world around him froze. Sunlight, previously piercing the grey mist of the square in fitful streaks, suddenly parted. With a gentleness akin to a smile, a stream of brilliance poured down, illuminating the crystal vase. The light spread, washing over Savonarola's face with a mild, glorious radiance.

An instantaneous shout tore through the Piazza: *"Behold the answer!"*

The warmth of the sun thrilled through the friar's frame, merging with the roar of the crowd. It was his final moment of untroubled triumph. In a rapturous vision, he saw a grander stage yet to come, where he would stand before all Christendom, sealed as the messenger of supreme righteousness, charged anew with divine strength. But the vision lasted only a heartbeat before it dissolved.

Within seconds, Savonarola turned and vanished into the sanctuary, retreating from the glare of the square. As the sunlight returned to its impartial, mundane scattering over clean and unclean alike, the silence broke. Voices erupted in a confused clamor, certain dissonant notes and varying scales of laughter revealing that the previous kneeling had been nothing more than a submission to a spell, hostile and scornful in its heart.

"It seems to me the plaudits are giving way to criticism," observed Tito, leaning from an upper loggia opposite the church, his gaze sharp and analytical. "Nevertheless, it was a striking moment, was it not, Messer Pietro? Fra Girolamo is a man who reminds us that there was a time when the monk's frock was a symbol of power over the human mind, rather than merely the keeper of keys to women's cupboards."

"Assuredly," replied Pietro Cennini, watching the dispersing crowd with a thoughtful expression. "And until I have seen proof that Fra Girolamo possesses far less faith in God's judgments than the common rabble—and indeed, considerably more—I shall not believe that he would dare to brave Heaven in this manner if his soul were laden with a conscious lie."

A month after the Carnival, near the close of March, Tito descended the marble steps of the Old Palace upon an urgent mission to San Marco. Yet, driven by some unspoken instinct, he eschewed the direct path—a mere slight bend—and chose instead a circuitous route through the Piazza di Santa Croce. There, he anticipated a throng emerging from the church following the early sermon.

Indeed, the great church of Santa Croce had lately become the arena for the largest audiences of the Lenten season. Savonarola's voice, once thunderous within San Marco, had been silenced by a hostile Signoria obeying a fresh mandate from the Pope. A threat of immediate interdict hung over the city unless the "wretched worm" and "monstrous idol" were forbidden to preach and summoned to Rome to beg pardon. Thus, while the friar himself fell silent, his detractors flourished. Next to listening to Fra Girolamo, the most stirring Lenten pastime was witnessing him torn apart and vilified. This fervor peaked at Santa Croce, where the appointed Franciscan preacher had proposed a dramatic climax: walking through the fire alongside Fra Girolamo. The Dominican had scoffed at the notion, claiming his prophetic doctrine would only be proven by a miracle at the proper time; yet here, the Franciscan declared the time fit. Let Savonarola traverse the flames; if emerged unscathed, the divine origin of his teaching would be demonstrated, proving his prophecy fulfilled. If consumed, his falsehood would be laid bare. To leave no room for evasion, the Franciscan offered himself as a willing victim to secure the necessary premise for this high logic.

Savonarola, as per his custom, had ignored these pulpit assaults. However, the zealous preacher of Santa Croce was no ordinary friar, but Fra Francesco di Puglia. The previous year at Prato, he had challenged Savonarola's devoted follower, Fra Domenico, to a similar ordeal, only to be recalled by superiors before the contest became physical. Consequently, honest Fra Domenico, currently preaching Lenten sermons to the women of the Via del Cocomero, seized the gauntlet the moment he heard of the new challenge, declaring his readiness to walk through the fire with Fra Francesco. The populace, viewing this as a swift and facile method of settling the debate, grew deeply engrossed. Yet, when Tito arrived in the square, finding the crowds pouring forth in vast numbers, he noted that instead of scattering, many converged toward the entrance of the Franciscan monastery. Threading the crowd with a careless, leisurely gait, Tito kept a vigilant eye on that monastic door, expecting some object of interest to emerge.

What occupied the multitude was not a figure, but a large placard affixed by order of the Signoria, covered in legible official handwriting. Curiosity was somewhat dampened by the fact that the document was chiefly in Latin. Though nearly every man knew roughly what the placard contained, an appetite for precise knowledge gnawed at them, creating an irritating sense of their neighbors' ignorance regarding the learned tongue. An ear trained to Latin phrases from pulpit quotations offered little aid when confronted with written script; moreover, the spelling of the vernacular was in a chaotic state for the literate masses, and most gathered nearest the board lacked even that fragmentary knowledge.

"It is the Frate's doctrines he must prove by being burned," declared Goro, a prominent figure among the gazers. "The Signoria has taken charge, and the writing informs us. It is what the Padre has been preaching."

"Nay, Goro," countered a sleek shopkeeper with compassion. "Thou hast thy legs entangled in twisted hose. The Frate must prove his doctrines by *not* being burned; he walks through the fire and emerges sound and whole."

"Yes, yes," chimed in a young sculptor, wearing a white-streaked cap and tunic with jaunty pride. "But Fra Girolamo objects to walking through the fire. Already sound and whole, he sees no reason to undergo the ordeal to remain in that same condition. He leaves such trifling odds and ends to Fra Domenico."

"Then I say he flinches like a coward!" cried Goro in a wheezy treble. "Suffocation! That was what befell him at the Carnival. He had us all in the Piazza watching lightning strike him, and naught came of it."

"Hold thy bleating," retorted a tall shoemaker, stepping forward with bunches of slippers draped over his shoulders. "Seemeth to me, friend, that thou art as wise as a calf with water on its brain. The Frate will flinch from naught: perchance he saith nothing beforehand, but when the hour cometh, he shall walk through the fire without asking any grey-frock to keep him company. Yet, I would give a shoestring to know wherein this Latin lieth."

"There is so much thereof," sighed the shopkeeper, "save that I am fairly adept at guessing. Is there no scholar to be seen?" he added with a trace of disgust.

A general turning of heads revealed Tito approaching in their rear.

"Here is one," smiled the young sculptor, raising his cap.

"It is the secretary of the Ten," announced the shopkeeper, bowing low, though such marks of respect were rarely bestowed save upon the highest officials. "He goes to the convent, doubtless; make way." The reverence Tito elicited stemmed from the splendor and grace of his appearance; his black mantle, fastened with a gold fibula, resembled a royal robe, and his velvet cap seemed an entirely exceptional headgear. The hardening of his cheeks and mouth, the principal alteration in his visage since arriving in Florence, lent a sterner masculinity to his beauty. He raised his own cap instantly and addressed them—

"Thanks, my friend. I merely wished, as ye did, to behold what rests beneath this placard—ah, 'tis as I expected. I had been apprised that the government permits any who will to subscribe their name as a candidate to enter the fire—an act of liberality worthy of the magnificent Signoria, reserving, of course, the right to select. And doubtless many faithful souls will be eager to sign. What is it to enter the fire, to one whose faith is firm? A man fears the fire because he believes it will burn him; but if he believes the contrary?" Here Tito lifted his shoulders and paused oratorically—"for which reason I have never been wont to disbelieve the Frate when he hath said he would enter the fire to prove his doctrine. For in his place, if ye believed the fire would not burn ye, which of ye, my friends, would not step therein as readily as ye would walk along the dry bed of the Mugnone?"

As Tito scanned the assembly, a transformation swept over some of them, akin to the change in an eager dog invited to sniff something pungent. As the matter of burning became practical, few rashly committed themselves to a general view of the relation between faith and flame. The scene might have overwhelmed a gravity less under command than Tito's.

"Then, Messer Segretario," ventured the young sculptor, "it seemeth to me Fra Francesco is the greater hero, for he offereth to enter the fire for the truth, though he is certain the fire will consume him."

"I do not deny it," replied Tito, blandly. "Yet if it transpires that Fra Francesco is mistaken, he will have been burned for the wrong side, and the Church hath never reckoned such victims as martyrs. We must suspend our judgment until the trial hath truly taken place."

'Tis true, Messer Segretario,' murmured the shopkeeper with subdued impatience. 'But wilt thou favor us by interpreting the Latin?'

"Assuredly," said Tito, smiling. "It doth but express the conclusions or doctrines which the Frate specially teacheth, and which the trial by fire is to prove true or false. They are doubtless familiar to thee. First, that Florence—"

"Let us have the Latin bit by bit, and then tell us what it meaneth," urged the shoemaker, a frequent hearer of Fra Girolamo.

"Willingly," said Tito. "Ye shall then judge if I render the right meaning."

"Yes, yes; that's fair," assented Goro.

"_Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione_," recited Tito. "That is, the Church of God needeth purifying or regenerating."

"It is true," chorused several voices.

"That meaneth the priests ought to lead better lives; there needeth no miracle to prove that. That's what the Frate hath always been saying," observed the shoemaker.

"_Flagellabitur_," continued Tito. "That is, it shall be scourged. _Renovabitur_: it shall be purified. _Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur_: Florence also, after the scourging, shall be purified and shall prosper."

"That meaneth we shall get Pisa again," remarked the shopkeeper.

"And recover the wool from England as we used to do, I should hope," added an elderly gentleman in an old-fashioned beretta, who had remained silent until now. "There hath been scourging enough with the sinking of the trade."

At that instant, a tall personage, surmounted by a red feather, issued from the convent door and exchanged an indifferent glance with Tito. The secretary tossed his becchetto carelessly over his left shoulder and turned back to his reading, while the bystanders, with

The Prophet in His Cell.

Tito's arrival at San Marco had been anticipated, and Fra Niccolò, Savonarola's secretary, ushered him immediately up the spiraling staircases into the long, echoing corridors. These halls were lined with cells, some adorned with the frescoes of Fra Angelico. To the unaccustomed eye, these delicate paintings, soft as rainbows on melting clouds, appeared as sudden reflections from an ethereal realm, where the Virgin sat crowned in radiance and the Infant Christ gazed forth with perpetual promise.

It was an hour of quietude within the monastery; most cells stood empty. Through narrow slits, pale light illuminated nothing but bare plaster, a hard straw pallet, and a wooden crucifix. Yet, at the far end of a lengthy corridor, behind a door leading to an antechamber where the Prior typically worked, a shaft of sunlight struck a solitary figure. It was the back of a man clad in the long white tunic and scapular of the Dominican Order, kneeling with bowed head before the cross. To the casual observer, this might have been any ordinary friar, burdened only by trivial sins—a fleeting thought of impropriety during choir service, or a petty resentment when a colleague spilled ink upon his illuminations. Such a soul would seek merely the safety of Paradise via the narrow ladder of prayer, fasting, and obedience.

But beneath this simple garb beat a heart vibrating with a consciousness alien to the average monk, perhaps difficult even for a man of self-knowledge attained through turmoil to fully grasp. Within that breast, irrevocable errors and lapses from truth were so intricately woven with noble aspirations and sincere convictions that a clear path for the conscience remained elusive. Self-serving expediency was inextricably bound to the fabric of a grand design, one that the entire being seemed incapable of abandoning, just as the flesh could not ignore the glimmer and tremble of hope and dread.

Savonarola was not merely in the posture of prayer; Latin supplications flowed from his lips, yet his spirit was not truly praying. Having entered his cell and collapsed to his knees, he had sought a divine influx of calmness, hoping it would validate the resolve urged upon him by swirling thoughts and passions, assuring him that he was not being dragged away from God's support. However, the visions and impulses that had tormented him for the past hour were too imperious to be stilled. While his hands pressed against his face and his mouth articulated "Create in me a clean heart," his mind remained occupied with the intricate snares his enemies had devised, busy constructing arguments to counter their taunts and accusations.

His defense extended beyond his adversaries. Earlier that morning, he had received fresh evidence that his own friends and followers were as eager to press forward with the Trial by Fire as his foes. They desired, indeed tacitly expected, that he finally accept the challenge and evoke the long-awaited miracle designed to dispel doubt and vanquish malice. Had he not proclaimed that God would reveal Himself at the appointed time? To the plain Florentines, impatient to settle partisan disputes, no moment seemed more fitting than this. Surely, if Fra Domenico walked through the flames unscathed, *that* would be a miracle, and the fervor of that devoted brother served as a comforting omen. Yet Savonarola remained acutely aware that the secret, feverish longing of his disciples to see him step into the fire had not been quelled by his previous reasons for refusal.

Nevertheless, satisfying them proved impossible, and with bitter anguish, he realized he could no longer resist the prosecution of the trial regarding Fra Domenico. It was not that Savonarola had spoken a falsehood when declaring his belief in a future supernatural attestation of his work; his constitution allowed him to easily envision a miracle distant and undefined, shielded by strong reasons for its occurrence. It was equally easy for him to believe in internal miracles, such as his prophetic inspiration and divinely wrought intuitions. Conversely, it was insurmountably difficult for him to believe in the probability of a miracle involving specific details, like walking unharmed through fire, which demanded not only faith but extraordinary action.

Savarola's nature possessed a duality wherein opposing tendencies coexisted with near-equal strength. A passionate sensibility, impatient of fixed thought and flooding every idea with emotion, tended toward contemplative ecstasy, alternating with a keen perception of external facts and a vigorous, practical judgment of men and circumstances. In this instance, the latter qualities were stimulated into unusual activity by an acute physical sensitivity, which endowed the concept of pain and destruction with overpowering force. This sensitivity stemmed from the realization that such suffering was a necessary sequence of causes already felt. Just as men readily consent to touch red-hot iron with a wet finger despite theoretically knowing it will burn them—their practical belief depending on what is most vividly represented in the mind at the moment—so too did Savonarola's constitution react. When the Trial by Fire was presented as an immediate demand to his imagination, it became impossible for him to believe that he, or any other man, could traverse the flames unscathed. Even if he resolved to offer himself, he would inevitably shrink at the last instant.

However, the common Florentine was unlikely to make such nuanced distinctions. To the general run of mankind, finding moral questions easy and judging conduct by concise alternatives appears a mark of mental vigor. Nothing seemed plainer than that a man who once declared God would not leave him without a miraculous guarantee, yet recoiled when the proposal to test that declaration arose, had spoken what he did not truly believe. Were not Fra Domenico and Fra Mariano, along with countless *Piagnoni*, ready to enter the fire? What caused their superior courage if not superior faith? Savonarola could not explain his conduct satisfactorily to his friends, nor could he fully articulate it to himself. Human naked feelings hasten to clothe themselves in convenient propositions, and giving a true account of one's interior state requires something beyond mere sincerity, even when that sincerity is unmixed. In these very moments, as Savonarola knelt in audible prayer, he had ceased to hear the words on his lips. They were drowned by argumentative voices within, shaping reasons increasingly for an outward audience.

"To appeal to Heaven for a miracle by rashly accepting a challenge, which is but a snare prepared by ignoble foes, would be tempting God, and the appeal would remain unanswered. Let the Pope's legate come, let the ambassadors of all great powers arrive promising that the convocation of a General Council and the reform of the Church shall hinge upon this miracle, and *then* I will enter the flames, trusting that God will not withhold His seal from that great work. Until then, I reserve myself for higher duties directly laid upon me: it is not permitted to me to leap from the chariot merely to wrestle with every loud vaunter. Yet, Fra Domenico's invincible zeal to enter the trial may signify a Divine vocation, may serve as a pledge that the miracle—"

"No!" The thought shattered the sentence. When Savonarola brought his mind close to the threatened scene in the Piazza, imagining a human body stepping into the fire, his belief recoiled anew. It was not an event his imagination could simply visualize; he felt it with shuddering vibrations coursing through his sensitive extremities. The miracle could not occur. Indeed, the trial itself was not destined to take place; he was warranted in exerting all his power to prevent it. The fuel might be gathered in the Piazza, the crowd assembled, the formalities completed—all this was perhaps inevitable now, and he could no longer resist it without bringing dishonor upon himself, yes, and thereby upon the Cause of God. Yet, it was not truly intended that the Franciscan should enter the fire. While Savonarola hesitated, there would be means to prevent Fra Domenico's entrance. At the very worst, if Fra Domenico were compelled to proceed, he would carry the consecrated Host with him, and with that Mystery in his hand, there might be a warrant for expecting the ordinary effects of fire to be suspended. More probably, this demand would be resisted, thus becoming a final obstacle to the trial.

These intentions, however, could not be avowed. He must appear frankly awaiting the trial and trusting in its outcome. This dissidence between inward reality and outward semblance was not the Christian simplicity for which he had stroven through his youth and prime, nor the fruit of the Divine life he had preached. In the stress and heat of the day, with cheeks burning and shouts ringing in his ears, how blessed was he who remembered the yearnings of the cool, silent morning and knew he had not betrayed them?

"Oh God, it is for the sake of the people—for they are blind—and because their faith depends upon me. If I put on sackcloth and cast myself among the ashes, who will take up the standard and lead the battle? Have I not been led by a path unknown to the work that lies before me?"

The conflict could not end. In the effort of prayerful pleading, his uneasy mind laved its sting continually in thoughts of the greatness of that task for which no other man existed to fulfill it if he abandoned it. It was no commonplace matter for a man to be inspired with the vision and daring that made a sacred rebel.

Even the words of prayer had faded. He continued to kneel, but his mind was filled with images of consequences rippling across Europe. The sense of immediate difficulties was being lost in the glow of that vast vision when the knock at the door announced the expected visitor.

Savonarola drew on his mantle before leaving his cell, as was his custom when receiving guests. With that immediate responsiveness to any appeal from without which belongs to a

A week later, on the seventh of April, the Piazza della Signoria transformed into a scene of unparalleled magnitude, surpassing even the legendary Bonfire of Vanities. A crowd, denser than any that had ever pressed upon the square since the feast of San Giovanni, sought purchase on the cobblestones. They climbed every available ledge, clinging to iron rods jutting from walls, wedging themselves between pillars, and perching atop the stooped shoulders of ancient statues. Their limbs cramped and skins raw, they endured the physical discomfort for the sake of a single promise: to witness a miracle. While humanity often hears of wonders, few expect to stand before one unfolding in real time. The Florentines believed they would see at least half of it; if the monk emerged unscathed, the truth would speak for itself. If he burned, the judgment would be equally swift.

The stage for this drama was laid with formidable precision. Before the eyes of the throng lay a long platform, twenty yards wide, crowned with a terrifying grove of fuel: dry oak branches, crackling thorns, and tow soaked in oil, the ingredients of famous Florentine illuminations. This pyre stood before the Old Palace, looming beside the stern gaze of the stone lion, the Marzocco. To protect the spectacle, three distinct armies held the square: five hundred hired soldiers of the Signoria guarding the palace door, five hundred *Compagnacci* led by Dolfo Spini on the far side, and three hundred armed citizens under Marco Salviati, a close ally of Savonarola, positioned before Orgagna's Loggia.

The atmosphere hummed with anticipation. Half the Franciscan friars, clad in grey, had already filed into the loggia, filling its western wing in solemn silence. Opposite them, however, the eastern side erupted into a deafening chant of "Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered." The sound, amplified by the acoustics of the square, suggested a confidence bordering on hubris. Leading the procession was Fra Domenico, wrapped in a blaze of flame-colored velvet, his simple face lifted by genuine faith. Following him walked Savonarola, dressed in white priestly vestments, holding the consecrated Host. As he stepped onto the marble terrace, his countenance hardened under the scrutiny of thousands. His eyes burned with an intensity that seemed to absorb the gaze of the crowd, reflecting a duality that only he could feel: outwardly, he projected the invincible assurance of a prophet facing fire; inwardly, he wrestled with a heavy, leaden foreboding.

Savonarola was not merely playing a role today; he was fighting a battle against his own crumbling foundations. He struggled not against the threat of death, but against the imminent collapse of his reputation and power. Yet, amidst his turmoil, a shadow of relief flickered in his mind—a specific turn of events from two nights prior. News had reached the city that a courier of the Ten, dispatched to Milan, had been arrested and stripped of his dispatches. Consequently, Savonarola's secret letter regarding the conspiracy now rested in the hands of the Duke of Milan and, shortly thereafter, the Pope. This revelation would provide the ultimate justification for extreme measures against him.

Watching this unraveling from a high window of the Old Palace was Tito Melema. With a calm, self-gratulating smile, the scholar observed the chaos below. He viewed Savonarola not as a tragic figure, but as a clever, albeit superstitious, actor who had played his final hand poorly. Tito saw no malice in his observation, only the satisfaction of a game well-played. The bundle of human desires known as Savonarola had served Tito perfectly, providing the stepping-stones he needed to ascend to a position of smooth authority. Now, with the wind at his back, Tito prepared to leave Florence, leaving the "comedy" of the trial to unfold as a prelude to his departure.

Back in the square, the chanting swelled to a roar, joined by the *Piagnoni*, their red crosses bobbing like flags of devotion. They awaited the moment when the friars would step toward the pyre. But the expected movement did not occur. Instead, pale faces peered anxiously over the wooden dividers of the loggia. A hush fell over the crowd as rumors circulated that Fra Domenico was being summoned to the palace, perhaps to exchange clothes with a brother to avoid the heat of the flames. Moments later, indeed, a Dominican emerged, followed quickly by another, suggesting a frantic reshuffling behind closed doors.

Then, slowly, Fra Domenico reappeared, flanked by guards, having exchanged robes. The crowd sighed with relief; the Dominican would not burn. Attention shifted instantly to Savonarola. He remained motionless, save for a bold, ironic speech reminiscent of Elijah confronting the prophets of Baal. He demanded the cessation of delay, but his words fell flat on the ears of the starving, crammed masses. Why speak? If the miracle was not to begin, the fault lay with Savonarola himself for failing to offer himself to the fire when the wood was piled high.

Time slipped away as the clouds gathered, darkening the sky and sending a chill through the square. The air grew heavier, and the chanting faded into confused muttering and indistinct arguments. Suddenly, the focus shifted. Fra Domenico, desperate to prevent the profanation of the holy crucifix, refused to enter the fire without it. Savonarola, seeing an opening to concede, yielded, handing the vessel containing the Host to his brother. "Enter simply with the Sacrament," he proposed quietly.

Horror rippled through the Franciscans. "It is impious!" they cried. "If the Host burns, the scandal will be immense!" Savonarola countered firmly, invoking the scholastic distinction that accidents, not substance, would be consumed. The argument spiraled into a theological quagmire that neither side could resolve, leaving both trapped in a stalemate.

As the debate dragged on, the sky opened. Rain began to fall, soft at first, then with violent urgency. Streams of water ran off the platform, soaking the dry timber and extinguishing the kindling. The crowd, already hungry and disgusted, roared in frustration. "Light the fire!" "Drive them in!" "Where is the prophet?" The shouts dissolved into an uproar of clashing steel and trampled feet. Dolfo Spini and his *Compagnacci*, spurred by the irritation of the mob, surged forward toward the loggia, seeking to seize Savonarola. But Salviati's men held the line, repelling the rush until the tide turned back toward the Palazzo Rucellai.

In that moment of utter confusion, the heavens unleashed their wrath. The rain poured down, drowning the fuel and rendering the trial impossible. The crowd, drenched and furious, accepted the weather as divine intervention, a convenient excuse for the authorities to declare the matter settled. Both sides retreated, the fire never lit, the miracle never performed.

Yet, as the rain ceased and the broken clouds revealed a pale, indifferent light, Savonarola felt no triumph. He requested a guard from the Signoria, which was granted. He had told them he was willing to die for his cause, and he spoke the truth. But to die in dishonor—to be cast as a hypocrite and a false prophet? That was not martyrdom; it was the erasure of a life dedicated to justice. "Let me die because of the worth within me," he prayed silently, "not because of my weakness."

He walked away from the loggia, the Sacrament heavy in his hand. The light falling upon him offered no glory, no smile from heaven. Only the cold, impartial illumination that exposes the rot beneath the surface. As he descended the steps, Tito Melema greeted him with a mocking cheer. "Well parried, Frate!" the scholar called out. "But I fear your career at Florence is ended. What say you, my Niccolò?" Machiavelli, standing nearby, shrugged with a melancholy air. "It is a pity his falsehoods were not all of a wise sort," he murmured. "With the times so much on his side as they are concerning church affairs, he might have done something great."

Savonarola moved on, his courage mounting despite the moral attacks, knowing that the bitterest days were yet to come.

The following morning was Palm Sunday, known locally in the olive-rich Valdarno as Olive Sunday. The sky, washed clean by the previous night's rain, held a brilliance of terrible purity. Once more Savonarola ascended the rostrum of San Marco, gazing upon a congregation whose faith remained unbroken, yet their eyes held a solemn, tragic gravity. In a voice calm and heavy with foreknowledge, he proclaimed his readiness to die, seeing clearly behind every vision the shadow of his own execution. As he spoke the final benediction, he looked into the upturned, veneration-filled faces of the crowd, knowing that he must turn away from them forever.

Before the sun had fully surrendered the sky, Florence erupted into chaos. The grievances kindled the day prior, which had simmered beneath a deceptive quiet, now boiled over into a frenzy aided by malice and tacit approval from within the city's power structures. The disturbance began at the Duomo, where certain *Compagnacci* attempted to disrupt the evening sermon preached by the *Piagnoni*. But once blood surged and the gathering swelled into a mob, the cry rose, "To San Marco! Fire to San Marco!"

Long before dusk, the church and its adjoining convent were besieged by a swelling tide of rage. Resistance was fierce. The friars, acutely aware of mounting hostility, kept arms hidden within their walls; clad in their long white habits, they fought with the vigor of knights, their Dominican serges offering little protection against the steel of their attackers. Even Savonarola's authority could not quell the instinct for self-preservation. Laymen who had refused to flee joined the fray, hurling stones and burning embers from the roof while sword-fights raged in the cloisters near the high altar. Despite the overwhelming numbers of the assailants, the assault lasted until the deepest hours of night.

Throughout these hours, the Government's stance remained singular: the convent was the enemy. Early in the breach, guards were dispatched not to disperse the rioters, but to order everyone inside the walls to lay down arms, to expel all laymen, and to command Savonarola himself to leave Florentine soil within twelve hours. Had Savonarola obeyed then, he likely would have been torn apart; he was willing to go, but his friends interceded. It was deemed far too dangerous for any man of high standing to exit via the garden gate, yet among those who did slip away was Francesco Valori, hoping to rally aid from without.

By the time the struggle neared its inevitable conclusion, when the *Compagnacci* stood poised to storm the library where Savonarola knelt in prayer with the brethren who had sheathed their weapons, a second contingent of guards arrived. Commissioned by the Signoria, they demanded the surrender of Fra Girolamo and his two lieutenants, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

A roar of triumphant hatred followed as lantern-light revealed the friar stepping from the convent doors, escorted by a guard offering him nothing but the grim sanctuary of a prison. Now the contest became a race for position in the surging stream that flowed up the narrow streets, eager to witness the Prophet led in shame back to the Piazza where he had dared them days prior. Who should stand nearest to tear at his ears with insults, or strike him with blows? This was easily achieved by many armed *Compagnacci*, who mingled freely with the royal guard.

As Savonarola felt himself jostled and dragged through the jeering throng, illuminated by lanterns reflecting mocking faces, spat upon and kicked with words of vile abuse, it appeared to him that life's bitterest cup had been drained. If men deemed him guilty and thirsted for his blood, death alone awaited. Yet the deepest bitterness cannot be forced from without; the lowest abyss of resignation is not found in martyrdom, but in the silent realization that one is unworthy to bear the cross, that truth may prosper, but not through oneself.

However, this fleeting, imperfect victory of humiliating the friar, who soon vanished beneath the archway of the Old Palace, proved as insubstantial as blood to a tiger. Did not the homes of the hypocrite's allies await sack? Already half the armed multitude, lagging too far behind to participate heavily in the siege, turned to the more lucrative business of ransacking wealthy residences. They did not strike at random; they selected with discrimination the houses of the chief *Piagnoni*, revealing that this riot was guided, and that the rabble with clubs and staves was led by sword-bearing officers of the *Compagnacci*.

Next in rank of crime after the Friar himself stood the ambitious Francesco Valori, suspected of plotting with the Friar to secure the office of Doge or Gonfaloniere for life. And there was the gray-haired man who, eight months prior, had raised his voice in a ferocious call for justice against five citizens, only to escape San Marco and experience what others termed justice. His house was surrounded by an angry, greedy horde; his wife was slain by an arrow, and he himself was murdered as he traveled to answer a summons to the Palazzo, falling to the swords of men named Ridolfi and Tornabuoni.

Thus played out this Masque of the Furies, labeled simply as Riot, throughout the hours of night and the breaking dawn.

Yet the chief director remained invisible, issuing orders from a private retreat, for his name was too illustrious to let his red plume wave openly amidst the carnage. This retreat was the same house and chamber in a quiet street between Santa Croce and San Marco where Tito had previously paid a secret visit to Dolfo Spini. Here sat the Captain of the *Compagnacci* through this memorable night, receiving a revolving cast of visitors, some disguised as armed *Compagnacci*, others shrouded in obscurity without visible arms. Abundant wine filled the table, cups offered to chance arrivals, and though Spini guarded against excess, he drank often enough to heighten the fever of news constantly brought to him.

Among the visitors clothed in shadows, Ser Ceccone was the most frequent, remaining as Spini's constant companion as the hours advanced toward morning twilight, alongside Francesco Cei, who hid carelessly within Florence, awaiting the revocation of his banishment upon the Friar's fall.

Candles burned down to low, shapeless heaps, and holes in the shutters marked the somber outer light, when Spini, rising from his seat and pacing with an angry flush on his face at some conversation between his two unmilitary guests, suddenly burst forth—"The devil spit him! He shall pay for it, though. Ha, ha! The claws shall come down on him when he least expects them. So he was to be the great man after all! Pretending to toss everything toward my cap, as if I were a blind beggar, while all the while he winked and filled his own purse! I should like to hang skins about him and set my hounds on him! And he has that fine ruby of mine, I was a fool enough to give him yesterday. Malediction! And he laughed at me in his sleeve two years ago, ruining the best plan that ever was laid. I was a fool to trust myself with a rascal who had long-twisted contrivances that no one could see to the end but himself."

"A Greek, too," observed Francesco Cei, a slight smile of amusement playing on his lips at Spini's fury, "who dropped into Florence with gems packed about him. You did not choose your confidant very wisely, my Dolfo."

"He is a cursed deal cleverer than you, Francesco, and handsomer too," Spini retorted, turning on his associate with a general desire to worry anything that presented itself.

"I humbly conceive," interjected Ser Ceccone, "that Messer Francesco's poetic genius will outweigh—"

"Yes, yes, rub your hands! I hate that notary's trick of yours," interrupted Spini, whose patronage relied largely on this frankness. "But here comes Taddeo, or someone else: now is the time! What news, eh?" he continued as two *Compagnacci* entered with heated looks.

"Bad!" said one. "The people have made up their minds they were going to sack Soderini's house, and now they have been balked; we shall have them turning on us if we do not take care. I suspect there are some Mediceans buzzing about among them, and we may see them attacking your palace over the bridge before long, unless we can find another bait for them."

"I have it!" cried Spini, seizing Taddeo by the belt and drawing him aside to whisper his instructions, while the other went on telling Cei how the Signoria had intervened regarding Soderini's home.

"Ecco!" exclaimed Spini, presently, giving Taddeo a slight push toward the door. "Go, and make quick work."

On the far bank of the Arno, where the chill grey twilight deepened, another figure emerged from the shadows, bound for a desolate stretch of the riverbank. Unlike the armed Compagnacci moving elsewhere, whose errands were violent and communal, this man sought isolation. He made his way to a spot overlooked by no dwellings, hidden behind warehouses and granaries that turned their backs upon the water. Here, a sloping expanse of long grass and rushes grew damp, fed by broad gutters emptying their murky contents directly into the swift, channeled flow of the river.

These gutters and the profound silence drew him. He sat down among the reeds, bending over the rushing water, waiting for a chance to feed. He remembered times when a kind "runlet" had slipped him a large piece of bread, or a raw carrot and apple parings. In a world where visibility meant danger, this secluded corner offered a sanctuary for survival. Often, in his restless wakefulness, he returned here before dawn, hoping to catch another scrap and thereby avoid the humiliating, silent begging on the church-steps beyond the Porta San Frediano.

Baldassarre hated the thought of begging more than anything else; he would have chosen death over the act. Yet, he desired to live. This desire was no longer anchored in hope, but in a persistent, haunting possibility that clung to every obsession that completely possesses the mind—a delirious certainty akin to a woman watching a headland for a ship known long ago to be a wreck. After leaving the convent hospital of San Miniato, where monks had cared for him in his helpless state, he had waited in vain for the Wife who was to aid him. She had vanished, likely claimed by the pestilence that seemed to consume everything since the night he parted from her. He had been unable to conceive how sacred vengeance could be satisfied through his arm alone. His knife was lost, and his body too frail to earn another weapon through labor; his mind, shattered and bewildered, too weak to devise a plan even if he possessed the blade. He was an old man, broken and lonely, yet he waited. He awaited something dim and formless, a vague spark that startled him and caused strong pulsations within his chest, much like the phantom objects we seek when jolted awake from sleep by no voice or touch. He lived for this undefined future, creeping out into the grey light to sit in the long grass and watch the waters, which held a faint, elusive promise.

While Baldassarre waited in silence, the Compagnacci were busy with their work. These formidable bands of armed men, left to their own devices by an embarrassed, perhaps complicit, Signoria, had split into two masses but converged swiftly toward the Arno. The smaller group headed for the Ponte Rubaconte, the larger for the Ponte Vecchio. A single phrase had passed from mouth to mouth like a signal, uniting the rabble: they were going to the Via de' Bardi to sack a specific house. If they knew no other reason, what better justification could exist? The leaders of these streaming mobs understood something more: a brief command flew between them, ensuring that both streams would meet before a particular house near the eastern end of the street, where the master was expected to be asleep and caught unawares.

However, the master of that house was neither sleeping nor in bed. Tito's anxiety regarding his departure from Florence, spurred by the investigations of the previous day, had kept him awake. He feared that any delay would invite further inquiry, and in stalling, he sensed only impending danger. Falsehood had flourished and grown strong, but it had nurtured its twin, Fear. Though he no longer wore armor and was no longer terrified of Baldassarre specifically, a spirit had risen from the corpse of that dead fear—the undying habit of fear. He felt unsafe anywhere within this fierce, turbid Florence until he could escape. Now, he was ready. Maso was to hand over his house to a new tenant; his horses and mules awaited him in San Gallo; Tessa and the children were lodged for the night in the Borgo outside the gate, dressed and ready to mount and join him.

Tito descended the stone steps into the courtyard, passing through the great doorway. He was not the same man who had first entered this house and made the fatal mistake of falling in love with Romola; that mistake was remedied, and the old life was soon to be far behind him. He turned with rapid steps toward the Piazza dei Mozzi, intending to cross the Ponte Rubaconte. However, certain sounds reached his ears, causing him to wheel around and walk faster in the opposite direction. Was the mob approaching from Oltrarno? It was a vexation, as he would have preferred a private road. He decided he must now take the Ponte Vecchio. Unpleasant sensations made him draw his mantle tight around him, forcing him to walk at his utmost speed. Though no one saw him in the grey twilight, before he reached the end of the Via de' Bardi, the sounds fell upon his ear again, much louder and nearer. Had he been deceived before? The mob must be crossing the Ponte Vecchio. Driven by an impulse of fear stronger than reflection, he turned again, only to confirm that the crowd was indeed entering the street from the opposite end. He chose not to return to his house; surely they would not attack *him*. Still, he carried valuables, and with a mob, all things except reason and order became possible. Necessity, however, performed the work of courage. He proceeded toward the Ponte Vecchio, the rush, trample, and confused voices growing so loud ahead that he could no longer hear them behind.

Having reached the end of the street, the crowd pouring from the bridge hemmed him in. He had no time to wonder at a sudden shout before he felt himself surrounded—not initially by unarmed rabble, but by armed Compagnacci. The next sensation was his cap falling from his head as he was thrust violently forward into the throng, driven along the narrow passage of the bridge. Then the shouts erupted: "Piagnone! Medicean! Throw him over the bridge!" His mantle was torn from him with strong pulls that would have throttled him if the fibula holding it together had not given way. Someone snatched at his *scarsella* (purse), but amidst the hustle and drag, the snatch failed, and it remained at his side. Shouting, yelling, and half-motiveless execrations rang stunningly in his ears, spreading even among those who had not yet seen him and only knew there was a man to be reviled. Tito's horrible dread was that he would be struck down or trampled before reaching the open arches spanning the center of the bridge. His sole hope lay in being thrown over before they wounded him or beat the strength out of him; his entire soul was absorbed in this hope and its terrifying reverse.

Yes, they were at the arches. In that critical moment, Tito, with a bloodless face and dilated eyes, experienced one of those self-preservation inspirations that arrive in extremity. With a sudden, desperate effort, he mastered the clasp of his belt and flung the belt and *scarsella* forward toward a yard of clear space against the parapet, crying in a ringing voice, "There are diamonds! There is gold!"

In the instant the hold on him relaxed, a rush surged toward the purse. Tito threw himself onto the parapet with a desperate leap, and the next moment he plunged—plunged with a great splash—into the dark river far below.

It was his chance of salvation, and a good one. His life had been saved once before by his fine swimming, and as he rose to the surface after a long dive, he felt a sense of deliverance. He struck out with all the energy of his strong youth, aided by the current. If he could swim beyond the Ponte alla Carraia, he might land in a remote part of the city and eventually reach San Gallo. Life was still before him, and the idiot mob shouting and bellowing on the bridge would undoubtedly think him drowned.

They did think so. Peering over the parapet along the dark stream, they could not see the moving blackness of floating hair or the velvet sleeves of his tunic drifting downstream.

It was only from the other side that a pale olive face could be seen looking white above the dark water—a face not easy even for the indifferent to forget, marked by a square forehead, long low arches of eyebrows, and long, lustrous, agate-like eyes. Onward the face drifted on the dark current, with inflated, quivering nostrils and blue veins distended on the temples. One bridge was passed—the Bridge of Santa Trinita. Should he risk landing now rather than trust to his strength? No. He heard, or fancied he heard, yells and cries pursuing him. Terror pressed him most from the side of his fellow-men; he was less afraid of indefinite chances and swam on, panting and straining, not as fresh as he would have been had he slept the night away.

Yet the next bridge—the last—was passed. He was conscious of it, but in the tumult of his blood, he could only feel vaguely that he was safe and might land. But where? The current was doing its work upon him; he hardly knew where he was. Exhaustion was bringing on the dreamy state that precedes unconsciousness.

Now, eyes discerned him—aged eyes, strong for the distance. Baldassarre, looking up blankly from his futile search in the gutter that yielded

Romola's Awakening.

From the depths of a dream, where the flickering torchlight of the Bargello court burned brighter and brighter until darkness dissolved, Romola drifted into a profound slumber, only to wake again into a restless, vivid reverie. Finally, her consciousness cleared. The heavy, agitated visions of the past had slipped away like smoke, replaced by the soft, indistinct sensations of a new dawn. She stretched her arms, not in the stone courtyard, but in the silence of a boat anchored in a secluded creek. To her right stretched the flawless, sapphire expanse of the Mediterranean; to her left, a verdant curve of mountains draped in gentle, shadowed slopes, leading up to a rugged height crowned by a solitary bell tower.

The sun, rising in the east, cast oblique rays upon the western corner of this crescent bay, leaving the rest bathed in a cool, dewy shadow. The air was utterly still; the water seemed to have curved itself into a perfect circle of repose. The warm sunlight touched Romola, thrilling her skin like a caress. For a fleeting moment, mere passive existence became a kind of Lethe, washing away the turmoil of her recent life. In this exquisite, indefinite quiet, the agonies of the evening faded, and the tranquil dreams of her girlhood returned. She felt no desire, no memory, only a profound, untroubled rest.

Gradually, however, the spell broke. She realized she was lying in a boat that had borne her across the waters all night, not to a grave, but to a sanctuary. Despite her despair of the previous day, she was grateful for the return of morning, for the familiarity of the sunlight rather than the unknown realm of death. *Could* she not remain here forever? No sounds from Florence could reach this hidden cove. Already, though, the oblivion was disturbed. Through the golden haze of the morning, the shapes of domes, towers, and walls pierced the silence, separated by a river and hemmed in by green hills.

Romola rose, sitting up in the boat, willing to resist the sudden rush of thoughts concerning her location. How far had the current carried her? Why did it matter? Here was a sheltered haven where simple villagers dwelt, unlikely to cause harm. For now, she could rest and resolve on nothing. Soon, she would procure bread and milk, nestle into this green solitude, and find a pause in her chaotic life. Turning her gaze back to the valley, she sought to reclaim the soothing peace she had felt upon first waking.

Her contemplation lasted but minutes before a piercing cry shattered the stillness. It was not a brief wail but a continuous, intensifying scream. Romola felt certain it was a child in distress, unheard by any neighbor. Starting up, she placed one foot on the shore, ready to leap, but paused to listen. Surely the mother was nearby; surely the cry would soon stop. Instead, it grew louder, drawing her irresistibly forward, sounding more pitiful precisely because it contrasted with the preceding peace. Before she knew where she was heading, she had leaped onto the beach and walked many paces.

The sound seemed to emanate from a rough, ruined hovel nestled among wild vegetation yards to her right. Climbing a broken stone fence, she traversed patches of weedy greens and neglected cornfields. The cry grew clearer, confirming her direction, yet as she hurried toward the hut, an oppressive change in the air seized her. Was there some taint lurking beneath this lush greenery, which had seemed such an inviting refuge from the coming heat? Seeing the entrance to the hovel, she felt the cry dart through her like a physical pain.

Stepping inside, the somber light revealed a scene that struck her with a shock of awe and horror. Lying on the straw-strewn floor were three dead bodies: a tall man, a girl of about eight, and a young woman whose long black hair was being clutched and pulled by a living child—the source of the screaming. Romola, hardened by her experiences in the haunts of death and disease, acted with prompt thought and swift motion. She lifted the shivering child to her bosom, attempting to soothe it, while casting furtive glances at the corpses to confirm they were truly dead. Their strongly marked features and peculiar garb suggested they were Spanish or Portuguese Jews, perhaps cast ashore by rapacious sailors who saw their property as plunder. Such cruelties, driven by greed and superstition, were common to those forced from their homes by the Inquisition.

*"Surely,"* Romola thought, *"I shall find a woman in this village whose maternal heart will not allow her to refuse aid to this helpless infant, even if the true mother is indeed dead."*

Yet a doubt lingered. While the man and the girl appeared emaciated and had been dead for some time, the young woman seemed harder, retaining the robustness of her form. Kneeling, Romola prepared to check for a pulse beneath a piece of yellow woolen drapery covering the bosom. But lifting the cloth, she saw purple spots marking the familiar pestilence. Instantly, her fears shifted. If the villagers knew of the plague, they might shrink from her with the child in her arms. However, she possessed money; they would not refuse goat's milk in exchange for gold.

Setting out for the nearest cluster of houses, her mind filled with the effort to soothe the dark, trembling creature, she wondered how she might win a neighbor's compassion. She hoped slightly in the awe she might inspire, appearing unknown and unexpected in her religious attire. As she crossed a stretch of cultivated ground, she noticed with wonder that patches of corn were left to over-ripeness, untouched by the sickle, while golden apples and dark figs rotted on the weedy earth. There were grassy spaces, yet no sign of a cow, sheep, or goat. The stillness had acquired a fearful quality, and she hurried toward the thickest group of dwellings, seeking the life necessary to plead for the helpless life she carried. She had picked up two figs along the way, biting small pieces from the sweet pulp to quiet the child.

Between two lines of dwellings, she entered a street where villagers should have been stirring long ago, but not a soul was in sight. The air grew increasingly oppressive, laden with some horrible impurity. An open door revealed grim emptiness; another showed a man lying dead, dressed in his clothes, his head resting across a spade handle, an earthenware cruse in his hand as if he had collapsed suddenly. Horror took possession of Romola. Was she in a village of the unburied dead? She wished to listen for any faint sound, but the child cried out anew when she ceased feeding it, filling her ears.

Finally, she saw a figure crawling slowly from a house, sinking back into a sitting posture against the wall. Hastening toward it, she found a young woman in fevered anguish, holding a pitcher. As Romola approached, the sufferer did not start; the urgent need was too absorbing for any other thought.

"Water!" gasped the woman, "Get me water!"

Romola stooped, taking the pitcher gently. "You shall have water," she whispered in her ear. "Can you point toward the well?"

The hand lifted toward the distant end of the street. Romola set off at once, moving as fast as her burden allowed. The child, however, grew calmer as the morsels of sweet pulp were repeated, ceasing to distress her with cries, allowing Romola to pay less distracted attention to her surroundings.

The well lay twenty yards beyond the street's end. As Romola approached, her eyes were drawn to the opposite green slope below the church. High up, between the trees, she had spotted a cow and a couple of goats. Trying to trace a path to that cheering sight after her errand, she was unaware of how near she was to the well, nor of someone approaching from the other side who fixed astonished eyes upon her.

Romola presented a sight that, in that place and moment, demanded a pause and palpitation. Her gaze was fixed intently on the distant slope, the long lines of her thick grey garment giving a gliding character to her rapid walk. Her hair rolled backward, illuminated on the left side by the sun-rays. On her right arm, a little olive baby with jet-black eyes gazed out. She might well startle the youth of fifteen accustomed to swinging censers before a Madonna less fair and marvellous than this.

"She carries a pitcher to fetch water for the sick," the youth murmured, awestruck. "It is the Holy Mother, come to care for those stricken by the pestilence."

It was a vision of awe. Perhaps she would be angry with those who fetched water only for themselves. Terrified, the youth flung down his vessel and ran, fleeing as if for his life toward the slope she had been contemplating. Remembering the parched sufferer, Romola half-filled her pitcher quickly and hastened back.

Entering a house to find a small cup, she saw salt meat and meal stored there; there were no signs of want. With nimble movements, she seated the baby on the ground and lifted a cup of water to the sufferer, who drank eagerly, then closed her eyes and leaned back, surrendering to the sense of relief. Presently, she opened her eyes and looked at Romola.

"Who are you?" she asked languidly.

"I came over the sea," Romola replied. "I arrived this morning. Are all the people dead

In those hushed, wintry hours when Romola lay spent from her labors, her mind drifted backward through the river of days, peering into the misty shore of tomorrow. Everything she had witnessed since awakening in the boat struck her with the sudden, clarifying impact of a fresh seal pressed upon dissolving wax. For the first time, she felt untethered, unburdened by motive, sinking into a selfish lament that existence offered her no satisfaction, whispering a desperate wish for death. That cry had broken from her lips as sleep took hold, yet from the moment she woke to the sound of the weeping, reflection held no sway. Unlike her former days in Florence, where she had rationalized her survival as a duty to alleviate sorrow, she now simply *lived*. A fierce, impulsive energy surged within her, driving her to share the burdens of the world, to answer every cry of need, and to perform the labor that screamed for completion, without ever needing to construct an argument for why she must endure.

This transformation felt like a second baptism. In Florence, the simple connections between human beings had become tangled for her by the heavy knots of marriage, statecraft, and religious devotion; when these institutions failed her, the blow had severed her from life and numbed her compassion. Now, however, she declared, "It was merely base in me to crave death. If all else remains uncertain, this suffering that I can ease is absolute truth; if the glory of the cross is a phantom, the sorrow it relieves is the only reality." As long as strength remained in her arm, she vowed to extend it to the fainting; as long as vision stayed in her eyes, she would guide those left in darkness.

Then, the past rose up with a renewed urgency. Her work in this green valley was concluded, and the emotions freed from the immediate circle of neighbors rushed back into the deep, ancient channels of habit and love. This rare silence, born of total separation from her accustomed life, allowed her to judge herself with a severity unknown before. The remorse inherent in a sympathetic soul, acutely aware of the pain of others, stirred within her with growing force. She questioned the justice of her own judgments and actions: she had been rash, arrogant, perpetually unsatisfied because others lacked virtue while she had betrayed the highest principles her soul once recognized. She condemned her flight as a cowardly act of self-preservation, realizing that the grounds Savonarola had used to welcome her back were far truer and deeper than the flimsy excuses for her second departure. How could she claim to feel the needs of strangers while ignoring the desperate plight of those closest to her?

Yet, a counter-current swiftly arose against this torrent of self-reproach. The vivid memories of her life with Tito flooded her mind—the circumstances that rendered their spiritual union impossible, while their outward bond forced upon her a mask of false duties, concealing and sanctifying what her spirit revolted against. These recollections convinced her that flight had indeed been her only recourse. All minds, save those dulled by ignorance, must wrestle with this recurring torment where life's intricate tangles forbid the fulfillment of a sacred vow. For in strictest truth, no relation can truly replace another; the arrival of the new does not erase the breach of the old. Life, imperfect and maimed as it is, casts conscience backward constantly, offering doubting glances until the wounds heal completely.

Romola shrank with terror at the prospect of returning to Tito, yet unease gnawed at her regarding his fate. She feared the moment might soon arrive when he suffered and required her aid. A lingering thread of pain testified to Fra Girolamo's words: she could never truly cease to be a wife. Could anything ever utterly vanish from her heart that had once mingled with the very current of her blood?

Florence, and every shadow and sunbeam of her youth there, returned to her like a ravenous hunger. Her feelings refused to wander aimlessly among possibilities; their vital fiber was nourished only by the memory of familiar streets and faces. The thought that she had irrevocably severed ties with them grew increasingly insistent during these idle, empty hours. What if Fra Girolamo had erred? What if the life of Florence was a tapestry woven from inconsistencies rather than truths? Was she truly some exalted being, destined to shake the dust from her feet and declare, "This world is not fit for me"? If she possessed such loftiness, surely she would not have lost her faith so easily.

The indignant grief she had harbored for her godfather no longer held complete dominion over her spirit; instead, her profound sense of debt to Savonarola regained its ascendancy. Nothing that had occurred, nor anything yet to come, could obliterate the fact that he had kindled a new life within her through a great inspiration. Who, in all her vast experience, could rightfully demand equal gratitude? Yet, the shadow of his errors lingered, raising the chilling question: might they not unleash calamity?

Rest eluded her. She could scarcely discern whether it was the returning vigor of spring, signaled by the budding leaves, that fueled her activity, or an eager, magnetic longing to draw nearer to Florence. Though she did not imagine herself having the courage to step directly into the city, a powerful desire compelled her to remain close enough to discern what transpired there, a purpose that eclipsed all others.

On one crisp March morning, the villagers gathered to witness the departure of the Blessed Lady. Jacopo had procured a sturdy mule for her journey and prepared to accompany her over the mountains. The Padre likewise traveled with her to the nearest town, intending to assist her in finding the safest route to Pistoja. Her hoard of trinkets and coin, untouched in the valley, stood ample to meet her needs.

Had Romola's heart been less tethered to the valley, leaving it would have been a harrowing trial. Walking the village street for the final time, she found her progress impeded by the wailing crowd. People knelt to kiss her hands, clinging to her skirts to press kisses onto the grey folds, pleading, "Ah, why will you leave, when the good season begins and the crops promise plenty? Why must you go?"

"Do not mourn," Romola answered gently. "You are well now, and I shall keep your memory alive. I must go to see if my own kin require me."

"Ah, yes—if they have the pestilence!" someone cried out.

"Look upon us again, Madonna!"

"Yes, yes, we will be faithful to little Benedetto!"

Finally, Romola mounted the mule. Yet, a frantic, vigorous scream from Benedetto, seeing her turn away, served as an excuse for the entire assembly to follow, insisting that the child must ride upon the mule's neck down to the foot of the slope. The parting was inevitable, yet as Romola paused and turned repeatedly before vanishing from sight, she beheld the little flock lingering, desperate to catch the final wave of her hand.

April 14th marked Romola's return to the walls of Florence, a city she had fled with a trembling heart yet felt irresistibly drawn back to. Her journey from Pistoia, where conflicting rumors of Savonarola's trial by fire unsettled her, to Prato, had been driven by a dread that paradoxically propelled her forward. At Prato, she encountered a friar from San Spirito, former confessor to her late father, who became the conduit for the terrible truths awaiting her. He spoke of the wagon bearing Savonarola into the Piazza, describing the scene witnessed by the black-robed monks who had followed it. With a tone heavy with the prejudice typical of the Frati Neri toward their fallen brother, the monk revealed that Savonarola had confessed to deceiving the people, his spirit broken under torture. More devastating still, he recounted the fate of Tito. Though he had leaped into the Arno to escape a frenzied mob, he had perished on the riverbank, slain by an old enemy who harbored ancient grudge. To Romola, however, the monk's account offered no solace, nor did it capture the depth of the tragedy; she alone understood the weight of his sacrifice and the finality of their separation.

By evening, Romola sat in agitated silence within the city, surrounded by the cacophony of grief and jubilation. Monna Brigida, her companion, filled the air with frantic narratives, her hair hastily restored from its wild state during Romola's absence, now combed and pinned with a desperate determination to keep her "dear child" close. Yet, Romola remained impervious to Brigida's gossipy details. The shadow of Tito's murder and Savonarola's fall cast a pall so thick that she could scarcely register the trivialities around her. Her mind, burdened by a profound, awe-struck sorrow, focused entirely on two urgent objectives: to locate Savonarola, if possible, and to discover the whereabouts of Tessa and her children.

"Tell me, cousin," Romola interrupted abruptly, cutting short Brigida's ramblings about future domestic arrangements, "since Tito's passing, has anyone seen or heard of a young woman accompanied by two small children?"

Brigida gasped, her hands rising in alarm. "Christ! No! Was he so wicked as to abandon them? Ah, then, that explains your departure. You left me believing you had chosen freedom, unaware of the depths of your despair. If I had known..." She trailed off, shaking her head. "Well, never mind. Since that was your reason for fleeing—"

"No, cousin," Romola pressed, her voice firm despite her fragility, "do not speak thus. Above all, I must find that woman and her children. They are utterly helpless. Let it be my first act."

"If that makes you a Piagnone," Brigida muttered, shrinking back, "then I have been counting peas instead of prayers. Two children!" she cried softly. "But if you intend to raise others' offspring, surely you must first secure them."

"Cousin, the poor soul knows no wrong," Romola whispered, unable to bear the speculation. "I will explain later. First, let us find them."

Dawn broke early the following morning, guiding Romola to the house beyond San Ambrogio where she had previously sought refuge with Tessa. There, however, lay only emptiness. Tito must have sent her away beforehand, perhaps intending to retrieve them later, though Romola feared he could not bring himself to part with the children. A painful conjecture took root: Tessa was likely wandering outside Florence, trapped in a state of wondering, helpless misery at some roadside halt. Neighbors confirmed only that the elderly Lisa had departed a week prior with her belongings, offering no clue as to Tessa's destination.

Days stretched into weeks of anxious inactivity. Despite the urging of others, Romola could not muster the strength to launch a broad search. Her nature, reserved and sensitive, forbade her from exposing Tessa's connection to Tito, fearing judgment. Still, every glimpse of round brown legs or a peasant's dress caused her heart to race. She did not view this vigilance as heroic charity, but rather as a binding necessity; she needed someone to protect, a reason to cling to life amidst the ashes of her world. She dreamed of clutching those children, of weaving a bond that would heal the wounds of her past sorrows. While Tito's estate held claims to wealth, Romola distrusted its purity and resolved to surrender most of it to the State, retaining only enough to purchase books for her father's library—a modest sum sufficient for Tessa's survival.

Yet, Monna Brigida's influence proved insistent. She clamored that Romola must reside with her permanently, arguing that if one wished to nurture strangers' children, the household must expand to include them. "Find them first," Brigida urged, her voice tinged with superstition and hope, echoing Romola's own internal conviction. Some unseen force must guide them together.

A new path emerged through rumor: Tito had arranged horses and mules at San Gallo, indicating his intent to flee the city via that gate. Romola resolved to investigate, walking the Via San Gallo beneath her long widow's veil, scanning the crowds for any sign of Tessa. As she passed, she spotted Bratti, the itinerant peddler, haggling near a stall. A glimmer of hope ignited within her when she noticed something dangling from his basket.

"Bratti, my friend," she called out, approaching him with sudden urgency, "where did you acquire this necklace?"

"It is worth money, madonna," Bratti replied calmly, his gaze steady. "But I cannot sell it; I have pledged it, bound by a promise. My heart is too tender for trade."

"Tell me where you obtained it. Did you receive it from a little woman named Tessa?"

"Aye," Bratti admitted, his expression softening. "If you know her and would redeem it for a florin, sparing her the pain of selling it, you perform a great kindness. She wept as if plunging into a brook when she parted with it. I charge you little, for I am not hard-hearted."

Romola handed over the coin, unclipping the necklace from the basket with trembling fingers. "Where is she?"

"Just beyond the gate at the far end of the Borgo," Bratti indicated, pointing toward Old Sibilla Manetti's house. "Any neighbor will direct you."

With wings of purpose, Romola hastened toward the destination. The house stood quiet, but inside, in a dim chamber, Tessa and the children awaited. They had lingered there far longer than intended, possessing neither coin nor provisions, save for their tattered garments. But when Romola knocked, the door swung open instantly.

Within, Tesa sat upon a low bed, her tears having dried into hollow sobs, staring vacantly at her two children. Lillo covered his face with his skirt, roaring to terrify Ninna, who cowered in the corner, only to peek out, testing the limits of her fear. The children played in a corner distant from the door, oblivious to the moment until it arrived. Romola stepped inside, removing her veil, holding the necklace aloft.

"Tessa!"

The name shattered the silence. Tessa sprang to her feet, turning with wide, startled eyes.

"Look," Romola said gently, sliding the beads onto Tessa's neck. "God has brought me back to you."

A scream tore from Tessa's lips, followed by a flood of sobs as she collapsed into Romola's embrace, clinging desperately to the arms that held her sacred token. The children, sensing the shift in atmosphere, crawled from their corner, grasping their mother's skirts and gazing upward with wide, bewildered eyes at the stranger who had returned.

That same day, they traveled to Monna Brigida's dwelling in the Borgo degli Albizzi. In the safety of the new home, Romola gently broke the news to Tessa that Naldo could never return—not due to cruelty, but because death had taken him. "Be comforted, my Tessa," Romola soothed, pressing her hand to the grieving mother's shoulder. "I am here to care for you forever. And now, we have Lillo and Ninna."

Monna Brigida watched the reunion, her lips twitching in a mixture of reverence and suppressed ambition. She wished to speak, to assert her authority over the household, yet held her tongue, knowing that in the presence of Romola, the soul's ownership seemed suspended. "Let it wait," she thought, watching the "puss-faced minx" who clearly lacked worldly wisdom. "Some day I shall remind her of her place. Until then, I must ensure she remains humble."

Later, as supper prepared, Brigida's practical nature surfaced. She insisted on treating the children with excessive sweets, confessing to Romola just before retiring that it would be a shame to neglect such cherubs. "You must yield to me on their feeding, Romola," Brigida argued, her voice laced with maternal pride. "You have never borne a babe, whereas I gave birth to twins, though they died swiftly after arrival. Surely, experience guides the hand."

By the time Romola returned home with Tessa and the children, the month of April was drawing to a close. Yet, a far heavier burden weighed upon her spirit than the passing season: the scandalous publication of Fra Girolamo's trial. More accurately, it was the transcript of his confessions, extracted by sixteen Florentine citizens commissioned by the Signoria to interrogate him. This document, released officially, had ignited a firestorm of public suspicion and outrage. So fierce was the backlash that the authorities moved swiftly to recall it. However, fate is rarely so tidy; copies were inevitably scattered, and within days, an unauthorized second edition found its way into the hands of eager, hungry readers.

Romola, having begun to fear that she would never speak to Fra Girolamo again, devoured this evidence repeatedly. She sought a light to cut through the contradictory assertions swirling through the city—from the partisans who still clung to him to his bitterest enemies—who offered nothing but noise. To the devout followers of Savonarola, the friar's lack of constancy under torture and his retraction of prophetic claims caused a consternation so deep it took time to heal. Soon, however, a darker rumor took root among them: that any words contradicting their faith, specifically the report of the notary Ser Ceccone, had not escaped the prophet's lips but were forged by a hand of malice. They suspected the notary of fabricating a "digest" to serve his own ends.

Yet, the official record carried obvious stains. Half the panel of sixteen examiners consisted of the prophet's fiercest foes. Among them stood Dolfo Spini, a notorious figure recently elevated to the new Eight—a body clearly stacked to ensure the downfall of a man whose destruction had already been decided by the powerful parties in control. To many, the proceedings in the Old Palace resembled a murder dressed in the slow, formal robes of justice. The Signoria, desperate for a trade with the Pope and the Duke of Milan, viewed Savonarola as a nuisance to the corrupt clergy, greedy foreign tyrants, and vicious citizens alike. They had already sentenced him; the only debate remaining was transactional: Would the Republic deliver him alive to the Papacy in exchange for lifting taxes on ecclesiastical property, or would the Pope grant Florence the dignity of executing its own prophet on the Piazza?

Under such treacherous circumstances, could anyone accept the validity of this so-called confession? If the Friar had truly renounced his gift, it was likely wrung from him by the agonizing pressure of torture—an agony that, given his sensitive constitution, must quickly drive a man to madness. What if the wicked examiners claimed he suffered only three times over a single day, free from physical coercion, before confessing? Who could believe that? He had been tortured far more extensively, proportional to the distress his words caused in the hearts of those who loved him.

Others, less ardent in their devotion, did not deny the substance of the confession, though they chafed at its implications. They argued indignantly that no evidence warranted a death sentence, let alone the fire. To any impartial observer, if this examination stood as the sole proof against him, the Friar died not for a crime, but because he had become an inconvenience to the Pope, to the rapacious Italian states eyeing Tuscany, and to ambitious citizens seeking to undermine the common weal.

No shadow of political crime hung over him. No stain marred his private conduct. His former monks, including a secretary of many years' standing who possessed above-average culture, testified even after the shock of his retraction to a life of unimpeachable purity and consistency. The Pope himself could find no charge of heresy save for disobedience to a mandate and the disregard of an excommunication sentence.
